"Wooed and married and a'."

"Edith!" Margaret whispered, her voice soft and gentle. "Edith!"

But as Margaret half suspected, her cousin had already surrendered to sleep. Edith lay curled upon the crimson damask sofa in the back drawing-room of Harley Street, a vision of white muslin and blue ribbons. If the fairy Titania had ever donned such attire and drifted off upon a similar cushion, she could hardly have been distinguished from the sleeping girl. Margaret, struck anew by the radiant beauty of her cousin, felt a fresh pang of affection. They had grown up side by side since childhood, yet while the world had long remarked upon Edith's prettiness, Margaret had remained oblivious to it until recently. Now, with the impending loss of her constant companion looming, every sweet quality and charm Edith possessed seemed amplified. Their conversation had drifted from wedding dresses and ceremonies to Captain Lennox's descriptions of life at Corfu, where his regiment was stationed, and the formidable challenge of keeping a piano in tune during the Scottish visits that would follow their marriage. But the whispers had grown drowsier; after a pause, Margaret realized that despite the hum of voices in the next room, Edith had rolled herself into a soft ball of muslin, ribbon, and silken curls, drifting into a peaceful afternoon nap.

Margaret had been on the verge of sharing her own visions of the future—the quiet life awaiting her in the country parsonage where her parents resided, a place where her brightest holidays had once been spent, though for the last decade, Aunt Shaw's house in London had been her true home. Deprived of a listener, she was forced to brood silently on the coming change. It was a happy brooding, albeit tinged with regret at the indefinite separation from her gentle aunt and beloved cousin. As she contemplated the joy of becoming the only daughter in Helstone parsonage, fragments of conversation from the adjacent room drifted to her ears.

Aunt Shaw was speaking to the five or six ladies who had dined there, whose husbands remained in the dining-room. These were the familiar acquaintances of the house, neighbors whom Mrs. Shaw termed "friends" simply because she dined with them more frequently than others, and because they were willing to exchange calls before luncheon if either she or Edith needed something. These ladies and their husbands had been invited as friends to a farewell dinner honoring Edith's approaching marriage. Edith had initially objected, knowing Captain Lennox was expected by a late train that very evening; yet, being a spoiled child who lacked a strong will, she had yielded when her mother insisted on ordering the extra delicacies of the season, deemed efficacious against the grief of farewells. Edith contented herself by leaning back, playing absently with the food on her plate, looking grave and distant, while everyone else enjoyed the witty remarks of Mr. Grey, the gentleman who invariably sat at the bottom of the table and had asked Edith to provide music for the drawing-room. Mr. Grey was particularly agreeable tonight, and the gentlemen lingered downstairs longer than usual—a mercy, judging by the snippets Margaret overheard.

"I suffered too much myself," a voice murmured. "Not that I was not extremely happy with the poor dear General, but disparity of age is a drawback; one I was resolved Edith should not encounter. Without maternal partiality, I foresaw the dear child was likely to marry early; indeed, I often said I was sure she would be wed before nineteen. I had quite a prophetic feeling regarding Captain Lennox"—here the voice dropped to a whisper, but Margaret easily supplied the missing name. The course of true love in Edith's case had run remarkably smooth. Mrs. Shaw had yielded to this presentiment, urging the marriage forward, even though it fell below the expectations many held for a young, pretty heiress. Mrs. Shaw declared her only child should marry for love, sighing emphatically as if love had not been her motive for marrying the General. She seemed to enjoy the romance of the current engagement more than her daughter did. Edith was thoroughly and properly in love, yet she would certainly have preferred a grand house in Belgravia to all the picturesque hardships of life at Corfu. The very aspects that made Margaret glow with anticipation caused Edith to pretend to shiver and shudder; partly for the pleasure of being coaxed out of her dislike by her fond lover, and partly because anything resembling a gypsy or makeshift existence was truly distasteful to her. Yet, had anyone offered a fine house, a vast estate, and a noble title, Edith would have clung to Captain Lennox while the temptation lasted. Once it faded, she might well have harbored little-concealed regrets that her husband could not unite every desirable trait in his person. In this, she was merely her mother's child, who, after deliberately marrying General Shaw out of respect for his character and establishment, constantly, though quietly, lamented her hard lot in being united to one she could not love.

"I have spared no expense in her trousseau," another voice declared. "She possesses all the beautiful Indian shawls and scarves the General gave me, which I shall never wear again."

"She is a lucky girl," replied another voice, which Margaret recognized as Mrs. Gibson, a lady taking a double interest in the conversation because one of her daughters had married within the last few weeks. "Helen had set her heart upon an Indian shawl, but when I found the extravagant price, I was obliged to refuse her. She will be quite envious when she hears of Edith's shawls. What kind are they? Delhi? with the lovely little borders?"

Margaret heard her aunt's voice again, rising as if she had lifted herself from her half-recumbent position to gaze into the dimly lit back drawing-room. "Edith! Edith!" she cried, then sank back as if weary by the exertion. Margaret stepped forward.

"Edith is asleep, Aunt Shaw. Is there anything I can do?"

All the ladies exclaimed "poor child!" upon receiving this distressing news, and the minute lap-dog in Mrs. Shaw's arms began to bark, seemingly excited by the burst of pity.

"Hush, Tiny! You naughty little girl! You will wake your mistress. It was only to ask Edith if she would tell Newton to bring down her shawls; perhaps you would go, Margaret dear?"

Margaret ascended to the old nursery at the very top of the house, where Newton was busy preparing laces required for the wedding. While Newton muttered grumblings as he went to undo the shawls—which had already been displayed four or five times that day—Margaret looked around the nursery. It was the first room in that house with which she had become familiar nine years ago, when she was brought, untamed from the forest, to share the home, play, and lessons of her cousin Edith. She recalled the dark, dim atmosphere of the London nursery, presided over by an austere and ceremonious nurse who was terribly particular about clean hands and torn frocks. She remembered the first tea served there—separate from her father and aunt, who dined far below, an infinite depth of stairs; for unless she were up in the sky, the children thought, they must be deep down in the bowels of the earth. At home, before moving to Harley Street, her mother's dressing-room had been her nursery; and as they kept early hours in the country parsonage, Margaret had always eaten with her parents. Oh! How well the tall, stately girl of eighteen remembered the tears shed with such wild passion of grief by the little girl of nine, hiding her face under the bedclothes on that first night; how she was bid not to cry by the nurse lest it disturb Miss Edith; and how she had wept more quietly and bitterly until her newly seen, grand, pretty aunt arrived with Mr. Hale to show him his little sleeping daughter. Then the little Margaret had hushed her sobs, striving to lie quiet as if asleep, fearing to make her father unhappy by her grief, which she dared not express before her aunt and which she rather thought wrong to feel after the long hoping, planning, and contriving they had undertaken at home to arrange her wardrobe for her grander circumstances and to bring her father up to London, even for a few days.

Now she loved the old nursery, though it was dismantled, and she surveyed it with a kind of cat-like regret at the idea of leaving it forever in three days.

"Ah, Newton!" she said. "I think we shall all be sorry to leave this dear old room."

"Indeed, miss, I shan't for one. My eyes are not so good as they were, and the light here is so bad I can't see to mend laces except just at the window, where there's always a shocking draught—enough to give one one's death of cold."

"Well, I dare say you will have both good light and plenty of warmth at Naples. You must keep as much of your darning as you can till then. Thank you, Newton, I can take them down—you're busy."

Thus Margaret descended, laden with shawls, inhaling their spicy Eastern scent. Her aunt asked her to stand as a sort of lay figure to display them, as Edith remained asleep. No one noticed, but Margaret's tall, finely made figure, clad in a black silk dress worn as mourning for a distant relative of her father's, set off the long, beautiful folds of the gorgeous shawls that would have half-smothered Edith. Margaret stood directly under the chandelier, silent and passive, while her aunt adjusted the draperies. Occasionally, as she was turned, she caught a glimpse of herself in the mirror over the chimney-piece and smiled at her own appearance—the familiar features in the usual garb of a princess. She touched the shawls gently as they hung around her, taking pleasure in their soft feel and brilliant colors, and rather liked to be dressed in such splendor, enjoying it much as a child would, with a quiet, pleased smile on her lips.

Just then the door opened, and Mr. Henry Lennox was suddenly announced. Some of the ladies started back, half-ashamed of their feminine interest in dress. Mrs. Shaw held out her hand to the newcomer; Margaret stood perfectly still, thinking she might still be needed as a block for the shawls, but looking at Mr. Lennox with a bright, amused face, certain of his sympathy in her sense of the ludicrousness of being thus surprised.

Her aunt was so absorbed in asking Mr. Henry Lennox—who had not been able to attend dinner—all sorts of questions about his brother the bridegroom, his sister the bridesmaid (coming from Scotland with the Captain), and various other members of the Lennox family, that Margaret realized she was no longer wanted as a shawl-bearer. She devoted herself to amusing the other visitors, whom her aunt had momentarily forgotten. Almost immediately, Edith emerged from the back drawing-room, winking and blinking her eyes against the stronger light, shaking back her slightly ruffled curls, and looking altogether like Sleeping Beauty just startled from her dreams. Even in her slumber, she had instinctively felt that a Lennox was worth rousing herself for. She had a multitude of questions to ask about dear Janet, the future, unseen sister-in-law, for whom she professed such affection that, had Margaret not been very proud, she might have almost felt jealous of the mushroom rival. As Margaret sank further into the background upon her aunt joining the conversation, she saw Henry Lennox directing his gaze toward a vacant seat near her; she knew perfectly well that as soon as Edith released him from her questioning, he would take possession of that chair. She had not been quite sure, from her aunt's rather confused account of his engagements, whether he would come that night; it was almost a surprise to see him, and now she was assured of a pleasant evening. He liked and disliked nearly the same things she did. Margaret's face lightened into an honest, open brightness. Soon he arrived. She received him with a smile devoid of any tinge of shyness or self-consciousness.

"Well, I suppose you are all in the depth of business—ladies' business, I mean. Very different to my business, which is the real true law business. Playing with shawls is very different work to drawing up settlements."

"Ah, I knew how you would be amused to find us all so occupied in admiring finery. But really Indian shawls are very perfect things of their kind."

"I have no doubt they are. Their prices are very perfect, too. Nothing wanting."

The gentlemen began to drop in one by one, and the buzz and noise deepened in tone.

"This is your last dinner-party, is it not? There are no more before Thursday?"

"No. I think after this evening we shall feel at rest, which I am sure I have not done for many weeks; at least, that kind of rest when the hands have nothing more to do, and all the arrangements are complete for an event which must occupy one's head and heart. I shall be glad to have time to think, and I am sure Edith will."

"I am not so sure about her, but I can fancy that you will. Whenever I have seen you lately, you have been carried away by a whirlwind of some other person's making."

"Yes," said Margaret, rather sadly, remembering the never-ending commotion about trifles that had persisted for over a month. "I wonder if a marriage must always be preceded by what you call a whirlwind, or whether in some cases there might not rather be a calm and peaceful time just before it."

"Cinderella's godmother ordering the trousseau, the wedding-breakfast, writing the notes of invitation, for instance," said Mr. Lennox, laughing.

"But are all these quite necessary troubles?" asked Margaret, looking up straight at him for an answer. A sense of indescribable weariness regarding all the arrangements for a pretty effect, in which Edith had been busied as supreme authority for the last six weeks, oppressed her just then; and she truly wanted someone to help her find a few pleasant, quiet ideas connected with a marriage.

"Oh, of course," he replied, his tone shifting to gravity. "There are forms and ceremonies to be gone through, not so much to satisfy oneself as

"By the soft green light in the woody glade,
On the banks of moss where thy childhood played..."

Margaret stood once more in her morning attire, traveling quietly homeward with her father, who had journeyed north to assist at the wedding. Her mother remained behind, held captive by a multitude of half-reasons that defied clear explanation to anyone save Mr. Hale. He alone understood that his earnest pleas for a grey satin gown—somewhere between the faded and the fresh—had fallen on deaf ears. Lacking the funds to outfit his wife anew from head to toe, Mrs. Hale simply would not make the trip to her only sister's only child's nuptials. Had Mrs. Shaw guessed the true cause of her sister's absence, she might have flooded the parsonage with gowns; but nearly twenty years had passed since Mrs. Shaw was the poor, pretty Miss Beresford, and she had long since forgotten all grievances save the unhappiness born of age disparity in marriage. She could discourse on this topic for half an hour at a stretch. "Dearest Maria married the man of her heart," Mrs. Shaw would muse, noting the groom was merely eight years her senior, possessing a sweet temper and that rare blue-black hair. "He is one of the most delightful preachers I've ever heard, a perfect model of a parish priest." From these premises, Mrs. Shaw drew her characteristic, illogical conclusion: "Married for love, what can dearest Maria wish for?" To which Mrs. Hale, if she chose to speak truth, might have replied with a ready list: "A silver-grey glacé silk, a white chip bonnet, dozens of things for the wedding, hundreds for the house."

Margaret knew only that her mother had deemed it inconvenient to come, and she was secretly relieved that their reunion would occur at Helstone parsonage rather than amidst the chaotic whirlwind of the last few days in Harley Street, where she had played the part of Figaro, summoned everywhere simultaneously. Her mind and body ached with the recollection of everything she had done and said within forty-eight hours. The hurried farewells, especially those of those she had lived with so long, now pressed upon her with a sad regret for times that were gone forever. It mattered not what those times had been; they were lost. As the carriage rolled toward her dear home—the place and life she had yearned for since childhood—Margaret felt a heaviness in her heart she had never anticipated. Yet, she forced her mind away from the past to the bright, serene contemplation of the hopeful future. Her eyes adjusted to the present reality: her father leaning back asleep in the railway carriage. His blue-black hair was now grey, lying thinly over his brow. The bones of his face were plainly visible, too stark for beauty unless his features were less finely cut; yet, they possessed a grace of their own. His face was in repose, but it was the rest of weariness, not the serene calm of a placid life. Margaret was painfully struck by this worn, anxious expression. She traced the lines of habitual distress back to the open facts of her father's life.

"Poor Frederick!" she sighed inwardly. "Oh! if Frederick had been a clergyman instead of going into the navy, and being lost to us all! I wish I knew all about it. I never understood it from Aunt Shaw; I only knew he could not return to England because of that terrible affair. Poor dear papa! How sad he looks! I am so glad I am going home, to be at hand to comfort him and mamma."

She was ready with a bright smile, devoid of fatigue, to greet her father upon his awakening. He smiled back, faintly, as if it were an unusual exertion. His face settled back into its lines of anxiety. He had a habit of half-opening his mouth as if to speak, which constantly unsettled the form of his lips, giving his face an undecided expression. Yet, he retained the same large, soft eyes as his daughter—eyes that moved slowly and almost grandly in their orbits, veiled by transparent white eyelids. Margaret was more like him than like her mother. Sometimes people marveled that parents so handsome should have a daughter who was so regularly beautiful; occasionally, they whispered she was not beautiful at all. Her mouth was wide, not a rosebud that opened just enough to utter a "yes" or "no," but a wide curve of rich red lips. Her skin, though not white and fair, possessed an ivory smoothness and delicacy. While her general demeanor was too dignified and reserved for one so young, now, talking to her father, it was bright as the morning—full of dimples and glances that spoke of childish gladness and boundless hope.

It was the latter part of July when Margaret returned home. The forest trees were a dark, full, dusky green; the fern below caught the slanting sunbeams. The weather was sultry and broodingly still. Margaret used to tramp along by her father's side, crushing down the fern with a cruel glee as she felt it yield under her light foot, sending up its peculiar fragrance. Out on the broad commons into the warm, scented light, she saw multitudes of wild, free creatures revelling in the sunshine and the herbs it called forth. This life—at least these walks—realized all her anticipations. She took a pride in her forest; its people were her people. She made hearty friends with them, learned and delighted in their peculiar words, took up her freedom amongst them, nursed their babies, and talked or read with slow distinctness to their elders. She carried dainty messes to the sick and resolved soon to teach at the school where her father went daily as an appointed task, though she was continually tempted off to visit some individual friend—man, woman, or child—in a cottage in the green shade of the forest. Her out-of-doors life was perfect. Her in-doors life had its drawbacks. With the healthy shame of a child, she blamed herself for her keen sight in perceiving that all was not as it should be there. Her mother, always so kind and tender, seemed now and then discontented with their situation. She thought the bishop strangely neglected his episcopal duties in not granting Mr. Hale a better living, and almost reproached her husband for not wishing to leave the parish for a larger charge. He would sigh aloud, saying that if he could do what he ought in little Helstone, he should be thankful, yet every day he seemed more overpowered; the world became more bewildering. At each repeated urging from his wife to seek preferment, Margaret saw her father shrink further, and she strove to reconcile her mother to Helstone. Mrs. Hale claimed the near neighbourhood of so many trees affected her health, and Margaret would try to tempt her forth onto the beautiful, broad, upland common. For a time, this did good; but as autumn drew on and the weather grew changeable, her mother's idea of the place's unhealthiness increased, and she repined more frequently that her husband, more learned than Mr. Hume and a better priest than Mr. Houldsworth, had not met with the preferment those two former neighbours had achieved.

This marring of the peace of home by long hours of discontent was what Margaret was unprepared for. She had known, and rather revelled in the idea, that she would have to give up many luxuries which had been troubles and trammels to her freedom in Harley Street. Her keen enjoyment of every sensuous pleasure was balanced finely, if not overbalanced, by her conscious pride in being able to do without them all if need were. But the cloud never comes from the quarter of the horizon from which we watch for it. There had been slight complaints and passing regrets from her mother regarding trifles connected with Helstone and her father's position when Margaret spent her holidays there previously, but in the general happiness of recalling those times, she had forgotten the small, unpleasant details.

In the latter half of September, the autumnal rains and storms arrived, and Margaret was obliged to remain more in the house than she had hitherto done. Helstone was at some distance from neighbours of their own standard of cultivation.

"It is undoubtedly one of the most out-of-the-way places in England," said Mrs. Hale in one of her plaintive moods. "I can't help regretting constantly that papa has really no one to associate with here; he is so thrown away, seeing no one but farmers and labourers from week's end to week's end. If we only lived at the other side of the parish, it would be something; there we should be almost within walking distance of the Stansfields; certainly the Gormons would be within a walk."

"Gormons," said Margaret, "are those the Gormons who made their fortunes in trade at Southampton? Oh! I'm glad we don't visit them. I don't like shoppy people. I think we are far better off, knowing only cottagers and labourers, and people without pretence."

"You must not be so fastidious, Margaret, dear!" said her mother, secretly thinking of a young and handsome Mr. Gormon whom she had once met at Mr. Hume's.

"No! I call mine a very comprehensive taste; I like all people whose occupations have to do with land; I like soldiers and sailors, and the three learned professions. I'm sure you don't want me to admire butchers and bakers and candlestick-makers, do you, mamma?"

"But the Gormons were neither butchers nor bakers, but very respectable coach-builders."

"Very well. Coach-building is a trade all the same, and I think a much more useless one than that of butchers or bakers. Oh! how tired I used to be of the drives every day in Aunt Shaw's carriage, and how I longed to walk!"

And walk Margaret did, in spite of the weather. She was so happy out of doors, at her father's side, that she almost danced; and with the soft violence of the west wind behind her as she crossed some heath, she seemed borne onwards, lightly and easily as a fallen leaf wafted by the autumnal breeze. But the evenings were rather difficult to fill agreeably. Immediately after tea, her father withdrew into his small library, leaving her and her mother alone. Mrs. Hale had never cared much for books and had discouraged her husband's desire to read aloud to her while she worked early in their married life. At one time they had tried backgammon as a resource, but as Mr. Hale grew to take an increasing interest in his school and parishioners, he found the interruptions arising from these duties were regarded as hardships by his wife, not to be accepted as the natural conditions of his profession. So he withdrew, while the children were yet young, into his library to spend his evenings in reading the speculative and metaphysical books which were his delight.

When Margaret had been there before, she had brought down a great box of books recommended by masters or governesses, finding the summer's day too short to complete her reading before returning to town. Now, there were only the well-bound, little-read English Classics, weeded out of her father's library to fill the small bookshelves in the drawing-room. Thomson's *Seasons*, Hayley's *Cowper*, Middleton's *Cicero* were by far the lightest, newest, and most amusing. The shelves afforded little resource. Margaret told her mother every particular of her London life, to which Mrs. Hale listened with interest, sometimes amused and questioning, at others slightly inclined to compare her sister's circumstances of ease and comfort with the narrower means at Helstone Vicarage. On such evenings, Margaret was apt to stop talking abruptly and listen to the drip-drip of the rain upon the leads of the little bow window. Once or twice, she found herself mechanically counting the repetition of the monotonous sound, wondering if she might venture to ask where Frederick was now, what he was doing, and how long it had been since they had heard from him. But a consciousness that her mother's delicate health and positive dislike to Helstone dated from the time of the mutiny in which Frederick had been engaged—the full account of which Margaret had never heard, and which now seemed doomed to be buried in sad oblivion—made her pause and turn away from the subject each time she approached it. When with her mother, her father seemed the best person to apply to for information; and when with him, she thought she could speak more easily to her mother. Probably there was nothing much to be heard that was new. In one of the letters she had received before leaving Harley Street, her father had told her they had heard from Frederick; he was still at Rio, very well in health, and sent his best love. These were dry bones, not the living intelligence she longed for. Frederick was always spoken of, in the rare times his name was mentioned, as "Poor Frederick." His room was kept exactly as he had left it, regularly dusted and put into order by Dixon, Mrs. Hale's maid, who touched no other part of the household work but always remembered the day she had been engaged by Lady Beresford as ladies' maid to Sir John's wards, the pretty Miss Beresfords, the belles of Rutlandshire. Dixon had always considered Mr. Hale the blight that had fallen upon her young lady's prospects. If Miss Beresford had not been in such a hurry to marry a poor country clergyman, there was no knowing what she might have become. But Dixon was too loyal to desert her in her affliction and downfall. She remained with her, devoted to her interests, always considering herself the good and protecting fairy whose duty it was to baffle the malignant giant, Mr. Hale. Master Frederick had been her favourite and pride, and it was with a little softening of her dignified look and manner that she went weekly to arrange the chamber as carefully as if he might be coming home that very evening.

Margaret could not help believing there had been some late intelligence of Frederick, unknown to her mother, which was making her father anxious and uneasy. Mrs. Hale did not seem to perceive any alteration in her husband's looks or ways. His spirits were tender and gentle, readily affected by any small piece of intelligence concerning the welfare of others. He would be depressed for many days after witnessing a death-bed or hearing of any crime. But now Margaret noticed an absence of mind, as if his thoughts were

"Learn to win a lady's faith
Nobly, as the thing is high;
Bravely, as for life and death—
With a loyal gravity.

Lead her from the festive boards,
Point her to the starry skies,
Guard her, by your truthful words,
Pure from courtship's flatteries."

Margaret's thoughts had drifted to Henry Lennox only moments prior, recalling his inquiry regarding her domestic duties. It was a sentiment akin to the French proverb, "parler du soleil et l'on en voit les rayons"—to speak of the sun and to see its rays. A genuine brightness illuminated Margaret's face as she set down her embroidery board and stepped forward to greet him. "Tell Mamma, Sarah," she said, turning to her companion. "Mamma and I have so many questions for you concerning Edith; I am deeply grateful for your visit."

"Did I not promise that I would?" he replied, his voice dropping to a softer register than before.

"But I heard of you only from the distant Highlands," Margaret noted, surprised. "I never imagined Hampshire could reach you."

"Oh!" he dismissed the notion lightly. "Our young couple were engaging in such reckless pranks—climbing mountains, sailing on lakes—that I felt compelled to act as a Mentor. They were entirely beyond my uncle's supervision, keeping the old gentleman in a state of panic for sixteen hours a day. Upon realizing they could not be trusted alone, I deemed it my duty to remain until I had seen them safely embarked at Plymouth."

"You have been to Plymouth? Edith never mentioned that. She has been writing so hurriedly lately. Did they actually sail on Tuesday?"

"They certainly did, relieving me of considerable responsibility. Edith entrusted me with various messages for you. I believe I have a small note somewhere; yes, here it is."

"Oh! thank you," Margaret exclaimed. Yet, half-wishing to read the note privately, she feigned a need to inform her mother again—perhaps Sarah had made a mistake—that Mr. Lennox had arrived.

Once Margaret had withdrawn, Henry began his characteristic scrutiny of the room. The drawing-room appeared at its finest in the morning light. The central window of the bow stood open, allowing clusters of roses and scarlet honeysuckle to spill onto the sill; the lawn below was vibrant with verbenas and geraniums. However, the brilliance of the outdoors rendered the interior colors somewhat pale by comparison. The carpet showed signs of age, the chintz had been washed frequently, and the entire apartment seemed smaller and shabbier than he anticipated, serving as a humble backdrop for Margaret, who stood before him with a queenly grace. He picked up a book from the table: *The Paradise of Dante*, bound in white vellum and gold. Beside it lay a dictionary and a page of words copied in Margaret's handwriting. The list was dull, yet he found himself lingering over it with a sigh before setting it down.

"The living is evidently as modest as she described," he murmured. "It seems strange, considering the Beresfords are of a respectable family."

Meanwhile, Margaret had located her mother. It was one of Mrs. Hale's fitful days, where every circumstance felt like a hardship. Mr. Lennox's arrival took on a burdensome shape, though she secretly felt complimented that he deemed it worthy of a call.

"It is most unfortunate!" Mrs. Hale fretted. "We are dining early today, serving only cold meat so the servants may finish ironing. Yet, of course, we must invite him—Edith's brother-in-law and all. And your father is in such low spirits this morning about something—I know not what. I entered the study just now, and he had his face buried in his hands, covering it completely. I told him I was certain the Helstone air agreed with neither him nor me, and he suddenly lifted his head, begging me not to speak another word against Helstone. He claimed he could not bear it; if there is one place he loves on earth, it is Helstone. Nevertheless, I am convinced it is the damp, relaxing atmosphere."

Margaret felt as though a thin, cold cloud had descended between her and the sunlight. She had listened patiently, hoping her mother's venting might bring relief, but now it was time to redirect her attention to Mr. Lennox.

"Papa adores Mr. Lennox; they bonded famously at the wedding breakfast. I daresay his presence will lift Papa's spirits. And do not worry about the dinner, dear Mamma. Cold meat suits a lunch perfectly, which is precisely how Mr. Lennox will view a two o'clock meal."

"But what shall we do with him until then? It is only half-past ten."

"I shall ask him to go sketching with me. I know he draws, and that will remove him from your path, Mamma. Only do come in now; he will find it peculiar if you do not."

Mrs. Hale removed her black silk apron and smoothed her face. She appeared a lovely, ladylike woman as she greeted Mr. Lennox with the cordiality due to a near relative. He evidently expected to spend the day and accepted the invitation with a glad readiness that made Mrs. Hale wish she could improve the quality of the cold beef. He was pleased with everything, delighted by Margaret's suggestion of a sketching excursion, and eager to avoid disturbing Mr. Hale, anticipating their meeting at dinner. Margaret produced her drawing materials for his selection. After the paper and brushes were chosen, the two departed in high spirits.

"Now, please, stop here for a moment," Margaret instructed. "These are the cottages that haunted me during the rainy fortnight, reproaching me for failing to sketch them."

"Before they collapsed and vanished. Truly, if they are to be sketched—and they are quite picturesque—we should not delay until next year. But where shall we sit?"

"Oh! You might have come straight from chambers in the Temple instead of spending two months in the Highlands! Look at this magnificent trunk of a tree, left by the woodcutters in the perfect spot for the light. I will drape my plaid over it, creating a regular forest throne."

"With your feet in that puddle for a regal footstool! Stay, I will move, and then you can approach closer. Who resides in these cottages?"

"They were constructed by squatters fifty or sixty years ago. One is uninhabited; the foresters intend to demolish it once the other tenant, poor old fellow, passes away! Look—there he is. I must speak to him; he is so deaf you will overhear all our secrets."

The old man stood bareheaded in the sun, leaning on his stick before his cottage. His stiff features relaxed into a slow smile as Margaret approached. Mr. Lennox quickly incorporated the two figures into his sketch, finishing the landscape with a subordinate reference to them—as Margaret observed later, when they packed their water and scraps of paper to compare their work. She laughed and blushed; Mr. Lennox watched her countenance intently.

"Now, I call that treacherous," she said. "I little thought you were making old Isaac and me into subjects when you asked me to inquire about the cottages' history."

"It was irresistible. You cannot imagine the strength of the temptation. I hardly dare tell you how much I shall enjoy this sketch."

He was uncertain whether she heard this last remark before she went to the brook to wash her palette. She returned flushed, yet appearing perfectly innocent and unconscious. He was glad, for the words had slipped from him unawares—a rare occurrence for a man like Henry Lennox, who premeditated his actions with such precision.

The atmosphere of home brightened upon their return. The clouds on her mother's brow had lifted under the auspicious influence of a brace of carp, conveniently presented by a neighbor. Mr. Hale had returned from his morning rounds and awaited his visitor just outside the wicket gate leading to the garden. He looked like a complete gentleman in his slightly threadbare coat and well-worn hat. Margaret felt a fresh, tender pride in her father, always struck by how favorably he impressed strangers; yet her keen eye detected traces of unusual disturbance on his face, a turmoil that was merely set aside, not erased.

Mr. Hale requested to examine their sketches.

"I believe you have made the tints on the thatch too dark, have you not?" he remarked, returning Margaret's drawing and extending his hand for Mr. Lennox's, which was withheld for a fleeting moment.

"No, Papa! I do not think so. The house-leek and stone-crop have grown significantly darker in the rain. Does it not look right, Papa?" she asked, peering over his shoulder as he inspected the figures in Mr. Lennox's drawing.

"Yes, very accurate. Your posture and carriage are capital. And it captures poor old Isaac's stiff way of stooping his long, rheumatic back perfectly. What hangs from the branch of the tree? Not a bird's nest, surely."

"Oh, no! That is my bonnet. I can never draw with it on; it makes my head too hot. I wonder if I could manage figures. There are so many people here whom I would like to sketch."

"I should say that a likeness you earnestly desire to capture you would always succeed in," Mr. Lennox replied. "I have great faith in the power of will. I think I have succeeded quite well with yours." Mr. Hale had already preceded them inside, while Margaret lingered to pluck some roses to adorn her morning gown for dinner.

"A regular London girl would understand the implied meaning of that speech," Mr. Lennox thought. "She would be adept at detecting the *arrière-pensée* in every compliment a young man offers. But I do not believe Margaret—" Suddenly, he exclaimed, "Let me help you"; and he gathered some velvety cramoisy roses beyond her reach. Dividing the spoils, he placed two in his buttonhole and sent her inside, pleased and happy, to arrange her flowers.

The dinner conversation flowed quietly and agreeably. Questions were exchanged freely regarding Mrs. Shaw's movements in Italy, and amidst the interest of the topics discussed, the unpretending simplicity of the parsonage life—especially in Margaret's company—caused Mr. Lennox to forget the initial disappointment he felt upon learning the modesty of her father's living.

"Margaret, my child, you might have gathered some pears for our dessert," Mr. Hale suggested as a bottle of wine was uncorked.

Mrs. Hale moved hurriedly. Desserts seemed impromptu and unusual at the parsonage, whereas, had Mr. Hale glanced behind him, he would have seen biscuits and marmalade arranged formally on the sideboard. The idea of pears had seized Mr. Hale's imagination and could not be dispelled.

"There are a few brown *beurrés* against the south wall worth all foreign fruits and preserves. Run, Margaret, and gather some."

"I propose we adjourn to the garden and eat them there," Mr. Lennox suggested. "Nothing is as delicious as biting into crisp, juicy fruit, warm and scented by the sun. The only drawback is that the wasps are impudent enough to dispute it with one, even at the very climax of enjoyment."

He rose to follow Margaret, who had vanished through the window, awaiting Mrs. Hale's permission. She would have preferred to conclude the dinner with proper ceremony, especially since she and Dixon had retrieved the finger-glasses from the store-room to appear as correct as General Shaw's widow's sister; but as Mr. Hale rose immediately and prepared to accompany his guest, she could only submit.

"I shall arm myself with a knife," Mr. Hale declared. "The days of eating fruit so primitively as you describe are over with me. I must pare and quarter it before I can enjoy it."

Margaret fashioned a plate for the pears using a beet-root leaf, which highlighted their brown-gold hue beautifully. Mr. Lennox looked more at her than at the fruit; her father, inclined to select the very essence of the moment he had stolen from his anxieties, chose the ripest fruit daintily and sat on the garden bench to savor it at leisure. Margaret and Mr. Lennox strolled along the terrace under the south wall, where bees still hummed industriously in their hives.

"What a perfect life you seem to lead here!" Mr. Lennox exclaimed. "I have always felt somewhat contemptuously toward poets with their wishes for 'a cot beside a hill,' but now I fear I have been nothing better than a Cockney. Just now, I feel as if twenty years of hard legal study would be amply rewarded by one year of such an exquisite, serene existence as this—such skies!" He looked upward. "Such crimson and amber foliage, so perfectly motionless as that!" He pointed to the great forest trees enclosing the garden like a nest.

"You must remember that our skies are not always this deep blue. We have rain, and our leaves fall and become sodden. Though I think Helstone is about as perfect a place as any in the world. Recall how you rather scorned my description of it one evening in Harley Street: 'a village in a tale.'"

"Scorned, Margaret? That is rather a harsh word."

"Perhaps it is. Only I know I would have liked to discuss what filled my mind at the time, and you... what shall I call it then?—spoke disrespectfully of Helstone as a mere village in a tale."

"I will never do so again," he said warmly. They turned the corner of the walk.

"I could almost wish, Margaret..." He stopped, hesitating. It was so unusual for the fluent lawyer to hesitate that Margaret looked up at him with questioning wonder. But in an instant, from some internal cause she could not identify, she wished herself back with her mother, her father, anywhere away from him, certain he was about to say something she would not know how to answer. In another moment, the strong pride within her conquered her sudden agitation, which she hoped he had not perceived. Of course she could answer, and answer correctly; it was poor and despicable of her to shrink from hearing any speech, as

"Cast me upon some naked shore,
Where I may tracke
Only the print of some sad wracke,
If thou be there though the seas roare,
I shall no gentler calm implore."

He was gone. The house stood silent for the evening, stripped of its usual vibrant hues. Margaret ascended to prepare for the early tea, finding Dixon in a foul temper, his irritation stemming from the interruption a visitor had caused on such a busy day. He displayed his mood by brushing Margaret's hair with vicious haste, claiming urgency to reach Mrs. Hale. Yet, despite his haste, Margaret was forced to wait a long time in the drawing-room before her mother descended. She sat alone by the fire, unlit candles standing like silent witnesses behind her, reflecting on the day's trajectory: the joyous walk, the pleasant sketching, the cheerful dinner, followed by the miserable, uncomfortable stroll in the garden.

How vastly different men were from women! Here she was, disturbed and unhappy because her instinct had rendered a refusal impossible; while he, mere minutes after meeting with a rejection of what ought to have been the deepest, holiest proposal of his life, spoke as if briefs, success, and the superficial trappings of a good house and clever society were his sole objectives. Oh dear! How she could have loved him if he had been different, possessing a difference she felt, on reflection, went low—deep down. Then, a thought crossed her mind: perhaps his lightness was merely assumed, a mask covering a bitterness of disappointment that would have branded her own heart had she loved and been rejected.

Her mother entered before this whirlwind of thoughts could organize itself. Margaret had to shake off the recollections of the day's events and turn a sympathetic ear to Dixon's complaints about the ironing-blanket being burnt again, and to the gossip that Susan Lightfoot had been seen with artificial flowers in her bonnet, evidence of a vain and giddy character. Mr. Hale sipped his tea in abstracted silence; Margaret bore the brunt of the conversation. She wondered how her parents could be so forgetful, so careless of their companion throughout the day, failing to mention his name. She momentarily forgot that he had not yet made them an offer.

After tea, Mr. Hale rose, leaning his head on his hand with his elbow on the chimney-piece, lost in musing, sighing deeply from time to time. Mrs. Hale departed to consult with Dixon regarding winter clothing for the poor. Margaret prepared her mother's worsted work, shrinking from the prospect of the long evening and wishing for bed so she could revisit the day's events in solitude.

"Margaret!" Mr. Hale called out suddenly, in a tone of desperate urgency that made her startle. "Is that tapestry work of immediate consequence? Can you leave it and come into my study? I wish to speak to you about something very serious to us all."

"Very serious to us all." Mr. Lennox had never had the chance for private conversation with her father following her refusal, or else this would indeed be a grave affair. First, Margaret felt guilty and ashamed of having grown into a woman deemed worthy of marriage; secondly, she feared her father might be displeased that she had taken upon herself to decline Mr. Lennox's proposal. Yet, she soon realized this was not about any recent complication. He gestured for her to sit beside him, stirred the fire, snuffed the candles, and sighed several times before composing himself to speak—and it came out with a jerk nonetheless—"Margaret! I am going to leave Helstone."

"Leave Helstone, papa! But why?"

Mr. Hale remained silent for a minute or two, playing nervously with papers on the table, opening his lips to speak only to close them again without the courage to utter a word. Margaret could not bear the suspense, which distressed her father even more than herself.

"But why, dear papa? Do tell me!"

He looked up suddenly, then spoke with a slow, enforced calmness: "Because I must no longer be a minister in the Church of England."

Margaret had imagined nothing less than that some preferment her mother had so desired had finally befallen her father—something forcing him to leave beautiful, beloved Helstone for the stately, silent closes of cathedral towns. They were grand and imposing, but to leave Helstone forever would have been a sad, lingering pain. Yet, nothing compared to the shock of her father's final speech. What could he mean? It was worse for being so mysterious. The aspect of piteous distress on his face, almost imploring a merciful judgment from his child, gave her a sudden sickness. Could he be implicated in something Frederick had done? Frederick was an outlaw. Had her father, out of natural love for his son, connived at anything?

"Oh! what is it? Do speak, papa! Tell me all! Why can you no longer be a clergyman? Surely, if the bishop were told all we know about Frederick, and the hard injustice—"

"It is nothing about Frederick; the bishop would have nothing to do with that. It is all myself. Margaret, I will tell you about it. I will answer any questions this once, but after tonight, let us never speak of it again. I can meet the consequences of my painful, miserable doubts; but it is an effort beyond me to speak of what has caused me so much suffering."

"Doubts, papa! Doubts as to religion?" Margaret asked, more shocked than ever.

"No! Not doubts as to religion; not the slightest injury to that."

He paused. Margaret sighed as if standing on the verge of some new horror. He began again, speaking rapidly as if to complete a set task: "You could not understand it all if I told you—my anxiety for years past to know whether I had any right to hold my living—my efforts to quench my smouldering doubts by the authority of the Church. Oh! Margaret, how I love the holy Church from which I am to be shut out!" He could not continue for a moment or two. Margaret could not speak; it seemed to her as terribly mysterious as if her father were about to turn Mahometan.

"I have been reading today of the two thousand who were ejected from their churches," Mr. Hale continued, smiling faintly, "trying to steal some of their bravery; but it is of no use—no use—I cannot help feeling it acutely."

"But, papa, have you well considered? Oh! It seems so terrible, so shocking," Margaret said, suddenly bursting into tears. The one staid foundation of her home, of her idea of her beloved father, seemed to reel and rock. What could she say? What was to be done? The sight of her distress made Mr. Hale steel himself to try and comfort her. He swallowed down the dry, choking sobs that had been heaving up from his heart, went to his bookcase, and took down a volume he had often read lately, believing it had given him strength to embark on the course he now pursued.

"Listen, dear Margaret," he said, putting one arm around her waist. She took his hand in hers and grasped it tightly, but she could not lift her head; nor could she attend to what he read, so great was her internal agitation.

"This is the soliloquy of one who was once a clergyman in a country parish, like me; it was written by Mr. Oldfield, minister of Carsington, in Derbyshire, a hundred and sixty years ago, or more. His trials are over. He fought the good fight." These last two sentences he spoke low, as if to himself. Then he read aloud:

"When thou canst no longer continue in thy work without dishonour to God, discredit to religion, foregoing thy integrity, wounding conscience, spoiling thy peace, and hazarding the loss of thy salvation; in a word, when the conditions upon which thou must continue (if thou wilt continue) in thy employments are sinful, and unwarranted by the word of God, thou mayest, yea, thou must believe that God will turn thy very silence, suspension, deprivation, and laying aside, to His glory, and the advancement of the Gospel's interest. When God will not use thee in one kind, yet He will in another. A soul that desires to serve and honour Him shall never want opportunity to do it; nor must thou so limit the Holy One of Israel, as to think He hath but one way in which He can glorify Himself by thee. He can do it by thy silence as well as by thy preaching; the laying aside as well as thy continuance in thy work. It is not pretence of doing God the greatest service, or performing the weightiest duty, that will excuse the least sin, though that sin capacitated or gave us the opportunity for doing that duty. Thou wilt have little thanks, O my soul! if, when thou art charged with corrupting God's worship, falsifying thy vows, thou pretendest a necessity for it in order to a continuance in the ministry."

As he read this, glancing at much more which he did not read, he gained resolution for himself and felt as if he too could be brave and firm in doing what he believed to be right; but as he ceased, he heard Margaret's low, convulsive sob, and his courage sank under the keen sense of suffering.

"Margaret, dear!" he said, drawing her closer, "think of the early martyrs; think of the thousands who have suffered."

"But, father," she said, suddenly lifting her flushed, tear-wet face, "the early martyrs suffered for the truth, while you—oh! dear, dear papa!"

"I suffer for conscience' sake, my child," he said, with a dignity only tremulous from the acute sensitiveness of his character; "I must do what my conscience bids. I have borne long with self-reproach that would have roused any mind less torpid and cowardly than mine." He shook his head as he went on. "Your poor mother's fond wish, gratified at last in the mocking way in which over-fond wishes are too often fulfilled—Sodom apples as they are—has brought on this crisis, for which I ought to be, and I hope I am thankful. It is not a month since the bishop offered me another living; if I had accepted it, I should have had to make a fresh declaration of conformity to the Liturgy at my institution. Margaret, I tried to do it; I tried to content myself with simply refusing the additional preferment and stopping quietly here,—strangling my conscience now, as I had strained it before. God forgive me!"

He rose and walked up and down the room, speaking low words of self-reproach and humiliation, of which Margaret was thankful to hear but few. At last he said, "Margaret, I return to the old sad burden: we must leave Helstone."

"Yes! I see. But when?"

"I have written to the bishop—I dare say I have told you so, but I forget things just now," Mr. Hale said, collapsing into his depressed manner as soon as he came to talk of hard, matter-of-fact details, "informing him of my intention to resign this vicarage. He has been most kind; he has used arguments and expostulations, all in vain—in vain. They are but what I have tried upon myself, without avail. I shall have to take my deed of resignation, and wait upon the bishop myself to bid him farewell. That will be a trial. But worse, far worse, will be the parting from my dear people. There is a curate appointed to read prayers—a Mr. Brown. He will come to stay with us tomorrow. Next Sunday I preach my farewell sermon."

Was it to be so sudden? Margaret thought; and yet perhaps it was as well. Lingering would only add stings to the pain; it was better to be stunned into numbness by hearing of all these arrangements, which seemed nearly completed before she had been told. "What does mamma say?" she asked with a deep sigh.

To her surprise, her father began to walk about again before answering. At length he stopped and replied: "Margaret, I am a poor coward after all. I cannot bear to give pain. I know so well your mother's married life has not been all she hoped—all she had a right to expect—and this will be such a blow to her, that I have never had the heart, the power to tell her. She must be told though, now," he said, looking wistfully at his daughter. Margaret was almost overpowered by the idea that her mother knew nothing of it all, yet the affair was so far advanced!

"Yes, indeed she must," said Margaret. "Perhaps, after all, she may not—Oh yes! she will, she must be shocked"—as the force of the blow returned upon herself in trying to realize how another would take it. "Where are we to go to?" she asked at last, struck with a fresh wonder as to their future plans, if plans indeed her father had.

"To Milton-Northern," he answered with a dull indifference, for he had perceived that, although his daughter's love made her cling to him and for a moment strive to soothe him, the keenness of the pain was as fresh as ever in her mind.

"Milton-Northern! The manufacturing town in Darkshire?"

"Yes," he said in the same despondent, indifferent way.

"Why there, papa?" she asked.

"Because there I can earn bread for my family. Because I know no one there, and no one knows Helstone, or can ever talk to me about it."

"Bread for your family! I thought you and mamma had"—and then she stopped, checking her natural interest regarding their future life as she saw the gathering gloom on her father's brow. But he, with his quick intuitive sympathy, read in her face, as in a mirror, the reflections of his own moody depression, and turned it off with an effort.

"You shall be told all, Margaret. Only help me to tell your mother. I think I could do anything but that: the idea of her distress turns me sick with dread. If I tell you all, perhaps you could break it to her tomorrow. I am going out for the day to bid farmer Dobson and the poor people on Bracy Common good-bye. Would you dislike breaking it to

"I ask Thee for a thoughtful love,
Through constant watching wise,
To meet the glad with joyful smiles,
And to wipe the weeping eyes;
And a heart at leisure from itself
To soothe and sympathise."

Margaret Hale listened with a patience that bordered on adoration to her mother's schemes for alleviating the hardships of the parish poor. Yet, beneath the surface of her compliance, a quiet grief churned. Every charitable plan her mother devised felt like a final farewell, a prelude to their departure from Helstone. The frost was already biting; soon, the winter would seal them away from the people they loved. Old Simon's rheumatism would ache in the cold, and his failing eyes would search in vain for the familiar face of the girl who brought him broth and red flannel. Mary Domville's crippled boy would crawl to the door, hoping to catch a glimpse of Margaret walking through the forest, but she would be gone. These neighbors would never understand the sudden severance, nor the many others whose lives had been woven into the fabric of the vicarage. "Father has always spent his stipend entirely on the parish," Mrs. Hale explained, her voice tinged with a sense of duty that Margaret found heavy. "I may be encroaching on the future, but the winter is cruel, and the poor must be fed."

"Oh, Mother, let us do all we can," Margaret urged, her eagerness masking the dread that this might be their last chance to serve. "We may not be here long."

"Do you feel unwell, my darling?" Mrs. Hale asked, her anxiety sharpening as she noted her daughter's pallor. "You look exhausted. Perhaps this damp, unhealthy air of Helstone is affecting you."

"No, Mother, it is not that. It is the finest air imaginable, smelling of purity after the smog of Harley Street. But I am weary; surely it is near bedtime."

"It is half-past nine. You should retire immediately. Ask Dixon for some gruel. I shall check on you once you are settled. I fear you have caught a chill, or perhaps the stagnant ponds..."

"Oh, Mother," Margaret murmured, offering a faint smile as she kissed her mother's cheek. "I am perfectly well. I am simply tired."

Retiring upstairs, Margaret submitted to a bowl of gruel to soothe her mother's fears. Languidly, she lay in bed as Mrs. Hale made her final inquiries before locking her own door. The moment the latch clicked, Margaret sprang up. Throwing on her dressing-gown, she paced the room until the floorboards groaned under her feet, forcing her to stop. She curled herself onto the window seat, a small, deeply recessed nook. Earlier that morning, the bright lights on the church tower had promised a sunny day. Now, sixteen hours later, she sat there, too consumed by sorrow to weep, feeling a cold, dull ache that had drained the vitality from her young heart.

Mr. Henry Lennox's visit, his bold proposal, seemed like a distant dream, severed from her reality. The harsh truth was that her father had allowed doubts to fester until he became a schismatic, an outcast. All the changes looming over them stemmed from this single, blighting fact. She gazed out at the dark grey silhouettes of the church towers, square and rigid against the deep blue void of the night sky. Into those depths she looked, searching for a sign of God, finding none. It seemed to her that the earth was more desolate than if enclosed by an iron dome; the infinite, serene spaces of the universe mocked her suffering, shutting in the cries of the afflicted so they might ascend to a throne that would never hear them.

In this mood, her father entered silently. The moonlight revealed his daughter in her unusual posture. He approached and touched her shoulder.

"Margaret, I heard you were awake. I could not resist coming in to pray with you—to recite the Lord's Prayer. It will benefit us both."

Mr. Hale and Margaret knelt by the window. He looked upward; she bowed her head in humble shame. God was present, close and listening to her father's whispered words. Her father might be a heretic, but had not she, in her despairing doubts moments before, revealed herself as a skeptic even more profound? She said nothing, retreating to bed after her father left, like a child fleeing from a discovered fault. If the world was filled with perplexing problems, she would trust, asking only for the one necessary step for the hour. Mr. Lennox haunted her dreams that night. He climbed a tree of impossible height to retrieve her bonnet; he fell, and she struggled to save him, held back by an invisible, powerful hand. Then, the scene shifted: she was back in the Harley Street drawing-room, talking to him as of old, yet acutely aware that she had witnessed his death by that terrible fall.

It was a miserable, unresting night, a poor preparation for the day ahead. She awoke with a start, unrefreshed, sensing a reality far worse than her feverish dreams. The sorrow returned, accompanied by a terrible discord. Where had her father wandered, led by doubts that were temptations of the Evil One? She longed to ask, yet dreaded the answer.

The crisp morning brought a false cheer to Mrs. Hale at breakfast. She chattered happily about village kindness, ignoring her husband's silence and Margaret's monosyllabic responses. Before the meal was cleared, Mr. Hale rose, leaning heavily on the table.

"I shall not return until evening. I am going to Bracy Common and will ask Farmer Dobson for dinner. I will be back for tea at seven."

He did not look at either of them, but Margaret understood the subtext. By seven, the announcement must be made. Mr. Hale would have delayed, but Margaret possessed a different constitution. She could not bear the weight of the secret all day; it was better to confront the worst immediately. Standing by the window, she waited for the servant to leave, planning how to begin. Her mother ascended the stairs to prepare for the school, returning shortly in a brisker mood.

"Mother, come round the garden with me this morning. Just one turn," Margaret said, linking her arm through her mother's waist.

They stepped through the open window. Mrs. Hale spoke, but Margaret could not decipher the words. Her eye caught a bee entering a deep-bellied flower. When the insect emerged with its spoil, she would begin—that was the signal. Out flew the bee.

"Mother! Father is leaving Helstone!" Margaret blurted out. "He is leaving the Church and moving to Milton-Northern." The three hard facts hung in the air.

"What makes you say that?" Mrs. Hale asked, her voice incredulous. "Who has been telling you such nonsense?"

"Father himself," Margaret replied, wishing she could speak gently but lacking the words. They were near a garden bench. Mrs. Hale sat down and began to weep.

"I do not understand," she said. "Either you have made a grave mistake, or I do not comprehend you."

"No, Mother, I have made no mistake. Father has written to the Bishop stating that his doubts prevent him from remaining a priest of the Church of England, and that he must resign Helstone. He has also consulted Mr. Bell—Frederick's godfather—and it is arranged that we move to Milton-Northern." Mrs. Hale looked up, the shadow on her face confirming she believed the truth.

"I do not think it can be true," Mrs. Hale said finally. "He would surely have told me before it came to this."

Margaret realized her mother ought to have been informed sooner. Whatever her own faults of discontent might have been, it was an error for her father to leave her to learn his change of heart from a better-informed child. She sat beside her mother, resting her head on her breast, caressing her face.

"Dear, darling Mother! We were so afraid of causing you pain. Father felt it acutely. You are not strong, and the suspense must have been terrible."

"When did he tell you, Margaret?"

"Only yesterday," Margaret replied, detecting the jealousy in the question. "Poor Father!" She tried to steer her mother's thoughts toward compassion for her father's struggle. Mrs. Hale raised her head.

"What does he mean by having doubts?" she asked. "Does he not mean that he thinks differently—that he knows better than the Church?"

Margaret shook her head, tears filling her eyes as her mother touched the raw nerve of her own regret.

"Can the Bishop not set him right?" Mrs. Hale asked, half impatient.

"I am afraid not," Margaret said. "But I did not ask. I could not bear to hear his answer. It is settled anyway. He is leaving Helstone in a fortnight. I am not sure if he did not say he sent in his resignation."

"In a fortnight!" Mrs. Hale exclaimed. "I think this is very strange—not at all right. I call it unfeeling." Tears began to flow as she sought relief in lamentation. "He has doubts and gives up his living without consulting me. If he had told me at the first, I could have nipped it in the bud."

Though Margaret felt her father's conduct had been flawed, she could not bear to hear it blamed by her mother. She knew his reserve sprang from a tenderness that might seem cowardly but was not unfeeling.

"I almost hoped you might be glad to leave Helstone, Mother," she said after a pause. "You have never been well in this air."

"You cannot imagine the smoky air of a manufacturing town like Milton-Northern, full of chimneys and dirt. How could it compare to this pure, sweet air, however soft? Imagine living amidst factories and factory people! Though, of course, if Father leaves the Church, we shall be excluded from society everywhere. It will be a disgrace! Poor dear Sir John! It is well he is not alive to see what your father has come to! Every day after dinner, when I lived with your Aunt Shaw at Beresford Court, Sir John would give the first toast: 'Church and King, and down with the Rump.'"

Margaret was relieved her mother's thoughts had shifted from her husband's silence to social status. Next to the vital anxiety of her father's doubts, this was the circumstance that caused her the most pain.

"You know, we have very little society here, Mother. The Gormans, our nearest neighbors, have been in trade just as much as these Milton-Northern people."

"Yes," Mrs. Hale said indignantly, "but at least the Gormans made carriages for half the county's gentlemen and were part of society. These factory people—who on earth wears cotton when they can afford linen?"

"Well, Mother, I give up the cotton-spinners. I am not defending them or any tradespeople. We shall have little enough to do with them."

"Why on earth has Father chosen Milton-Northern?"

"Partly," Margaret sighed, "because it is so different from Helstone. Partly because Mr. Bell says there is an opening for a private tutor."

"A private tutor in Milton? Why not go to Oxford and tutor gentlemen?"

"You forget, Mother! He is leaving the Church because of his opinions. His doubts would do him no good at Oxford."

Mrs. Hale sat in silence, quietly weeping. Finally, she said, "And the furniture? How in the world shall we manage the removal? I have never removed in my life, and we have only a fortnight!"

Margaret felt immense relief that her mother's distress had narrowed to this practical point, on which she could exert influence. She planned, promised, and guided her mother through arrangements that could be fixed before they knew more about Mr. Hale's intentions. Throughout the day, Margaret never left her mother's side, bending her entire soul to sympathize with her shifting emotions. Towards evening, as anxiety grew that her father would find no welcoming home after his day of fatigue, she dwelt on what he must have endured in secret. Her mother replied coldly that he ought to have told her, that then she would have had an adviser. Margaret felt faint at heart when she heard her father's step in the hall. She dared not go to meet him, fearing her mother's jealousy. She heard him linger, waiting for a sign. Seeing her mother's twitching lips and changing color, she knew her husband had returned.

Presently, he opened the room door and stood there, uncertain whether to enter. His face was grey and pale; his eyes held a timid, fearful look, something almost pitiful in a man's visage. That look of despondent uncertainty, of mental and bodily languor, touched his wife's heart. She went to him and threw herself on his breast, crying out:

"Oh! Richard, Richard, you should have told me sooner!"

Then, in tears, Margaret left her, rushing upstairs to throw herself on her bed and hide her face in the pillows to stifle the hysterical sobs that threatened to break free after the rigid self-control of the day.

How long she lay there she could not tell. She heard no noise, though the housemaid entered to arrange the room. The frightened girl stole out on tiptoe and told Mrs. Dixon that Miss Hale was crying as if her heart would break, certain she would become ill if she continued. Consequently, Margaret felt compelled to rise and sit up. She saw the familiar room, the figure of Dixon in shadow holding a candle behind her, fearing the effect on Miss Hale's swollen, blinded eyes.

"Oh, Dixon! I did not hear you come in!" Margaret said, resuming her trembling self-restraint. "Is it very late?" She lifted herself languidly, letting her feet touch the ground without standing firmly, shading her wet, ruffled hair from her face, trying to appear as if nothing were wrong, as if she had merely been asleep.

"I can hardly tell the time," Dixon replied in an aggrieved tone. "Since your mistress told me this terrible news when I dressed her for tea, I have lost all count of time. I am sure I do not know what is to become of us all. When Charlotte told me just now you were sobbing

"Unwatch'd the garden bough shall sway,
The tender blossom flutter down,
Unloved that beech will gather brown,
The maple burn itself away;

Unloved, the sun-flower, shining fair,
Ray round with flames her disk of seed,
And many a rose-carnation feed
With summer spice the humming air;

*       *       *       *       *

Till from the garden and the wild
A fresh association blow
And year by year the landscape grow
Familiar to the stranger's child;

As year by year the labourer tills
His wonted glebe, or lops the glades;
And year by year our memory fades
From all the circle of the hills."

The final day arrived, and the house stood besieged by the chaos of departure. Packing-cases lined the hall, destined for the railway station just beyond the gate, while the once-pristine lawn lay trampled and strewn with straw drifting through the open windows. The rooms themselves seemed to have lost their substance; light poured in harshly through the uncurtained glass, rendering the familiar spaces alien and stark. In Mrs. Hale's dressing-room, the atmosphere was thick with nostalgia. Mrs. Hale and Dixon worked slowly amidst the clothes, pausing frequently to unearth forgotten treasures—relics of the children's youth—and to handle them with a tenderness that betrayed their reluctance to let go. Their progress was negligible, weighed down by the gravity of the moment.

Downstairs, Margaret stood as a pillar of calm amidst the turmoil. She directed the men hired to assist the cook and Charlotte, her presence a steady anchor against the rising tide of emotion. The servants, weeping intermittently, marveled at her composure, convinced that her long residence in London had hardened her heart against the pain of leaving Helstone. They failed to perceive the heavy, crushing ache that pressed against her chest, a weight so profound that only the constant exertion of her senses could prevent her from breaking down entirely. To yield would be to leave her father and mother leaderless; her father was already deep in the vestry, sorting through papers and registers that only he could organize, while Margaret possessed neither the inclination nor the capacity to succumb to strangers or even to the household friends who surrounded her.

Eventually, the four packers retreated to the kitchen for tea, leaving Margaret to move stiffly through the echoing drawing-room and out into the twilight of an early November evening. A soft, lilac-hued mist veiled the world, obscuring but not hiding the landscape, while a robin sang somewhere nearby—perhaps the very bird her father had crafted a shelter for in his study. Leaves, vibrant and golden in the dying light, drifted down, already touched by frost. Margaret walked beneath the pear-tree wall, a path she had last traversed beside Henry Lennox. Here, at this bed of thyme, he had once spoken of the thoughts she must suppress. Her eyes lingered on a late-blooming rose as she recalled the vivid image of carrot leaves he had described, a memory sharp and painful from only two weeks prior.

Where was he now? Likely in London, navigating the same circular routine of Harley Street dinners and social gatherings. Perhaps even as she walked through this damp, decaying garden, he was enjoying a brisk walk in the Temple Gardens, listening to the roar of the city and catching glimpses of the lights rising from the river. The contrast was stark: here, silence reigned, save for the distant creak of a cottage door opening for a weary laborer. Beyond the garden, the forest offered a stealthy, crunching sound—the tread of poachers. Margaret remembered the thrill she had once felt watching them leap silently over the fence under the moonlight, admiring their wild freedom. But tonight, fear gripped her. A branch snapped heavily in the darkness near the tree line. Instinct took over; she sprinted to the window, tapping urgently until Charlotte, startled from her task of securing the house, rushed to the door.

"Let me in! It is only me, Charlotte!" Margaret gasped, her heart hammering against her ribs. Once safely inside, with the windows bolted and the familiar walls enclosing her, she sank onto a packing-case. The room was cold and cheerless, illuminated only by Charlotte's long, unsnuffed candle. Charlotte looked at her with surprise, but Margaret, sensing the concern rather than seeing it, rose with a faint smile.

"I feared you might shut me out entirely," she said softly. "Then I would never have reached the kitchen, and the doors to the lane and churchyard are already locked."

"Oh, miss, I would have missed you instantly," Charlotte replied, her voice trembling slightly. "The men would have needed your direction. I've put tea in Master's study; it is the most comfortable room, so to speak."

"Thank you, Charlotte. You are a kind girl. I shall be sorry to leave you. Please, try to write to me if I can ever offer help or advice. I shall always welcome a letter from Helstone. I will send my address as soon as I know it."

The study was prepared for tea, a warm sanctuary with a blazing fire and unlit candles waiting on the table. Margaret sat on the rug, seeking warmth against the damp chill of the evening and the exhaustion that weighed upon her. She clasped her hands tightly around her knees, her head bowed in a posture of quiet despondency. But when her father's footsteps approached on the gravel outside, she sprang up, shaking her heavy black hair back and wiping away tears she had not realized had fallen.

He entered looking far more broken than she did. She struggled to engage him in conversation, forcing herself to speak of topics she hoped would interest him, though every effort felt like a final exertion.

"Have you walked far today?" she asked, noting his refusal to eat.

"As far as Fordham Beeches. I visited Widow Maltby; she is deeply grieved that we could not say goodbye. She says little Susan has been watching down the lane for days." The mention of the child, waiting in vain because her father could not leave, broke Margaret completely. She sobbed, her heart feeling as though it would shatter. Mr. Hale paced the room, distressed and perplexed.

"I cannot bear it," he muttered, his voice tight with anguish. "I cannot bear to see the suffering of others. I think I could endure my own with patience. Oh, is there no way to go back?"

"No, father," Margaret said, meeting his gaze with steady resolve, her voice low but firm. "It is bad to believe you in error. It would be infinitely worse to have known you a hypocrite." She lowered her voice at the end, as if the mere suggestion of such a thought bordered on irreverence.

"Besides," she continued, "I am merely tired tonight. Do not think I am suffering from what you have done, dear papa. We cannot discuss it tonight, I believe." Tears threatened again, overwhelming her control. "I will take this tea to Mother. She had hers early while I was busy, and she will surely need it now."

By morning, the relentless rhythm of the railroad tore them away from Helstone. They departed before they could truly settle, leaving behind the parsonage, half-hidden by China-roses and pyracanthus, glowing in the morning sun. Almost immediately, they were gone, returning no more. A sharp pang struck Margaret's heart as she tried to catch one last glimpse of the church tower from the train window, but her father remembered her wish too well, claiming the right to the view. She leaned back, closing her eyes, and tears welled, hanging briefly on her lashes before rolling slowly down her cheeks to fall unnoticed upon her dress.

They spent the night in a quiet London hotel. Mrs. Hale wept throughout the day, while Dixon expressed her grief through irritability, constantly chafing against Mr. Hale, whom she blamed for their misery. As they walked through the familiar streets, passing shops and acquaintances, the morning stretched into an eternity. It was the height of the afternoon bustle in November, a stark contrast to their internal stillness. Mrs. Hale, roused like a child, pointed out old landmarks—"Harrison's, where I bought my wedding things"—and exclaimed at the changes, the vast plate-glass windows that dwarfed those of Southampton.

Suddenly, she stopped. "Margaret, we have just passed Mr. Henry Lennox. Where can he be going?"

Margaret surged forward, then recoiled, a half-smile touching her lips at her own sudden movement. They were already a hundred yards away. To her, he remained a relic of Helstone, tied to a bright morning and an eventful day. She would have loved to see him, unseen and unheard, to preserve the memory of him without the complication of their past.

The evening passed in the heavy silence of the hotel room. Mr. Hale ventured out to visit booksellers and friends, but everyone they encountered seemed to be rushing toward some appointment, bound by the city's frantic pace. To them, the world was a whirlwind of purpose; to the Hales, they were strangers and outcasts. Yet, Margaret knew that within a mile lay homes where she and her mother would be welcomed with open arms, provided they came in gladness or peace. But in their current state of sorrow and complicated trouble, they would be perceived as shadows in those houses of intimacy, unable to find the deep, silent sympathy that true friends offered in times of grief.

"Mist clogs the sunshine, / Smoky dwarf houses / Hem me round everywhere."
—Matthew Arnold.

The following afternoon, roughly twenty miles from Milton-Northern, the carriage turned onto the narrow branch line leading to Heston. Heston itself stretched as a single, elongated street running parallel to the coast. It possessed a distinct character, differing sharply from the southern bathing resorts, which in turn differed from those on the continent. To use a Scottish term, everything here appeared more "purpose-like." The country carts were heavier, laden with iron rather than wood and leather; the people in the streets, though ostensibly on holiday, moved with a busy, industrious intent. The colors were grayer, more enduring, lacking the fleeting gaiety of the south. There were no smock-frocks among the rural folk; they hindered movement and snagged on machinery, a fashion that had long since died out. In the southern towns Margaret had visited, shopmen, when not occupied, would lounge at their doors to enjoy the fresh air. Here, however, if they had a moment's leisure from customers, they made their shops their entire world—even, Margaret suspected, to the point of unnecessarily unrolling and re-rolling ribbons. These contrasts struck her keenly as she and her mother sought lodgings the next morning.
