Their two nights in hotels had exceeded Mr. Hale's budget, and they were relieved to secure the first clean, cheerful rooms available. For the first time in days, Margaret felt a profound sense of rest. There was a dreamlike quality to this repose, making it feel almost luxurious. The distant sea lapped the sandy shore with a rhythmic sound; the cries of donkey-boys drifted nearby; unusual scenes unfolded before her like paintings she cared not to fully decipher before they faded. A stroll down to the beach to breathe the soft, warm sea-air, even in late November; the vast, misty sea-line touching the pale sky; the white sail of a distant boat turning silver in a faint sunbeam—it seemed she could dream her life away in such a luxury of pensiveness, making the present all-encompassing, daring neither to dwell on the past nor contemplate the future.

Yet the future must be faced, however stern. It was arranged that Margaret and her father would travel to Milton-Northern the following day to find a house. Mr. Hale had received several letters from Mr. Bell and a few from Mr. Thornton, and he was eager to clarify his position and chances of success there, a matter requiring a direct interview with the latter. Margaret knew they must move, yet she harbored a repugnance toward the idea of a manufacturing town and believed her mother was benefiting from the Heston air. She would have gladly deferred the journey.

For several miles before reaching Milton, a deep lead-colored cloud hung over the horizon, darkened by contrast against the pale gray-blue of the wintry sky. Frost had already touched Heston. Nearer the town, the air carried a faint taste and smell of smoke; perhaps it was more a loss of the fragrance of grass than a positive odor. They were whirled quickly over long, straight, seemingly endless streets of uniformly built brick houses. Here and there, a great oblong factory with many windows stood like a hen among her chicks, puffing out black, "unparliamentary" smoke, the source of the cloud Margaret had mistaken for rain. As they drove from the station to the hotel through the wider streets, they stopped constantly; great, heavily loaded wagons blocked the thoroughfares. Margaret had occasionally driven into the city with her aunt, but there the heavy vehicles seemed varied in purpose. Here, every van, wagon, and truck bore cotton, either in raw bags or woven bales of calico. People crowded the sidewalks, mostly well-dressed in material, yet with a slovenly looseness that struck Margaret as distinct from the shabby, threadbare smartness of a similar class in London.

"New Street," Mr. Hale noted. "I believe this is the principal street in Milton. Bell has often spoken of it. It was the opening of this street from a lane into a great thoroughfare thirty years ago that caused his property to rise so much in value. Mr. Thornton's mill must be nearby, for he is Mr. Bell's tenant, but I suspect he dates from his warehouse."

"Where is our hotel, papa?"

"Close to the end of this street, I believe. Shall we have lunch before or after we inspect the houses listed in the *Milton Times*?"

"Oh, let us get our work done first."

"Very well. Then I will only check if there is any note from Mr. Thornton, who promised to inform me of anything regarding these houses, and then we shall depart. We will keep the cab; it is safer than losing ourselves and arriving too late for the afternoon train."

There were no letters awaiting him. They set out house-hunting. Thirty pounds a year was all they could afford; in Hampshire, they might have secured a roomy house with a pleasant garden for that sum. Here, even the basic requirement of two sitting-rooms and four bedrooms seemed unattainable. They visited a list of properties, rejecting each as they went. Finally, they exchanged looks of dismay.

"We must settle for the second option, I think. That one in Crampton, don't they call the suburb? It had three sitting-rooms. Remember how we laughed at the disparity between the three sitting-rooms and only three bedrooms? But I have planned it all. The front room downstairs will be your study and our dining-room (poor papa!). You know we agreed that mother shall have as cheerful a sitting-room as possible. That front room upstairs, with its atrocious blue and pink paper and heavy cornice, actually had a pretty view over the plain, with a great bend of river or canal below. Then I could have the little bedroom behind, in that projection at the head of the first flight of stairs over the kitchen. You and mother could take the room behind the drawing-room, and that closet in the roof will make you a splendid dressing-room."

"But Dixon, and the girl we are to employ?"

"Oh, wait. I am overwhelmed by the discovery of my own genius for management. Dixon shall have—the back sitting-room. I think she will like that. She grumbles so much about the stairs at Heston. The girl will have that sloping attic over your room and mother's. Won't that suffice?"

"I dare say it will. But the papers. What taste! And the overloading of such a house with color and such heavy cornices!"

"Never mind, papa! Surely you can charm the landlord into re-papering one or two rooms—the drawing-room and your bedroom—for mother will be in contact with them most. Your bookshelves will hide much of that gaudy pattern in the dining-room."

"Then you consider it the best? If so, I had better go immediately to call on this Mr. Donkin, to whom the advertisement refers. I will take you back to the hotel, where you can order lunch and rest. By the time it is ready, I shall be with you. I hope I shall be able to procure new papers."

Margaret hoped so too, though she remained silent. She had never truly encountered the taste that adorns ornament, however poor, more than the plainness and simplicity which constitute the true framework of elegance.

Her father led her through the hotel entrance and, leaving her at the foot of the staircase, proceeded to the address of the landlord of the house they had selected. Just as Margaret placed her hand on the door of their sitting-room, a quick-stepping waiter followed her.

"I beg your pardon, ma'am. The gentleman departed so swiftly I had no time to inform him. Mr. Thornton called almost immediately after you left. Since I understood, from what the gentleman said, that you would return within an hour, I told him so. He came back about five minutes ago and requested to wait for Mr. Hale. He is in your room now, ma'am."

"Thank you. My father will return shortly, and then you may inform him."

Margaret opened the door and entered with the straight, fearless, dignified presence habitual to her. She felt no awkwardness; she possessed too many habits of society for that. Here was a person come on business to her father, and since he had shown himself obliging, she intended to treat him with a full measure of civility. Mr. Thornton, however, was considerably more surprised and discomfited. Instead of a quiet, middle-aged clergyman, a young lady advanced with frank dignity—a young lady of a different type from most he encountered. Her dress was plain: a close straw bonnet of the finest material and shape, trimmed with white ribbon; a large Indian shawl draped about her in long, heavy folds, worn as an empress wears her drapery. He did not recognize her, for he caught the simple, straight, unabashed look that indicated his presence was of no concern to her beautiful countenance, calling up no flush of surprise to the pale ivory of her complexion. He had heard Mr. Hale had a daughter but had imagined her to be a child.

"Mr. Thornton, I believe!" Margaret said after a half-second's pause, during which his words failed to come. "Will you sit down? My father brought me to the door not a minute ago, but unfortunately he was not informed of your arrival and has gone away on some business. He will return almost directly. I am sorry you have had the trouble of calling twice."

Mr. Thornton was accustomed to authority, yet she seemed to assume a kind of rule over him instantly. He had grown impatient at the loss of his time on a market day just before her appearance, yet now he calmly took a seat at her bidding.

"Do you know where Mr. Hale has gone? Perhaps I might find him."

"He has gone to a Mr. Donkin in Canute Street. He is the landlord of the house my father wishes to rent in Crampton."

Mr. Thornton knew the house. He had seen the advertisement and visited it in compliance with Mr. Bell's request to assist Mr. Hale, as well as motivated by his own interest in the case of a clergyman who had resigned under circumstances similar to Mr. Hale's. Mr. Thornton had initially thought the house in Crampton was ideal; but now, seeing Margaret with her superb manner of moving and bearing, he began to feel ashamed of having imagined it would suffice for the Hales, despite a certain vulgarity that had struck him during his initial inspection.

Margaret could not help her demeanor; the short, curled upper lip, the round, massive upturned chin, the way she carried her head, and her movements, full of a soft feminine defiance, always gave strangers the impression of haughtiness. She was tired and would much rather have remained silent to enjoy the rest her father had planned; yet, of course, she owed it to herself to be a gentlewoman and speak courteously to this stranger, albeit not over-brushed or over-polished after his rough encounter with the Milton streets and crowds. She wished he would leave, as he had once mentioned doing, instead of sitting there, answering her remarks with curt sentences. She had removed her shawl and hung it over the back of her chair. She sat facing him and the light; her full beauty met his gaze; her round, white, flexible throat rose from her full yet lithe figure; her lips moved slightly as she spoke, not breaking the cold, serene look of her face with any variation from that one lovely, haughty curve; her eyes, with their soft gloom, met his with quiet maiden freedom. He almost told himself that he did not like her before their conversation ended. He tried so hard to compensate for his mortified feeling that, while he looked upon her with an admiration he could not suppress, she looked at him with proud indifference, taking him, he thought, for what, in his irritation, he told himself he was—a great rough fellow with no grace or refinement. Her quiet coldness of demeanor he interpreted as contempt, and he resented it in his heart to the point of almost rising to leave and have nothing more to do with the Hales and their superciliousness.

Just as Margaret exhausted her last subject of conversation—which could hardly be called conversation, consisting of so few and short speeches—her father entered. With his pleasant, gentlemanly courtesy of apology, he reinstated his name and family in Mr. Thornton's good opinion.

Mr. Hale and his visitor had much to discuss regarding their mutual friend, Mr. Bell. Glad that her part in entertaining the visitor was over, Margaret went to the window to try to become more familiar with the strange aspect of the street. She became so absorbed in watching the activity outside that she barely heard her father until he repeated himself:

"Margaret! The landlord will persist in admiring that hideous paper, and I fear we must let it remain."

"Oh dear! I am sorry!" she replied, beginning to turn over in her mind the possibility of hiding part of it with some of her sketches, but giving up the idea as likely to make matters worse. Meanwhile, her father, with his kindly country hospitality, pressed Mr. Thornton to stay for luncheon. It would have been very inconvenient for him to do so, yet he felt he should have accepted if Margaret, by word or look, had seconded her father's invitation. He was glad she did not, yet he was irritated at her for not doing so. She gave him a low, grave bow when he left, and he felt more awkward and self-conscious in every limb than he ever had in his life.

"Well, Margaret, now to luncheon as fast as we can. Have you ordered it?"

"No, papa; that man was here when I arrived, and I never had an opportunity."

"Then we must take whatever we can get. He must have been waiting a long time, I'm afraid."

"It seemed exceedingly long to me. I was just at the last gasp when you came in. He never continued any subject but gave little, short, abrupt answers."

"Very much to the point, I should think. He is a clear-headed fellow. He said, did you hear? That Crampton is on gravelly soil and by far the healthiest suburb in the neighborhood of Milton."

When they returned to Heston, the day's account was given to Mrs. Hale, who

"And it's hame, hame, hame,
Hame fain wad I be."

The delicate, floral wallpaper of the rooms was a mere consolation for Milton; it lacked the essential ingredient that could not be conjured: the absence of the thick, yellow November fogs. When Mrs. Hale arrived, the sweeping bend of the river had already shut out the view of the valley plain, leaving them trapped in a world of choking white mist.

Margaret and Dixon had labored for two days, unpacking and arranging, yet the house remained a chaotic shell. Outside, the fog pressed against the glass, driven inward through every open door in suffocating wreaths of unwholesome vapor.

"Oh, Margaret! Are we to live here?" Mrs. Hale asked, her voice hollow with dismay.

Margaret's heart mirrored the dreariness of the tone. She struggled to summon the composure to reply, "Oh, the fogs in London are sometimes far worse!"

"But then you knew that London itself, and friends, lay behind it. Here—well! We are desolate. Oh, Dixon, what a place this is!"

"Indeed, ma'am, I'm sure it will be your death before long, and then I know who'll—" Dixon paused, realizing the weight of her words. "Stay! Miss Hale, that's far too heavy for you to lift."

"Not at all, thank you, Dixon," Margaret replied, her tone cool. "The best thing we can do for mamma is to get her room ready for rest, while I bring her a cup of coffee."

Mr. Hale shared his wife's gloom and sought sympathy from Margaret. "Margaret, I do believe this is an unhealthy place. Suppose your mother's health, or yours, should suffer. I wish I had gone to some country place in Wales; this is truly terrible," he said, moving to the window.

There was no comfort to offer. They were settled in Milton and must endure the smoke and fogs for the season; indeed, all other life seemed shut out by a thick fog of circumstance. Only the day before, Mr. Hale had calculated with dismay how their removal and fortnight at Heston had consumed nearly all his ready money. No! Here they were, and here they must remain.

That night, as Margaret realized the permanence of their situation, she felt inclined to sink into a stupor of despair. The heavy, smoky air clung to her bedroom, a long narrow projection at the back of the house. Its window, placed at the side of the oblong room, faced the blank wall of a similar projection barely ten feet away. Through the fog, it loomed like a great barrier to hope. Inside, everything was in confusion. Their efforts had been directed solely toward making her mother's room comfortable. Margaret sat on a box, the direction card upon which struck her as having been written at Helstone—beautiful, beloved Helstone! She lost herself in dismal thought until she resolved to distract her mind. Suddenly, she remembered a letter from Edith she had only half-read in the morning's bustle.

It spoke of their arrival at Corfu, their voyage along the Mediterranean—the music and dancing on board ship; the gay new life opening before her. Edith described her house with its trellised balcony and views over white cliffs and deep blue sea. She wrote fluently, seizing the salient points of a scene and enumerating particulars that allowed Margaret to visualize it all. Captain Lennox and another newly married officer shared a villa high on the precipitous rocks overlooking the sea. Their days, though late in the year, seemed spent in boating and land picnics; all outdoors, pleasure-seeking and glad. Edith's life seemed like the deep vault of blue sky above her, free—utterly free from fleck or cloud. Her husband attended drill, and she, the most musical officer's wife there, copied new tunes for the bandmaster; those seemed their most severe duties. She expressed an affectionate hope that, if the regiment stayed another year, Margaret might come for a long visit. She asked if Margaret remembered the rainy day in Harley Street when Edith refused to wear her new gown to a dinner, getting it wet and splashed on the way to the carriage—the very dinner where they had first met Captain Lennox.

Yes! Margaret remembered it well. Edith and Mrs. Shaw had gone to dinner; Margaret had joined the party later. The recollection of the plentiful luxury of those arrangements, the stately handsomeness of the furniture, the size of the house, the peaceful, untroubled ease of the visitors—all came vividly before her, in strange contrast to the present. The smooth sea of that old life closed up, without a mark left to tell where they had all been. The habitual dinners, the calls, the dancing evenings, went on forever, though her Aunt Shaw and Edith were no longer there; and she, of course, was even less missed. She doubted if anyone of that old set ever thought of her, except Henry Lennox. He too, she knew, would strive to forget her, because of the pain she had caused him. She had heard him often boast of his power to put any disagreeable thought far away from him.

Then she penetrated deeper into what might have been. If she had cared for him as a lover, accepted him, and this change in her father's opinions and consequent station had taken place, she could not doubt but that it would have been impatiently received by Mr. Lennox. It was a bitter mortification to her in one sense; but she could bear it patiently, because she knew her father's purity of purpose, and that strengthened her to endure his errors, grave and serious though they were. But the fact of the world esteeming her father degraded, in its rough wholesale judgment, would have oppressed and irritated Mr. Lennox. As she realized what might have been, she grew thankful for what was. They were at the lowest now; they could not be worse. Edith's astonishment and her Aunt Shaw's dismay would have to be met bravely when their letters came. So Margaret rose and began slowly to undress herself, feeling the full luxury of acting leisurely, late as it was, after all the past hurry of the day. She fell asleep, hoping for some brightness, either internal or external. But if she had known how long it would be before the brightness came, her heart would have sunk low. The time of the year was most unpropitious to health as well as to spirits.

Her mother caught a severe cold, and Dixon herself was evidently not well, though Margaret could not insult her more than by trying to save her or by taking any care of her. They could hear of no girl to assist; all were at work in the factories; at least, those who applied were well scolded by Dixon for thinking that such as they could ever be trusted to work in a gentleman's house. So they had to keep a charwoman in almost constant employ. Margaret longed to send for Charlotte; but besides the objection of her being a better servant than they could now afford, the distance was too great.

Mr. Hale met with several pupils, recommended to him by Mr. Bell or by the immediate influence of Mr. Thornton. They were mostly of an age when many boys would still be at school, but, according to the prevalent and apparently well-founded notions of Milton, to make a lad into a good tradesman he must be caught young and acclimated to the life of the mill, office, or warehouse. If he were sent to even the Scotch Universities, he came back unsettled for commercial pursuits; how much more so if he went to Oxford or Cambridge, where he could not be entered till he was eighteen? So most manufacturers placed their sons in situations at fourteen or fifteen years of age, unsparingly cutting away all offshoots in the direction of literature or high mental cultivation, hoping to throw the whole strength and vigor of the plant into commerce. Still, there were some wiser parents; and some who had sense enough to perceive their own deficiencies and strive to remedy them. Nay, there were a few no longer youths, but men in the prime of life, who had the stern wisdom to acknowledge their own ignorance and to learn late what they should have learned early. Mr. Thornton was perhaps the oldest of Mr. Hale's pupils. He was certainly the favorite. Mr. Hale got into the habit of quoting his opinions so frequently and with such regard that it became a little domestic joke to wonder what time, during the hour appointed for instruction, could be given to absolute learning, so much of it appeared to have been spent in conversation.

Margaret rather encouraged this light, merry way of viewing her father's acquaintance with Mr. Thornton, because she felt that her mother was inclined to look upon this new friendship with jealous eyes. As long as his time had been solely occupied with his books and his parishioners, as at Helstone, she had appeared to care little whether she saw much of him or not; but now that he looked eagerly forward to each renewal of his intercourse with Mr. Thornton, she seemed hurt and annoyed, as if he were slighting her companionship for the first time. Mr. Hale's over-praise had the usual effect of over-praise upon his auditors; they were a little inclined to rebel against Aristides being always called the Just.

After a quiet life in a country parsonage for more than twenty years, there was something dazzling to Mr. Hale in the energy which conquered immense difficulties with ease; the power of the machinery of Milton, the power of the men of Milton, impressed him with a sense of grandeur which he yielded to without caring to inquire into the details of its exercise. But Margaret went less abroad among machinery and men; saw less of power in its public effect, and, as it happened, she was thrown with one or two of those who, in all measures affecting masses of people, must be acute sufferers for the good of many. The question always is: has everything been done to make the sufferings of these exceptions as small as possible? Or, in the triumph of the crowded procession, have the helpless been trampled on, instead of being gently lifted aside out of the roadway of the conqueror, whom they have no power to accompany on his march?

It fell to Margaret's share to look out for a servant to assist Dixon, who had at first undertaken to find just the person she wanted to do all the rough work of the house. But Dixon's ideas of helpful girls were founded on the recollection of tidy elder scholars at Helstone School, who were only too proud to be allowed to come to the parsonage on a busy day, and treated Mrs. Dixon with all the respect, and a good deal more of fright, which they paid to Mr. and Mrs. Hale. Dixon was not unconscious of this awed reverence which was given to her; nor did she dislike it: it flattered her much as Louis the Fourteenth was flattered by his courtiers shading their eyes from the dazzling light of his presence. But nothing short of her faithful love for Mrs. Hale could have made her endure the rough, independent way in which all the Milton girls, who applied for the servant's place, replied to her inquiries regarding their qualifications. They even went the length of questioning her back again, having doubts and fears of their own as to the solvency of a family who lived in a house costing thirty pounds a year, and yet gave themselves airs and kept two servants, one of them so very high and mighty. Mr. Hale was no longer looked upon as Vicar of Helstone, but as a man who only spent at a certain rate. Margaret was weary and impatient of the accounts which Dixon perpetually brought to Mrs. Hale of the behavior of these would-be servants. Not but what Margaret was repelled by the rough, uncourteous manners of these people; not but what she shrunk with fastidious pride from their hail-fellow-well-met accost, and severely resented their unconcealed curiosity as to the means and position of any family who lived in Milton and yet were not engaged in trade of some kind. But the more Margaret felt impertinence, the more likely she was to be silent on the subject; and, at any rate, if she took upon herself to make inquiry for a servant, she could spare her mother the recital of all her disappointments and fancied or real insults.

Margaret accordingly went up and down to butchers and grocers, seeking for a nonpareil of a girl; and lowering her hopes and expectations every week as she found the difficulty of meeting with anyone in a manufacturing town who did not prefer the better wages and greater independence of working in a mill. It was something of a trial to Margaret to go out by herself in this busy, bustling place. Mrs. Shaw's ideas of propriety and her own helpless dependence on others had always made her insist that a footman should accompany Edith and Margaret if they went beyond Harley Street or the immediate neighborhood. The limits by which this rule of her aunt's had circumscribed Margaret's independence had been silently rebelled against at the time; and she had doubly enjoyed the free walks and rambles of her forest life from the contrast which they presented. She went along there with a boundless, fearless step, that occasionally broke out into a run if she were in a hurry, and occasionally was stilled into perfect repose as she stood listening to, or watching any of the wild creatures who sang in the leafy courts, or glanced out with their keen, bright eyes from the low brushwood or tangled furze. It was a trial to come down from such motion or such stillness, only guided by her own sweet will, to the even and decorous pace necessary in streets. But she could have laughed at herself for minding this change if it had not been accompanied by what was a more serious annoyance.

The side of the town on which Crampton lay was especially a thoroughfare for the factory people. In the back streets around them there were many mills, out of which poured streams of men and women two or three times a day. Until Margaret had learned the times of their ingress and egress, she was very unfortunate in constantly falling in with them. They came rushing along, with bold, fearless faces and loud laughs and jests, particularly aimed at all those who appeared to be above them in rank or station. The tones of their unrestrained voices and their carelessness of all common rules of street politeness frightened Margaret a little at first. The girls, with their rough but not unf

"Let China's earth, enriched with coloured stains,
Pencil'd with gold, and streaked with azure veins,
The grateful flavour of the Indian leaf,
Or Mocha's sunburnt berry glad receive."

The following day, Mr. Hale ascended the stairs to the small drawing-room at an hour that was decidedly unconventional. He moved among the furnishings with a distracted air, his hands hovering over objects as if inspecting them, yet Margaret perceived the gesture for what it truly was: a nervous deferral, a stalling tactic for words he desired to utter but dreaded to speak. Finally, the truth emerged.

"My dear," he announced, "I have invited Mr. Thornton to tea tonight."

Mrs. Hale reclined in her armchair, eyes closed, her face bearing a habitual mask of suffering. Yet, at her husband's announcement, she sprang upright, her demeanor shifting instantly to querulous agitation.

"Mr. Thornton! And tonight! What on earth could possibly bring that man here? Besides, Dixon is scrubbing my muslins and laces, and there is no soft water to be found amidst these dreadful eastern winds, which I suspect will plague us in Milton for the remainder of the year."

"The wind is turning, my love," Mr. Hale replied, gazing out at the smoke drifting squarely from the east, though he had not yet mastered the cardinal points and often rearranged them to suit his convenience.

"Don't bother telling me!" Mrs. Hale shuddered, tightening her shawl around her shoulders. "Be that as it may, I suppose the man is coming."

"Oh, Mother, that merely proves you have never truly met Mr. Thornton," Margaret interjected. "He is the sort of person who finds joy in battling every adversity life throws his way—whether enemies, storms, or misfortune. The harder the rain and wind blow, the more certain it is he will arrive. I shall go and assist Dixon; I am becoming quite proficient at starching. And Dixon will require nothing more than conversation with Father. Father, I am genuinely eager to introduce the Pythias to your Damon. You recall how awkward our first meeting was, how we struggled to find common ground?"

"I cannot say I expect you to find him agreeable, Margaret. He is certainly not a 'lady's man.'"

Margaret twisted her neck in a gesture of disdainful amusement.

"I hold no particular admiration for 'lady's men,' Father. However, Mr. Thornton has come as your friend, one who has recognized your worth—"

"The only person in Milton," Mrs. Hale muttered.

"Then let us offer him a warm welcome and some coconut cakes. Dixon would be delighted to prepare them, and I shall happily iron your caps, Mother."

That morning, Margaret had wished Mr. Thornton a thousand miles away. She had envisioned other pursuits: a letter to Edith, a passage of Dante, a visit to the Higgins household. Instead, she stood ironing, listening to Dixon's grievances, hoping that an overflow of sympathy might keep the laundry maid from recounting her sorrows to Mrs. Hale. Periodically, Margaret had to summon her father's high regard for Mr. Thornton to quell the creeping irritation and the onset of the severe headaches that plagued her lately. By the time she finally sat down to inform her mother that she was no longer Peggy the laundry-maid but Margaret Hale, the lady, she intended it as a jest. She was chagrined when her mother took the remark with deadly seriousness.

"Yes! If anyone had told me, when I was Miss Beresford and one of the country's belles, that my child would spend half a day standing in a cramped kitchen, laboring like a servant to prepare properly for the reception of a tradesman—and that this tradesman should be the only—"

"Oh, Mother!" Margaret interrupted, rising abruptly. "Do not punish me for such a careless slip. I do not mind ironing, or any work for you and Father. I remain a born and bred lady through it all, even if it means scouring floors or washing dishes. I am merely tired for a moment; in half an hour, I shall be ready to begin again. As for Mr. Thornton's trade, well, he cannot help that now, poor fellow. I doubt his education would qualify him for much else."

She rose slowly and retreated to her own room, unable to endure any further strain.

Simultaneously, in the Thornton household, a scene of stark contrast unfolded. In a grand, grimly furnished dining room, a large-boned woman, well past middle age, sat at work. Her features, matching her sturdy frame, were strong and massive rather than heavy. Her countenance shifted slowly between distinct expressions of resolve. There was little variety in her face, yet those who saw it once were compelled to look again; even passersby in the street turned their heads to gaze a moment longer at the firm, severe, dignified woman who never yielded in street courtesy nor paused from her straight path toward her clearly defined ends.

She wore handsome black silk, stout and immaculate, not a thread worn or discolored. She mended a vast tablecloth of the finest texture, holding it up against the light to inspect thin spots requiring her delicate attention. No books cluttered the room save for Matthew Henry's Bible commentaries, six volumes resting on the massive sideboard, flanked by a tea-urn and a lamp. In a distant room, the piano played an exercise piece. Someone practiced a salon morceau with frantic speed, missing every third note on average, rendering many chords indistinct or entirely absent, yet the performer remained entirely satisfied with the result. Mrs. Thornton heard a step, decisive and familiar, pass the dining-room door.

"John! Is that you?"

Her son appeared in the doorway.

"What brings you home so early? I thought you were going to tea with that friend of Mr. Bell's, Mr. Hale?"

"So I am, Mother. I have returned to dress."

"Dress! Humph! When I was a girl, young men were content to dress once a day. Why dress to take a cup of tea with an old parson?"

"Mr. Hale is a gentleman, and his wife and daughter are ladies."

"Wife and daughter! Do they teach? What do they do? You have never mentioned them."

"No, Mother, because I have never seen Mrs. Hale; I have seen Miss Hale for only half an hour."

"Take care you do not get caught by a penniless girl, John."

"I am not easily caught, Mother, as I believe you know. But I must not have Miss Hale spoken of in that manner, which you know is offensive to me. I have never been aware of any young lady attempting to catch me, nor do I believe anyone has ever given themselves such useless trouble."

Mrs. Thornton refused to yield the point, driven perhaps by pride or the general dignity of her sex.

"Well! I only say, take care. Perhaps our Milton girls possess too much spirit and good feeling to go angling for husbands; but this Miss Hale comes from the aristocratic counties, where, if all tales are true, rich husbands are considered prizes."

Mr. Thornton's brow furrowed, and he stepped forward into the room.

"Mother" (with a short, scornful laugh), "you will force me to confess. The only time I saw Miss Hale, she treated me with a haughty civility thick with contempt. She held herself aloof as if she were a queen and I her humble, unwashed vassal. Be easy, Mother."

"No! I am neither easy nor content. What business had she, a renegade clergyman's daughter, to turn her nose up at you! I would not dress for any of them—a saucy set! If I were you." As he left the room, he added:

"Mr. Hale is good, gentle, and learned. He is not saucy. As for Mrs. Hale, I shall tell you what she is like tonight, if you care to hear."

He closed the door and departed.

"Despise my son! Treat him as her vassal, indeed! Humph! I should like to know where she could find such another! Boy and man, he is the noblest, stoutest heart I have ever known. I do not care if I am his mother; I can see what is what and not be blind. I know what Fanny is, and I know what John is. Despise him! I hate her!"

"We are the trees whom shaking fastens more."

Mr. Thornton departed from the house without returning to the dining-room, driven by a sense of urgency that bordered on anxiety. He feared that any delay might be construed as disrespect toward his new acquaintance. As he hurried toward Crampton's residence, the church clock tolled half-past seven. He paused at the door, watching Dixon's inevitable slowness; she was invariably tardy whenever the duty of answering the bell fell upon her. Finally admitted into the small drawing-room, he was greeted warmly by Mr. Hale, who guided him to his wife. Margaret sat there, her pallor and the shawl draped loosely around her shoulders offering a silent apology for her cool reception. She was lighting the lamp as he entered, the twilight deepening outside.

The lamplight cast a soft glow into the center of the room, a space left open to the night sky and the encroaching darkness—a stark contrast to the heavy, mirror-less dining room he had just left. That other room felt designed solely for consumption, dominated by his mother's presence and her absolute will. This drawing-room, however, was vastly finer, though far less comfortable. It lacked mirrors, devoid of glass to catch the light, and held no gilding. Instead, it possessed a sober, warm breadth of color, softened by the familiar Helstone chintz curtains and chair covers. An open davenport stood by the window, while another held a tall white china vase overflowing with English ivy, pale green birch, and copper-toned beech leaves. Baskets of needlework were scattered about, and books lay open on a table, seemingly abandoned mid-read. Behind the door, a second table was set for tea, draped in white linen and laden with coconut cakes, oranges, and red American apples resting on leaves.

To Mr. Thornton, these graceful preparations seemed habitual to the family, particularly to Margaret. She stood by the tea table in a light muslin gown touched with pink, appearing entirely absorbed in the arrangement of the cups. Her round, ivory hands moved with a dainty, noiseless precision among the porcelain. A bracelet adorned one slender arm, threatening to slide down over her wrist. Mr. Thornton found himself watching the movement of this ornament with more fascination than he paid to Mr. Hale's words. It seemed to captivate him to see her push the band up impatiently until it constricted her soft flesh, only to watch it loosen and fall again. "There it goes again!" he almost whispered. Once the tea service was complete, he felt a pang of regret that the necessity of eating and drinking would soon interrupt his observation of Margaret. She served him his cup with the proud demeanor of a reluctant servant, yet her eyes betrayed her eagerness to refill his cup. He longed to ask her to perform the task he saw her father compelling her to do for him—holding her tiny fingers and thumbs as sugar tongs. He watched her beautiful eyes lift to her father, filled with a mixture of light, laughter, and love during this silent pantomime, unaware that they thought themselves unseen. Though her head still ached, betraying itself through her pale complexion and silence, Margaret resolved to fill any awkward pauses rather than allow her father's guest to feel neglected.

After the tea things were cleared, Margaret retreated to a corner near her mother with her work, allowing her thoughts to wander freely. Meanwhile, Mr. Thornton and Mr. Hale remained deeply engrossed in their discussion, a topic carried over from their previous meeting. Margaret was only reminded of the present moment by a casual remark from her mother. Looking up from her work, she was struck by the profound difference in appearance between her father and Mr. Thornton, a reflection of their opposing natures. Her father was slight, appearing taller than he was when not contrasted with the massive, towering frame of the other man. His face bore soft, wavering lines, traversed by a constant, trembling movement that revealed every shifting emotion. His eyelids were large and arched, lending his eyes a languid, almost feminine beauty, while his brows were finely curved but raised high by the weight of those dreamy lids.

In stark contrast, Mr. Thornton's straight brows fell low over clear, deep-set, earnest eyes that seemed intent on penetrating the very core of his subject. The lines on his face were few but firm, carved in marble, concentrated mainly around his lips. These lips were slightly compressed, framing teeth so perfect they resembled sudden sunlight. When a rare, bright smile flashed across his face, transforming his severe, resolved expression into one of keen, honest enjoyment, it was a joyfulness seldom displayed so fearlessly by adults. Margaret admired this smile; it was the first quality she had found admirable in her father's new friend. The visible opposition of their characters seemed to explain the magnetic attraction they clearly felt for one another.

She rearranged her mother's worsted work and sank into her own reverie, completely forgotten by Mr. Thornton, who was fully occupied explaining the magnificent yet delicate power of the steam-hammer to Mr. Hale. He spoke of the machine's ability to stretch from earth to sky, filling the horizon, and then obediently compress into a vessel small enough for a child to carry.

"This imagination of power, this practical realization of a gigantic thought, sprang from one man's brain in our town," Mr. Thornton declared. "That very man possesses the capacity to ascend, step by step, to higher marvels. I am certain there are many among us who, if he were gone, could spring into the breach and carry on the war that compels all material power to yield to science."

"Your boast reminds me of the old lines:
'I've a hundred captains in England,' he said,
'As good as ever was he.'"

At her father's sudden shift from cogwheels to *Chevy Chase*, Margaret looked up in wondering surprise. How had they leaped so far from industry to romance?

"It is no boast of mine," Mr. Thornton replied, his tone matter-of-fact. "I need not deny that I am proud to belong to a district where necessity births such grandeur of conception. I would rather be a man toiling, suffering—even failing—here, than lead a dull, prosperous life in the old, worn grooves of the aristocratic South, with its slow days of careless ease. One may become clogged with honey and lose the ability to rise and fly."

"You are mistaken," Margaret interrupted, her defense of the beloved South bringing color to her cheeks and angry tears to her eyes. "You know nothing of the South. If there is less adventure or progress—perhaps less excitement, driven by a gambling spirit of trade required to force out such inventions—there is also less suffering. I see men here walking the streets who look ground down by some pinching sorrow or care; they are not only sufferers but haters. In the South, we have our poor, but there is not that terrible expression of sullen injustice I see here. You do not know the South, Mr. Thornton," she concluded, collapsing into a determined silence, furious with herself for speaking so freely.

"And may I say you do not know the North?" he asked, his voice possessing an inexpressible gentleness as he sensed he had wounded her. She remained resolutely silent, yearning for the lovely haunts of Hampshire, feeling her voice would tremble if she attempted to speak.

"At any rate, Mr. Thornton," Mrs. Hale interjected, "you will allow that Milton is a much more smoky, dirty town than you will ever encounter in the South."

"I'm afraid I must concede its cleanliness," Mr. Thornton said, a quick, gleaming smile breaking across his face. "But we are bidden by Parliament to burn our own smoke; so I suppose, like good little children, we shall do as we are bid—eventually."

"But I thought you told me you had altered your chimneys to consume the smoke, did you not?" Mr. Hale asked.

"My chimneys were altered by my own will before Parliament intervened," Mr. Thornton corrected. "It was an immediate expense, but it repays me in coal savings. I am not sure I would have done it had I waited for the Act. At least, I would have waited to be informed against and fined, giving all the legal trouble possible. But all laws relying on informers and fines become inert due to the odiousness of their machinery. I doubt a single chimney in Milton has been reported in the last five years, despite some constantly emitting what we call unparliamentary smoke."

"I only know it is impossible to keep the muslin blinds clean here above a week," Mrs. Hale noted. "At Helstone, we have had them up for a month or more, and they remain unstained. And as for hands—Margaret, how many times did you wash them this morning before noon? Three times, was it not?"

"Yes, Mamma."

"You seem to have a strong objection to Acts of Parliament and legislation affecting your management here in Milton," Mr. Hale observed.

"Yes, I do; and many others share this view, with justice, I believe. The entire machinery of the cotton trade is so new that it is no wonder it does not function perfectly everywhere simultaneously. Seventy years ago, what was it? And now, what is it not? Raw, crude materials converged; men of similar education and station suddenly found themselves divided into masters and workers based on foresight regarding the future potential of Sir Richard Arkwright's model. This rapid development of a new trade granted those early masters enormous power over wealth and command—not just over workmen, but over purchasers and the global market. Consider an advertisement inserted in a Milton paper not fifty years ago, stating that a calico-printer would close his warehouse at noon, dictating that all buyers must arrive before that hour. Imagine a man dictating the hours of sale! Today, if a good customer chose to come at midnight, I would rise and stand hat in hand to receive their orders."

Margaret's lip curled, yet she was compelled to listen; she could no longer retreat into her own thoughts.

"I mention such things to illustrate the almost unlimited power manufacturers held at the beginning of this century. Success in ventures rendered them dizzy. Because a man prospered in his ventures, there was no reason his mind should remain balanced in other matters. On the contrary, his sense of justice and simplicity were often smothered under the glut of wealth descending upon him. They tell strange tales of the wild extravagance indulged in by those early cotton lords on gala days. There can be no doubt of the tyranny they exercised over their workpeople. As the proverb says, 'Set a beggar on horseback, and he'll ride to the devil'; some of these early manufacturers rode to the devil in magnificent style, crushing human bone and flesh under their horses' hooves without remorse. But eventually, a reaction occurred. More factories arose, more masters emerged, and more labor was needed. The balance of power between masters and men became more even, and now the battle is fairly waged between us. We will hardly submit to the decision of an umpire, much less to the interference of a meddler with only a smattering of knowledge, even if that meddler is called the High Court of Parliament."

"Is there necessity for calling it a battle between the two classes?" Mr. Hale asked, noting that the term accurately reflected his son-in-law's perception.

"It is true, and I believe it is as necessary as the conflict between prudent wisdom and ignorance. It is one of the great beauties of our system that a working-man may raise himself into the power and position of a master through his own exertions. Every one who rules themselves with decency, sobriety, and attention to duty joins our ranks; it may not be as a master, but as an overlooker, cashier, bookkeeper, or clerk—one on the side of authority and order."

"You consider all who fail to raise themselves in the world, from whatever cause, as your enemies, then, if I understand you rightly," Margaret said in a clear, cold voice.

"As their own enemies, certainly," he replied quickly, stung by the haughty disapproval in her tone. Yet, his straightforward honesty soon compelled him to realize his words were a poor, quibbling answer. Regardless of her scorn, it was a duty he owed himself to explain his meaning truthfully. It was difficult to separate her interpretation from his intent. He could have illustrated his point better by recounting his own life, but was it too personal for strangers? Still, it was the simplest way to clarify. Putting aside the momentary flush of shyness that colored his dark cheek, he spoke:

"I speak without book. Sixteen years ago, my father died under miserable circumstances. I was taken from school and forced to become a man in a few days. I had a mother of rare strength and firm resolve. We moved to a small country town where living was cheaper than in Milton, and I found employment in a draper's shop—an excellent place for learning about goods. Week by week, our income was fifteen shillings, from which three people had to live. My mother managed so that I saved three shillings regularly. This was the beginning; this taught me self-denial. Now that I can afford my mother such comforts as her age requires rather than her own wishes, I thank her silently for that early training. When I feel that in my own case it was no luck, merit, or talent, but simply the habits of life that taught me to despise indulgences not thoroughly earned—and never to think twice about them—I believe the suffering Miss Hale observes on the faces of Milton's people is merely the natural punishment for dishonestly enjoyed pleasure in some former period of their lives. I do not look upon self-indulgent, sensual people with hatred; I simply regard them with contempt for their poverty of character."

"But you have the rudiments of a good education," Mr. Hale remarked. "The zest with which you now read Homer shows you do not approach it as an unknown book; you have read it before and are only recalling old knowledge."

"That is true; I blundered along it at school. I dare say I was considered a fair classic in those days, though my Latin and Greek have slipped away since. But I ask you, what preparation was that for the life I had to lead? None at all. Utterly none. On the point of education, any man who can read and

"There's iron, they say, in all our blood,
And a grain or two perhaps is good;
But his, he makes me harshly feel,
Has got a little too much of steel."

"Margaret!" Mr. Hale called out as he guided his guest downstairs, his tone betraying a flicker of concern. "I couldn't help watching your face with some anxiety when Mr. Thornton confessed to being a shop-boy. I knew the truth from Mr. Bell beforehand, so I expected nothing less than to see you rise abruptly and flee the room."

"Oh, papa! Did you truly think me so foolish?" Margaret replied, her voice lightening. "I found that admission far more endearing than anything else he said. Everything else grated on me due to its hardness, yet he spoke of himself with such simplicity, devoid of the pretense that often marks the vulgarity of shopkeepers, and with such tender reverence for his mother. In that moment, I felt less inclined to leave than when he boasted of Milton as if no other place existed, or when he dismissed others for their careless improvidence without considering it his duty to offer them the guidance his mother had given him. No, his confession of humble origins was the very thing I admired most."

"I am surprised, Margaret," her mother interjected. "You who have always accused people of being 'shoppy' at Helstone! I do not think, Mr. Hale, you were right to introduce such a person without disclosing his background. I was genuinely terrified of showing how shocked I was by certain remarks. His father 'dying in miserable circumstances'—one might have assumed it was in a workhouse."

"I cannot be certain it was not worse," her husband answered gravely. "Mr. Bell informed me of much regarding his past before we arrived. Since he has shared a portion, I shall complete the picture. His father speculated recklessly, failed, and then took his own life, unable to bear the disgrace. All former friends recoiled from the revelations of his dishonest gambling—wild, desperate struggles using other people's money to recover his modest fortune. No one stepped forward to aid the mother and this boy. There was another child, a girl, too young to work, yet she had to be supported. Initially, no friend came forward, and Mrs. Thornton, I suspect, is not the type to wait for tardy kindness. They left Milton. I knew he had entered a shop, and that his earnings, combined with a small inheritance for his mother, sustained them for years. Mr. Bell noted they subsisted on water-porridge for a long time—how, he could not say. Long after creditors had abandoned hope of repayment following the father's suicide, this young man returned to Milton. He went quietly to each creditor, paying the first installment owed. There was no clamor, no assembly of debtors; it was done silently, yet eventually, everything was settled. This was aided significantly by one creditor, a grumpy old man, who took Mr. Thornton in as a partner."

"That is truly admirable," Margaret remarked. "What a pity such a nature should be tarnished by his position as a Milton manufacturer."

"How so?" her father asked.

"Oh, papa, by his tendency to judge everything by the standard of wealth. When he discussed mechanical powers, he clearly viewed them merely as new methods to expand trade and accumulate money. And the poor men around him—they were poor because they were vicious, excluded from his sympathies because they lacked his 'iron nature' and the capabilities it bestowed for becoming rich."

"Not vicious; he used the words 'improvident and self-indulgent,'" her father corrected gently.

Margaret was gathering her mother's sewing materials, preparing for bed. Just as she turned to leave, she hesitated. She felt compelled to make an acknowledgment that would please her father, though to be entirely truthful, it required admitting a degree of personal dislike.

"Papa, I consider Mr. Thornton a remarkably capable man; however, personally, I do not like him at all."

"And I do!" her father laughed. "Personally, as you term it, and all. I do not elevate him to the status of a hero or similar figure. But good night, child. Your mother appears exceedingly fatigued tonight, Margaret."

Margaret had observed her mother's worn appearance with growing alarm. Her father's comment sent her to her room with a heavy, dim fear settling upon her heart. Life in Milton differed starkly from her existence in Helstone, where she was constantly immersed in fresh, open air. Here, the air seemed stripped of all revitalizing quality. Domestic worries pressed upon the women of the family with such intensity and in such sordid forms that there was legitimate cause to fear her mother's health was deteriorating seriously. There were other ominous signs as well. She and Dixon engaged in secretive consultations in her bedroom, from which Dixon emerged crying and irritable, as was her custom whenever her mistress's distress demanded sympathy. Once, Margaret entered the chamber shortly after Dixon departed and found her mother on her knees. As she slipped away, she caught a few words that were clearly a prayer for strength and patience to endure severe physical suffering. Margaret yearned to restore the bond of intimate confidence severed by her long absence at her aunt Shaw's, striving with gentle touches and softened words to reclaim a place in her mother's heart. Yet, though she received affection in abundance, sufficient to gladden her former self, she sensed a secret withheld, one she believed pertained seriously to her mother's health. She lay awake late that night, devising ways to mitigate the harmful influence of their Milton life. She considered hiring a servant to assist Dixon permanently, freeing her to dedicate her time to finding a suitable replacement for her mother's care.

For several days, visiting registration offices and encountering a myriad of unlikely individuals consumed Margaret's time and thoughts. One afternoon, she encountered Bessy Higgins in the street and stopped to speak.

"Well, Bessy, how are you? Better, I hope, now the wind has changed."

"Better and not better, if you understand what that implies," Bessy replied.

"Not exactly," Margaret smiled.

"I am better in that I am no longer torn apart by coughing all night, but I am weary and tired of Milton, longing to escape to the Land of Beulah. And when I realize I am drifting further and further away, my heart sinks, and I am no better; I am worse."

Margaret turned to walk beside the girl in her feeble progress home. For a moment or two, she remained silent. Finally, she whispered, "Bessy, do you wish to die?" For she, too, shrank from death, clinging to life with the natural vigor of youth and health.

Bessy was silent for a moment. Then she answered, "If you had lived the life I have, grown as weary of it as I have, and occasionally thought, 'perhaps it will last fifty or sixty years—it does for some,' and felt dizzy and dazed, and sick, as each of those sixty years seemed to spin around me, mocking me with its endless hours and minutes and fragments of time—oh, wench! I tell you, you would be glad enough when the doctor said he feared you would never see another winter."

"Why, Bessy, what sort of life has yours been?"

"Nought worse than many others', I reckon. Only I fretted against it, and they did not."

"But what was it? You know, I am a stranger here, so perhaps I am not as quick to understand your meaning as someone who has lived all their life in Milton."

"If you had come to our house when you promised, I might have told you. But father says you are just like the rest of them; it is out of sight, out of mind with you."

"I do not know who the rest are; and I have been very busy. To tell the truth, I had forgotten my promise—"

"You offered it; we asked for nothing," Bessy retorted.

"I had forgotten what I said for the time," Margaret continued calmly. "I would have remembered again when I was less occupied. May I accompany you now?"

Bessy cast a quick glance at Margaret's face to gauge the sincerity of the wish. The sharpness in her eyes softened into a wistful longing as she met Margaret's gentle, friendly gaze.

"I have no one else to care for me; if you care, you may come."

Thus, they walked on in silence. As they turned up into a small court from a squalid street, Bessy said, "You will not be daunted if father is at home and speaks a bit gruffly at first. He has taken a liking to you, you see, and he values your visit greatly; and precisely because he likes you, he was vexed and troubled."

"Do not fear, Bessy."

Yet Nicholas was not present when they entered. A large, unkempt girl, younger than Bessy but taller and stronger, was busy at the wash-tub, knocking about the furniture in a rough, capable manner, creating such a racket that Margaret shrank back, sympathetic to poor Bessy, who sat down on the first chair as if utterly exhausted by the walk. Margaret asked the sister for a cup of water. While she ran to fetch it, knocking over fire-irons and stumbling over a chair in her haste, she unfastened Bessy's bonnet strings to relieve her labored breathing.

"Do you think such a life as this is worth caring for?" Bessy gasped finally. Margaret said nothing but held the water to her lips. Bessy took a long, feverish draught and then collapsed, closing her eyes. Margaret heard her muttering to herself: "They shall hunger no more, neither thirst any more: neither shall the sun light on them, nor any heat."

Margaret leaned over and said, "Bessy, do not be impatient with your life, whatever it is—or may have been. Remember who gave it you and shaped it as it is!"

She was startled by the sound of Nicholas speaking behind her; he had entered unnoticed.

"Now, I will not have my wench preached to. She is bad enough as it is, with her dreams and her Methodist fancies and her visions of cities with golden gates and precious stones. But if it amuses her, I let it be; I am not going to have more nonsense poured into her head."

"But surely," Margaret turned to face him, "you believe in what I said, that God gave her life and ordained the kind of life it would be?"

"I believe what I see, and no more. That is my belief, young woman. I do not believe everything I hear—not by a long shot. I did hear a young lass make a fuss about knowing where we lived and coming to see us. My wench here thought a great deal about it and flushed many times when she little knew I was watching her at the sound of a strange step. But she has come at last—and she is welcome, as long as she keeps from preaching on what she knows nothing about."

Bessy had been watching Margaret's face; she half rose to speak now, laying her hand on Margaret's arm in an entreaty. "Do not be vexed with him—many think like him; many, many do here. If you could hear them speak, you would not be shocked at him; he is a rare good man, is father—but oh!" she fell back in despair, "what he says at times makes me long to die more than ever, for I want to know so many things and am so tossed about with wonder."

"Poor wench—poor old wench—I am loath to vex you, I am; but a man must speak out for the truth. When I see the world going all wrong at this time of day, bothering itself with things it knows nothing about, and leaving undone all the things that lie in disorder close at its hand—why, I say, leave all this talk about religion alone and set to work on what you see and know. That is my creed. It is simple, not far to reach, nor hard to work."

But the girl only pleaded with Margaret the more.

"Do not think hardly on him—he is a good man, he is. I sometimes think I shall be moped with sorrow even in the City of God if father is not there." A feverish color rose in her cheek, and a feverish flame ignited in her eye. "But you will be there, father! You shall! Oh! my heart!" She clutched her chest and turned ghastly pale.

Margaret held her in her arms, resting the weary head upon her bosom. She lifted the thin, soft hair from the temples and bathed them with water. Nicholas understood all her gestures for different articles with the quickness of love, and even the wide-eyed sister moved with laborious gentleness at Margaret's "hush!" Presently, the spasm that foreshadowed death passed, and Bessy roused herself to say, "I'll go to bed—it's the best place; but," catching at Margaret's gown, "you'll come again—I know you will—but just say it!"

"I will come again tomorrow," Margaret promised.

Bessy leaned back against her father, who prepared to carry her upstairs. But as Margaret rose to leave, he struggled to say something: "I could wish there were a God, if it were only to ask Him to bless thee."

Margaret departed very sad and thoughtful.

She was late for tea at home. At Helstone, unpunctuality at meal-times was a grave fault in her mother's eyes; but now, this, along with many other minor irregularities, seemed to have lost its power to irritate, and Margaret almost longed for the old complaints.

"Have you met with a servant, dear?"

"No, mamma; that Anne Buckley would never have done."

"Suppose I try," Mr. Hale suggested. "Everybody else has had their turn at this great difficulty. Now let me try. I may be the Cinderella to put on the slipper after all."

Margaret could hardly smile at this little joke, so oppressed was she by her visit to the Higginses.

"What would you do, papa? How would you set about it?"

"Why, I should apply to some good house-mother

The morning sun cast a sharp, unyielding light over the Thornton household, illuminating the tension that had settled within the drawing room like dust motes in a shaft of light. Mr. Thornton had spent considerable effort attempting to elevate his mother's spirits to a level of mere civility. Mrs. Thornton was not a woman accustomed to casual visits; she approached social duties with the gravity of a military campaign. Her son had procured a carriage, yet she had steadfastly refused to allow him to retain the horses, insisting on hiring them only for these solemn occasions of calling. For three days, she had ridden out with borrowed steeds, systematically exhausting the patience and resources of her acquaintances, leaving them to scramble for their own transport in return. However, Crampton remained too distant for a walk, prompting Mrs. Thornton to question her son repeatedly regarding the necessity of the cab fare. She confessed a reluctance to incur such expense, fearing that establishing intimacy with the Hales might merely be the prelude to being asked to visit Fanny's dancing master's wife.

"And so I would, mother," Mr. Thornton replied, "if Mr. Mason and his wife were friendless in a strange place, like the Hales."

"Oh! you need not speak so hastily," she countered. "I am going tomorrow. I only wished you to understand the arrangement precisely."

"If you are going tomorrow, I shall order the horses."

"Nonsense, John. One would think you were made of money."

"Not quite, yet. But regarding the horses, I am determined. The last time you rode out in a cab, you returned with a headache from the jolting."

"I never complained of it, I'm sure."

"No!" Mr. Thornton said, a note of pride coloring his voice. "My mother is not given to complaints. But precisely because of that, I have all the more reason to watch over you. Now, as for Fanny, a little hardship would do her good."

"She is not made of the same stuff as you are, John," Mrs. Thornton replied softly. "She could not bear it."

Silence fell upon the room. Mrs. Thornton's final words touched upon a subject that mortified her. She harbored an unconscious contempt for weakness, and Fanny was weak in the very areas where her mother and brother possessed strength. Mrs. Thornton was not a woman inclined toward lengthy reasoning; her quick judgment and firm resolution substituted for internal debate. Instinctively, she knew that nothing could strengthen Fanny to endure hardship patiently or face difficulties bravely. Though she winced at this admission regarding her daughter, it bred only a pitying tenderness in her manner, akin to the way mothers treat their frail, sickly children. To a careless observer, Mrs. Thornton's demeanor might have suggested she loved Fanny more than John. Such an interpretation would have been profoundly mistaken. The boldness with which mother and son exchanged unpalatable truths revealed a reliance on the firm center of each other's souls. In contrast, Mrs. Thornton's uneasy tenderness toward her daughter, coupled with her shame at hiding Fanny's poverty behind the grand qualities she herself possessed and valued so highly, betrayed a lack of a secure resting place for her affection. She never addressed her son by any name other than John; terms of endearment like "love" and "dear" were reserved exclusively for Fanny. Yet, her heart gave thanks for her son day and night, and she walked proudly among women solely for his sake.

"Fanny dear!" Mrs. Thornton broke the silence. "I shall have horses attached to the carriage today to visit the Hales. Should you not go and see Nurse? It is in the same direction, and she is always so glad to see you. You could go on ahead while I am at Mrs. Hale's."

"Oh! Mamma, it is such a long way, and I am so tired."

"With what?" Mrs. Thornton asked, her brow contracting slightly.

"I don't know—the weather, I think. It is so relaxing. Couldn't you bring Nurse here, Mamma? The carriage could fetch her, and she could spend the rest of the day here, which I know she would like."

Mrs. Thornton said nothing, laying her work upon the table as if in deep thought.

"It will be a long way for her to walk back at night!" she remarked finally.

"Oh, but I will send her home in a cab. I never thought of her walking."

Just then, Mr. Thornton entered, preparing to depart for the mill.

"Mother! I need hardly say that if there is any little thing that could serve Mrs. Hale as an invalid, you will offer it, I'm sure."

"If I can find it out, I will. But I have never been ill myself, so I am not much up to invalids' fancies."

"Well! Here is Fanny then, who is seldom without an ailment. She will be able to suggest something, perhaps—won't you, Fan?"

"I have not always an ailment," Fanny said pettishly. "And I am not going with Mamma. I have a headache today, and I shan't go out."

Mr. Thornton looked annoyed. His mother's eyes were fixed on her work, where she stitched away busily.

"Fanny! I wish you to go," he said authoritatively. "It will do you good, instead of harm. You will oblige me by going, without my saying anything more about it."

He left the room abruptly. Had he stayed a moment longer, Fanny would have cried at his tone of command, even with the words "You will oblige me." As it was, she merely grumbled.

"John always speaks as if I fancied I was ill, and I am sure I never do fancy any such thing. Who are these Hales that he makes such a fuss about?"

"Fanny, don't speak so of your brother. He has good reasons of some kind or other, or he would not wish us to go. Make haste and put your things on."

However, the slight altercation between her son and her daughter did not incline Mrs. Thornton more favorably toward "these Hales." Her jealous heart repeated her daughter's question, "Who are they, that he is so anxious we should pay them all this attention?" It lingered like a burden in a song, long after Fanny had forgotten it in the pleasant excitement of admiring her new bonnet in the looking-glass.

Mrs. Thornton was shy. Only in recent years had she possessed the leisure to enter society, and she did not truly enjoy it. She found satisfaction in giving dinners and critiquing others', but making acquaintance with strangers was a wholly different matter. She felt ill at ease, appearing more stern and forbidding than usual as she entered the Hales' small drawing-room.

Margaret was busy embroidering a small piece of cambric for an article of dress for Edith's expected baby—"Flimsy, useless work," Mrs. Thornton observed to herself. She preferred Mrs. Hale's double knitting; that was sensible work of its kind. The room was filled with knick-knacks that required significant time to dust, and time was money for people of limited income.

Mrs. Thornton made these reflections as she spoke in her stately manner to Mrs. Hale, uttering the stereotyped commonplaces that most people find when their senses are blindfolded. Mrs. Hale exerted herself more in her answers, captivated by a real old lace Mrs. Thornton wore. "Lace," she later told Dixon, "of that old English point which has not been made for seventy years, and which cannot be bought. It must have been an heirloom, showing that she had ancestors." Thus, the owner of ancestral lace became worthy of more than the languid exertion required to be agreeable to a visitor, which would otherwise have been the limit of Mrs. Hale's conversational efforts. Soon, Margaret, straining her brain to converse with Fanny, heard her mother and Mrs. Thornton plunge into the interminable subject of servants.

"I suppose you are not musical," Fanny remarked, "as I see no piano."

"I am fond of hearing good music; I cannot play well myself; and Papa and Mamma don't care much about it; so we sold our old piano when we came here."

"I wonder how you can exist without one. It almost seems to me a necessary of life."

"Fifteen shillings a week, and three saved out of them!" Margaret thought to herself. "But she must have been very young. She probably has forgotten her own personal experience. But she must know of those days." Margaret's manner acquired an extra tinge of coldness when she next spoke.

"You have good concerts here, I believe."

"Oh, yes! Delicious! Too crowded, that is the worst. The directors admit so indiscriminately. But one is sure to hear the newest music there. I always have a large order to give to Johnson's the day after a concert."

"Do you like new music simply for its newness, then?"

"Oh; one knows it is the fashion in London, or else the singers would not bring it down here. You have been in London, of course."

"Yes," said Margaret, "I have lived there for several years."

"Oh! London and the Alhambra are the two places I long to see!"

"London and the Alhambra!"

"Yes! Ever since I read the Tales of the Alhambra. Don't you know them?"

"I don't think I do. But surely, it is a very easy journey to London."

"Yes; but somehow," Fanny said, lowering her voice, "Mamma has never been to London herself, and can't understand my longing. She is very proud of Milton; a dirty, smoky place as I feel it to be. I believe she admires it the more for those very qualities."

"If it has been Mrs. Thornton's home for some years, I can well understand her loving it," Margaret said in her clear, bell-like voice.

"What are you saying about me, Miss Hale? May I inquire?"

Margaret had no ready words for this question, which took her slightly by surprise, so Miss Thornton replied:

"Oh, Mamma! We are only trying to account for your being so fond of Milton."

"Thank you," said Mrs. Thornton. "I do not feel that my very natural liking for the place where I was born and brought up—and which has since been my residence for some years—requires any accounting for."

Margaret was vexed. As Fanny had put it, it seemed as if they had been impertinently discussing Mrs. Thornton's feelings; yet she also rose against that lady's manner of showing offense.

After a moment's pause, Mrs. Thornton continued:

"Do you know anything of Milton, Miss Hale? Have you seen any of our factories? Our magnificent warehouses?"

"No!" said Margaret. "I have not seen anything of that description as yet."

Then she felt that, by concealing her utter indifference to all such places, she was hardly speaking with truth; so she went on:

"I dare say, Papa would have taken me before now if I had cared. But I really do not find much pleasure in going over manufactories."

"They are very curious places," said Mrs. Hale, "but there is so much noise and dirt always. I remember once going in a lilac silk to see candles made, and my gown was utterly ruined."

"Very probably," said Mrs. Thornton in a short, displeased manner. "I merely thought that as strangers newly come to reside in a town which has risen to eminence in the country from the character and progress of its peculiar business, you might have cared to visit some of the places where it is carried on; places unique in the kingdom, I am informed. If Miss Hale changes her mind and condescends to be curious as to the manufactures of Milton, I can only say I shall be glad to procure her admission to print-works, or reed-making, or the more simple operations of spinning carried on in my son's mill. Every improvement of machinery is, I believe, to be seen there in its highest perfection."

"I am so glad you don't like mills and manufactories, and all those kind of things," Fanny whispered half-audibly as she rose to accompany her mother, who was taking leave of Mrs. Hale with rustling dignity.

"I think I should like to know all about them, if I were you," Margaret replied quietly.

"Fanny!" said her mother as they drove away, "we will be civil to these Hales: but don't form one of your hasty friendships with the daughter. She will do you no good, I see. The mother looks very ill, and seems a nice, quiet kind of person."

"I don't want to form any friendship with Miss Hale, Mamma," Fanny pouted. "I thought I was doing my duty by talking to her, and trying to amuse her."

"Well! At any rate, John must be satisfied now."

"That doubt and trouble, fear and pain,
And anguish, all, are shadows vain,
That death itself shall not remain;

That weary deserts we may tread,
A dreary labyrinth may thread.
Thro' dark ways underground be led;

Yet, if we will one Guide obey,
The dreariest path, the darkest way
Shall issue out in heavenly day;

And we, on divers shores now cast,
Shall meet, our perilous voyage past,
All in our Father's house at last!"

Margaret ascended the stairs with hurried steps the moment her visitors departed, donning her bonnet and shawl to hasten to the Higgins household. Her purpose was twofold: to inquire after Betsy's condition and to offer her companionship until dinner. As she navigated the crowded, narrow thoroughfares, she felt a profound shift within herself; the mere act of caring for a resident of these streets had imbued the environment with a new depth of significance.

Inside the Higgins home, Mary, the younger sister whose appearance was often described as slatternly, had attempted to prepare for the arrival with whatever dignity she could muster. Rough stoning covered the center of the floor, yet the flags beneath the chairs and table remained dark and unwashed. Despite the sweltering heat outside, a large fire burned brightly in the grate, transforming the interior into an oven. Margaret, however, did not interpret this lavish consumption of coal as a gesture of warm hospitality; rather, she suspected the stifling warmth was a necessity for Bessy's fragile state. Bessy lay upon a small sofa positioned beneath the window, appearing far more debilitated than the previous day. Exhausted from repeatedly rising to peer out the window for Margaret's approach, she now sank back into silence, her gaze fixed on Margaret's face with a childlike wonder as she traced the fine textures of Margaret's clothing.

"I never understood why the scriptures spoke so highly of soft garments," Bessy remarked, her voice weak. "It must be delightful to be dressed as you are. It is unlike the common attire. Most fine clothes tire my eyes with their vibrant colors, yet yours possess a quality that soothes me. Where did you acquire this frock?"

"In London," Margaret replied, amused by the question.

"London! Have you actually been there?"

"Yes. I resided there for several years. Though my true home lies in the countryside, amidst the forests."

"Tell me of it," Bessy urged, leaning back and closing her eyes, crossing her hands over her chest as if to receive the incoming imagery. "I crave to hear of the country, of trees, and such things."

Margaret, who had rarely spoken of Helstone since leaving it—mentioning it only incidentally—found her heart suddenly opened to this young girl. "Oh, Bessy, I loved the home we left so dearly! I wish you could see it. I cannot describe half its beauty. Great trees stand everywhere, their branches stretching long and level to create a deep, resting shade even at noon. Yet, though every leaf appears still, there is a constant rushing sound of movement all around—not close at hand. Sometimes the turf is as soft and fine as velvet; at other times, it is lush with the perpetual moisture of a hidden, tinkling brook nearby. In other sections, there are billowy ferns—vast stretches of them, some in green shadow, others streaked with golden sunlight, resembling the sea."

"I have never seen the sea," Bessy murmured. "But continue."

"And here and there lie wide commons, elevated as if above the very treetops—"

"That pleases me," Bessy interjected. "I felt suffocated down below. Whenever I ventured out, I always sought to ascend high, to gaze far away and draw a deep breath of fullness from the air. I become suffocated enough in Milton, and I imagine the eternal sound you describe among the trees would render me dazed; it was precisely that which caused my head to ache in the mill. On these commons, I suppose, there is little noise?"

"No," Margaret assured her. "Nothing but occasionally a lark high in the sky. Sometimes I would hear a farmer speaking sharply to his servants, but it was so distant that it merely reminded me pleasantly that others were laboring hard in some remote place while I sat on the heather, doing nothing."

"I once thought that if I could possess a day of pure idleness, resting in a quiet place like the one you describe, it might restore me. But now, after many days of idleness, I am just as weary of them as I was of my work. Sometimes I am so utterly exhausted that I fear I cannot enjoy heaven without a period of rest first. I am somewhat afraid of going straight there without first receiving a good sleep in the grave to strengthen me."

"Do not be afraid, Bessy," Margaret said, placing her hand gently on the girl's. "God can grant you a far more perfect rest than either earthly idleness or the dead sleep of the grave can provide."

Bessy shifted uneasily. "I wish my father would not speak to me as he does. He means well, as I told you yesterday, and I tell you again. But you see, though I do not believe him during the day, by night—when I am feverish, half-asleep and half-awake—it returns upon me. Oh! so terribly!
