And I think, if this is truly the end of all, and if all I was born for was to wear out my heart and life, to sicken in this dreary place with those mill-noises ringing in my ears forever until I scream for them to stop and let me have a little quiet—and with the fluff filling my lungs until I thirst to death for one long, deep breath of the clear air you speak of—and my mother gone, unable to tell her again how I loved her, and all my troubles... I think, if this life is the end, and there is no God to wipe away all tears from all eyes—you wench, you!" She sat up abruptly, clutching Margaret's hand violently, almost fiercely. "I could go mad, and kill you, I could!" She collapsed back, completely drained by her outburst.

Margaret knelt beside her. "Bessy, we have a Father in Heaven."

"I know it! I know it," Bessy moaned, turning her head restlessly from side to side. "I am very wicked. I have spoken very wickedly. Oh! Do not be frightened by me and never come again. I would not harm a hair of your head. And," opening her eyes to look earnestly at Margaret, "I believe, perhaps, more than you do, of what is to come. I have read the Book of Revelations until I know it by heart, and I never doubt, when I am awake and in my senses, of all the glory I am destined to reach."

"Let us not discuss the fancies that arise in your feverish mind," Margaret suggested gently. "I would rather hear of what you used to do when you were well."

"I think I was well when my mother died, but I have never been truly strong since that time. I began working in a carding-room shortly thereafter, and the fluff entered my lungs, poisoning me."

"Fluff?" Margaret asked, inquiringly.

"Fluff," Bessy repeated. "Tiny bits that fly off from the cotton when it is being carded, filling the air until it looks like fine white dust. They say it winds around the lungs and tightens them up. Anyway, many who work in a carding-room fall into a waste state, coughing and spitting blood, because they are simply poisoned by the fluff."

"But can it not be prevented?" Margaret asked.

"I do not know. Some factories have a great wheel at one end to create a draft and carry the dust away, but that wheel costs a great deal of money—perhaps five or six hundred pounds—and yields no profit. Consequently, only a few masters will install them. I have heard of men who refused to work in places equipped with such wheels, claiming that after becoming accustomed to swallowing fluff, they found it difficult to go without it, and that their wages should be raised if they were forced to work in cleaner conditions. Thus, between masters and men, the wheels often fail to be installed. I know I wished there had been one in our place, though."

"Did your father know about it?" Margaret asked.

"Yes. And he was sorry. But our factory was generally a good one, employing a steady and reliable group of people. My father feared sending me to a strange place; though you might not think it now, many considered me a respectable girl then. I did not wish to be regarded as weak and soft, and Mary's education needed to continue, as my mother insisted. Furthermore, my father always desired to purchase books and attend various lectures, all of which required money. So, I simply continued working until I shall never get the whirring out of my ears or the fluff out of my throat in this world. That is all."

"How old are you?" Margaret asked.

"Nineteen, coming July."

"And I too am nineteen." Margaret thought more sorrowfully than Bessy did of the contrast between them. She could not speak for a moment or two, struggling to contain the rising emotion.

"Regarding Mary," Bessy said. "I wanted to ask you to be a friend to her. She is seventeen, but she is the last of us. I do not want her to go to the mill, yet I do not know what she is fit for."

"She could not," Margaret replied, glancing unconsciously at the uncleaned corners of the room. "She could hardly undertake a servant's position, could she? We have an old, faithful servant, almost a friend, who requires assistance, but she is very particular. It would not be right to burden her with help that would truly become an annoyance and an irritation."

"No, I see. I reckon you are right. Our Mary is a good girl; but who has taught her how to manage a house? No mother, and me at the mill until I was good for nothing but scolding her for doing poorly what I did not know how to do myself. But I wish she could have lived with you, despite everything."

"Even though she may not be exactly suited to come and live with us as a servant—and I am uncertain about that—I will always strive to be a friend to her for your sake, Bessy. And now I must depart. I will return as soon as possible; but if it is not tomorrow, or the next day, or even a week or a fortnight hence, do not think I have forgotten you. I may be occupied."

"I will know you will not forget me again. I will not mistrust you anymore. But remember, in a week or a fortnight, I may be dead and buried!"

"I will come again as soon as I can, Bessy," Margaret said, squeezing her hand tightly. "But you will let me know if you grow worse."

"Ay, that I will," Bessy replied, returning the pressure.

From that day forward, Mrs. Hale became increasingly a suffering invalid. It was approaching the anniversary of Edith's marriage, and looking back upon the year's accumulated heap of troubles, Margaret wondered how they had been endured. If she could have anticipated them, how she would have shrunk away and hidden herself from the coming time! Yet, day by day, existence had been surprisingly endurable in itself, small, keen, bright spots of positive enjoyment sparkling into the very midst of sorrows. A year prior, or when she first arrived in Helstone and became silently aware of the querulousness in her mother's temper, she would have groaned bitterly at the prospect of enduring a long illness in a strange, desolate, noisy, and busy place, with diminished comforts surrounding every aspect of home life. But with the increase of serious and just grounds for complaint, a new kind of patience had sprung up in her mother's mind. She was gentle and quiet in intense bodily suffering, almost in proportion to how restless and depressed she had been when there was no real cause for grief.

Mr. Hale, however, was in exactly that stage of apprehension which, in men of his stamp, takes the form of willful blindness. He was more irritated than Margaret had ever known him to be regarding his daughter's expressed anxiety.

"Indeed, Margaret, you are growing fanciful! God knows I should be the first to take the alarm if your mother were really ill; we always knew when she had her headaches at Helstone, even without her telling us. She looks quite pale and white when she is ill; and now she has a bright, healthy color in her cheeks, just as she used to have when I first knew her."

"But papa," Margaret said with hesitation, "do you know I think that is the flush of pain?"

"Nonsense, Margaret. I tell you, you are the one not well, I think. Send for the doctor tomorrow for yourself; and then, if it will make your mind easier, he can see your mother."

"Thank you, dear papa. It will make me happier, indeed." She rose to kiss him. But he pushed her away—gently enough, yet as if she had suggested unpleasant ideas which he would be glad to dispel as readily as he could her presence. He paced uneasily up and down the room.

"Poor Maria!" he muttered, half to himself. "I wish one could do right without sacrificing others. I shall hate this town, and myself too, if she..." He turned to Margaret. "Pray, Margaret, does your mother often talk to you of the old places: of Helstone, I mean?"

"No, papa," Margaret said sadly.

"Then, you see, she cannot be fretting after them, eh? It has always been a comfort to me to think that your mother was so simple and open that I knew every little grievance she had. She never would conceal anything seriously affecting her health from me: would she, eh, Margaret? I am quite sure she would not

The wind howled through the chimney that night, a mournful, whistling intrusion that shattered the peace of the Hale household. For Mrs. Hale, the gale was not merely weather; it was a summons from the past, a physical manifestation of the terror she had carried since her son Frederick sailed away. She sat awake, her body rigid, her mind conjuring images of glass-green walls of water towering higher than the masts, curling over the *Russell* with the cruel, white foam of a giant serpent. It was a nightmare that returned only when the air grew rough, leaving her sitting upright in bed, stiff with dread, grateful only for the momentary reprieve of waking consciousness.

"It is an old dream," she whispered to the darkness, "but it always comes back on windy nights."

Margaret, sensing the tremor in her mother's voice, moved closer. In recent times, the distance between them had narrowed; her mother, usually reserved, now sought Margaret's counsel with an intimacy Margaret had longed for since childhood. She welcomed the role of confidante, responding to every plea for sympathy, however trivial, with a devotion that felt like a quiet reward.

"Margaret," Mrs. Hale began, her voice breaking the silence, "it was so windy last night! I could not sleep. I never can when there is such a terrible wind. I got into a wakeful habit when poor Frederick was at sea; and now, even if I don't waken all at once, I dream of him in some stormy sea..."

She paused, her gaze distant. "Poor Frederick! He is on land now, so wind can do him no harm. Though I did think it might shake down some of those tall chimneys."

"Where is Frederick now, mamma?" Margaret asked gently. "Our letters are directed to the care of Messrs. Barbour, at Cadiz. I know: but where is he himself?"

"I can't remember the name of the place," Mrs. Hale replied, her face flushing with a mixture of pride and pain. "But he is not called Hale; you must remember that, Margaret. Notice the F. D. in every corner of the letters. He has taken the name of Dickenson. I wanted him to have been called Beresford, to which he had a kind of right, but your father thought he had better not. He might be recognised, you know, if he were called by my name."

"Did something happen to him?" Margaret pressed, her curiosity overcoming her natural timidity. "I was at Aunt Shaw's when it all happened; and I suppose I was not old enough to be told plainly about it. But I should like to know now, if I may—if it does not give you too much pain to speak about it."

"Pain! No," Mrs. Hale replied, though her eyes glistened. "Yet it is pain to think that perhaps I may never see my darling boy again. Or else he did right, Margaret. They may say what they like, but I have his own letters to show, and I'll believe him, though he is my son, sooner than any court-martial on earth."

She gestured toward a small Japanese cabinet. "Go to my little japan cabinet, dear, and in the second left-hand drawer you will find a packet of letters."

Margaret retrieved the bundle. The papers were yellowed and stained with salt, carrying the peculiar, briny fragrance of the ocean. Her mother untied the silken string with trembling fingers, examining the dates before handing them to her daughter.

"You see, Margaret, how from the very first he disliked Captain Reid," Mrs. Hale explained, her voice rising with emotion. "He was second lieutenant in the ship—the *Orion*—in which Frederick sailed the very first time. Poor little fellow, how well he looked in his midshipman's dress, with his dirk in his hand, cutting open all the newspapers with it as if it were a paper-knife! But this Mr. Reid, as he was then, seemed to take a dislike to Frederick from the very beginning."

Mrs. Hale stopped, searching for the specific letter. "And then—stay! these are the letters he wrote on board the *Russell*. When he was appointed to her, and found his old enemy Captain Reid in command, he did mean to bear all his tyranny patiently. Look! this is the letter. Just read it, Margaret. Where is it he says—'my father may rely upon me, that I will bear with all proper patience everything that one officer and gentleman can take from another. But from my former knowledge of my present captain, I confess I look forward with apprehension to a long course of tyranny on board the *Russell*.' You see, he promises to bear patiently, and I am sure he did, for he was the sweetest-tempered boy, when he was not vexed, that could possibly be."

She pointed to another sheet. "Is that the letter in which he speaks of Captain Reid's impatience with the men, for not going through the ship's manœuvres as quickly as the *Avenger*? You see, he says that they had many new hands on board the *Russell*, while the *Avenger* had been nearly three years on the station, with nothing to do but to keep slavers off, and work her men, till they ran up and down the rigging like rats or monkeys."

Margaret read the faded script slowly. It described an incident where sailors aloft in the main-topsail rigging were ordered to race down, threatened with the cat-of-nine tails if they lagged behind. One sailor, terrified of the disgrace of flogging and unable to pass his companions, had thrown himself desperately down to catch a lower rope, failed, and fallen senseless onto the deck. He survived only a few hours. The indignation of the crew was at a boiling point when young Hale wrote.

"But we did not receive this letter till long, long after we heard of the mutiny," Mrs. Hale continued, her voice thick with sorrow. "Poor Fred! I dare say it was a comfort to him to write it, even though he could not have known how to send it, poor fellow! And then we saw a report in the papers—that's to say, long before Fred's letter reached us—of an atrocious mutiny having broken out on board the *Russell*, and that the mutineers had remained in possession of the ship, which had gone off, it was supposed, to be a pirate; and that Captain Reid was sent adrift in a boat with some men—officers or something—whose names were all given, for they were picked up by a West Indian steamer."

"Oh, Margaret!" Mrs. Hale gasped, clutching her chest. "How your father and I turned sick over that list, when there was no name of Frederick Hale. We thought it must be some mistake; for poor Fred was such a fine fellow, only perhaps rather too passionate; and we hoped that the name of Carr, which was in the list, was a misprint for that of Hale—newspapers are so careless."

"And towards post-time the next day, papa set off to walk to Southampton to get the papers," Mrs. Hale recounted, her eyes filling with tears. "And I could not stop at home, so I went to meet him. He was very late—much later than I thought he would have been; and I sat down under the hedge to wait for him. He came at last, his arms hanging loose down, his head sunk, and walking heavily along, as if every step was a labour and a trouble. Margaret, I see him now."

"Don't go on, mamma. I can understand it all," said Margaret, leaning up caressingly against her mother's side, and kissing her hand.

"No, you can't, Margaret. No one can who did not see him then. I could hardly lift myself up to go and meet him—everything seemed so to reel around me all at once. And when I got to him, he did not speak, or seem surprised to see me there, more than three miles from home, beside the Oldham beech-tree; but he put my arm in his, and kept stroking my hand, as if he wanted to soothe me to be very quiet under some great heavy blow; and when I trembled so all over that I could not speak, he took me in his arms, and stooped down his head on mine, and began to shake and to cry in a strange muffled, groaning voice, till I, for very fright, stood quite still, and only begging him to tell me what he had heard."

"And then?" Margaret whispered.

"And then, with his hand jerking, as if some one else moved it against his will, he gave me a wicked newspaper to read, calling our Frederick a 'traitor of the blackest dye,' 'a base, ungrateful disgrace to his profession.' Oh! I cannot tell what bad words they did not use. I took the paper in my hands as soon as I had read it—I tore it up to little bits—I tore it—oh! I believe, Margaret, I tore it with my teeth. I did not cry. I could not. My cheeks were as hot as fire, and my very eyes burnt in my head. I saw your father looking grave at me. I said it was a lie, and so it was."

Mrs. Hale paused, her breath coming in short gasps. "Months after, this letter came, and you see what provocation Frederick had. It was not for himself, or his own injuries, he rebelled; but he would speak his mind to Captain Reid, and so it went on from bad to worse; and you see, most of the sailors stuck by Frederick."

"I think, Margaret," she continued, after a pause, in a weak, trembling, exhausted voice, "I am glad of it—I am prouder of Frederick standing up against injustice, than if he had been simply a good officer."

"I am sure I am," said Margaret, in a firm, decided tone. "Loyalty and obedience to wisdom and justice are fine; but it is still finer to defy arbitrary power, unjustly and cruelly used—not on behalf of ourselves, but on behalf of others more helpless."

"For all that, I wish I could see Frederick once more—just once. He was my first baby, Margaret." Mrs. Hale spoke wistfully, almost as if apologising for the yearning, as though it were a depreciation of her remaining child. But such an idea never crossed Margaret's mind. She was thinking how her mother's desire could be fulfilled.

"It is six or seven years ago—would they still prosecute him, mother? If he came and stood his trial, what would be the punishment? Surely, he might bring evidence of his great provocation."

"It would do no good," replied Mrs. Hale. "Some of the sailors who accompanied Frederick were taken, and there was a court-martial held on them on board the *Amicia*; I believed all they said in their defence, poor fellows, because it just agreed with Frederick's story—but it was of no use,—" and for the first time during the conversation Mrs. Hale began to cry; yet something possessed Margaret to force the information she foresaw, yet dreaded, from her mother.

"What happened to them, mamma?" asked she.

"They were hung at the yard-arm," said Mrs. Hale, solemnly. "And the worst was that the court, in condemning them to death, said they had suffered themselves to be led astray from their duty by their superior officers."

They were silent for a long time, the weight of the sentence hanging between them.

"And Frederick was in South America for several years, was he not?"

"Yes. And now he is in Spain. At Cadiz, or somewhere near it. If he comes to England he will be hung. I shall never see his face again—for if he comes to England he will be hung."

There was no comfort to be given. Mrs. Hale turned her face to the wall, and lay perfectly still in her mother's despair. Nothing could be said to console her. She took her hand out of Margaret's with a little impatient movement, as if she would fain be left alone with the recollection of her son. When Mr. Hale came in, Margaret went out, oppressed with gloom, and seeing no promise of brightness on any side of the horizon.

"Thought fights with thought; out springs a spark of truth
From the collision of the sword and shield."

The following day, Mr. Hale approached Margaret with a tentative proposal. "We must return Mrs. Thornton's call," he said. "Your mother is unwell and feels unable to walk so far, but you and I shall go this afternoon."

As they walked, Mr. Hale voiced his concern for his wife's health with a veiled anxiety that finally pierced Margaret's composure. "Did you consult the doctor, Margaret? Did you send for him?"

"No, papa," she replied, her voice steady despite her internal turmoil. "You mentioned his coming to see me when I was well. But if only I knew of a competent physician, I would ask him to come this afternoon; I am convinced mamma is seriously indisposed."

She spoke plainly because her father had previously dismissed her fears as fanciful. Now, however, the situation had shifted. He responded in a despondent tone, haunted by the fear that their move to Milton had caused her decline. "Do you think she has a hidden complaint? Is she truly very ill? Has Dixon said anything? Oh, Margaret! I am terrified that our arrival here has killed her. My poor Maria!"

"Oh, papa! Do not imagine such things," Margaret said, shocked. "She is merely unwell for the time being. With proper advice, she will recover stronger than ever."

"But has Dixon said anything?"

"No! You know Dixon loves to wrap trifles in mystery, and her secrecy regarding mamma's health has alarmed me. Yet, without cause, I dare say. You remember you once told me I was becoming fanciful."

"I hope and trust you are," Mr. Hale admitted quickly. "Forget what I said then. I like you to be fanciful about your mother's health! Do not fear telling me your worries. We shall ask Mrs. Thornton if she can recommend a good doctor. We shall not waste money on anyone but the very best. Stay, we are turning up this street."

The street offered no sign of a residence large enough for Mrs. Thornton. Her son's demeanor gave no clue to his living quarters, yet Margaret had unconsciously assumed that the tall, imposing figure of Mrs. Thornton must inhabit a home of similar grandeur. Instead, Marlborough Street revealed long rows of modest houses and blank walls.

"He told me he lived in Marlborough Street, I'm sure," Mr. Hale said, looking perplexed.

"Perhaps it is one of the economies he practices," Margaret suggested. "But there are people about; let me inquire."

She asked a passer-by, who informed her that Mr. Thornton resided near the mill, pointing out the factory lodge-door at the end of a long dead wall. The lodge resembled a common garden gate, flanked by massive closed gates for wagons. Beyond lay a vast oblong yard containing business offices on one side and the immense, multi-windowed mill on the other, where the deafening clank of machinery and the roaring steam engine created a constant din.

Opposite the wall stood a handsome stone-coped house, blackened by smoke yet meticulously maintained. Paint, windows, and steps were scrubbed clean. It was clearly an old structure, perhaps fifty or sixty years in existence, evidenced by its stone facings, narrow windows, and dual flights of steps leading to the front door. Margaret wondered why those who could afford such a fine dwelling did not prefer a smaller home in the countryside or suburbs, escaping the factory's perpetual whirl and noise.

Standing on the steps, Margaret barely heard her father's voice. The yard itself, bounded by the dead wall, seemed a dismal prospect for the sitting rooms inside. Upon entering the drawing-room, she confirmed her suspicion: the space appeared untouched since the furniture had been carefully bagged, as if prepared for a volcanic eruption. The walls were pink and gold; the floral carpet was covered by a colorless linen drugget. Lace curtains hung heavy, and every chair and sofa was draped in netting or knitting. Alabaster groups sat under glass shades, safe from dust. In the center, beneath the bagged chandelier, a large circular table held smartly bound books arranged like the spokes of a wheel.

Everything reflected light; nothing absorbed it. The room possessed a painfully spotted, spangled appearance that unsettled Margaret. She was hardly conscious of the icy, snowy discomfort required to maintain such purity amidst the smoky atmosphere. Everywhere, there was evidence of labor, but not labor aimed at comfort or tranquility; it was solely for ornamentation and the preservation of that ornament from dirt.

They waited in low voices before Mrs. Thornton appeared. She entered rustling in handsome black silk, her muslins and laces matching the room's pristine whiteness. Margaret explained her mother's inability to accompany them, but in her anxiety to avoid reviving her father's fears, she gave a clumsy account. She left the impression that Mrs. Hale's ailment was a temporary, perhaps fanciful, indisposition of a fine lady, easily dismissed if a strong motive existed, or that the call could simply be deferred. Remembering the horses hired for her own visit and the instruction given to Fanny to pay every respect to the Hales, Mrs. Thornton drew herself up slightly offended. She offered no sympathy and granted little credit for the statement of Mrs. Hale's illness.

"How is Mr. Thornton?" asked Mr. Hale. "I was afraid he was not well from his hurried note yesterday."

"My son is rarely ill," Mrs. Thornton replied smoothly. "When he is, he never speaks of it or uses it as an excuse. He told me he could not find leisure to read with you last night, sir. He regretted it; he values the time spent with you."

"I am sure they are equally agreeable to me," Mr. Hale said. "It makes me feel young again to see his enjoyment of classical literature."

"I have no doubt classics are desirable for those with leisure," Mrs. Thornton countered. "But I confess it was against my judgment that my son resumed their study. The time and place in which he lives demand all his energy and attention. Classics suit men who loiter in the country or colleges; but Milton men must have their thoughts absorbed in the work of today. That is my opinion." She spoke this final clause with a pride that mimicked humility.

"But surely, if the mind is directed too long at one object, it becomes stiff and rigid," Margaret argued.

"I do not understand what you mean by a mind getting stiff," Mrs. Thornton said. "Nor do I admire those whirligig characters who are full of this interest today and utterly forget it tomorrow. Having many interests does not suit the life of a Milton manufacturer. It is enough for him to have one great desire and to bring all his life's purposes to bear on fulfilling it."

"And that is—?" Mr. Hale prompted.

Mrs. Thornton's sallow cheek flushed, her eyes lighting up. "To hold and maintain a high, honorable place among the merchants of his country—the men of his town. Such a place my son has earned. Go where you will—I do not say in England only, but in Europe—the name of John Thornton of Milton is known and respected among all men of business. Of course, it is unknown in fashionable circles," she continued scornfully. "Idle gentlemen and ladies are unlikely to know much of a Milton manufacturer unless he enters parliament or marries a lord's daughter."

Both Mr. Hale and Margaret felt an uneasy, ludicrous consciousness that they had never heard of this great name until Mr. Bell wrote to them. The proud mother's world was distinct from their own, whether it be Harley Street gentilities or country clergymen. Margaret's face, despite her efforts to remain simply listening, betrayed her sensitivity to Mrs. Thornton's tone.

"You think you never heard of this wonderful son of mine, Miss Hale. You think I am an old woman whose ideas are hounded by Milton, and whose own crow is the whitest ever seen."

"No," Margaret replied with spirit. "It may be true that I had hardly heard Mr. Thornton's name before coming to Milton. But since I arrived, I have heard enough to respect and admire him, and to feel the justice in what you have said of him."

"Who spoke to you of him?" Mrs. Thornton asked, mollified yet jealous lest another's words did not do him full justice.

Margaret hesitated, disliking the authoritative questioning. Mr. Hale stepped in. "It was what Mr. Thornton said himself that made us know the kind of man he was. Was it not, Margaret?"

Mrs. Thornton drew herself up. "My son is not the one to tell of his own doings. May I ask again, Miss Hale, from whose account you formed your favorable opinion? A mother is curious and greedy of commendation for her children, you know."

"It was as much from what Mr. Thornton withheld," Margaret replied, "as from what Mr. Bell told us of his previous life. It was more that than what he said that made us feel the reason you have to be proud of him."

"Mr. Bell! What can he know of John? He lives a lazy life in a drowsy college." Mrs. Thornton paused, then added, "But I am obliged to you, Miss Hale. Many a young lady would shrink from giving an old woman the pleasure of hearing her son well spoken of."

"Why?" Margaret asked, looking straight at her in bewilderment.

"Because I suppose they might have consciences telling them how surely they are making the old mother into an advocate for them, in case they have plans on the son's heart."

She smiled grimly, pleased by Margaret's frankness, perhaps realizing she had been asking too many questions. Margaret laughed outright at the notion, a merry sound that grated on Mrs. Thornton's ear, suggesting the words must have been utterly ludicrous. Margaret stopped immediately upon seeing Mrs. Thornton's annoyed look.

"I beg your pardon, madam. But I am very much obliged to you for exonerating me from making any plans on Mr. Thornton's heart."

"Young ladies have, before now," Mrs. Thornton said stiffly.

"I hope Miss Thornton is well," Mr. Hale interjected, wishing to change the subject.

"She is as well as she ever is. She is not strong," Mrs. Thornton replied shortly.

"And Mr. Thornton? I suppose I may hope to see him on Thursday?"

"I cannot answer for my son's engagements. There is uncomfortable work in the town; a threat of a strike. If so, his experience will make him much consulted. But I think he can come on Thursday. At any rate, he will let you know if he cannot."

"A strike!" Margaret asked. "What for? What are they striking for?"

"For the mastership and ownership of other people's property," Mrs. Thornton snorted fiercely. "That is what they always strike for. If my son's workpeople strike, I will only say they are a pack of ungrateful hounds. But I have no doubt they will."

"They want higher wages, I suppose?" Mr. Hale asked.

"That is the face of the thing. But the truth is, they want to be masters and make the masters into slaves on their own ground. They always try; they always have it in their minds. Every five or six years, there comes a struggle between masters and men. They will find themselves mistaken this time, I fancy—a little out of their reckoning. If they turn out, they may not find it easy to go in again. I believe the masters have a thing or two in their heads which will teach the men not to strike again in a hurry."

"Does it make the town very rough?" Margaret asked.

"Of course it does. But surely you are not a coward, are you? Milton is not the place for cowards. I have known times when I had to thread my way through a crowd of white, angry men, all swearing they would have Makinson's blood as soon as he showed his nose out of his factory; and he, knowing nothing of it, someone had to tell him, or he was a dead man. It needed to be a woman—so I went. And when I got in, I could not get out. It was as much as my life was worth. So I went up to the roof, where stones were piled ready to drop on the crowd if they forced the factory doors. I would have lifted those heavy stones and dropped them with the aim of the best man there, but I fainted from the heat. If you live in Milton, you must learn to have a brave heart, Miss Hale."

"I would do my best," Margaret said, rather pale. "I do not know whether I am brave until tried; but I am afraid I should be a coward."

"South country people are often frightened by what our Darkshire men and women call living and struggling. But when you've been ten years among a people who always owe their betters a grudge, waiting for an opportunity to pay it off, you'll know whether you are a coward or not, take my word for it."

That evening, Mr. Thornton came to Mr. Hale's. He was shown into the drawing-room where Mr. Hale was reading aloud to his wife and daughter.

"I am come partly to bring you a note from my mother, and partly to apologize for not keeping to my time yesterday," Mr. Thornton said. "The note contains the address you asked for: Dr. Donaldson."

"Thank you!" Margaret said hastily, holding out her hand for the note, wishing her mother not to hear they had inquired about a doctor. She was pleased that Mr. Thornton seemed to understand her feeling immediately; he handed over the note without further explanation.

Mr. Hale began to discuss the strike. Mr. Thornton's face assumed a likeness to his mother's worst expressions, immediately repelling the watching Margaret.

"Yes; the fools will have a strike. Let them. It suits us well enough. But we gave them a chance. They think trade is flourishing as it was last year. We see the storm on the horizon and draw in our sails. But because we do not explain our reasons, they do not believe we act reasonably. We must give them line and letter for the way we choose to spend or save our money. Henderson tried a dodge with his men at Ashley and failed. He rather wanted a strike

"Trust in that veiled hand, which leads
None by the path that he would go;
And always be for change prepared,
For the world's law is ebb and flow."

The following afternoon, Dr. Donaldson arrived to pay his first visit to Mrs. Hale. The fragile veil of secrecy that Margaret had hoped their past intimacy might have pierced was drawn tighter once more. She was barred from the room, while Dixon was ushered in. Margaret was not naturally prone to affection, yet where her heart was engaged, it burned with a fierce, consuming passion, accompanied by a corresponding intensity of jealousy.

Retreating to her mother's bedroom, situated just behind the drawing-room, she paced the floor in a restless agitation, waiting for the physician to depart. Periodically, she halted, straining her ears. She imagined she heard a moan. Her fingers clenched until her nails dug into her palms, and she held her breath, terrified. She was certain she had heard a moan. Then, silence reigned for several minutes, broken only by the shifting of chairs, raised voices, and the minor disturbances of departure.

When the door opened, she moved swiftly out of the room.

"My father is away, Doctor; he must attend to a pupil at this hour. May I request that you come to his study downstairs?"

She observed the obstacles Dixon erected against her and triumphed over them, assuming the rightful station of the mistress's daughter with a spirit reminiscent of the Elder Brother. This assumed dignity quelled the old servant's officiousness effectively. Margaret's conscious adoption of this unusual gravity toward Dixon afforded her a fleeting moment of amusement amidst her mounting anxiety. She knew, from the startled expression on Dixon's face, how absurdly grand she must appear; the thought carried her downstairs into the study, granting her a brief respite from the sharp, stinging reality of her situation. But the moment passed, and the truth rushed back, stealing her breath. It took a moment before she could form a word.

Yet, she spoke with an air of command. "What is the matter with my mother? You will oblige me by telling the simple truth." Seeing a slight hesitation on the doctor's part, she added, "I am her only child here, I mean. My father is not sufficiently alarmed, I fear; and therefore, if there is any serious apprehension, it must be broken to him gently. I can do this. I can nurse my mother. Pray, speak, sir; to see your face and not be able to read it gives me a dread worse than I trust any words of yours will justify."

"My dear young lady, your mother seems to have a most attentive and efficient servant, who is more like her friend—"

"I am her daughter, sir."

"But when I tell you she expressly desired that you should not be told—"

"I am not good or patient enough to submit to such a prohibition. Besides, I am sure you are too wise, too experienced to have promised to keep a secret."

"Well," said he, half-smiling, though with a sadness that did not reach his eyes, "there you are right. I did not promise. In fact, I fear the secret will be known soon enough without my revealing it."

He paused. Margaret turned very pale, compressing her lips further. Otherwise, not a feature moved. Possessing the quick insight into character without which no medical man can attain the eminence of Dr. Donaldson, he saw that she would demand the full truth; that she would know if even a single iota was withheld; and that the withholding would be torture more acute than the knowledge itself. He spoke two short sentences in a low voice, watching her intently. For the pupils of her eyes dilated into a black horror, and the whiteness of her complexion became livid. He ceased speaking. He waited for that look to fade, for her gasping breath to return. Then she said:

"I thank you most truly, sir, for your confidence. That dread has haunted me for many weeks. It is a true, real agony. My poor, poor mother!" Her lips began to quiver, and he allowed her the relief of tears, confident in her power of self-control to check them.

A few tears—those were all she shed before she remembered the many questions she longed to ask.

"Will there be much suffering?"

He shook his head. "That we cannot tell. It depends on constitution; on a thousand things. But the late discoveries of medical science have given us large power of alleviation."

"My father!" said Margaret, trembling all over.

"I do not know Mr. Hale. I mean, it is difficult to give advice. But I should say, bear on, with the knowledge you have forced me to give you so abruptly, till the fact which I could not withhold has become in some degree familiar to you, so that you may, without too great an effort, be able to give what comfort you can to your father. Before then—my visits, which of course I shall repeat from time to time, although I fear I can do nothing but alleviate—a thousand little circumstances will have occurred to awaken his alarm, to deepen it—so that he will be all the better prepared. Nay, my dear young lady—nay, my dear—I saw Mr. Thornton, and I honour your father for the sacrifice he has made, however mistaken I may believe him to be. Well, this once, if it will please you, my dear. Only remember, when I come again, I come as a friend. And you must learn to look upon me as such, because seeing each other, getting to know each other at such times as these, is worth years of morning calls."

Margaret could not speak for crying; but she wrung his hand at parting.

"That's what I call a fine girl!" thought Dr. Donaldson, when he was seated in his carriage and had time to examine his ringed hand, which had slightly suffered from her pressure. "Who would have thought that little hand could have given such a squeeze? But the bones were well put together, and that gives immense power. What a queen she is! With her head thrown back at first, to force me into speaking the truth; and then bent so eagerly forward to listen. Poor thing! I must see she does not overstrain herself. Though it's astonishing how much these thorough-bred creatures can do and suffer. That girl's game to the backbone. Another, who had gone that deadly colour, could never have come round without either fainting or hysterics. But she wouldn't do either—not she! And the very force of her will brought her round. Such a girl as that would win my heart, if I were thirty years younger. It's too late now. Ah; here we are at the Archers'." So out he jumped, with thought, wisdom, experience, sympathy, and ready to attend to any of the calls made upon them by this family, just as if there were none other in the world.

Meanwhile, Margaret had returned to her father's study for a moment to recover her strength before going upstairs into her mother's presence.

"Oh, my God, my God! but this is terrible. How shall I bear it? Such a deadly disease! no hope! Oh, mamma, mamma, I wish I had never gone to Aunt Shaw's, and been all those precious years away from you! Poor mamma! how much she must have borne! Oh, pray thee, my God, that her sufferings may not be too acute, too dreadful. How shall I bear to see them? How can I bear papa's agony? He must not be told yet; not all at once. It would kill him. But I won't lose another moment of my own dear, precious mother."

She ran upstairs. Dixon was not in the room. Mrs. Hale lay back in an easy chair, with a soft white shawl wrapped around her and a becoming cap placed upon her head, in expectation of the doctor's visit. Her face had a little faint colour in it, and the exhaustion following the examination gave it a peaceful look. Margaret was surprised to see her look so calm.

"Why, Margaret, how strange you look! What is the matter?" And then, as the idea stole into her mind of what was indeed the real state of the case, she added, as if a little displeased, "you have not been seeing Dr. Donaldson, and asking him any questions—have you, child?" Margaret did not reply—only looked wistfully towards her. Mrs. Hale became more displeased. "He would not surely break his word to me, and"—

"Oh yes, mamma, he did. I made him. It was I—blame me." She knelt down by her mother's side and caught her hand; she would not let it go, though Mrs. Hale tried to pull it away. She kept kissing it, and the hot tears she shed bathed it.

"Margaret, it was very wrong of you. You knew I did not wish you to know." But, as if tired with the contest, she left her hand in Margaret's clasp, and by-and-by she returned the pressure faintly. That encouraged Margaret to speak.

"Oh, mamma! let me be your nurse. I will learn anything Dixon can teach me. But you know I am your child, and I do think I have a right to do everything for you."

"You don't know what you are asking," said Mrs. Hale, with a shudder.

"Yes, I do. I know a great deal more than you are aware of. Let me be your nurse. Let me try, at any rate. No one has ever, shall ever try so hard as I will do. It will be such a comfort, mamma."

"My poor child! Well, you shall try. Do you know, Margaret, Dixon and I thought you would quite shrink from me if you knew—"

"Dixon thought!" said Margaret, her lip curling. "Dixon could not give me credit for enough true love—for as much as herself! She thought, I suppose, that I was one of those poor sickly women who like to lie on rose leaves, and be fanned all day. Don't let Dixon's fancies come any more between you and me, mamma. Don't please!" she implored.

"Don't be angry with Dixon," said Mrs. Hale, anxiously. Margaret recovered herself.

"No! I won't. I will try and be humble, and learn her ways, if you will only let me do all I can for you. Let me be in the first place, mother—I am greedy of that. I used to fancy you would forget me while I was away at Aunt Shaw's, and cry myself to sleep at nights with that notion in my head."

"And I used to think, how will Margaret bear our make-shift poverty after the thorough comfort and luxury in Harley Street, till I have many a time been more ashamed of your seeing our contrivances at Helstone than of any stranger finding them out."

"Oh, mamma! and I did so enjoy them. They were so much more amusing than all the jog-trot Harley Street ways. The wardrobe shelf with handles that served as a supper-tray on grand occasions! And the old tea-chests stuffed and covered for ottomans! I think what you call the make-shift contrivances at dear Helstone were a charming part of the life there."

"I shall never see Helstone again, Margaret," said Mrs. Hale, the tears welling up into her eyes. Margaret could not reply. Mrs. Hale went on. "While I was there, I was for ever wanting to leave it. Every place seemed pleasanter. And now I shall die far away from it. I am rightly punished."

"You must not talk so," said Margaret impatiently. "He said you might live for years. Oh, mother! we will have you back at Helstone yet."

"No, never! That I must take as a just penance. But, Margaret—Frederick!"

At the mention of that one word, she suddenly cried out loud, as in some sharp agony. It seemed as if the thought of him upset all her composure, destroyed the calm, overcame the exhaustion. A wild, passionate cry succeeded to the cry—"Frederick! Frederick! Come to me. I am dying. Little first-born child, come to me once again!"

She was in violent hysterics. Margaret went and called Dixon in terror. Dixon came in a huff and accused Margaret of having over-excited her mother. Margaret bore all meekly, only trusting that her father might not return. In spite of her alarm, which was even greater than the occasion warranted, she obeyed all Dixon's directions promptly and well without a word of self-justification. By so doing she mollified her accuser. They put her mother to bed, and Margaret sat by her till she fell asleep, and afterwards till Dixon beckoned her out of the room. With a sour face, as if doing something against the grain, she bade her drink a cup of coffee which she had prepared for her in the drawing-room, and stood over her in a commanding attitude as she did so.

"You shouldn't have been so curious, Miss, and then you wouldn't have needed to fret before your time. It would have come soon enough. And now, I suppose, you'll tell master, and a pretty household I shall have of you!"

"No, Dixon," said Margaret, sorrowfully, "I will not tell papa. He could not bear it as I can." And by way of proving how well she bore it, she burst into tears.

"Ay! I knew how it would be. Now you'll waken your mamma, just after she's gone to sleep so quietly. Miss Margaret, my dear, I've had to keep it down this many a week: and though I don't pretend I can love her as you do, yet I loved her better than any other man, woman, or child—no one but Master Frederick ever came near her in my mind. Ever since Lady Beresford's maid first took me in to see her dressed out in white crape, and corn-ears, and scarlet poppies, and I ran a needle down into my finger, and broke it in, and she tore up her worked pocket-handkerchief, after they'd cut it out, and came in to wet the bandages again with lotion when she returned

"There are briars besetting every path,
Which call for patient care;
There is a cross in every lot,
And an earnest need for prayer."

Margaret stepped out of the house with a heaviness in her step that belied the cheerfulness of the morning. Yet, as she walked down the length of Milton Street, the very air seemed to lift her spirits. The grey gloom of her own home gave way to the bustling vitality of the city. Men with hands deep in their pockets sauntered with a casual arrogance; groups of girls laughed aloud, their voices ringing with a boisterous independence that struck her as strange and new. She noticed the rougher elements lingering near the beer-houses, smoking and offering sharp commentary on the passersby. While the prospect of walking through this raucous thoroughfare to reach the fields she intended to visit seemed daunting, a sudden impulse took hold of her. Instead of seeking the quiet solitude of the countryside, she decided to visit Bessy Higgins. It would lack the restorative peace of a country walk, but it offered a different kind of solace—one born of shared understanding.

Inside the Higgins household, Nicholas sat by the fire, the smoke of his pipe curling around him, while Bessy rocked gently on the opposite side. Nicholas rose as Margaret entered, pushing his chair aside to lean against the chimney-piece in a posture of relaxed authority.

"How fares she?" Margaret asked, turning her attention to Bessy.

"She is rather down in the mouth regarding her spirits," Bessy replied, her voice flat, "though her health holds. She dislikes this strike. She is set upon piece-work and quietness at any price."

"This is the third strike I have witnessed," Bessy sighed, as if the repetition itself explained the weariness.

"Third time pays for all," Nicholas declared, reclaiming his pipe. "See if we do not hang the masters this time. See if they do not come begging us to return on our own terms. We missed the mark before; this time, our plans are laid deep."

"Why do you strike?" Margaret asked, her curiosity piqued. "To stop work until you secure your desired wage, is that not the definition? I confess my ignorance; in my homeland, such a thing is unheard of."

"I wish I were there," Bessy murmured wearily. "But I am weary of strikes. This shall be the last I endure. Before it concludes, I shall be in the Great City—the Holy Jerusalem."

"She is so consumed with the life to come that she cannot grasp the present," Nicholas said, addressing Margaret. "I, however, am bound to do the best I can here. A bird in the hand is worth two in the bush. Thus, our paths diverge."

"But," Margaret pressed, "if the people strike, as they do in my country among the field laborers, the seed remains unsown, the hay uncut, the corn unreaped."

"Well?" Nicholas challenged, puffing thoughtfully.

"What becomes of the farmers?"

"They will either abandon their farms or pay a fair wage," Nicholas answered.

"Suppose they cannot, or refuse to do so?" Margaret reasoned. "They cannot relinquish their land instantly, yet without harvest, they have no income to pay the laborers' wages the following year."

Nicholas continued to puff, finally breaking his silence. "I know nothing of your ways in the South. I have heard they are a race of spiritless, downtrodden men, crushed by poverty, too dazed by their misery to recognize when they are exploited. Here, it is different. We know when we are wronged. We have enough blood in us to endure it no longer. We simply take our hands from our looms and declare, 'You may crush us, but you shall not exploit us, my masters!' And be damned to them, they shall not succeed this time!"

"I wished I lived in the South," Bessy interjected.

"There is much to bear there," Margaret said gently. "Sorrows exist everywhere. There is grueling physical labor performed with scant nourishment to sustain the body."

"But it is outdoors," Bessy countered. "Away from the endless noise and the sickening heat of the mills."

"It is sometimes in driving rain, sometimes in biting cold. A young person may endure it, but an old man is racked with rheumatism, bent and withered before his time, yet he must continue, or face the workhouse."

"You thought I was so enamored with the Southern ways," Nicholas observed.

"So I am," Margaret smiled, caught in the irony. "I only meant, Bessy, that there is good and evil in all things. Since you felt the evil here, I thought it fair you should know the evil there."

"And you say they never strike in the South?" Nicholas asked abruptly.

"No," Margaret replied. "I believe they possess too much sense."

"And I believe," Nicholas retorted, extinguishing his pipe with such force that it shattered, "that it is not that they possess too much sense, but too little spirit."

"Oh, father!" Bessy cried. "What have you gained from striking? Recall the first strike when Mother died. We were forced to stop work—you suffered most of all. Yet, many continued working weekly at the same wage until the jobs vanished, leaving them destitute for life."

"Aye," Nicholas admitted. "That strike was poorly managed. Fools or traitors seized control. You will see; this time shall be different."

"But you have not told me what you are striking for," Margaret insisted.

"There are five or six masters who have prospered for two years, increasing their wealth while paying us the same wages. Now they demand we accept less. We refuse. We shall starve them out first and see who works for them then. They will have killed the goose that laid the golden eggs."

"And so you plan to die in order to take revenge upon them!"

"No," Nicholas said firmly. "I merely anticipate the chance of dying at my post sooner than yielding. That is what people call noble in a soldier, and why not in a poor weaver? But," he added, "a soldier dies for the Nation, for others."

He laughed grimly. "My lass, you are young, but do you think I can support three people—Bessy, Mary, and myself—on sixteen shillings a week? Is it for myself I strike? It is as much for the cause of others as the soldier, only mine is personal. I fight for John Boucher, who lives next door with a sick wife and eight children, none of them of factory age. I do not fight only for him, though he is a poor soul who can barely manage two looms; I fight for justice. Why should we receive less now than two years ago?"

"Do not ask me," Margaret said, refusing to yield despite sensing she was irritating him. "I am ignorant. Ask your masters. Surely they will provide a reason. It cannot be an arbitrary decree without cause."

"You are merely a foreigner," Nicholas sneered. "Ask the masters! They will tell you to mind your own business while they mind theirs. Our duty is to accept the reduced wage and be grateful; their duty is to drive us down to the point of starvation to swell their profits. That is the truth."

"But," Margaret persisted, "perhaps the state of trade prevents them from offering the same remuneration."

"State of trade!" Nicholas scoffed. "That is mere masterly humbug. It is the rate of wages I discuss. The masters control the state of trade; they wield it like a scarecrow to frighten children into submission. Their role is to beat us down and enrich themselves; ours is to stand and fight—not just for ourselves, but for those around us, for justice and fair play. We help create their profits, and we ought to help spend them. It is not that we crave their coin so desperately as before; we have saved. We are resolved to stand or fall together. Not a single man among us will accept less than the Union deems our due. So, 'Hooray for the strike,' and let Thornton, Slickson, Hamper, and their ilk look to it!"

"Thornton!" Margaret exclaimed. "Mr. Thornton of Marlborough Street?"

"Aye! Thornton of Marlborough Mill," Nicholas confirmed.

"He is one of the masters you oppose. What manner of man is he?"

"Have you ever seen a bulldog? Set one on its hind legs, dress it in coat and breeches, and you have John Thornton."

"Nay," Margaret laughed. "I deny that. Mr. Thornton is plain enough, but he lacks the bulldog's short, broad nose and snarling lip."

"Not in appearance, I grant you. But once John Thornton grasps an idea, he clings to it like a bulldog; you might drag him away with a pitchfork before he lets go. He is worth fighting. As for Slickson, he will likely wheedle his men back with false promises, cheating them once they are under his thumb again. He works his fines ruthlessly. He is slippery as an eel, sleek and cunning, like a cat. It will never be an honest, straightforward struggle with him as it will be with Thornton. Thornton is as stubborn as a door-nail; an obstinate man, every inch of him—the old bulldog!"

"Poor Bessy," Margaret said, turning to her. "You sigh over it all. You dislike the struggle your father embraces, do you not?"

"No!" Bessy said heavily. "I am sick of it. I would have preferred other topics in my later years than the constant clashing and clanging of work, wages, masters, and foremen."

"Poor wench! Your later days are far off. Besides, I shall be here to make things livelier for you."

"Tobacco smoke chokes me!" Bessy complained querulously.

"Then I shall never smoke indoors again!" Nicholas replied tenderly. "But why did you not tell me before, you foolish wench?"

Bessy fell silent for a moment, then spoke so softly that only Margaret could hear.

"I reckon he will want all the comfort he can get from either pipe or drink before he is finished."

Her father stepped outside to finish his pipe. Bessy spoke passionately.

"Now am I not a fool, Miss? I knew I should have kept my father at home, away from those ready to tempt a man during a strike to drink. My tongue must needs quarrel with his pipe, and he will go off, as he often does when he wishes to smoke. Nobody knows where it will end. I wish I had let myself be choked first."

"Does your father drink?" Margaret asked.

"No—not strictly," Bessy replied, her voice wild and excited. "But what difference does it make? There are days, I suppose, for everyone, when you rise and move through hours longing for a change, a spark. I know I once bought a four-pounder from another baker's shop just to break the monotony of seeing the same sights, hearing the same sounds, tasting the same bread, thinking the same thoughts, day after day, forever. I longed to be a man to go on a spree, even if it were just a tramp to a new place in search of work. Fathers, all men, feel this weariness of sameness and endless labor more acutely than I. What are they to do? It is little blame if they enter the gin-shop to quicken their blood, to see pictures and reflections they never see otherwise. But my father was never a drunkard, though perhaps he has worsened with drink now and then. Only you see," her voice dropped to a mournful plea, "during a strike, there is much to knock a man down despite his hopeful start. Where does the comfort come from? He becomes angry and mad—they all do—and then, exhausted by rage, he may do things in his passion he would gladly forget. Bless your sweet, pitiful face! You do not know what a strike is."

"Come, Bessy," Margaret said. "I will not say you exaggerate, for I know too little of it. Perhaps, since you are unwell, you view only one side, while another, brighter side exists."

"It is all well enough for you to say so, having lived in pleasant green places your whole life, never knowing want, care, or wickedness."

"Take care," Margaret warned, her cheeks flushing and eyes lighting up. "How you judge, Bessy. I shall return home to my mother, who is so ill—so ill, Bessy, that death is the only outlet from the prison of her suffering. Yet I must speak cheerfully to my father, who has no notion of her true state, and to whom this knowledge must come gradually. The only person—the only one who could truly sympathize and comfort her more than any earthly thing—is falsely accused. He would risk death to see his dying mother. This I tell you—only you, Bessy. You must not mention it. No one else in Milton, hardly anyone in England, knows. Do I not care? Do I not know anxiety, though I walk well-dressed and have enough food? Oh, Bessy, God is just, and our lots are well portioned by Him, though none but He knows the bitterness of our souls."

"I ask your pardon," Bessy replied humbly. "Sometimes, reflecting on my life and its little pleasures, I have believed I was doomed to die by the falling of a star from heaven: 'And the name of the star is called Wormwood; and the third part of the waters became wormwood; and men died of the waters, because they were made bitter.' One can bear pain and sorrow better if one believes it was prophesied long before; somehow, then, the pain seems needed for fulfillment, rather than sent for nothing."

"Nay, Bessy—think!" Margaret urged. "God does not willingly inflict suffering. Do not dwell so much on prophecies, but read the clearer parts of the Bible."

"I dare say it would be wiser; but where would I hear such grand words of promise, anything so far removed from this dreary world and this town, as in Revelation?

"My heart revolts within me, and two voices
Make themselves audible within my bosom."

Upon returning home, Margaret discovered two letters resting on the table. One was a brief note for her mother; the other, bearing foreign postmarks and possessing a thin, silvery quality that rustled softly as she lifted it, was clearly from Aunt Shaw. As Margaret began to examine the letter, her father entered abruptly.

"So your mother is tired and has retired early!" he remarked, his tone shifting instantly to concern. "I fear such a thundery day was hardly conducive to the doctor's visit. What did he say? Dixon mentioned he spoke with you regarding her condition."

Margaret hesitated. Her father's expression grew grave and anxious. "Does he not consider her seriously ill?"

"Not at present," Margaret replied. "He believes she requires care. He was very kind and promised to return to observe how his medicines take effect."

"Only care?" Mr. Hale pressed, his voice rising slightly. "Did he not recommend a change of air? Did he not suggest this smoky town is harming her, Margaret?"

"No," she answered gravely. "He said nothing of the sort. He seemed merely anxious."

"Doctors possess that anxious manner; it is part of their profession," he dismissed, though his agitation betrayed him. Margaret perceived, in her father's nervous demeanor, that the initial shadow of danger had already taken root in his mind. Despite his attempts to minimize the situation, he could not shift his focus to other matters. Throughout the evening, he returned to the subject repeatedly, refusing to entertain even the slightest unfavorable thought, a behavior that filled Margaret with profound sadness.

"This letter is from Aunt Shaw, Papa," she said, breaking the silence. "She has arrived in Naples but found it too hot, so she has secured apartments in Sorrento. However, I suspect she does not truly enjoy Italy."

"He did not mention anything regarding diet, did he?"

"It was to be nourishing and easily digestible. Mother's appetite seems quite good, I believe."

"Yes! And that makes it all the stranger that he thought to speak of diet at all."

"I asked him, Papa." After a pause, Margaret continued, "Aunt Shaw mentions she has sent me some coral ornaments. But," she added with a half-smile, "she fears the Milton dissenters will not appreciate them. She seems to hold all her notions of Dissenters based solely on the Quakers, does she not?"

"If you ever hear or suspect your mother desires anything, ensure you inform me immediately. I am terrified she does not always tell me what she wishes. Please, attend to that girl Mrs. Thornton named. If we possessed a competent house-servant, Dixon could remain constantly with her, and I guarantee we would soon restore her to our midst if rest alone can achieve it. She has been exhausted lately due to the heat and the difficulty of securing help. A little repose will set her right entirely—eh, Margaret?"

"I hope so," Margaret replied, but her tone was so laden with sorrow that her father noticed immediately. He pinched her cheek gently.

"Come; if you appear so pale, I must apply a touch of rouge. Take care of yourself, child, or you will require the doctor next."

Yet he remained unsettled throughout the evening. He moved restlessly back and forth on the balls of his feet, peering into the bedroom to check if his wife was still asleep. Margaret's heart ached at his agitation—his desperate attempt to suppress and strangle the terrifying fear looming in the dark recesses of his soul.

Finally, he returned, somewhat comforted.

"She is awake now, Margaret. She smiled warmly upon seeing me standing by her. It was her old smile. She stated she feels refreshed and ready for tea. Where is the note for her? She wishes to see it. I shall read it aloud while you prepare the tea."

The note contained a formal invitation from Mrs. Thornton, extending an invitation to Mr., Mrs., and Miss Hale to dinner on the twenty-first. Margaret was surprised to find an acceptance being considered, given the grim probabilities she had learned that day. Nevertheless, the prospect of her husband and daughter attending captivated Mrs. Hale's imagination even before Margaret had read the contents. It represented an event to break the monotony of the invalid's existence, and she clung to the idea with fretful persistence whenever Margaret attempted to object.

"Nay, Margaret: if she wishes it, I am certain we shall both go willingly. She would never express such a desire unless she genuinely felt stronger—truly better than we believed, eh, Margaret?" Mr. Hale asked anxiously as Margaret prepared to draft the acceptance note the following day.

"Eh, Margaret?" he questioned, making a nervous gesture with his hands. It seemed cruel to deny him the comfort he craved. Moreover, his passionate refusal to acknowledge the existence of fear nearly inspired hope within Margaret herself.

"I do believe she is better since last night," she said. "Her eyes appear brighter, and her complexion clearer."

"God bless you," her father said earnestly. "But is it true? Yesterday was so sultry that everyone felt unwell. It was a most unfortunate day for Mr. Donaldson to visit."

Thus, he departed to his daily duties, which had recently expanded to include preparing lectures for the working people at a neighboring Lyceum. He had selected Ecclesiastical Architecture as his topic, a subject more aligned with his own tastes and knowledge than with the specific interests of his audience or the nature of the institution. The Lyceum, burdened by debt, was only too eager to secure a free course from an educated and accomplished man like Mr. Hale, regardless of the subject matter.

"Well, mother," Mr. Thornton asked that evening, "who have accepted your invitations for the twenty-first?"

"Fanny, where are the notes?" Mrs. Thornton replied, listing the responses. "The Slicksons accept, Collingbrooks accept, Stephenses accept, Browns decline. The Hales—father and daughter are coming; mother is too great an invalid. The Macphersons come, along with Mr. Horsfall and Mr. Young. I was considering asking the Porters since the Browns cannot attend."

"Very good. Do you know, I am genuinely afraid Mrs. Hale is far from well, based on what Dr. Donaldson said."

"It is strange they would accept a dinner invitation if she were so ill," Fanny observed.

"I did not say 'very ill,'" her brother replied sharply. "I only said 'far from well.' They may not realize it either." Then, suddenly, he remembered that from Dr. Donaldson's account, Margaret must be fully aware of the exact state of affairs.

"Very probably they are quite conscious of what you said yesterday, John—of the immense advantage it would be for them, for Mr. Hale specifically, to be introduced to individuals like the Stephenses and the Collingbrooks."

"I am certain that motive would not influence them. No! I believe I understand the situation now."

"John!" Fanny laughed in her small, weak, nervous manner. "How you profess to understand these Hales, and how you never allow that we can comprehend them. Are they really so vastly different from most people one encounters?"

She did not intend to vex him, yet if she had, she could not have succeeded more thoroughly. He chafed in silence, refusing to deign a reply.

"They do not seem to me out of the ordinary," Mrs. Thornton remarked. "He appears a worthy enough man, though perhaps too simple for trade—which is likely why he became a clergyman first and now a teacher. She is a bit of a fine lady, with her invalidism. As for the girl—she is the only one who puzzles me when I think of her, which I do not often do. She seems to have a great notion of giving herself airs, and I cannot fathom why. I could almost fancy she thinks herself too good for our company at times. And yet, they are not wealthy; from all I hear, they have never been."

"And she is not accomplished, mamma. She cannot play."

"Go on, Fanny. What else does she require to bring her up to your standard?"

"Nay! John," his mother interjected, "that speech of Fanny's caused no harm. I myself heard Miss Hale admit she could not play. If you would leave us alone, we might perhaps learn to like her and recognize her merits."

"I am sure I never could!" murmured Fanny, shielded by her mother. Mr. Thornton heard but chose not to respond. He paced up and down the dining room, wishing his mother would order candles so he could begin reading or writing, thereby halting the conversation. Yet he never considered interfering in the minor domestic regulations Mrs. Thornton observed, born of her habitual remembrance of past economies.

"Mother," he said, stopping and speaking the truth with brave earnestness, "I wish you would like Miss Hale."

"Why," she asked, startled by his tender yet serious manner. "Are you not thinking of marrying her? A girl without a penny."

"She would never have me," he said with a short laugh.

"No, I do not think she would," his mother answered. "She laughed in my face when I praised her for speaking out something Mr. Bell had said in your favor. I liked the girl for her frankness, as it assured me she had no thoughts of you; and the next minute she vexed me by seeming to think... Well, never mind! Only you are right in saying she holds herself too high to consider you! The saucy jade! I should like to know where she would find a better match!"

"Well, as I am just as convinced of the truth of what you have been saying as you can be, and as I have no thought or expectation of ever asking her to be my wife, you may believe me henceforth that I am entirely disinterested in speaking of her. I foresee trouble for that girl—perhaps a lack of maternal care—and I only wish you to be ready to be a friend to her should she need one. Now, Fanny," he continued, "I trust you have sufficient delicacy to understand that it is as great an injury to Miss Hale as to me—in fact, she would deem it a greater injury—to suppose that I have any reason, beyond what I have stated, for begging you and my mother to show her every kindly attention."

"I cannot forgive her pride," his mother declared. "I will befriend her if necessary, for your sake, John. I would befriend Jezebel herself if you asked me. But this girl, who turns up her nose at us all—who turns up her nose at you..."

"Nay, mother; I have never yet placed myself, and I mean never to place myself, within reach of her contempt."

"Contempt, indeed!" (One of Mrs. Thornton's expressive snorts.) "Do not speak of Miss Hale, John, if I am to be kind to her. When I am with her, I do not know whether I like or dislike her most; but when I think of her and hear you talk of her, I hate her. I can see she has given herself airs to you as if you had told me so yourself."

"And if she has," he said, pausing for a moment before continuing, "I am not a lad to be cowed by a proud look from a woman, nor to care for her misunderstanding me and my position. I can laugh at it!"

"To be sure! And at her too, with her fine notions and haughty tosses!"

"I only wonder why you talk so much about her, then," Fanny said, expressing her fatigue with the subject.

"Well!" her brother replied, with a shade of bitterness. "Suppose we find a more agreeable topic. How about a strike, by way of something pleasant to discuss?"

"Have the hands actually turned out?" Mrs. Thornton asked with vivid interest.

"Hamper's men are actually out. Mine are working out their week, fearing prosecution for breach of contract. I would have had every one of them arrested and punished for leaving before their time expired."

"The legal expenses would exceed the value of the hands themselves—a set of ungrateful naughts!" his mother commented.

"To be sure. But I would have shown them how to keep my word and how I expect them to keep theirs. They know me by now. Slickson's men are off; he is unlikely to spend money getting them punished. We are facing a turn-out, mother."

"I hope there are not many orders in hand?"

"Of course there are. They know that well enough, though they do not quite understand everything, although they think they do."

"What do you mean, John?"

Candles had been lit, and Fanny had resumed her interminable piece of worsted work, yawning periodically. She threw herself back in her chair from time to time to gaze into the void, thinking of nothing at her ease.

"Why," he explained, "the Americans are flooding the general market with their yarns, so our only chance lies in producing at a lower rate. If we cannot, we must close our shops immediately, and both hands and masters will be forced to wander. Yet these fools revert to the prices paid three years ago—nay, some of their leaders quote Dickinson's price now—though they know as well as we do that, with fines extorted from their wages, which no honorable man would demand, the real rate of wage paid at Dickinson's is less than at ours. Upon my word, mother, I wish the old Combination Laws were in force. It is too bad to discover that fools—ignorant, wayward men like these—by uniting their silly heads, are to rule over the fortunes of those who bring all the wisdom that knowledge, experience, and often painful thought and anxiety can provide. The next step will indeed be, and we have almost reached it now, that we shall have to go and stand hat in hand, humbly asking the secretary of the Spinner's Union to furnish us with labor at their own price. That is what they want—they, who lack the sense to see that if we do not receive a fair share of the profits to compensate us for our wear and tear here in England, we can simply move to another country

Angels, in some brighter dreams, call to the soul when man doth sleep; so strange thoughts transcend our wonted themes and into glory peep.

Mrs. Hale watched Margaret with a mixture of amusement and profound interest regarding the impending dinner at Mr. Thornton's. She pondered the details with the innocent curiosity of a child anticipating a feast, wishing to have every pleasure described beforehand. The monotonous confinement of illness often shrinks the world until the walls seem vast and the outside world nonexistent, yet it also restores a childish sense of wonder. Mrs. Hale, once a vain girl whose ambitions had been smothered by poverty and the humbling reality of being a clergyman's wife, found a strange joy in imagining her daughter dressed for society. It was a delightful vanity, one she had long suppressed, to see Margaret discuss her attire with such animated anxiety—a contrast to Mrs. Hale's own twenty-five years of Helstone, where Margaret had spent merely one year in Harley Street.

"Then you think you shall wear your white silk?" Mrs. Hale asked, her voice tinged with nervousness. "Are you sure it will fit? It's nearly a year since Edith was married!"

"Oh, yes, mamma! Mrs. Murray made it, and it's sure to be right," Margaret replied cheerfully. "It may be a straw's breadth shorter or longer-waisted, depending on whether I've grown fat or thin. But I don't think I've altered in the least."

"Perhaps you had better let Dixon see it," her mother suggested hesitantly. "It may have gone yellow with lying by."

"If you like, mamma. But if the worst comes to the worst, I have a very nice pink gauze which Aunt Shaw gave me only two or three months before Edith was married. That couldn't have gone yellow."

"No! but it may have faded."

"Well! then I have a green silk. I feel as though I have the embarrassment of riches."

"I wish I knew what you ought to wear," Mrs. Hale said, her composure fraying.

Margaret's demeanor shifted instantly from playful to practical. "Shall I go and try them on one after another, mamma, so you can see which you like best?"

"But—yes! perhaps that will be best."

Margaret retreated to her room. She felt a sudden impulse to play pranks, to make her rich white silk balloon out into a cheese or to retreat backward from her mother as if she were a queen. However, sensing that such antics were interruptions to her mother's serious preparations, she became grave and sedate. She could not understand why the world should fuss so over her dress, yet later that afternoon, when she mentioned her engagement to Bessy Higgins, the servant girl's reaction was immediate and fierce.

"Dear! And are you going to dine at Thornton's at Marlborough Mills?" Bessy exclaimed.

"Yes, Bessy. Why are you so surprised?"

"Oh, I dunno. But they visit with all the first folk in Milton."

"And you don't think we're quite the first folk in Milton, eh, Bessy?" Margaret teased gently.

Bessy's cheeks flushed slightly at the easy reading of her thoughts. "Well, you see, they think a deal of money here. And I reckon you haven't got much."

"No," Margaret admitted simply. "That's very true. But we are educated people, and have lived amongst educated people. Is there anything so wonderful in our being asked out to dinner by a man who owns himself inferior to my father, coming to him to be instructed? I don't mean to blame Mr. Thornton. Few drapers' assistants, as he was once, could have made themselves what he is."

"But can you give dinners back in your small house? Thornton's house is three times as big."

"Well, I think we could manage to give Mr. Thornton a dinner back, as you call it. Perhaps not in such a large room, nor with so many people. But I don't think we've thought about it at all in that way."

"I never thought you'd be dining with the Thorntons," Bessy repeated, her voice dropping. "Why, the mayor himself dines there, and the members of Parliament and all."

"I think I could support the honour of meeting the mayor of Milton."

"But them ladies dress so grand!" Bessy said, casting an anxious glance at Margaret's print gown, which her Milton eyes appraised at sevenpence a yard.

Margaret's face dimpled into a merry laugh. "Thank you, Bessy, for thinking so kindly about my looking nice among all the smart people. But I have plenty of grand gowns—a week ago, I should have said they were far too grand for anything I should ever want here. But as I'm to dine at Mr. Thornton's, and perhaps meet the mayor, I shall put on my very best gown, you may be sure."

"What will you wear?" Bessy asked, somewhat relieved.

"White silk," Margaret said. "A gown I had for a cousin's wedding a year ago."

"That'll do!" Bessy declared, sinking back into her chair. "I should be loath to have you looked down upon."

"Oh! I'll be fine enough, if that will save me from being looked down upon in Milton."

"I wish I could see you dressed up," Bessy murmured. "I reckon you're not what folk would call pretty; you've not red and white enough for that. But do you know, I have dreamt of you, long before ever I saw you."

"Nonsense, Bessy!"

"Ay, but I did. Your very face—looking with your clear steadfast eyes out of the darkness, with your hair blown off from your brow, and going out like rays round your forehead, which was just as smooth and straight as it is now—and you always came to give me strength, which I seemed to gather out of your deep comforting eyes—and you were dressed in shining raiment—just as you're going to be dressed. So, you see, it was you!"

"Nay, Bessy," Margaret said gently, "it was but a dream."

"And why might na I dream a dream in my affliction as well as others? Did not many a one in the Bible? Ay, and see visions too! Why, even my father thinks a deal of dreams! I tell you again, I saw you as plainly, coming swiftly towards me, with your hair blown back with the very swiftness of the motion, just like the way it grows, a little standing off like; and the white shining dress on you've gotten to wear. Let me come and see you in it. I want to see you and touch you as in very deed you were in my dream."

"My dear Bessy, it is quite a fancy of yours."

"Fancy or no fancy—you've come, as I knew you would, when I saw your movement in my dream—and when you're here about me, I reckon I feel easier in my mind, and comforted, just as a fire comforts one on a dreary day. You said it were on the twenty-first; please God, I'll come and see you."

"Oh Bessy! You may come and welcome; but don't talk so—it really makes me sorry. It does indeed."

"Then I'll keep it to myself, if I bite my tongue out. Not but what it's true for all that."

Margaret fell silent. Finally, she asked, "Let us talk about it sometimes, if you think it true. But not now. Tell me, has your father turned out?"

"Ay!" Bessy said heavily, her tone shifting from the previous levity. "He and many another—all Hamper's men—and many a one besides. The women are as bad as the men in their savagery this time.
