Food is high—and they must have food for their children, I reckon. Suppose the Thorntons sent them their dinner out—the same money, spent on potatoes and meal, would keep many a crying baby quiet, and hush up its mother's heart for a bit!"

"Don't speak so!" Margaret cried. "You'll make me feel wicked and guilty in going to this dinner."

"No!" Bessy insisted. "Some are pre-electured to sumptuous feasts and purple and fine linen—maybe you're one of them. Others toil and moil all their lives long—and the very dogs are not pitiful in our days, as they were in the days of Lazarus. But if you ask me to cool your tongue with the tip of my finger, I'll come across the great gulf to you just for the thought of what you've been to me here."

"Bessy, you're very feverish! I can tell it in the touch of your hand, as well as in what you're saying. It won't be division enough, in that awful day, that some of us have been beggars here, and some of us have been rich—we shall not be judged by that poor accident, but by our faithful following of Christ."

Margaret rose, fetched water, soaked her pocket-handkerchief, and laid the cool wetness on Bessy's forehead, beginning to chafe the stone-cold feet. Bessy shut her eyes, allowing herself to be soothed. At last she whispered, "You'd have been deaved out of your five wits, as well as me, if you'd had one body after another coming in to ask for father, and staying to tell me each one their tale. Some spoke of deadly hatred, and made my blood run cold with the terrible things they said of the masters—but more, being women, kept plaining, plaining (with the tears running down their cheeks, and never wiped away, nor heeded), of the price of meat, and how their children could not sleep at nights for the hunger."

"And do they think the strike will mend this?" Margaret asked softly.

"They say so," Bessy replied. "They do say trade has been good for long, and the masters have made no end of money; how much father doesn't know, but, in course, the Union does; and, as it is natural, they wanted their share of the profits, now that food is getting dear; and the Union says they'll not be doing their duty if they don't make the masters give 'em their share. But the masters have gotten the upper hand somehow; and I'm feared they'll keep it now and evermore. It's like the great battle of Armageddon, the way they keep on, grinning and fighting at each other, till even while they fight, they are picked off into the pit."

Just then, Nicholas Higgins entered. He caught his daughter's last words.

"Ay! and I'll fight on too; and I'll get it this time. It'll not take long for them to make 'em give in, for they've gotten a pretty lot of orders, all under contract; and they'll soon find out they'd better give us our five per cent. than lose the profit they'll gain; let alone the fine for not fulfilling the contract. Aha my masters! I know who'll win."

Margaret fancied, from his manner, that he must have been drinking, not so much from what he said as from the excited way in which he spoke. She was confirmed in this idea by the evident anxiety Bessy showed to hasten her departure. Bessy said to her, "The twenty-first—that's Thursday week. I may come and see you dressed for Thornton's, I reckon. What time is your dinner?"

Before Margaret could answer, Higgins broke out, "Thornton's! Are you going to dine at Thornton's? Ask him to give you a bumper to the success of his orders. By the twenty-first, I reckon, he'll be pottering in his brains how to get 'em done in time. Tell him, there's seven hundred'll come marching into Marlborough Mills the morning after he gives the five per cent., and will help him through his contract in no time. You'll have 'em all there. My master, Hamper. He's one of the old-fashioned sorts. Ne'er meets a man bout an oath or a curse; I should think he were going to die if he spoke me civil; but after all, his bark's worse than his bite, and you may tell him one of his turn-outs said so, if you like. Eh! but you'll have a lot of prize mill-owners at Thornton's! I should like to get speech of them, when they're a bit inclined to sit still after dinner, and could na run for the life on 'em. I'd tell 'em my mind. I'd speak up again the hard way they're driving on us!"

"Good-bye!" Margaret said hastily. "Good-bye, Bessy! I shall look to see you on the twenty-first, if you're well enough."

The medicines and treatment which Dr. Donaldson had ordered for Mrs. Hale did her so much good at first that not only she herself, but Margaret, began to hope that he might have been mistaken, and that she could recover permanently. As for Mr. Hale, although he had never had an idea of the serious nature of their apprehensions, he triumphed over their fears with an evident relief, which proved how much his glimpse into the nature of them had affected him. Only Dixon croaked forever into Margaret's ear. However, Margaret defied the raven, and would hope.

They needed this gleam of brightness indoors, for outdoors, even to their uninstructed eyes, there was a gloomy brooding appearance of discontent. Mr. Hale had his own acquaintances among the working men, and was depressed with their earnestly-told tales of suffering and long-endurance. They would have scorned to speak of what they had to bear to anyone who might, from his position, have understood it without their words. But here was this man, from a distant county, who was perplexed by the workings of a system into the midst of which he was thrown, and each was eager to make him a judge, and to bring witness of his own causes for irritation. Then Mr. Hale brought all his budget of grievances, and laid it before Mr. Thornton, for him, with his experience as a master, to arrange them, and explain their

"Old and young, boy, let 'em all eat, I have it;
Let 'em have ten tire of teeth a-piece, I care not."

Margaret returned home weighed down by the heavy burden of what she had witnessed and heard, finding it difficult to summon the energy required for the duties awaiting her. Her mother, confined to the house, relied upon Margaret's brief excursions to bring news and maintain a stream of cheerful conversation.

"And can your factory friend come on Thursday to see you dressed?"

"She was so ill I never thought of asking her," Margaret replied, her voice heavy with sorrow.

"Dear! It seems everyone is ill these days," Mrs. Hale remarked, a flicker of envy crossing her face as one invalid often feels toward another. "But it must be terribly sad to be sick in one of those narrow back streets." Her natural kindness soon overcame this sentiment, and the old habits of Helstone returned to her thoughts. "It is hard enough here. What could you possibly do for her, Margaret? Mr. Thornton has sent me some of his vintage port since you left. Do you think a bottle would do her any good?"

"No, mother! I do not believe they are destitute; they certainly do not speak as if they were. Besides, Bessy's illness is consumption—she will not require wine. Perhaps I might bring her a jar of preserves made from our beloved Helstone fruit. Oh, mother, mother! How am I to dress in my finery and attend these smart gatherings after the grief I have seen today?" Margaret finally broke the restraint she had imposed upon herself, pouring out the details of her visit to Higgins's cottage.

The news distressed Mrs. Hale profoundly, leaving her restless and irritated until compelled to act. She ordered Margaret to pack a basket in the drawing-room to be sent immediately to the family. She was nearly angry when Margaret suggested it did not matter if it waited until morning, knowing Higgins had arranged for their immediate needs and that she had left money with Bessy. Mrs. Hale chided her daughter for being unfeeling and refused to rest until the basket was dispatched.

"Nevertheless, we may have been acting wrongly," she mused later. "It was only the last time Mr. Thornton visited that he remarked those who prolong the struggle by aiding the strikers are not true friends. Was not that Boucher-man a striker?"

Mr. Hale, fresh from a lesson with Mr. Thornton that had devolved into conversation, was consulted by his wife. In her agitated state, Margaret did not dwell on the broader implications of their charity. Mr. Hale listened with the composure of a judge, recalling the stark clarity of Mr. Thornton's words from moments prior. He reached an uneasy compromise. While he admitted that, as a general rule, refusing aid that propped up folly during a strike—which would inevitably end with masters importing labor or inventing machines—was the kindest course, he could not condemn their specific action regarding Boucher. He resolved to visit the man the following morning to ascertain what could be done.

The next day, Mr. Hale found Boucher absent but spoke at length with his wife. Promising to secure an Infirmary order for her, and observing the ample provisions Mrs. Hale had left behind—lavishly consumed by the children who ruled the house in their father's absence—he returned with a report far more consoling than Margaret had dared to hope for. Indeed, her father's imagination, reacting to her previous alarm, painted the situation as better than it truly was.

"I shall go again to see the man himself," Mr. Hale declared. "I struggle to compare these dwellings with our cottages in Helstone. Here, laborers possess furniture our workers could never dream of buying, and foods they would deem luxuries. Yet, with their wages stopped, the pawnshop appears to be their only recourse. One must learn a different standard here in Milton."

Bessy, too, showed signs of improvement, though her weakness was such that she seemed to have forgotten her feverish desire to see Margaret dressed, or perhaps that desire had been a delirious fantasy.

Margaret could not help but contrast this strange preparation for a visit she dreaded with the joyful, girlish dressing sessions she and Edith had shared less than a year ago. Her only pleasure now in adorning herself was the thought that her mother would delight in seeing her. She blushed when Dixon opened the drawing-room door to offer his admiration.

"Miss Hale looks well, ma'am, doesn't she? Mrs. Shaw's coral could not have been chosen better. It provides the perfect touch of color, ma'am. Otherwise, Miss Margaret, you would have been too pale."

Margaret's thick black hair was too dense for braiding; instead, it was twisted into massive, silky coils that encircled her head like a crown, gathered into a large spiral knot behind, held fast by two large coral pins resembling small arrows. Her white silk sleeves were looped with matching cords, and heavy coral beads rested against her neck, just below the curve of her milk-white throat.

"Oh, Margaret! How I wish I could accompany you to one of the old Barrington assemblies, taking you as Lady Beresford used to take me!"

Margaret kissed her mother in response to this burst of vanity, though she could barely smile, her spirits so low.

"I would rather stay at home with you, much rather, mother."

"Nonsense, darling! Be sure to observe the dinner closely. I shall love to hear how they manage things in Milton. Especially the second course, dear. Look what they serve instead of game."

Mrs. Hale would have been astonished had she seen the sumptuousness of the table and its appointments. Margaret, with her London-bred palate, found the array of delicacies oppressive; half the quantity would have sufficed for a tighter, more elegant effect. Yet, it was one of Mrs. Thornton's rigid laws of hospitality that every separate dainty be provided for all guests who cared to partake. Careless of abstinence in her daily life, it was part of her pride to set a feast before her guests. Her son shared this sentiment. He had never known society that did not depend on the exchange of superb meals; even now, though he denied himself the unnecessary expense of a sixpence and regretted sending the invitations, he was glad to witness the old magnificence of preparation.

Margaret and her father arrived first. Mr. Hale was punctually anxious. The drawing-room was empty save for Mrs. Thornton and Fanny. Every cover had been removed, and the apartment blazed with yellow silk damask and a brilliantly flowered carpet. Every corner was filled with ornament until it became a weariness to the eye, presenting a strange contrast to the bald ugliness of the view into the great mill-yard, where wide folding gates stood open for carriages. The mill loomed high on the left, casting a shadow from its many stories that darkened the summer evening before its time.

"My son was engaged up to the last moment on business. He will be here directly, Mr. Hale. May I beg you to take a seat?"

Mr. Hale stood by a window as Mrs. Thornton spoke. Turning away, he asked, "Do you not find such close proximity to the mill rather unpleasant at times?"

She drew herself up proudly. "Never. I have not become so refined as to desire to forget the source of my son's wealth and power. Besides, there is not another factory in Milton. One room alone is two hundred and twenty square yards."

"I meant that the smoke and the noise—the constant comings and goings of the workpeople—might be annoying!"

"I agree with you, Mr. Hale!" Fanny interjected. "There is a continual smell of steam and oily machinery, and the noise is perfectly deafening."

"I have heard noise called music far more deafening," Mr. Hale replied calmly. "The engine-room is at the street-end; we hardly hear it except in summer when windows are open. As for the murmur of the workpeople, it disturbs me no more than the humming of a hive of bees. If I think of it at all, I connect it with my son and feel how all belongs to him, that his is the head that directs it. Just now, there are no sounds from the mill; the hands have been ungrateful enough to turn out, as perhaps you have heard. But the very business I mentioned refers to the steps he intends to take to teach them their place." The stern expression on her face deepened into dark anger as she spoke, and it did not clear when Mr. Thornton entered. She instantly perceived the weight of care and anxiety he carried, though his greeting to his guests appeared cheerful and cordial. He shook hands with Margaret. She was unaware it was the first time their hands had met. He inquired after Mrs. Hale and heard Mr. Hale's hopeful account; glancing at Margaret to gauge her agreement, he saw no dissenting shadow on her face. As he looked with this intention, he was struck anew by her great beauty. He had never seen her so dressed, yet now the elegance of her attire seemed befitting her noble figure and lofty serenity of countenance, suggesting she ought to always appear thus. She was talking to Fanny about things he could not hear, but he observed his sister's restless manner, her continual adjusting of her gown, her wandering, purposeless eyes. He contrasted this uneasily with Margaret's large, soft eyes that looked steadily at one object, as if from their light beamed a gentle influence of repose; the curving lines of her red lips, parted in listening; the head bent forward to create a long, sweeping line from the light-catching raven hair to the smooth ivory tip of her shoulder; the round white arms and tapering hands, laid lightly across each other in a perfectly motionless, pretty attitude. Mr. Thornton sighed as he took it all in with one of his sudden, comprehensive glances. Then he turned his back on the young ladies and threw himself, with effort but with all his heart and soul, into conversation with Mr. Hale.

More people arrived, increasing the crowd. Fanny left Margaret's side to assist her mother. Mr. Thornton felt that in this influx no one was speaking to Margaret, and he was restless under this apparent neglect. Yet, he never approached her himself; he did not look at her. Only he knew what she was doing—or not doing—better than anyone else in the room. Margaret was so unconscious of herself and so amused by watching others that she never considered whether she was left unnoticed. Someone escorted her to dinner; she did not catch the name, nor did her host seem inclined to talk to her. A very animated conversation proceeded among the gentlemen, while the ladies remained mostly silent, taking notes on the dinner and critiquing each other's dresses. Margaret caught the thread of the general conversation, grew interested, and listened attentively. Mr. Horsfall, the stranger whose visit had sparked the gathering, asked questions regarding the trade and manufactures of the place, while the other Milton men offered answers and explanations. A dispute arose, warmly contested, and was referred to Mr. Thornton, who had hardly spoken before but now gave an opinion so clearly stated that even the opponents yielded. Margaret's attention was drawn to her host; his manner as master of the house and entertainer was so straightforward, simple, and modest that it was thoroughly dignified. Margaret thought she had never seen him to such advantage. When he had come to their house, there had always been something of over-eagerness or a vexed annoyance, as if he felt unjustly judged yet too proud to explain himself. Now, among his peers, there was no uncertainty regarding his position. He was regarded as a man of great force of character and power. There was no need to struggle for their respect; he possessed it and knew it, and the security of this gave a fine, grand quietness to his voice and ways that Margaret had missed before.

He was not in the habit of talking to ladies, and what he did say was somewhat formal. To Margaret herself, he hardly spoke at all. She was surprised to realize how much she enjoyed the dinner. She knew enough now to understand many local interests, even some of the technical terms used by the eager millowners. She silently took a very decided part in the question they were discussing. At least, they spoke in desperate earnest—not in the used-up style that had worn her down at old London parties. She wondered that, with all the dwelling on manufacturers and trade, no allusion was made to the pending strike. She did not yet know how coolly such matters were taken by the masters, viewing them as having only one possible end. To be sure, the men were cutting their own throats, as they had done many times before; but if they were fools enough to put themselves into the hands of a rascally set of paid delegates, they must take the consequence. One or two thought Thornton looked out of spirits, and of course, he must lose by this turn-out. But it was an accident that might happen to anyone; and Thornton was as good at managing a strike as any in Milton. He was as iron-willed a chap as any in Milton. The hands had mistaken their man in trying that dodge on him. They chuckled inwardly at the idea of the workmen's discomfiture and defeat in their attempt to alter one iota of what Thornton had decreed.

It was rather dull for Margaret after dinner. She was glad when the gentlemen came, not merely because she caught her father's eye to brighten her sleepiness, but because she could listen to something larger and grander than the petty interests the ladies had discussed. She liked the exultation in the sense of power these Milton men displayed. It might be rather rampant, savoring of boasting, but still they seemed to defy the old limits of possibility in a kind of fine intoxication caused by the recollection of what had been achieved and what yet should be. If in her cooler moments she might not approve of the spirit in all things, there was much to admire in their forgetfulness of themselves and the present, in their anticipated triumphs over all inanimate matter at some future time none of them would live to see. She was startled when Mr. Thornton spoke close at her elbow:

"I could see you were on our side in our discussion at dinner,—were you not, Miss Hale?"

"Certainly. But then I

"On earth is known to none
The smile that is not sister to a tear."

Margaret and her father walked home under a sky of perfect clarity. The streets were clean, the air cool and invigorating, and Margaret, dressed in her white silk gown, felt a lightness in her step that matched the freshness of the evening. She moved with her father, ready to share in the quiet excitement of the night.

"I rather think Thornton is not quite at ease regarding this strike," Margaret observed as they walked. "He seemed very anxious tonight."

"I should not be surprised," Mr. Hale replied calmly. "Yet, when the others suggested various courses of action just before we left, he spoke with his customary composure. He maintained that cool demeanor even after dinner."

"Indeed, he does. It would take a great deal to rouse him from his usual reserve. However, his face... his face betrays a deep anxiety."

"I would be anxious in his place," Mr. Hale admitted. "He must perceive the growing anger, the barely contained hatred within his workforce. To them, he is what the Bible terms a 'hard man'—not necessarily unjust, but unfeeling. He stands firm on his rights with a rigidity that seems inhuman when one considers how petty our own rights appear before the Almighty. I am glad you notice his anxiety. When I recall Boucher's frantic words and actions, I cannot bear to think of how calmly Mr. Thornton spoke."

"In the first place, I am not entirely convinced of Boucher's utter distress. For the moment, he was certainly in a difficult position. Yet, there is often a mysterious supply of funds within these Unions. Furthermore, based on what you described, the man is of a passionate, demonstrative nature; he gives strong expression to whatever he feels."

"Oh, Papa!"

"Well! I merely wish you to render justice to Mr. Thornton. I suspect he is of an exactly opposite temperament—a man far too proud to display his feelings. That is precisely the character I would have expected you to admire beforehand, Margaret."

"So I do admire him, and I should; yet I do not feel entirely certain of the existence of those hidden feelings. He possesses a great strength of character and an unusual intellect, considering the few advantages he has enjoyed."

"Not so few," Mr. Hale corrected gently. "He has lived a practical life from a very early age, constantly called upon to exercise judgment and self-control. Such experiences develop a specific part of the intellect. It is true he lacks some knowledge of the past, which provides the truest basis for conjecturing the future; but he knows this deficiency, he perceives it, and that is something. You are quite prejudiced against Mr. Thornton, Margaret."

"He is the first specimen of a manufacturer, the first person engaged in trade, that I have ever had the opportunity to study closely, Papa. He is my first olive; let me make a face while I swallow it. I know he is good of his kind, and by and by I shall like the kind. I rather think I am already beginning to do so. I was deeply interested by what the gentlemen were discussing, although I did not understand half of it. I was quite sorry when Miss Thornton came to escort me to the other end of the room, assuring me I would be uncomfortable being the only lady among so many gentlemen. I had never considered it; I was so busy listening. Besides, the ladies were so dull, Papa—oh, so dull! Yet, I think it was clever too. It reminded me of our old game of introducing a set number of nouns into a sentence."

"What do you mean, child?" Mr. Hale asked.

"Why, they selected nouns that were signs of wealth—housekeepers, under-gardeners, the extent of glass, valuable lace, diamonds, and similar items. Each person formed her speech to incorporate them all in the most pleasing, accidental manner possible."

"You will be as proud of your one servant when you get her, if all is true about her that Mrs. Thornton says."

"To be sure, I shall. I felt like a great hypocrite tonight, sitting there in my white silk gown with my idle hands, when I remembered all the thorough housework they had accomplished today. They took me for a fine lady, I'm sure."

"Even I was mistaken enough to think you looked like a lady, my dear," Mr. Hale said, smiling quietly.

But their smiles vanished instantly, replaced by pale, trembling expressions, when they saw Dixon's face as she opened the door.

"Oh, Master!—Oh, Miss Margaret! Thank God you are come! Dr. Donaldson is here. The servant next door went for him, for the charwoman has gone home. She is better now; but, oh sir! I thought she'd have died an hour ago."

Mr. Hale caught Margaret's arm to steady himself from falling. He looked at her face and saw an expression of surprise and extreme sorrow, but not the agony of terror that contracted his own unprepared heart. She knew more than he did, yet she listened with a hopeless expression of awed apprehension.

"Oh! I should not have left her—wicked daughter that I am!" Margaret moaned, supporting her trembling father's hasty steps upstairs. Dr. Donaldson met them on the landing.

"She is better now," he whispered. "The opiate has taken effect. The spasms were very severe; no wonder they frightened your maid; but she will rally this time."

"This time! Let me go to her!" Half an hour ago Mr. Hale was a middle-aged man; now his sight was dim, his senses wavering, his walk tottering, as if he were seventy years of age.

Dr. Donaldson took his arm and led him into the bedroom. Margaret followed close. There lay her mother, with an unmistakable look upon her face. She might be better now; she was sleeping, but Death had signed her for his own, and it was clear that ere long he would return to take possession. Mr. Hale looked at her for some time without a word. Then he began to shake all over, and, turning away from Dr. Donaldson's anxious care, he groped to find the door; he could not see it, although several candles, brought in during the sudden fright, were burning and flaring there. He staggered into the drawing-room and felt about for a chair. Dr. Donaldson wheeled one to him and placed him in it. He felt his pulse.

"Speak to him, Miss Hale. We must rouse him."

"Papa!" Margaret cried, her voice wild with pain. "Papa! Speak to me!" The speculation returned to his eyes, and he made a great effort.

"Margaret, did you know of this? Oh, it was cruel of you!"

"No, sir, it was not cruel!" Dr. Donaldson replied with quick decision. "Miss Hale acted under my directions. There may have been a mistake, but it was not cruel. Your wife will be a different creature tomorrow, I trust. She has had spasms, as I anticipated, though I did not tell Miss Hale of my apprehensions. She has taken the opiate I brought with her; she will have a good long sleep; and tomorrow that look which has alarmed you so much will have passed away."

"But not the disease?"

Dr. Donaldson glanced at Margaret. Her bent head, her face raised with no appeal for a temporary reprieve, showed that quick observer of human nature that she thought it better that the whole truth should be told.

"Not the disease. We cannot touch the disease, with all our poor vaunted skill. We can only delay its progress—alleviate the pain it causes. Be a man, sir—a Christian. Have faith in the immortality of the soul, which no pain, no mortal disease, can assail or touch!"

But all the reply he got was in the choked words, "You have never been married, Dr. Donaldson; you don't know what it is," and in the deep, manly sobs, which went through the stillness of the night like heavy pulses of agony.

Margaret knelt by him, caressing him with tearful touches. No one, not even Dr. Donaldson, knew how the time went by. Mr. Hale was the first to dare to speak of the necessities of the present moment.

"What must we do?" he asked. "Tell us both. Margaret is my staff—my right hand."

Dr. Donaldson gave his clear, sensible directions. There was no fear for tonight—nay, even peace for tomorrow, and for many days yet. But there was no enduring hope of recovery. He advised Mr. Hale to go to bed and leave only one person to watch the slumber, which he hoped would be undisturbed. He promised to return early in the morning. With a warm and kindly shake of the hand, he left them.

They spoke but few words; they were too exhausted by their terror to do more than decide upon the immediate course of action. Mr. Hale was resolved to sit up through the night, and all Margaret could do was to prevail upon him to rest on the drawing-room sofa. Dixon stoutly and bluntly refused to go to bed; and as for Margaret, it was simply impossible that she should leave her mother, let all the doctors in the world speak of "husbanding resources" and "one watcher only being required." So, Dixon sat, stared, winked, drooped, and picked herself up again with a jerk, finally giving up the battle and fairly snoring. Margaret had taken off her gown and tossed it aside with a sort of impatient disgust, putting on her dressing-gown instead. She felt as if she could never sleep again; as if her whole senses were acutely vital, endowed with double keenness for the purpose of watching. Every sight and sound—even every thought—touched some nerve to the very quick. For more than two hours, she heard her father's restless movements in the next room. He came perpetually to the door of her mother's chamber, pausing there to listen, until she, not hearing his close unseen presence, went and opened it to tell him how all went on, in reply to the questions his baked lips could hardly form. At last he, too, fell asleep, and all the house was still. Margaret sat behind the curtain thinking. Far away in time, far away in space, seemed all the interests of past days. Not more than thirty-six hours ago, she cared for Bessy Higgins and her father, and her heart was wrung for Boucher; now, that was all like a dreaming memory of some former life; everything that had passed out of doors seemed dissevered from her mother, and therefore unreal. Even Harley Street appeared more distinct; there she remembered, as if it were yesterday, how she had pleased herself with tracing out her mother's features in her Aunt Shaw's face—and how letters had come, making her dwell on the thoughts of home with all the longing of love. Helstone, itself, was in the dim past. The dull gray days of the preceding winter and spring, so uneventful and monotonous, seemed more associated with what she cared for now above all price. She would fain have caught at the skirts of that departing time, and prayed it to return, and give her back what she had too little valued while it was yet in her possession. What a vain show Life seemed! How unsubstantial, and flickering, and flitting! It was as if from some aerial belfry, high up above the stir and jar of the earth, there was a bell continually tolling, "All are shadows!—all are passing!—all is past!" And when the morning dawned, cool and gray, like many a happier morning before—when Margaret looked one by one at the sleepers, it seemed as if the terrible night were unreal as a dream; it, too, was a shadow. It, too, was past.

Mrs. Hale herself was not aware when she awoke how ill she had been the night before. She was rather surprised at Dr. Donaldson's early visit, and perplexed by the anxious faces of husband and child. She consented to remain in bed that day, saying she certainly was tired; but the next, she insisted on getting up; and Dr. Donaldson gave his consent to her returning into the drawing-room. She was restless and uncomfortable in every position, and before night she became very feverish. Mr. Hale was utterly listless, and incapable of deciding on anything.

"What can we do to spare mamma such another night?" Margaret asked on the third day.

"It is to a certain degree the reaction after the powerful opiates I have been obliged to use. It is more painful for you to see than for her to bear, I believe. But, I think, if we could get a water-bed it might be a good thing. Not but what she will be better tomorrow; pretty much like herself as she was before this attack. Still, I should like her to have a water-bed. Mrs. Thornton has one, I know. I'll try and call there this afternoon. Stay," said he, his eye catching on Margaret's face, blanched with watching in a sick room, "I'm not sure whether I can go; I've a long round to take. It would do you no harm to have a brisk walk to Marlborough Street, and ask Mrs. Thornton if she can spare it."

"Certainly," said Margaret. "I could go while mamma is asleep this afternoon. I'm sure Mrs. Thornton would lend it to us."

Dr. Donaldson's experience told him rightly. Mrs. Hale seemed to shake off the consequences of her attack, and looked brighter and better this afternoon than Margaret had ever hoped to see her again. Her daughter left her after dinner, sitting in her easy chair, with her hand lying in her husband's, who looked more worn and suffering than she by far. Still, he could smile now—rather slowly, rather faintly, it is true; but a day or two before, Margaret never thought to see him smile again.

It was about two miles from their house in Crampton Crescent to Marlborough Street. It was too hot to walk very quickly. An August sun beat straight down into the street at three o'clock in the afternoon. Margaret went along, without noticing anything very different from usual in the first mile and a half of her journey

"But work grew scarce, while bread grew dear,
And wages lessened, too;
For Irish hordes were bidders here,
Our half-paid work to do."

Margaret was ushered into the drawing-room. The atmosphere within had settled back into its usual state of dust and heavy drapery, yet the heat pressed against the windows, forcing them half open. Venetian blinds obscured the glass, filtering the daylight into a gray, grim haze that reflected the pavement below. This distorted light, mingling with the greenish tint from above, cast shadows askew and rendered Margaret's own reflection in the mirrors ghastly and wan. She sat, waiting in silence. No one approached. Occasionally, the wind seemed to carry the distant, multitudinous roar of the street closer, only to die away into a profound, suffocating stillness.

Finally, Fanny entered.

"Mamma will be with you directly, Miss Hale," Fanny said, her voice trembling slightly. "She asked me to apologize for the delay. You must understand, my brother has imported hands from Ireland, and it has enraged the Milton people beyond measure—as if he lacked the right to secure labor wherever he could. These foolish locals refuse to work for him, and in their fury, they have terrified these poor Irish starvelings with threats so severe that we dare not let them out. You can see them huddled in the top room of the mill; they are to sleep there to protect them from the brutes who will neither work nor allow them to. Mamma is arranging their food, and John is speaking to them, for some of the women are weeping to return. Ah! Here comes Mamma!"

Mrs. Thornton entered, her face etched with a black, stern severity that made Margaret feel she had arrived at a perilous moment. Yet, she had come only in compliance with Mrs. Thornton's expressed wish to inquire about necessities for her mother's illness. Mrs. Thornton's brow furrowed, her mouth setting hard, as Margaret spoke gently of her mother's restlessness and Dr. Donaldson's recommendation for a water-bed. She paused, expecting a response. None came immediately. Then, Mrs. Thornton sprang up, her composure shattering.

"They are at the gates! Call John, Fanny—call him in from the mill! They are at the gates! They will batter them down! Call John, I say!"

Simultaneously, the gathering tramp she had been listening to, rather than hearing Margaret's words, erupted just outside the wall. An increasing din of angry voices raged behind the wooden barrier, shaking it as if the unseen, maddened crowd wielded their bodies as battering rams. They retreated a short distance only to surge forward again with more united, steady impetus, until their great beats made the strong gates quiver like reeds before a gale.

Women gathered round the windows, fascinated yet terrified by the scene. Mrs. Thornton, the servants, and Margaret were all there. Fanny had returned, screaming upstairs as if pursued at every step, and collapsed onto the sofa in hysterical sobbing. Mrs. Thornton watched for her son, who remained in the mill. He emerged, looked up at the pale cluster of faces, and offered a smile of good courage before locking the factory door. He then called to a servant to unlock his own door, which Fanny had fastened in her panic. Mrs. Thornton went to assist. The sound of his well-known, commanding voice seemed like the taste of blood to the infuriated multitude outside. Until now, they had been voiceless, needing all their breath for the hard labor of breaking down the gates. But hearing him speak inside, they set up a fierce, unearthly groan that made even Mrs. Thornton turn white with fear as she stepped back to let him pass.

Mr. Thornton came forward, flushed but his eyes gleaming with the fire of danger. He possessed a proud look of defiance that made him a noble, if not strictly handsome, man. Margaret, who had always dreaded proving herself a coward in an emergency, forgot her own fear entirely, feeling only an intense, painful sympathy for the moment.

"I am sorry, Miss Hale," Mr. Thornton said frankly, "that you have visited us at this unfortunate moment, when you may be involved in whatever risk we bear. Mother! Had you not better go into the back rooms? I am not certain they have not made their way from Pinner's Lane into the stable-yard, but you will be safer there. Go, Jane!" he commanded the upper servant.

"I stay here!" his mother declared firmly. "Where you are, there I stay." Indeed, retreat was futile; the crowd had surrounded the outbuildings at the rear, sending forth an awful, threatening roar. The servants fled to the garrets with cries and shrieks. Mr. Thornton smiled scornfully at their panic. He glanced at Margaret, who stood alone by the window nearest the factory. Her eyes glittered, her cheeks and lips deepened in color. Sensing his gaze, she turned to him, asking the question that had been forming in her mind:

"Where are the poor imported workpeople? In the factory?"

"Yes," he replied. "I left them cowed in a small room at the head of a back flight of stairs, bidding them flee if they heard an attack on the mill doors. But it is not them—it is me they want."

"When can the soldiers arrive?" his mother asked, her voice low but unsteady.

He consulted his watch with the same steady composure he applied to everything.

"Supposing Williams departed immediately and did not have to dodge through the crowd, it must be twenty minutes yet."

"Twenty minutes!" his mother exclaimed, her terror finally breaking through.

"Shut down the windows instantly, Mother! The gates cannot withstand another shock. Shut that window, Miss Hale."

Margaret closed her window and moved to support Mrs. Thornton's trembling fingers. For several minutes, there was a pause in the unseen street. Mrs. Thornton looked at her son's countenance with wild anxiety, seeking interpretation in his rigid lines of contemptuous defiance, where neither hope nor fear could be read.

Fanny raised herself: "Are they gone?" she whispered.

"Gone!" he replied. "Listen!"

They heard the one great straining breath, the creak of wood slowly yielding, the wrench of iron, and the mighty fall of the ponderous gates. Fanny stood up, tottering, took a step toward her mother, and collapsed into her arms in a fainting fit. Mrs. Thornton lifted her with a strength born of sheer will and carried her away.

"Thank God!" Mr. Thornton said, watching her exit. "Had you not better go upstairs, Miss Hale?"

Margaret's lips formed a "No," but he could not hear her, for the tramp of innumerable steps pounded right under the house wall, accompanied by a fierce growl of low, angry voices that held a ferocious murmur of satisfaction more dreadful than their baffled cries moments before.

"Never mind!" he said, attempting to encourage her. "I am sorry you were entrapped in this alarm, but it cannot last long. A few more minutes, and the soldiers will be here."

"Oh, God!" Margaret cried suddenly. "There is Boucher. I know his face, though he is livid with rage—he is fighting to get to the front! Look! Look!"

"Who is Boucher?" Mr. Thornton asked coolly, moving close to the window to identify the man. As soon as they saw him, the crowd set up a yell that was not human—it was the demoniac desire of a wild beast for food withheld from its ravenous jaws. Mr. Thornton drew back momentarily, dismayed by the intensity of hatred he had provoked.

"Let them yell!" he said. "In five minutes more... I only hope my poor Irishmen are not terrified out of their wits by such fiendlike noise. Keep your courage for five minutes, Miss Hale."

"Don't be afraid for me," she said hastily. "But what in five minutes? Can you do nothing to soothe these poor creatures? It is awful to see them."

"The soldiers will be here directly, and that will bring them to reason."

"To reason!" Margaret snapped. "What kind of reason?"

"The only reason that deals with men who make themselves into wild beasts. By heaven! They've turned to the mill-door!"

"Mr. Thornton," Margaret said, shaking with passion, "go down this instant, if you are not a coward. Go down and face them like a man. Save these poor strangers whom you have decoyed here. Speak to your workmen as if they were human beings. Speak kindly. Do not let the soldiers come in and cut down poor creatures driven mad. I see one there who is. If you have any courage or nobility, go out and speak to them, man to man!"

He turned to look at her while she spoke. A dark cloud gathered on his face as he listened. He set his teeth at her words.

"I will go. Perhaps I may ask you to accompany me downstairs and bar the door behind me; my mother and sister will need that protection."

"Oh! Mr. Thornton! I do not know—I may be wrong—only—"

But he was gone. He was downstairs in the hall, had unbarred the front door, and all she could do was follow him quickly, fasten it behind him, and climb the stairs again with a sick heart and a dizzy head. She took her place by the farthest window. He was on the steps below, visible to a thousand angry eyes, but she could see or hear nothing but the savage satisfaction of the rolling, angry murmur. She threw the window wide open. Many in the crowd were mere boys, cruel and thoughtless; some were men, gaunt as wolves and mad for prey. She understood now; they were like Boucher—men with starving children at home, relying on ultimate success to raise wages, and enraged beyond measure at discovering Irishmen were brought in to rob their little ones of bread. Margaret read this desperation in Boucher's face, forlorn and livid with rage. If Mr. Thornton would but speak—if they heard his voice alone—it seemed it would be better than this wild beating against stony silence. Perhaps he was speaking now; there was a momentary hush in their noise, inarticulate as the sound of a troop of animals. She tore her bonnet off and bent forward to listen. She could only see; for if Mr. Thornton had indeed attempted to speak, the instinct to listen was gone, and the people were raging worse than ever. He stood with arms folded, still as a statue, his face pale with repressed excitement. They were trying to intimidate him, urging each other on to immediate violence. Margaret felt intuitively that in an instant all would be uproar; the first touch would cause an explosion in which even Mr. Thornton's life would be unsafe. Even as she watched, she saw lads in the background stooping to remove their heavy wooden clogs—the readiest missiles they could find. She saw it was the spark to the gunpowder. With a cry no one heard, she rushed out of the room, down the stairs. She had lifted the great iron bar of the door with imperious force, thrown it open wide, and stood there, facing that angry sea of men, her eyes smiting them with flaming arrows of reproach. The clogs were arrested in the hands that held them; the countenances, so fell a moment before, now looked irresolute, as if asking what this meant. For she stood between them and their enemy. She could not speak, but held out her arms until she could recover breath.

"Oh, do not use violence! He is one man, and you are many." Her words died away, for there was no tone in her voice; it was but a hoarse whisper. Mr. Thornton stood a little to one side; he had moved away from behind her, as if jealous of anything that should come between him and danger.

"Go!" she said once more, and now her voice was like a cry. "The soldiers are sent for—they are coming. Go peaceably. Go away. You shall have relief from your complaints, whatever they are."

"Shall them Irish blackguards be packed back again?" asked one from the crowd with fierce threat.

"Never, for your bidding!" exclaimed Mr. Thornton. And instantly the storm broke. The hootings rose and filled the air, but Margaret did not hear them. Her eye was fixed on the group of lads who had armed themselves with their clogs. She saw their gesture, knew its meaning, read their aim. Another moment, and Mr. Thornton might be smitten down—he whom she had urged and goaded to this perilous place. She only thought how she could save him. She threw her arms around him, making her body a shield from the fierce people beyond. Still, with his arms folded, he shook her off.

"Go away," he said in his deep voice. "This is no place for you."

"It is," she said. "You did not see what I saw." If she thought her sex would be a protection—if, with shrinking eyes, she had turned away from their terrible anger hoping they would pause and reflect—she was wrong. Their reckless passion had carried them too far. It was always the savage lads, with their love of cruel excitement, who headed the riot, reckless to what bloodshed it might lead. A clog whizzed through the air. Margaret's fascinated eyes watched its progress; it missed its aim, and she turned sick with affright, but did not change her position, only hiding her face on Mr. Thornton's arm. Then she turned and spoke again:

"For God's sake! Do not damage your cause by this violence. You do not know what you are doing." She strove to make her words distinct.

A sharp pebble flew by her, grazing forehead and cheek, drawing a blinding sheet of light before her eyes. She lay like one dead on Mr. Thornton's shoulder. Then he unfolded his arms and held her encircled for an instant.

"You do well!" he said. "You come to oust the innocent stranger. You fall—you hundreds—on one man; and when a woman comes

"Which when his mother saw, she in her mind
Was troubled sore, he wist well what to ween."

Margaret had scarcely vanished from the room before Mr. Thornton burst in, his face flushed with a heat that seemed to burn through his skin.

"I could not come sooner," he panted, his eyes darting around the dining-room as if searching for a ghost. "The superintendent would have delayed me. Where is she?"

He found his mother quietly rearranging the displaced chairs, her movements brisk and untroubled. She did not answer immediately.

"Gone home," she said, her tone clipped.

"Gone home!" The word hung in the air, sharp with disbelief.

"Yes. She was remarkably composed. I do not believe it was a serious injury; some women simply faint at the slightest shock."

"I am sorry she has gone," Mr. Thornton muttered, pacing the floor with restless energy. "She could not possibly have been fit for the journey."

"She insisted she was," his mother replied. "Mr. Lowe confirmed it. I fetched him myself."

"Thank you, mother." He paused, extending his hand in a gesture of gratitude she missed entirely. "What have you done with the Irishmen?"

"Sent them to the Dragon for a meal, poor souls. Fortunately, I intercepted Father Grady and persuaded him to stay and reason with them before they fled in a panic. How did Miss Hale manage to walk home? I am certain she could not have."

"She took a cab. Everything was handled with proper decorum, even the payment. Let us discuss something less volatile."

"I do not know where I should have been without her intervention."

"Have you become so helpless that you require a girl to defend you?" Mrs. Thornton asked, her voice dripping with scorn.

Mr. Thornton's face flamed red. "Few girls would have taken the blows intended for me—blows delivered with such genuine goodwill."

"A girl in love will endure much," his mother replied, turning away.

"Mother!" He stepped forward, then halted, his chest heaving with a suppressed storm.

His mother was startled by the sheer physical force he exerted to maintain his composure. She could not decipher the turmoil within him. Was it rage? His eyes burned, his frame expanded, his breath came in ragged gasps. It was a chaotic blend of joy, fury, pride, and a panting uncertainty that she could not interpret. Yet, the intensity of it unsettled her, as the presence of raw emotion, whose roots remain hidden, invariably does. She moved to the sideboard, opened a drawer, and retrieved a duster, ostensibly to wipe a spot of eau de Cologne from the sofa arm. In truth, she kept her back turned to her son far longer than necessary. When she finally spoke, her voice sounded strained and artificial.

"You have taken steps regarding the rioters, I presume? Do you anticipate further violence? Where were the police? They are never present when needed!"

"On the contrary," he replied, his voice steadying. "I saw three or four of them struggling near the gates when the barrier fell, fighting with surprising vigor. More arrived just as the yard cleared. I could have detained several if I had been quicker. There will be no difficulty; many witnesses can identify them."

"But will they not return tonight?"

"I intend to arrange a sufficient guard for the premises. I have agreed to meet Captain Hanbury at the station in half an hour."

"You should have tea first."

"Tea! Yes, I suppose I must. It is half-past six, and I may be gone for some time. Do not wait up for me, mother."

"Do you expect me to retire before ensuring your safety?"

"Well, perhaps not." He hesitated. "But if I have time, I shall stop by Crampton after settling affairs with the police and Hamper and Clarkson."

Their eyes locked. They stared at one another intently for a minute. Then she asked, "Why are you going via Crampton?"

"To inquire after Miss Hale."

"I will send. Williams will deliver the water-bed she requested and ask how she fares."

"I must go myself."

"Not merely to ask how Miss Hale is?"

"No, not merely for that. I wish to thank her for standing between me and the mob."

"What compelled you to go down at all? You were throwing your head into the lion's mouth!"

He glanced sharply at her, realizing she knew nothing of the exchange between him and Margaret in the drawing-room. He deflected her gaze with a question of his own.

"Will you be afraid to remain alone until I secure the police? Or should we send Williams now so they arrive before we finish tea? There is no time to lose. I must depart in a quarter of an hour."

Mrs. Thornton left the room. Her servants, accustomed to her sharp, decisive commands, found her instructions confused and uncertain. Mr. Thornton remained in the dining-room, ostensibly planning his visit to the police office, but his mind was entirely occupied with Margaret. Everything beyond the immediate moment seemed dim and vague, overshadowed by the memory of her arms around his neck, the soft clinging that had sent a flush of color racing across his cheeks.

The tea service was nearly silent, broken only by Fanny's incessant narration of her own sensations: her alarm, her relief, her subsequent sickness and trembling limbs.

"There, that is enough," her brother said, rising abruptly. "The reality was sufficient for me." He prepared to leave when his mother placed a hand upon his arm.

"You will return here before you go to the Hales'," she said in a low, anxious whisper.

"I know what I know," Fanny murmured to herself.

"Why? Will it be too late to disturb them?"

"John, come back to me for this one evening. It will be late for Mrs. Hale. But that is not the point. Tomorrow, you will... Come back tonight, John!" She rarely pleaded with her son; her pride usually forbade it. Yet, she had never pleaded in vain.

"I will return straight here after my business. You will surely inquire after them? After her?"

Mrs. Thornton was not a talkative companion to Fanny, nor a good listener while her son was away. But upon his return, her eyes and ears were keen to capture every detail of his precautions against further outrage. He clearly understood his objective. Punishment and suffering were the natural consequences for those who had participated in the riot. All that was required to protect property and assert the proprietor's will was a clean, sharp execution of justice.

"Mother! Do you know what I must say to Miss Hale tomorrow?"

The question caught her off guard during a pause where she had momentarily forgotten Margaret.

She looked up at him.

"Yes! You can hardly avoid it."

"Do otherwise! I do not understand you."

"I mean that, having allowed her feelings to overwhelm her, I consider you bound in honor—"

"Bound in honor," he scoffed. "I am afraid honor has nothing to do with it. 'Her feelings overwhelmed her!' What feelings do you mean?"

"Nay, John, there is no need for anger. Did she not rush down and cling to you to save you from danger?"

"She did!" he admitted. "But, mother," he stopped abruptly, standing directly before her. "I dare not hope. I have never been faint-hearted before; yet I cannot believe such a creature cares for me."

"Don't be foolish, John. Such a creature! Why, she might be a duke's daughter, by your account. And what proof more would you desire of her affection? I can believe she struggled against her aristocratic upbringing, but I like her the better for seeing clearly at last. It is a significant concession on her part," Mrs. Thornton said, smiling slowly while tears gathered in her eyes. "For after tonight, I stand second. It was to have you to myself, all to myself, for a few more hours, that I begged you not to leave until tomorrow."

"Dearest mother!" (Yet love remains selfish, and instantly he reverted to his own hopes and fears, casting a cold shadow over Mrs. Thornton's heart.) "But I know she does not care for me. I shall place myself at her feet—I must. If it were but one chance in a thousand—or a million—I would do it."

"Don't fear!" his mother said, suppressing her own mortification at his lack of attention to her rare maternal outburst, hiding the pang of jealousy that revealed the intensity of her disregarded love. "Don't be afraid," she said coldly. "As far as love extends, she may be worthy of you. It must have taken considerable effort to overcome her pride. Don't be afraid, John," she added, kissing him as she bid him goodnight. She exited the room slowly and majestically. Once inside her own chamber, however, she locked the door and sat down to shed unwonted tears.

* * * * *

Margaret entered the room, where her father and mother still sat in hushed conversation, appearing very pale and wan. She approached them closely before trusting herself to speak.

"Mrs. Thornton will send the water-bed, mamma."

"Dear, how tired you look! Is it very hot, Margaret?"

"Very hot, and the streets are rough from the strike."

Margaret's color returned with vivid brightness, only to fade instantly.

"Here is a message from Bessy Higgins asking you to visit her," Mrs. Hale said. "But I am sure you look too exhausted."

"Yes!" Margaret replied. "I am tired; I cannot go."

She remained silent and trembling while preparing tea. She was relieved to see her father so engrossed with her mother that he failed to notice her appearance. Even after her mother retired, he refused to leave her side, taking upon himself the duty of reading to her until sleep claimed her. Margaret was alone.

"Now I will think of it—now I will remember it all. I could not before—I dared not." She sat motionless in her chair, hands clasped on her knees, lips pressed tight, eyes fixed as if gazing at a vision. She drew a deep breath.

"I, who detest scenes—I, who have despised people for displaying emotion—I went down and threw myself into the fray like a romantic fool! Did I accomplish anything? They would likely have dispersed without me, I dare say." But this was an overreach of rationality; her well-balanced judgment immediately corrected itself. "No, perhaps they would not. I did some good. But what possessed me to defend that man as if he were a helpless child! Ah!" she cried, clutching her hands together. "It is no wonder those people believed I was in love with him, after disgracing myself so. I in love—and with him too!" Her pale cheeks suddenly blazed like fire, and she covered her face with her hands. When she lowered them, her palms were wet with scalding tears.

"Oh, how low I have fallen that they should say such things of me! I could not have been so brave for anyone else, precisely because he was so utterly indifferent to me—if, indeed, I do not positively dislike him. It made me anxious that there should be fair play on both sides; and I could see what fair play was. It was not fair," she said vehemently, "that he should stand there—sheltered, awaiting the soldiers, who might catch those poor maddened creatures as in a trap—without making an effort to bring them to reason. And it was worse than unfair for them to attack him as they threatened. I would do it again, let whoever say what they like of me. If I saved one blow, one cruel, angry act that might otherwise have been committed, I did a woman's work. Let them insult my maiden pride as they will—I walk pure before God!"

She looked up, and a noble peace seemed to descend, calming her face until it was "stiller than chiselled marble."

Dixon entered.

"If you please, Miss Margaret, here is the water-bed from Mrs. Thornton's. It is too late for tonight, I fear, as missus is nearly asleep: but it will serve nicely for tomorrow."

"Very," said Margaret. "You must convey our best thanks."

Dixon left the room for a moment.

"If you please, Miss Margaret, he says he is to ask particularly how you are. I think he must mean missus; but his last words were to ask how Miss Hale was."

"Me!" Margaret exclaimed, drawing herself up. "I am quite well. Tell him I am perfectly well." Yet her complexion was as deathly white as her handkerchief, and her head ached with intense pain.

Mr. Hale now entered. Having left his sleeping wife, he wished, as Margaret observed, to be amused and engaged by whatever she had to tell him. With sweet patience, she endured her pain without complaint, dredging up countless trivial subjects for conversation—all except the riot, which she never once mentioned. The thought of it made her sick.

"Good-night, Margaret. I have every chance of a good night myself, and you are looking very pale from your vigil. I shall call Dixon if your mother needs anything. Go to bed and sleep like a top; for I am sure you need it, poor child!"

"Good-night, papa!"

She let her color fade—the false smile dissolve—the eyes grow dull with heavy pain. She released her strong will from its laborious task. Until morning, she might feel ill and weary.

She lay down and never stirred. To move hand or foot, or even a single finger, would have exceeded the powers of either volition or motion. So tired, so stunned, she thought she never slept at all; her feverish thoughts passed and repassed the boundary between sleep and waking, maintaining their own miserable identity. She could not be alone, prostrate and powerless as she was; a cloud of faces looked up at her, offering no sense of fierce anger or personal danger, but a profound shame that she should be the object of universal regard—a shame so acute that it seemed she would rather burrow into the earth to hide, yet she could not escape the unwinking glare of many eyes.

"Your beauty was the first that won the place
And scal'd the walls of my undaunted heart,
Which, captive now, pines in a caitive case,
Unkindly met with rigour for desert:—
Yet not the less your servant shall abide,
In spite of rude repulse or silent pride."

The dawn broke gray and heavy, yet Margaret found a strange, weary peace in the silence of the house. She rose with a body that felt heavy as lead, though her spirit was strangely lightened. The night had passed without the sharp jarring of her mother's waking, leaving the household in a fragile, suspended calm. Outside, the stifling heat of the morning air began to stir, carrying with it the faint, distant murmur of wind through unseen foliage—a sound that, to Margaret's imagination, transformed itself into a cool, dancing stream, washing away the dust of the previous day.

Sitting by the shaded window in Mrs. Hale's room, she picked up her needlework, her mind already rehearsing the day's duties: helping her mother dress, visiting Bessy Higgins after dinner. She intended to build a wall of forgetfulness around the Thornton family, to keep them at bay until they appeared in reality rather than in her fevered thoughts. Yet, the very effort to suppress them only sharpened their image. From time to time, a sudden, hot flush would sweep across her pale cheeks, rising like a sunbeam breaking through storm clouds, betraying the turmoil she tried so desperately to hide.

The door opened with a soft click, and Dixon slipped in, her footsteps silent on the floorboards.

"Mr. Thornton, Miss Margaret," she whispered, approaching the chair. "He is in the drawing-room."

Margaret's needle dropped from her fingers, clattering onto the cloth. "Did he ask for me? Has Papa returned?"

"He asked for you, miss; and Master is out."

"Very well," Margaret said, her voice steady despite the sudden stillness in her chest. "I will come." Yet she hesitated, lingering as if anchored by an invisible weight.

In the drawing-room, Mr. Thornton stood before a window, his back to the entrance, ostensibly watching the street. In truth, he was a prisoner of his own emotions. The memory of her arms around his neck the day before burned within him, a sensation that now threatened to dissolve his resolve entirely. He feared the moment she entered, terrified that he might reach out, that she might return to him as she had yesterday, seeking shelter in his embrace. His heart hammered against his ribs, a frantic drumbeat that betrayed the strength he claimed to possess. He oscillated between a desperate hope that she would fall into his arms and a paralyzing dread that she would reject him, a thought that seemed to wither his future before it had truly begun.

He was startled from his reverie by the soft rustle of fabric. She had entered so quietly that the noise of the street outside had drowned out her approach.

She stood near the table, refusing to sit. Her eyes were half-closed, her lips slightly parted, revealing the pale line beneath. Her breathing was slow and deep, causing the fine muscles of her chin and nose to rise and fall. Her complexion was wan, the usual healthful color drained away, obscured further by the heavy shadow of her dark hair falling across her temples, hiding the mark of the blow she had sustained. Despite the droop of her eyelids, her head remained tilted back in that familiar, proud posture. She looked, he thought, like a noble prisoner falsely accused of a crime she despised, too indignant to defend herself.

Mr. Thornton took a step forward, then stopped himself, regaining his composure. He moved to the door, which she had left ajar, and closed it firmly. Returning to the center of the room, he stood opposite her, absorbing the sheer force of her presence before daring to speak, fearing that his words might shatter the fragile atmosphere he had created.

"Miss Hale, I was very ungrateful yesterday—"

"You had nothing to be grateful for," she interrupted, lifting her gaze to meet his directly. "You mean, I suppose, that you believe you ought to thank me for what I did." Despite her anger, a deep blush spread across her face, burning into her eyes, yet she did not look away. "It was only a natural instinct; any woman would have done just the same. We all feel the sanctity of our sex as a high privilege when we see danger. I ought rather," she added quickly, "to apologize to you for having said thoughtless words which sent you down into the danger."

"It was not your words; it was the truth they conveyed, pungently as it was expressed. But you shall not drive me off upon that, and so escape the expression of my deep gratitude, my—" He paused, the heat of his passion threatening to overwhelm his reason. He forced himself to stop, weighing each syllable.

"I do not try to escape from anything," she replied calmly. "I simply say that you owe me no gratitude; and I may add that any expression of it will be painful to me, because I do not feel that I deserve it. Still, if it will relieve you from even a fancied obligation, speak on."

"I do not want to be relieved from any obligation," he said, driven by her serene demeanor. "Fancied, or not fancied—I question not myself to know which—I choose to believe that I owe my very life to you—ay—smile, and think it an exaggeration if you will. I believe it, because it adds a value to that life to think—oh, Miss Hale!" His voice dropped to a tender, intense whisper that made her shiver. "To think circumstances so wrought, that whenever I exult in existence henceforward, I may say to myself, 'All this gladness in life, all honest pride in doing my work in the world, all this keen sense of being, I owe to her!' And it doubles the gladness, it makes the pride glow, it sharpens the sense of existence till I hardly know if it is pain or pleasure to think that I owe it to one—nay, you must, you shall hear," he stepped forward with stern determination, "to one whom I love, as I do not believe man ever loved woman before."

He seized her hand tightly, panting as he awaited her response. But when he heard her icy tone, he withdrew his hand with indignation. The words had come faltering, as if she struggled to find them.

"Your way of speaking shocks me. It is blasphemous. I cannot help it, if that is my first feeling. It might not be so, I dare say, if I understood the kind of feeling you describe. I do not want to vex you; and besides, we must speak gently, for mamma is asleep; but your whole manner offends me—"

"How!" he exclaimed. "Offends you! I am indeed most unfortunate."

"Yes!" she said, recovering her dignity. "I do feel offended; and, I think, justly. You seem to fancy that my conduct of yesterday"—again the deep carnation blush, but this time her eyes kindled with indignation rather than shame—"was a personal act between you and me; and that you may come and thank me for it, instead of perceiving, as a gentleman would—yes! a gentleman," she repeated, alluding to their past debate on the term, "that any woman, worthy of the name of woman, would come forward to shield, with her reverenced helplessness, a man in danger from the violence of numbers."

"And the gentleman thus rescued is forbidden the relief of thanks!" he broke in contemptuously. "I am a man. I claim the right of expressing my feelings."

"And I yielded to the right; simply saying that you gave me pain by insisting upon it," she replied, proudly. "But you seem to have imagined, that I was not merely guided by womanly instinct, but"—here the passionate tears, held back for so long, surged up, choking her voice—"but that I was prompted by some particular feeling for you—you! Why, there was not a man—not a poor desperate man in all that crowd—for whom I had not more sympathy—for whom I should not have done what little I could more heartily."

"You may speak on, Miss Hale. I am aware of these misplaced sympathies of yours. I now believe that it was only your innate sense of oppression—(yes; I, though a master, may be oppressed)—that made you act so nobly as you did. I know you despise me; allow me to say, it is because you do not understand me."

"I do not care to understand," she replied, gripping the edge of the table to steady herself. She thought him cruel—as, indeed, he was—and she was weak with her indignation.

"No, I see you do not. You are unfair and unjust."

Margaret compressed her lips. She would not speak in answer to such accusations. Yet, despite his savage words, he could have thrown himself at her feet and kissed the hem of her garment. She remained silent, unmoving. Tears of wounded pride fell hot and fast. He waited a while, longing for her to say something, even a taunt, to which he might reply. But she was silent. He took up his hat.

"One word more. You look as if you thought it tainted you to be loved by me. You cannot avoid it. Nay, I, if I would, cannot cleanse you from it. But I would not if I could. I have never loved any woman before: my life has been too busy, my thoughts too much absorbed with other things. Now I love, and will love. But do not be afraid of too much expression on my part."

"I am not afraid," she replied, lifting herself straight up. "No one yet has ever dared to be impertinent to me, and no one ever shall. But, Mr. Thornton, you have been very kind to my father," she said, changing her whole tone and bearing to a most womanly softness. "Don't let us go on making each other angry. Pray don't!"

He took no notice of her words. For half a minute he occupied himself in smoothing the nap of his hat with his coat-sleeve; and then, rejecting her offered hand, and making as if he did not see her grave look of regret, he turned abruptly away and left the room. Margaret caught one glance at his face before he went.

When he was gone, she thought she had seen the gleam of washed tears in his eyes; and that turned her proud dislike into something different and kinder, if nearly as painful—self-reproach for having caused such mortification to any one.

"But how could I help it?" she asked herself. "I never liked him. I was civil; but I took no trouble to conceal my indifference. Indeed, I never thought about myself or him, so my manners must have shown the truth. All that yesterday, he might mistake. But that is his fault, not mine. I would do it again, if need were, though it does lead me into all this shame and trouble."

"Revenge may have her own;
Roused discipline aloud proclaims their cause,
And injured navies urge their broken laws."

Margaret found herself wondering if all proposals carried the same weight of shock, the same crushing immediacy, as the two she had recently endured. An involuntary comparison between Mr. Lennox and Mr. Thornton surfaced in her mind. With Lennox, she had felt a profound regret that circumstances had dragged an expression of anything other than friendship from him; that sorrow had dominated her first encounter with a suitor. But now, the echo of Mr. Thornton's voice still lingered in the room, leaving her stunned and overwhelmed. In Lennox's case, he had seemed to slip across the threshold of love only to recoil almost instantly, driven by a regret she shared, albeit for different reasons. With Mr. Thornton, however, there had been no gentle slope of friendship to traverse. Their history had been a continuous series of clashes, their opinions colliding like stones against a wall. She had never sensed that he valued her thoughts as extensions of her own self; rather, whenever they challenged his rock-like character or his fierce passion, he dismissed them with a contempt that made her protests feel futile. And now, in this strange, wild, and passionate outburst, he had declared his love! At first, it had struck her as forced, goaded forth by his sharp compassion for her exposed vulnerability—a sentiment others might easily misunderstand. Yet, even before he departed, and certainly within five minutes, a clear conviction had dawned upon her, shining brightly: he loved her; he had loved her; he would love her. She shrank and shuddered as if under the spell of a great, repugnant power, alien to her entire previous life. She crept away, hiding from the idea, but it was useless. Parodying a line from Fairfax's *Tasso*:

"His strong idea wandered through her thought."

She disliked him all the more for having mastered her inner will. How dared he claim he would love her still, even if she shook him off with contempt? She wished she had spoken more strongly, more decisively. Sharp, resolute words thronged into her mind now that it was too late to utter them. The deep impression of the interview was like a horror in a dream that refuses to leave the room once we awaken, rub our eyes, and force a stiff, rigid smile upon our lips. It remains there—cowering and gibbering with fixed, ghastly eyes in some corner of the chamber, waiting to hear if we dare breathe of its presence to anyone. And we dare not; poor cowards that we are!

So she shuddered away from the threat of his enduring love. What did he mean? Did she not possess the power to daunt him? She would see. It seemed more daring than became a man to threaten her so. Was his claim grounded in the miserable events of yesterday? If need be, she would do the same tomorrow—by a crippled beggar, willingly and gladly—but by him, she would do it just as bravely, in spite of his deductions and the cold slime of woman's impertinence. She acted because it was right, simple, and true to save where she could save; even to try to save. *Fais ce que dois, advienne que pourra.*

Hitherto, she had not stirred from where he had left her; no outward circumstance had roused her from the trance of thought into which his last words and the look of his deep, intent, passionate eyes had plunged her, as their flames had made her own fall before them. She went to the window and threw it open to dispel the oppression hanging around her. Then she opened the door with an impetuous wish to shake off the recollection of the past hour in the company of others or through active exertion. But the house was profoundly hushed in the noonday stillness of a home where an invalid catches the unrefreshing sleep denied to the night-hours. Margaret would not be alone. What should she do? "Go and see Bessy Higgins, of course," she thought, as the memory of the message sent the night before flashed into her mind. And away she went.

When she arrived, she found Bessy lying on the settle, drawn close to the fire despite the sultry, oppressive heat. She was laid down quite flat, as if resting languidly after some paroxysm of pain. Margaret felt certain Bessy needed the greater freedom of breathing afforded by a sitting posture. Without a word, she raised her and arranged the pillows so Bessy was more at ease, though still very languid.

"I thought I should na' ha' seen yo' again," Bessy said finally, looking wistfully into Margaret's face.

"I'm afraid you're much worse. But I could not have come yesterday; my mother was so ill—for many reasons," Margaret replied, coloring.

"You'd m'appen think I went beyond my place in sending Mary for yo'. But the wraglin' and the loud voices had just torn me to pieces. I thought when father left, oh! if I could just hear her voice, reading me some words of peace and promise, I could die away into the silence and rest of God, just as a baby is hushed to sleep by its mother's lullaby."

"Shall I read you a chapter now?"

"Ay, do! M'appen I shan't listen to the sense at first; it will seem far away—but when you come to words I like, to the comforting texts, it'll seem close in my ear, going through me as it were."

Margaret began. Bessy tossed to and fro. If, by an effort, she attended for one moment, she seemed convulsed into double restlessness the next. Finally, she burst out: "Don't go on reading. It's no use. I'm blaspheming all the time in my mind, thinking angrily on what canna be helped. You'd hear of the riot yesterday at Marlborough Mills? Thornton's factory, you know."

"Your father was not there, was he?" Margaret asked, coloring deeply.

"Not he. He'd ha' given his right hand if it had never come to pass. It's that that's fretting me. He's fairly knocked down in his mind by it. It's no use telling him; fools will always break out of bounds. You never saw a man so downhearted as he is."

"But why?" Margaret asked. "I don't understand."

"Why, you see, he's a committee-man on this special strike. The Union appointed him because, though I say it as shouldn't say it, he's reckoned a deep chap, and true to the backbone. He and the other committee-men laid their plans. They were to hold together through thick and thin; whatever the majority thought, the others were to think, whether they would or no. Above all, there was to be no going against the law of the land. Folk would follow them if they saw them striving and starving with dumb patience; but if there was once any noise of fighting and struggling—even with knobsticks—all was up, as they know by the experience of many, many times before. They would try to get speech of the knobsticks, coax them, reason with them, and maybe warn them off; but whatever came, the Committee charged all members of the Union to lie down and die, if need were, without striking a blow. Then they reckoned they were sure of carrying the public with them. Besides all that, the Committee knew they were right in their demand, and they didn't want to have right mixed up with wrong till folk can't separate it, no more than I can separate the physic-powder from the jelly you gave me to mix; the jelly is much the bigger, but the powder tastes it all through. Well, I've told you at length about this, but I am tired out. You just think for yourself what it must be for father to have all his work undone by such a fool as Boucher, who must needs go right against the orders of the Committee and ruin the strike, just as bad as if he meant to be a Judas. Eh! but father gave it him last night! He went so far as to say he'd go and tell the police where they might find the ringleader of the riot; he'd give him up to the mill-owners to do what they would with him. He'd show the world that the real leaders of the strike were not such as Boucher, but steady, thoughtful men; good hands, and good citizens, friendly to law and judgment, who would uphold order; who only wanted their right wage and wouldn't work, even though they starved, till they got it, but who would ne'er injure property or life. For," dropping her voice, "they do say that Boucher threw a stone at Thornton's sister that well-nigh killed her."

"That's not true," said Margaret. "It was not Boucher that threw the stone"—she went first red, then white.

"You'd be there then, were you?" Bessie asked languidly; indeed, she had spoken with many pauses, as if speech was unusually difficult for her.

"Yes. Never mind. Go on. Only it was not Boucher that threw the stone. But what did he answer to your father?"

"He did na' speak words. He was all in such a tremble with spent passion I could na' bear to look at him. I heard his breath coming quick, and at one time I thought he were sobbing. But when father said he'd give him up to the police, he gave a great cry and struck father on the face with his closed fist, and he off like lightning. Father was stunned with the blow at first, for all Boucher was weak with passion and trembling. He sat down a bit and put his hand before his eyes; then he made for the door.
