I dunno' where I got strength, but I threw myself on the settle and clung to him. 'Father, father!' I said. 'Thou'll never go peach on that poor trembling man. I'll never leave thee till thou sayst thou wunnot.' 'Dunnot be a fool,' he says, 'words come readier than deeds to most men. I never thought of telling the police on him; though by G—, he deserves it, and I should na' ha' minded if someone else had done the dirty work and got him clapped up. But now he has strucken me, I could do it less nor ever, for it would be getting other men to take up my quarrel. But if ever he gets well over this trembling and is in good condition, he and I'll have an up and down fight, purring an' a', and I'll see what I can do for him.' And so father shook me off—for indeed I was low and faint enough, and his face was all clay-white where it wasn't bloody, and turned me sick to look at. And I know not if I slept or waked, or was in a dead swoon, till Mary came in; and I told her to fetch you to me. And now dunnot talk to me, but just read out the chapter. I'm easier in my mind for having spit it out; but I want some thoughts of the world that's far away to take the weary taste out of my mouth. Read me not a sermon chapter, but a story chapter; they've pictures in them which I see when my eyes are shut. Read about the New Heavens and the New Earth; and m'appen I'll forget this."

Margaret read in her soft, low voice. Though Bessy's eyes were shut, she listened for some time, for the moisture of tears gathered heavily on her eyelashes. At last, she slept, with many starts and muttered pleadings. Margaret covered her up and left her, for she had an uneasy consciousness that she might be wanted at home, and yet, until now, it seemed cruel to leave the dying girl.

Mrs. Hale was in the drawing-room on Margaret's return. It was one of her better days, and she was full of praises of the water-bed. It had been more like the beds at Sir John Beresford's than anything she had slept on since. She did not know how it was, but people seemed to have lost the art of making the same kind of beds as they used to do in her youth. One would think it was easy enough; there was the same kind of feathers to be had, and yet somehow, till this last night, she did not know when she had had a good, sound resting sleep.

Mr. Hale suggested that something of the merits of the feather-beds of former days might be attributed to the activity of youth, which gave a relish to rest; but this idea was not kindly received by his wife.

"No, indeed, Mr. Hale, it was those beds at Sir John's. Now, Margaret, you're young enough and go about in the day; are the beds comfortable? I appeal to you. Do they give you a feeling of perfect repose when you lie down upon them; or rather, don't you toss about, try in vain to find an easy position, and wake in the morning as tired as when you went to bed?"

Margaret laughed. "To tell the truth, mamma, I've never thought about my bed at all, what kind it is. I'm so sleepy at night that if I only lie down anywhere, I nap off directly. So I don't think I'm a competent witness. But then, you know, I never had the opportunity of trying Sir John Beresford's beds. I never was at Oxenham."

"Were you not? Oh, no! to be sure. It was poor darling Fred I took with me, I remember. I only went to Oxenham once after I was married—to your Aunt Shaw's wedding; and poor little Fred was the baby then. And I know Dixon did not like changing from lady's maid to nurse, and I was afraid that if I took her near her old home, amongst her own people, she might want to leave me. But poor baby was taken ill at Oxenham with his teething; and, what with my being a great deal with Anna just before her marriage and not being very strong myself, Dixon had more of the charge of him than she ever had before; and it made

"I have found that holy place of rest / Still changeless."
— *Mrs. Hemans*

When Mr. Thornton left the house that morning, the world seemed to tilt beneath his feet. His passion had not merely cooled; it had curdled into a physical blindness. He felt as though Margaret, rather than the graceful woman she appeared, had been a rough fish-wife striking him with clenched fists. There was a genuine, throbbing agony in his skull, a pulse hammering against his ribs like a trapped bird. The noise of the street, the glare of the sun, the endless rumble of carriages—all of it grated against his nerves. He cursed himself for this weakness, yet he could not recall the precise trigger, only the magnitude of the fallout.

In that moment of raw exposure, a strange comfort seized him: the ability to weep openly, not for himself, but as a child might rage against a perceived injustice. He told himself he hated her. But as the word formed, a jagged bolt of love tore through his fury, illuminating the darkness. His greatest solace was in embracing his torment. He resolved that even if she despised him, treated him with sovereign indifference, or scorned him utterly, he would remain unchanged. She could not alter his nature. He loved her, and he would love her, defying both her and this crushing physical pain.

He paused to cement this resolve. An omnibus rolled by, heading toward the countryside. The conductor, mistaking his stillness for hesitation, pulled up. Apologizing felt like too great an effort, so he climbed aboard. The vehicle carried him past rows of terraced houses, then detached villas with manicured gardens, until the scenery shifted to hedgerows and finally a small rural town. As the passengers alighted, Thornton followed, stepping out into the open fields. He walked briskly, seeking relief in the sharp motion of his stride.

Now, the fog lifted. He remembered the absurdity of his morning self—the pitiful figure he must have cut, doing precisely what he had sworn was the most foolish act imaginable, only to meet the exact consequences he had always predicted. Was he bewitched by those beautiful eyes, that soft, sighing mouth pressed against his shoulder just yesterday? He could not shake the memory of her arms around him, even if never again. He saw only fragments of her: sometimes brave, sometimes timid; tender one moment, haughty and regal the next. He reviewed every encounter, trying to erase her image by cataloging it, yet she remained vivid in every dress, every mood. This morning, she had looked magnificent, her eyes flashing with the conviction that sharing his danger proved her affection.

If he was a fool in the morning, he remained so in the afternoon. The sixpenny ride offered no wisdom, only a sharper conviction: there was no one like Margaret. She did not love him, and never would. Yet, nothing in the world should hinder him from loving her. He returned to the market square and boarded the omnibus for Milton.

By late afternoon, he was set down near his warehouse. The familiar surroundings triggered familiar routines. His workload was heavier than usual due to the previous day's commotion. He had to meet with his fellow magistrates, finalize arrangements for the safety of his newly imported Irish workers, and ensure they remained isolated from the discontented Milton workforce. Finally, he had to face his mother.

Mrs. Thornton had spent the entire day in the dining room, poised for news of her son's acceptance by Miss Hale. Every sudden noise made her jump; every time the door opened, she braced herself, only to find an insignificant errand-runner. Her rigid face would soften into a look of despondency, then snap back to its stern composure. She forced her mind into domestic channels, calculating the needs of a future household. She sorted through baskets of linen, distinguishing her own stock, marked "G.H.T.," from her son's purchases. Some of his linens were exquisite Dutch damask, a pride she had once held dear. She knitted her brows, pinching her lips, and carefully unpicked the old initials, searching for Turkey-red thread to replace them. Finding none, she stared into the empty air, her mind drifting through visions where her son was the sole focus—her pride, her property.

Still, he did not arrive. Surely he was with Miss Hale. A pang of jealous pain, both physical and mental, shot through her. She rose, straightening her spine, a grim smile replacing her despair. She prepared for the triumphant visitor who would never know the depth of her regret. To her, the future daughter-in-law was not an individual but a function: John's wife, a vessel for honor, obedience, and the restoration of household plenty. If Miss Hale were a Milton lass, she might have liked her; she possessed spirit and flavor, despite her prejudice and ignorance. A bitter comparison with Fanny played in her mind, leading her to speak harshly to her daughter before retreating to Henry's Commentaries to distract herself from inspecting the tablecloths.

Then, she heard his step.

Even as her eye scanned the page and her memory mechanically recited the sentence, her heightened senses registered his approach. He moved from the hall to the hat-stand, then to the room door. Why did he pause? Let her know the truth.

She kept her head down over the book. He approached the table, waiting for her to finish the paragraph. With an effort, she looked up. "Well, John?"

She knew the weight of those few words. He wanted to jest, to mask the bitterness in his heart, but he owed his mother better. He stepped behind her, bending her gray, stony face back so she could not see his expression, and kissed her cheek.

"No one loves me," he murmured, "no one cares for me, but you, mother."

He turned away, leaning his head against the mantelpiece, tears forcing their way into his manly eyes. She rose, swaying slightly. For the first time in her life, the strong woman tottered. She placed her hands on his shoulders, her gaze locking onto his.

"Mother's love is given by God, John. It holds fast forever. A girl's love is like a puff of smoke—it changes with every wind. And she would not have you, my own lad, would she?" She gritted her teeth, baring them like a dog. He shook his head.

"I am not fit for her, mother. I knew I was not."

She ground out words between her closed teeth, a silent curse that fell heavy in intent if not in wording. Yet, her heart leaped with the knowledge that he was hers again.

"Mother!" he urged, hurriedly. "I cannot hear a word against her. Spare me! My heart is weak and sore; I love her yet, I love her more than ever."

"And I hate her," Mrs. Thornton said in a low, fierce voice. "I tried not to hate her when she stood between you and me, telling myself she would make you happy. I would give my heart's blood to do that. But now, I hate her for your misery's sake. Yes, John, there is no use hiding your aching heart from me. I am the mother who bore you, and your sorrow is my agony. If you do not hate her, I do."

"Then, mother, you make me love her more. She is unjustly treated by you, and I must balance the scales. But why speak of love or hatred? She does not care for me, and that is enough. Let us never name the subject again. It is the only thing you can do for me. Let us never mention her."

"With all my heart," she replied. "I only wish that she, and all belonging to her, were swept back to the place they came from."

He stood gazing into the fire for a moment or two. Dry, dim eyes filled with unwonted tears as she watched him, yet she remained grim and quiet when he spoke again.

"Warrants are out against three men for conspiracy, mother. The riot yesterday helped to break the strike."

Margaret's name was never mentioned again between mother and son. They reverted to their usual mode of discourse—facts, not opinions, and certainly not feelings. Their voices and tones were calm and cold. A stranger passing by might have concluded that such frigid indifference between such close relations was entirely natural.

"For never any thing can be amiss
When simpleness and duty tender it."

Mr. Thornton approached the business of the following day with a singular clarity. There was a slight demand for finished goods, and seizing the opportunity, he drove hard bargains. At the meeting of the magistrates, he was sharp to the hour, offering the best assistance of his strong sense and his power of seeing consequences at a glance, leading to rapid decisions. Older men, those of long standing and greater wealth, whose fortunes were turned into land, looked to him for prompt wisdom. He was the one deputed to arrange with the police and lead the requisite steps. Yet, he cared for their unconscious deference no more than for the soft west wind that scarcely made the smoke from the great chimneys swerve. He was unaware of the silent respect paid to him; if he had been, he would have felt it as an obstacle. His sole aim was the speedy accomplishment of his object. It was his mother's greedy ears that absorbed the gossip of the womenfolk regarding how highly Mr. Thornton was thought of, how things would have gone badly without him. He swept off his business that day. It seemed as though the deep mortification of yesterday and the stunned purposelessness of the hours that followed had cleared all mists from his intellect. He felt his power and revelled in it, almost defying his heart. If he had known it, he could have sung the song of the miller by the river Dee:

"I care for nobody—
Nobody cares for me."

The evidence against Boucher and other ring-leaders of the riot was taken before him; the charge against the three others for conspiracy failed. But he sternly charged the police to remain vigilant, for the swift right arm of the law should be ready to strike the moment a fault could be proven. Then he left the hot, reeking room of the borough court and stepped out into the fresher, yet still sultry, street. Suddenly, he seemed to give way; he was so languid that he could not control his wandering thoughts. They drifted inevitably to her, bringing back not the scene of his repulse the day before, but the looks and actions of the day prior to that. He walked along the crowded streets mechanically, winding in and out among the people without seeing them, almost sick with longing for that one half-hour, that brief space when she had clung to him and her heart had beaten against his, to come once again.

"Why, Mr. Thornton! you're cutting me very coolly, I must say. And how is Mrs. Thornton? Brave weather this! We doctors don't like it, I can tell you!"

"I beg your pardon, Dr. Donaldson. I really didn't see you. My mother's quite well, thank you. It is a fine day, and good for the harvest, I hope. If the wheat is well got in, we shall have a brisk trade next year, whatever you doctors have."

"Ay, ay. Each man for himself. Your bad weather, and your bad times, are my good ones. When trade is bad, there's more undermining of health, and preparation for death, going on among you Milton men than you're aware of."

"Not with me, Doctor. I'm made of iron. The news of the very worst bad debt I ever had, never made my pulse vary. This strike, which affects me more than any one else in Milton,—more than Hamper,—never comes near my appetite. You must go elsewhere for a patient, Doctor."

"By the way, you've recommended me a good patient, poor lady! Not to go on talking in this heartless way, I seriously believe that Mrs. Hale—that lady in Crampton, you know—hasn't many weeks to live. I never had any hope of cure, as I think I told you; but I've been seeing her to-day, and I think very badly of her."

Mr. Thornton was silent. The vaunted steadiness of his pulse failed him for an instant.

"Can I do anything, Doctor?" he asked, in an altered voice. "You know—you would see, that money is not very plentiful, are there any comforts or dainties she ought to have?"

"No," replied the Doctor, shaking his head. "She craves for fruit,—she has a constant fever on her; but jargonelle pears will do as well as anything, and there are quantities of them in the market."

"You will tell me if there is anything I can do, I'm sure," replied Mr. Thornton. "I rely upon you."

"Oh! never fear! I'll not spare your purse,—I know it's deep enough. I wish you'd give me a carte-blanche for all my patients, and all their wants."

But Mr. Thornton possessed no general benevolence, nor universal philanthropy; few would have credited him with strong affections. Yet, he went straight to the first fruit-shop in Milton and chose out the bunch of purple grapes with the most delicate bloom, the richest-coloured peaches, and the freshest vine-leaves. They were packed into a basket, and the shopman awaited the answer to his inquiry, "Where shall we send them to, sir?"

There was no reply. "To Marlborough Mills, I suppose, sir?"

"No!" Mr. Thornton said. "Give the basket to me,—I'll take it."

It took up both his hands to carry it, and he had to pass through the busiest part of the town, occupied just like a porter or an errand-boy. Many a young lady of his acquaintance turned to look after him, finding the sight strange.

He was thinking, "I will not be daunted from doing as I choose by the thought of her. I like to take this fruit to the poor mother, and it is simply right that I should. She shall never scorn me out of doing what I please. A pretty joke, indeed, if, for fear of a haughty girl, I failed in doing a kindness to a man I liked! I do it for Mr. Hale; I do it in defiance of her."

He moved at an unusual pace and was soon at Crampton. He went upstairs two steps at a time and entered the drawing-room before Dixon could announce him, his face flushed, his eyes shining with kindly earnestness. Mrs. Hale lay on the sofa, heated with fever. Mr. Hale was reading aloud. Margaret was working on a low stool by her mother's side. Her heart fluttered, if his did not at this interview. But he took no notice of her, hardly of Mr. Hale himself; he went straight with his basket to Mrs. Hale and said, in that subdued and gentle tone so touching when used by a robust man in full health speaking to a feeble invalid,—

"I met Dr. Donaldson, ma'am, and as he said fruit would be good for you, I have taken the liberty—the great liberty—of bringing you some that seemed to me fine." Mrs. Hale was excessively pleased, quite in a tremble of eagerness. Mr. Hale, with fewer words, expressed a deeper gratitude.

"Fetch a plate, Margaret—a basket—anything." Margaret stood up by the table, half afraid of moving or making any noise to arouse Mr. Thornton into a consciousness of her being in the room. She thought it would be awkward for both to be brought into conscious collision, and fancied that, from her being on a low seat at first and now standing behind her father, he had overlooked her in his haste. As if he did not feel the consciousness of her presence all over, though his eyes had never rested on her!

"I must go," said he. "I cannot stay. If you will forgive this liberty,—my rough ways,—too abrupt, I fear—but I will be more gentle next time. You will allow me the pleasure of bringing you some fruit again, if I should see any that is tempting. Good afternoon, Mr. Hale. Good-bye, ma'am."

He was gone. Not one word: not one look to Margaret. She believed that he had not seen her. She went for a plate in silence and lifted the fruit out tenderly, with the points of her delicate taper fingers. It was good of him to bring it; and after yesterday too!

"Oh! it is so delicious!" said Mrs. Hale, in a feeble voice. "How kind of him to think of me! Margaret love, only taste these grapes! Was it not good of him?"

"Yes!" said Margaret, quietly.

"Margaret!" said Mrs. Hale, rather querulously, "you won't like anything Mr. Thornton does. I never saw anybody so prejudiced."

Mr. Hale had been peeling a peach for his wife; and, cutting off a small piece for himself, he said:

"If I had any prejudices, the gift of such delicious fruit as this would melt them away. I have not tasted such fruit—no! not even in Hampshire—since I was a boy; and, to boys, I fancy, all fruit is good. I remember eating sloes and crabs with a relish. Do you remember the matted-up currant bushes, Margaret, at the corner of the west-wall at the garden at home?"

Did she not? Did she not remember every weather-stain on the old stone wall; the gray and yellow lichens that marked it like a map; the little crane's-bill that grew in the crevices? She had been shaken by the events of the last two days; her whole life just now was a strain upon her fortitude; and, somehow, these careless words of her father's, touching on the remembrance of the sunny times of old, made her start up, and, dropping her sewing on the ground, she went hastily out of the room into her own little chamber. She had hardly given way to the first choking sob, when she became aware of Dixon standing at her drawers, evidently searching for something.

"Bless me, miss! How you startled me! Missus is not worse, is she? Is anything the matter?"

"No, nothing. Only I'm silly, Dixon, and want a glass of water. What are you looking for? I keep my muslins in that drawer."

Dixon did not speak, but went on rummaging. The scent of lavender came out and perfumed the room.

At last Dixon found what she wanted; what it was Margaret could not see. Dixon faced round and spoke to her:

"Now I don't like telling what I wanted, because you've fretting enough to go through, and I know you'll fret about this. I meant to have kept it from you till night, may be, or such times as that."

"What is the matter? Pray, tell me, Dixon, at once."

"That young woman you go to see—Higgins I mean."

"Well?"

"Well! she died this morning, and her sister is here—come to beg a strange thing. It seems, the young woman who died had a fancy for being buried in something of yours, and so the sister's come to ask for it,—and I was looking for a night-cap that wasn't too good to give away."

"Oh! let me find one," said Margaret, in the midst of her tears. "Poor Bessy! I never thought I should not see her again."

"Why, that's another thing. This girl downstairs wanted me to ask you, if you would like to see her."

"But she's dead!" said Margaret, turning a little pale. "I never saw a dead person. No! I would rather not."

"I should never have asked you, if you hadn't come in. I told her you wouldn't."

"I will go down and speak to her," said Margaret, afraid lest Dixon's harshness of manner might wound the poor girl. So, taking the cap in her hand, she went to the kitchen. Mary's face was all swollen with crying, and she burst out afresh when she saw Margaret.

"Oh, ma'am, she loved yo', she loved yo', she did indeed!" And for a long time, Margaret could not get her to say anything more than this. At last, her sympathy, and Dixon's scolding, forced out a few facts. Nicholas Higgins had gone out in the morning, leaving Bessy as well as on the day before. But in an hour she was taken worse; some neighbour ran to the room where Mary was working; they did not know where to find her father; Mary had only come in a few minutes before she died.

"It were a day or two ago she axed to be buried in somewhat o' yourn. She were never tired o' talking o' yo'. She used to say yo' were the prettiest thing she ever clapped eyes on. She loved yo' dearly. Her last words were 'Give her my affectionate respects; and keep father fro' drink.' Yo'll come and see her ma'am. She would ha' thought it a great compliment, I know."

Margaret shrank a little from answering.

"Yes, perhaps I may. Yes, I will. I'll come before tea. But where's your father, Mary?"

Mary shook her head and stood up to be going.

"Miss Hale," said Dixon, in a low voice, "where's the use o' your going to see the poor thing laid out? I'd never say a word against it, if it could do the girl any good; and I wouldn't mind a bit going myself, if that would satisfy her. They've just a notion, these common folks, of its being a respect to the departed. Here," said she, turning sharply round, "I'll come and see your sister. Miss Hale is busy, and she can't come, or else she would."

The girl looked wistfully at Margaret. Dixon's coming might be a compliment, but it was not the same thing to the poor sister, who had had her little pangs of jealousy, during Bessy's lifetime, at the intimacy between her and the young lady.

"No, Dixon!" said Margaret with decision. "I will go. Mary, you shall see me this afternoon." And for fear of her own cowardice, she went away, in order to take from herself any chance of changing her determination.

"Through cross to crown!—And though thy spirit's life
Trials untold assail with giant strength,
Good cheer! good cheer! Soon ends the bitter strife,
And thou shalt reign in peace with Christ at length."

"Ay sooth, we feel too strong in weal, to need Thee on that road;
But woe being come, the soul is dumb, that crieth not on 'God.'"

That afternoon, Margaret walked with purpose toward the Higgins's home. Mary awaited her at the door, her expression a mixture of hope and wary suspicion. Margaret offered a gentle, reassuring smile, and together they moved through the house, ascending the stairs to the quiet chamber where the dead lay. Upon entering, Margaret felt a profound relief settle over her. The face, usually etched with the fatigue of pain and the unrest of troubled thoughts, now bore the faint, soft smile of eternal rest. Slow tears welled in Margaret's eyes, yet a deep, abiding calm settled within her soul. That was death; it appeared more peaceful than life itself. Sacred verses filled her mind: "They rest from their labours," "The weary are at rest," "He giveth his beloved sleep."

Slowly, Margaret turned from the bedside. Behind her, Mary sobbed humbly. They descended the stairs in silence.

Nicholas Higgins stood in the center of the floor, his hand resting heavily on the table. His great eyes, startled wide by the news he had heard from the bustling court below, were dry and fierce. He studied the reality of her absence, forcing himself to accept that her place would know her no more. He had been so convinced of her resilience, so certain she would "pull through," that the finality of her illness had taken him completely by surprise.

Margaret felt an acute sense of intrusion, as if she had no right to be so familiar with the surroundings of death, a reality her father had only just begun to comprehend. There had been a fleeting pause on the steep, crooked stair when she first saw him, but now she attempted to slip past his abstracted gaze, leaving him to the solemn circle of his household's misery.

Mary collapsed onto the nearest chair, pulling her apron over her head as she began to weep. The sound seemed to snap Nicholas out of his trance. He seized Margaret's arm, holding her tightly until he could gather the strength to speak. His throat felt parched; his words came thick, choking, and hoarse.

"Were yo' with her? Did yo' see her die?"

"No!" Margaret replied, standing still with immense patience, now that she realized she was being observed. It took some time before he spoke again, though he maintained his grip on her arm.

"All men must die," he said finally, with a strange, heavy gravity that first suggested to Margaret he had been drinking—not enough to intoxicate, but enough to cloud his thoughts. "But she were younger than me." He continued to ponder the event, not looking at Margaret, though his grasp remained tight. Suddenly, he looked up at her with a wild, searching inquiry. "Yo're sure and certain she's dead—not in a dwam, a faint? she's been so before, often."

"She is dead," Margaret answered. She felt no fear in speaking to him, despite the pain of his grip and the wild, vacant gleams in his eyes.

"She is dead!" she repeated.

He stared at her with that searching look, which seemed to fade as he gazed. Then, abruptly, he released her. Throwing his body half across the table, he shook it and every piece of furniture in the room with violent sobs. Mary trembled forward toward him.

"Get thee gone!—get thee gone!" he cried, striking wildly and blindly at her. "What do I care for thee?" Margaret took her hand and held it softly. He tore at his hair, beat his head against the hard wood, and then lay exhausted and stuporous. Still, neither his daughter nor Margaret moved. Mary trembled from head to foot.

At last—it might have been a quarter of an hour, or perhaps an hour—he lifted himself up. His eyes were swollen and bloodshot, and he seemed to have forgotten anyone was present; he scowled at the watchers when he noticed them. He shook himself heavily, gave them one more sullen look, spoke not a word, and made for the door.

"Oh, father, father!" Mary cried, throwing herself upon his arm. "Not to-night! Any night but to-night. Oh, help me! he's going out to drink again! Father, I'll not leave yo'. Yo' may strike, but I'll not leave yo'. She told me last of all to keep yo' fro' drink!"

But Margaret stood in the doorway, silent yet commanding. He looked up at her defyingly.

"It's my own house. Stand out o' the way, wench, or I'll make yo'!" He had shaken Mary off with violence; he looked ready to strike Margaret. Yet she never moved a muscle, never took her deep, serious eyes off him. He stared back with gloomy fierceness. If she had stirred, he would have thrust her aside with even more violence than he had used against his own daughter, whose face was bleeding from her fall against a chair.

"What are yo' looking at me in that way for?" he asked finally, daunted and awed by her severe calm. "If yo' think for to keep me from going what gait I choose, because she loved yo'—and in my own house, too, where I never asked yo' to come, yo're mista'en. It's very hard upon a man that he can't go to the only comfort left."

Margaret felt that he acknowledged her power. What could she do next? He had seated himself on a chair close to the door; half-conquered, half-resenting; intending to leave as soon as she vacated her position, but unwilling to use the violence he had threatened moments before. Margaret laid her hand on his arm.

"Come with me," she said. "Come and see her!"

The voice in which she spoke was very low and solemn, yet there was no fear or doubt expressed in it, either of him or of his compliance. He sullenly rose. He stood uncertain, with dogged irresolution upon his face. She waited him there; quietly and patiently waiting for his time to move. He had a strange pleasure in making her wait, but at last he moved toward the stairs.

They stood by the corpse.

"Her last words to Mary were, 'Keep my father fro' drink.'"

"It canna hurt her now," he muttered. "Nought can hurt her now." Then, raising his voice to a wailing cry, he continued: "We may quarrel and fall out—we may make peace and be friends—we may clem to skin and bone—and nought o' all our griefs will ever touch her more. Hoo's had her portion on 'em. What wi' hard work first, and sickness at last, hoo' led the life of a dog. And to die without knowing one good piece o' rejoicing in all her days! Nay, wench, whatever hoo said, hoo can know nought about it now, and I mun ha' a sup o' drink just to steady me again sorrow."

"No," said Margaret, softening with his softened manner. "You shall not. If her life has been what you say, at any rate she did not fear death as some do. Oh, you should have heard her speak of the life to come—the life hidden with God, that she is now gone to."

He shook his head, glancing sideways up at Margaret as he did so. His pale, haggard face struck her painfully.

"You are sorely tried. Where have you been all day—not at work?"

"Not at work, sure enough," he said, with a short, grim laugh. "Not at what you call work. I were at the Committee, till I were sickened out wi' trying to make fools hear reason. I were fetched to Boucher's wife afore seven this morning. She's bed-fast, but she were raving and raging to know where her dunner-headed brute of a chap was, as if I'd to keep him—as if he were fit to be ruled by me. The d—d fool, who has put his foot in all our plans! And I've walked my feet sore wi' going about for to see men who wouldn't be seen, now the law is raised against us. And I were sore-hearted, too, which is worse than sore-footed; and if I did see a friend who ossed to treat me, I never knew hoo lay a-dying here. Bess, lass, thou'd believe me, thou wouldst—wouldstn't thou?" turning to the poor dumb form with wild appeal.

"I am sure," said Margaret, "I am sure you did not know; it was quite sudden. But, now, you see, it would be different; you do know; you do see her lying there; you hear what she said with her last breath. You will not go?"

No answer. In fact, where was he to look for comfort?

"Come home with me," she said at last, with a bold venture, half trembling at her own proposal as she made it. "At least you shall have some comfortable food, which I'm sure you need."

"Yo'r father's a parson?" he asked, with a sudden turn in his ideas.

"He was," said Margaret, shortly.

"I'll go and take a dish o' tea with him, since yo've asked me. I've many a thing I often wished to say to a parson, and I'm not particular as to whether he's preaching now, or not."

Margaret was perplexed; his drinking tea with her father, who would be totally unprepared for his visitor—her mother so ill—seemed utterly out of the question; and yet, if she drew back now, it would be worse than ever—sure to drive him to the gin-shop. She thought that if she could only get him to their own house, it was so great a step gained that she would trust to the chapter of accidents for the next.

"Good-bye, ou'd wench! We've parted company at last, we have! But thou'st been a blessin' to thy father ever sin' thou wert born. Bless thy white lips, lass,—they've a smile on 'em now! and I'm glad to see it once again, though I'm lone and forlorn for evermore."

He stooped down and fondly kissed his daughter; covered up her face, and turned to follow Margaret. She had hastily gone downstairs to tell Mary of the arrangement; to say it was the only place she could think of to keep him from the gin-palace; to urge Mary to come too, for her heart smote her at the idea of leaving the poor affectionate girl alone. But Mary had friends amongst the neighbours, she said, who would come in and sit a bit with her; it was all right; but father—

He was there by them or she would have spoken more. He had shaken off his emotion, as if he was ashamed of having ever given way to it; and had even o'erleaped himself so much that he assumed a sort of bitter mirth, like the crackling of thorns under a pot.

"I'm going to take my tea wi' her father, I am."

But he slouched his cap low down over his brow as he went out into the street, and looked neither to the right nor to the left, while he tramped along by Margaret's side; he feared being upset by the words, still more the looks, of sympathising neighbours. So he and Margaret walked in silence.

As he got near the street in which she lived, he looked down at his clothes, his hands, his shoes.

"I should m'appen ha' cleaned mysel', first."

It certainly would have been desirable, but Margaret assured him he should be allowed to go into the yard, and have soap and towel provided; she would not let him slip out of her hands just then.

While he followed the house-servant along the passage, and through the kitchen, stepping cautiously on every dark mark in the pattern of the oil-cloth, in order to conceal his dirty foot-prints, Margaret ran upstairs. She met Dixon on the landing.

"How is mamma?—where is papa?"

Missus was tired, and gone into her own room. She had wanted to go to bed, but Dixon had persuaded her to lie down on the sofa, and have her tea brought to her there; it would be better than getting restless by being too long in bed.

So far, so good. But where was Mr. Hale? In the drawing-room. Margaret went in half breathless with the hurried story she had to tell. Of course, she told it incompletely; and her father was rather "taken aback" by the idea of the drunken weaver awaiting him in his quiet study, with whom he was expected to drink tea, and on whose behalf Margaret was anxiously pleading. The meek, kind-hearted Mr. Hale would have readily tried to console him in his grief, but, unluckily, the point Margaret dwelt upon most forcibly was the fact of his having been drinking, and her having brought him home with her as a last expedient to keep him from the gin-shop. One little event had come out of another so naturally that Margaret was hardly conscious of what she had done, till she saw the slight look of repugnance on her father's face.

"Oh, papa! he really is a man you will not dislike—if you won't be shocked to begin with."

"But, Margaret, to bring a drunken man home—and your mother so ill!"

Margaret's countenance fell. "I am sorry, papa. He is very quiet—he is not tipsy at all. He was only rather strange at first, but that might be the shock of poor Bessy's death." Margaret's eyes filled with tears. Mr. Hale took hold of her sweet pleading face in both his hands, and kissed her forehead.

"It is all right,

"Some wishes crossed my mind and dimly cheered it,
And one or two poor melancholy pleasures,
Each in the pale unwarming light of hope,
Silvering its flimsy wing, flew silent by—
Moths in the moonbeam!"

The following morning delivered a letter to Margaret from Edith. It was affectionate and scattered, much like its author, yet the warmth of the sentiment charmed Margaret's own generous heart, and she had grown accustomed to Edith's lack of sequence, failing to notice the disjointed nature of the writing. The message read:

"Oh, Margaret, it is worth a journey from England to see my boy! He is a superb little fellow, especially in his caps, and most especially in the one you sent him, you good, dainty-fingered, persevering little lady! Having made all the mothers here envious, I want to show him to somebody new, and hear a fresh set of admiring expressions; perhaps, that's all the reason; perhaps it is not—nay, possibly, there is just a little cousinly love mixed with it; but I do want you so much to come here, Margaret! I'm sure it would be the very best thing for Aunt Hale's health; everybody here is young and well, and our skies are always blue, and our sun always shines, and the band plays deliciously from morning till night; and, to come back to the burden of my ditty, my baby always smiles. I am constantly wanting you to draw him from me, Margaret. It does not signify what he is doing; that very thing is prettiest, gracefullest, best. I think I love him a great deal better than my husband, who is getting stout, and grumpy—what he calls 'busy.' No! he is not. He has just come in with news of such a charming picnic, given by the officers of the Hazard, at anchor in the bay below. Because he has brought in such a pleasant piece of news, I retract all I said just now. Did not somebody burn his hand for having said or done something he was sorry for? Well, I can't burn mine, because it would hurt me, and the scar would be ugly; but I'll retract all I said as fast as I can. Cosmo is quite as great a darling as baby, and not a bit stout, and as ungrumpy as ever husband was; only, sometimes he is very, very busy. I may say that without love—wifely duty—where was I?—I had something very particular to say, I know, once. Oh, it is this—Dearest Margaret!—you must come and see me; it would do Aunt Hale good, as I said before. Get the doctor to order it for her. Tell him that it's the smoke of Milton that does her harm. I have no doubt it is that, really. Three months (you must not come for less) of this delicious climate—all sunshine, and grapes as common as blackberries, would quite cure her. I don't ask my uncle"—(Here the letter became more constrained, and better written. Mr. Hale was in the corner, like a naughty child, for having given up his living.)—"because, I dare say, he disapproves of war, soldiers, and bands of music; at least, I know that many Dissenters are members of the Peace Society, and I am afraid he would not like to come; but, if he would, dear, pray say that Cosmo and I will do our best to make him happy; and I'll hide up Cosmo's red coat and sword, and make the band play all sorts of grave, solemn things; or, if they do play pomps and vanities, it shall be in double slow time. Dear Margaret, if he would like to accompany you and Aunt Hale, we will try and make it pleasant, though I'm rather afraid of any one who has done anything for conscience' sake. You never did, I hope. Tell Aunt Hale not to bring any warm clothes, though I'm afraid it will be late in the year before you can come. But you have no idea of the heat here! I tried to wear my great beauty Indian shawl at a picnic. I kept myself up with proverbs as long as I could; 'Pride must abide,'—and such wholesome pieces of pith; but it was no use. I was like mamma's little dog Tiny with an elephant's trappings on; smothered, hidden, killed with my finery; so I made it into a capital carpet for us all to sit down upon. Here's this boy of mine, Margaret—if you don't pack up your things as soon as you get this letter, and come straight off to see him, I shall think you're descended from King Herod!"

Margaret longed for a day of Edith's life—her freedom from care, her cheerful home, her sunny skies. If a wish could have transported her, she would have gone off; just for one day. She yearned for the strength which such a change would give—even for a few hours to be in the midst of that bright life, and to feel young again. Not yet twenty! and she had had to bear up against such hard pressure that she felt quite old. That was her first feeling after reading Edith's letter. Then she read it again, and, forgetting herself, was amused at its likeness to Edith's self, and was laughing merrily over it when Mrs. Hale came into the drawing-room, leaning on Dixon's arm. Margaret flew to adjust the pillows. Her mother seemed more than usually feeble.

"What were you laughing at, Margaret?" asked she, as soon as she had recovered from the exertion of settling herself on the sofa.

"A letter I have had this morning from Edith. Shall I read it to you, mamma?"

She read it aloud, and for a time it seemed to interest her mother, who kept wondering what name Edith had given to her boy, and suggesting all probable names, and all possible reasons why each and all of these names should be given. Into the very midst of these wonders Mr. Thornton came, bringing another offering of fruit for Mrs. Hale. He could not—say rather, he would not—deny himself the chance of the pleasure of seeing Margaret. He had no end in this but the present gratification. It was the sturdy wilfulness of a man usually most reasonable and self-controlled. He entered the room, taking in at a glance the fact of Margaret's presence; but after the first cold distant bow, he never seemed to let his eyes fall on her again. He only stayed to present his peaches—to speak some gentle kindly words—and then his cold offended eyes met Margaret's with a grave farewell, as he left the room. She sat down silent and pale.

"Do you know, Margaret, I really begin to like Mr. Thornton."

No answer at first. Then Margaret forced out an icy "Do you?"

"Yes! I think he is really getting quite polished in his manners."

Margaret's voice was more in order now. She replied, "He is very kind and attentive—there is no doubt of that."

"I wonder Mrs. Thornton never calls. She must know I am ill, because of the water-bed."

"I dare say, she hears how you are from her son."

"Still, I should like to see her. You have so few friends here, Margaret."

Margaret felt what was in her mother's thoughts—a tender craving to bespeak the kindness of some woman towards the daughter that might soon be left motherless. But she could not speak.

"Do you think," said Mrs. Hale, after a pause, "that you could go and ask Mrs. Thornton to come and see me? Only once—I don't want to be troublesome."

"I will do anything, if you wish it, mamma—but if—but when Frederick comes——"

"Ah, to be sure! we must keep our doors shut—we must let no one in. I hardly know whether I dare wish him to come or not. Sometimes I think I would rather not. Sometimes I have such frightful dreams about him."

"Oh, mamma! we'll take good care. I will put my arm in the bolt sooner than he should come to the slightest harm. Trust the care of him to me, mamma. I will watch over him like a lioness over her young."

"When can we hear from him?"

"Not for a week yet, certainly—perhaps more."

"We must send Martha away in good time. It would never do to have her here when he comes, and then send her off in a hurry."

"Dixon is sure to remind us of that. I was thinking that, if we wanted any help in the house while he is here, we could perhaps get Mary Higgins. She is very slack of work, and is a good girl, and would take pains to do her best, I am sure, and would sleep at home, and need never come upstairs, so as to know who is in the house."

"As you please. As Dixon pleases. But, Margaret, don't get to use these horrid Milton words. 'Slack of work:' it is a provincialism. What will your aunt Shaw say, if she hears you use it on her return?"

"Oh, mamma! don't try and make a bugbear of aunt Shaw," said Margaret, laughing. "Edith picked up all sorts of military slang from Captain Lennox, and aunt Shaw never took any notice of it."

"But yours is factory slang."

"And if I live in a factory town, I must speak factory language when I want it. Why, mamma, I could astonish you with a great many words you never heard in your life. I don't believe you know what a knobstick is."

"Not I, child. I only know it has a very vulgar sound; and I don't want to hear you using it."

"Very well, dearest mother, I won't. Only I shall have to use a whole explanatory sentence instead."

"I don't like this Milton," said Mrs. Hale. "Edith is right enough in saying it's the smoke that has made me so ill."

Margaret started up as her mother said this. Her father had just entered the room, and she was most anxious that the faint impression she had seen on his mind that the Milton air had injured her mother's health, should not be deepened—should not receive any confirmation. She could not tell whether he had heard what Mrs. Hale had said or not; but she began speaking hurriedly of other things, unaware that Mr. Thornton was following him.

"Mamma is accusing me of having picked up a great deal of vulgarity since we came to Milton."

The "vulgarity" Margaret spoke of, referred purely to the use of local words, and the expression arose out of the conversation they had just been holding. But Mr. Thornton's brow darkened; and Margaret suddenly felt how her speech might be misunderstood by him; so, in the natural sweet desire to avoid giving unnecessary pain, she forced herself to go forwards with a little greeting, and continue what she was saying, addressing herself to him expressly.

"Now, Mr. Thornton, though 'knobstick' has not a very pretty sound, is it not expressive? Could I do without it, in speaking of the thing it represents? If using local words is vulgar, I was very vulgar in the Forest,—was I not, mamma?"

It was unusual with Margaret to obtrude her own subject of conversation on others; but, in this case, she was so anxious to prevent Mr. Thornton from feeling annoyance at the words he had accidentally overheard, that it was not until she had done speaking that she coloured all over with consciousness, more especially as Mr. Thornton seemed hardly to understand the exact gist or bearing of what she was saying, but passed her by, with a cold reserve of ceremonious movement, to speak to Mrs. Hale.

The sight of him reminded her of the wish to see his mother, and commend Margaret to her care. Margaret, sitting in burning silence, vexed and ashamed of her difficulty in keeping her right place, and her calm unconsciousness of heart, when Mr. Thornton was by, heard her mother's slow entreaty that Mrs. Thornton would come and see her; see her soon; to-morrow, if it were possible. Mr. Thornton promised that she should—conversed a little, and then took his leave; and Margaret's movements and voice seemed at once released from some invisible chains. He never looked at her; and yet, the careful avoidance of his eyes betokened that in some way he knew exactly where, if they fell by chance, they would rest on her. If she spoke, he gave no sign of attention, and yet his next speech to any one else was modified by what she had said; sometimes there was an express answer to what she had remarked, but given to another person as though unsuggested by her. It was not the bad manners of ignorance; it was the wilful bad manners arising from deep offence. It was wilful at the time; repented of afterwards. But no deep plan, no careful cunning could have stood him in such good stead. Margaret thought about him more than she had ever done before; not with any tinge of what is called love, but with regret that she had wounded him so deeply,—and with a gentle, patient striving to return to their former position of antagonistic friendship; for a friend's position was what she found that he had held in her regard, as well as in that of the rest of the family. There was a pretty humility in her behaviour to him, as if mutely apologising for the over-strong words which were the reaction from the deeds of the day of the riot.

But he resented those words bitterly. They rang in his ears; and he was proud of the sense of justice which made him go in every kindness he could offer to her parents. He exulted in the power he showed in compelling himself to face her, whenever he could think of any action which might give her father or mother pleasure. He thought that he disliked seeing one who had mortified him so keenly; but he was mistaken. It was a stinging pleasure to be in the room with her, and feel her presence. But he was no great analyser of his own motives and was mistaken, as I have said.

"The saddest birds a season find to sing."

"Never to fold the robe o'er secret pain,
Never, weighed down by memory's clouds again,
To bow thy head! Thou art gone home!"

The following morning, Mrs. Thornton visited Mrs. Hale. The invalid was far worse. During the night, one of those sudden, terrifying leaps toward death had occurred, leaving her family stunned by the gray, sunken hollow of her features after twelve hours of agony. Mrs. Thornton, who had not seen her for weeks, felt a sudden softening within her. She had come because her son had begged it as a personal favor, yet she carried the proud, bitter defenses of her nature against the Hale family, of whom Margaret was a part. She doubted the reality of Mrs. Hale's illness; she suspected it was merely a fleeting fancy on the lady's part to escape her daily duties. She told her son she wished they had never approached the place, that he should never have known them, and that no such useless tongues as Latin and Greek had ever been invented. He bore this invective silently, but when she finished her tirade against the dead languages, he calmly reiterated his wish that she visit Mrs. Hale at the appointed hour, deeming it most convenient for the invalid. Mrs. Thornton submitted with as poor grace as she could muster, secretly liking her son the more for his insistence, while internally exaggerating his extraordinary goodness in persevering with the Hales.

Her view of his goodness as verging on weakness, combined with her contempt for Mr. and Mrs. Hale and her positive dislike for Margaret, filled her mind until she was struck into nothingness by the dark shadow of the Angel of Death. There lay Mrs. Hale—a mother, like herself, yet much younger—on a bed offering no sign of hope for rising again. There was no variety of light and shade in that darkened room; no power of action, scarcely a change of movement, only faint alternations of whispered sound and studious silence. Yet that monotonous life seemed almost too much to bear. When Mrs. Thornton, strong and prosperous with life, entered, Mrs. Hale lay still, though conscious of her presence. She did not open her eyes for a minute or two. Heavy moisture of tears stood on her eyelashes before she looked up; then, with her hand groping feebly over the bedclothes for the touch of Mrs. Thornton's large, firm fingers, she whispered, barely audible. Mrs. Thornton had to stoop from her erectness to listen.

"Margaret—you have a daughter—my sister is in Italy. My child will be without a mother; in a strange place—if I die—will you..."

Her filmy, wandering eyes fixed themselves with an intensity of wistfulness on Mrs. Thornton's face. For a moment, there was no change in its rigidness; it was stern and unmoved. Nay, but for the eyes of the sick woman growing dim with slow-gathering tears, a dark cloud might have crossed the cold features. It was no thought of her son or her living daughter Fanny that stirred her heart at last, but a sudden remembrance, suggested by something in the arrangement of the room, of a little daughter dead in infancy long years ago. Like a sudden sunbeam, this memory melted the icy crust behind which lay a real, tender woman.

"You wish me to be a friend to Miss Hale," said Mrs. Thornton in her measured voice, one that would not soften with her heart but came out distinct and clear.

Mrs. Hale, her eyes still fixed on Mrs. Thornton's face, pressed the hand lying below hers on the coverlet. She could not speak. Mrs. Thornton sighed, "I will be a true friend, if circumstances require it. Not a tender friend. That I cannot be"—("to her," she was on the point of adding, but she relented at the sight of that poor, anxious face.)—"It is not my nature to show affection even where I feel it, nor do I volunteer advice in general. Still, at your request—if it will be any comfort to you, I will promise you." Then came a pause. Mrs. Thornton was too conscientious to promise what she did not mean to perform; and to perform anything in the way of kindness on behalf of Margaret, more disliked at this moment than ever, was difficult; almost impossible.

"I promise," said she, with grave severity, which, after all, inspired the dying woman with faith as in something more stable than life itself—flickering, flittering, wavering life! "I promise that in any difficulty in which Miss Hale..."

"Call her Margaret!" gasped Mrs. Hale.

"In which she comes to me for help, I will help her with every power I have, as if she were my own daughter. I also promise that if ever I see her doing what I think is wrong..."

"But Margaret never does wrong—not wilfully wrong," pleaded Mrs. Hale.

Mrs. Thornton went on as before, as if she had not heard: "If ever I see her doing what I believe to be wrong—such wrong not touching me or mine, in which case I might be supposed to have an interested motive—I will tell her of it, faithfully and plainly, as I should wish my own daughter to be told."

There was a long pause. Mrs. Hale felt that this promise did not include all; and yet it was much. It had reservations in it which she did not understand; but then she was weak, dizzy, and tired. Mrs. Thornton reviewed all the probable cases in which she had pledged herself to act. She had a fierce pleasure in the idea of telling Margaret unwelcome truths, in the shape of performing duty. Mrs. Hale began to speak:

"I thank you. I pray God to bless you. I shall never see you again in this world. But my last words are, I thank you for your promise of kindness to my child."

"Not kindness!" testified Mrs. Thornton, ungraciously truthful to the last. But having eased her conscience by saying these words, she was not sorry that they were not heard. She pressed Mrs. Hale's soft, languid hand; and rose up and went her way out of the house without seeing a creature.

During the time Mrs. Thornton had this interview with Mrs. Hale, Margaret and Dixon were laying their heads together, consulting how they should keep Frederick's coming a profound secret to all outside the house. A letter from him might now be expected any day; and he would assuredly follow quickly on its heels. Martha must be sent away on her holiday; Dixon must keep stern guard on the front door, admitting only the few visitors that ever came into Mr. Hale's room downstairs—Mrs. Hale's extreme illness giving her a good excuse for this. If Mary Higgins was required as a help to Dixon in the kitchen, she was to hear and see as little of Frederick as possible; and he was, if necessary, to be spoken of to her under the name of Mr. Dickinson. But her sluggish and incurious nature was the greatest safeguard of all.

They resolved that Martha should leave them that very afternoon for this visit to her mother. Margaret wished that she had been sent away on the previous day, as she fancied it might be thought strange to give a servant a holiday when her mistress's state required so much attendance.

Poor Margaret! All that afternoon she had to act the part of a Roman daughter, giving strength out of her own scanty stock to her father. Mr. Hale would hope, would not despair, between the attacks of his wife's malady; he buoyed himself up in every respite from her pain, and believed that it was the beginning of ultimate recovery. And so, when the paroxysms came on, each more severe than the last, they were fresh agonies, and greater disappointments to him. This afternoon, he sat in the drawing-room, unable to bear the solitude of his study, or to employ himself in any way. He buried his head in his arms, which lay folded on the table. Margaret's heart ached to see him; yet, as he did not speak, she did not like to volunteer any attempt at comfort. Martha was gone. Dixon sat with Mrs. Hale while she slept. The house was very still and quiet, and darkness came on without any movement to procure candles. Margaret sat at the window, looking out at the lamps and the street, but seeing nothing—only alive to her father's heavy sighs. She did not like to go down for lights, lest the tacit restraint of her presence being withdrawn, he might give way to more violent emotion without her being at hand to comfort him. Yet she was just thinking that she ought to go and see after the well-doing of the kitchen fire, which there was nobody but herself to attend to, when she heard the muffled door-bell ring with so violent a pull that the wires jingled all through the house, though the positive sound was not great. She started up, passed her father, who had never moved at the veiled, dull sound, returned, and kissed him tenderly. And still he never moved, nor took any notice of her fond embrace. Then she went down softly, through the dark, to the door. Dixon would have put the chain on before she opened it, but Margaret had not a thought of fear in her pre-occupied mind. A man's tall figure stood between her and the luminous street. He was looking away; but at the sound of the latch he turned quickly round.

"Is this Mr. Hale's?" said he, in a clear, full, delicate voice.

Margaret trembled all over; at first she did not answer. In a moment she sighed out, "Frederick!" and stretched out both her hands to catch his, and draw him in.

"Oh, Margaret!" said he, holding her off by her shoulders after they had kissed each other, as if even in that darkness he could see her face and read in its expression a quicker answer to his question than words could give.

"My mother! is she alive!"

"Yes, she is alive, dear, dear brother! She—as ill as she can be she is; but alive! She is alive!"

"Thank God!" said he.

"Papa is utterly prostrate with this great grief."

"You expect me, don't you?"

"No, we have had no letter."

"Then I have come before it. But my mother knows I am coming."

"Oh! we all knew you would come. But wait a little! Step in here. Give me your hand. What is this? Oh! your carpet-bag. Dixon has shut the shutters; but this is papa's study, and I can take you to a chair to rest yourself for a few minutes; while I go and tell him."

She groped her way to the taper and the lucifer matches. She suddenly felt shy when the little feeble light made them visible. All she could see was that her brother's face was unusually dark in complexion, and she caught the stealthy look of a pair of remarkably long-cut blue eyes that suddenly twinkled up with a droll consciousness of their mutual purpose of inspecting each other. But though the brother and sister had an instant of sympathy in their reciprocal glances, they did not exchange a word; only Margaret felt sure that she should like her brother as a companion as much as she already loved him as a near relation. Her heart was wonderfully lighter as she went upstairs; the sorrow was no less in reality, but it became less oppressive from having someone in precisely the same relation to it as that in which she stood. Not her father's desponding attitude had power to damp her now. He lay across the table, helpless as ever; but she had the spell by which to rouse him. She used it perhaps too violently in her own great relief.

"Papa," said she, throwing her arms fondly round his neck, pulling his weary head up in fact with her gentle violence till it rested in her arms, and she could look into his eyes and let them gain strength and assurance from hers.

"Papa! guess who is here!"

He looked at her; she saw the idea of the truth glimmer into their filmy sadness and be dismissed thence as a wild imagination.

He threw himself forward and hid his face once more in his stretched-out arms, resting upon the table as heretofore. She heard him whisper; she bent tenderly down to listen. "I don't know; don't tell me it is Frederick—not Frederick. I cannot bear it,—I am too weak. And his mother is dying!"

He began to cry and wail like a child. It was so different to all which Margaret had hoped and expected that she turned sick with disappointment and was silent for an instant. Then she spoke again—very differently—not so exultingly, far more tenderly and carefully.

"Papa, it is Frederick! Think of mamma, how glad she will be! And oh, for her sake, how glad we ought to be! For his sake, too,—our poor, poor boy!"

Her father did not change his attitude, but he seemed to be trying to understand the fact.

"Where is he?" asked he at last, his face still hidden in his prostrate arms.

"In your study, quite alone. I lighted the taper, and ran up to tell you. He is quite alone, and will be wondering why—"

"I will go to him," broke in her father; and he lifted himself up and leant on her arm as on that of a guide.

Margaret led him to the study door, but her spirits were so agitated that she felt she could not bear to see the meeting. She turned away, ran upstairs, and cried most heartily. It was the first time she had dared to allow herself this relief for days. The strain had been terrible, as she now felt. But Frederick was come! He, the one precious brother, was there, safe, amongst them again! She could hardly believe it. She stopped her crying and opened her bedroom door. She heard no sound of voices, and almost feared she might have dreamt. She went downstairs and listened at the study door. She heard the buzz of voices; and that was enough. She went into the kitchen, stirred up the fire, lighted the house, and prepared for the wanderer's refreshment. How fortunate it was that her mother slept! She knew that she did, from the candle

The October morning arrived, not with the gentle, silvery mist of the countryside that clears before the sun to reveal vibrant colors, but with the heavy, oppressive fog of Milton. The sun struggled to pierce the gloom, casting only long, dusky shadows across the streets. Inside the Hale household, the air was thick with a different kind of chill. Margaret moved through the rooms with a languid, mechanical grace, assisting Dixon in preparing the house for the coming days. Tears constantly blurred her vision, yet she found no time to weep openly. Her father and brother were consumed by grief; she was the anchor required to keep them afloat. Every detail of the funeral—from the arrangements to the guests—fell upon her shoulders.

As the fire crackled brightly and the tea-kettle sang its familiar song, Margaret paused to survey the room one last time before summoning Mr. Hale and Frederick. She insisted on a cheerful appearance, a stark contrast to the desolation in her heart. Kneeling by the sofa, she hid her face in the cushions, muffling her sobs until Dixon's hand touched her shoulder.

"Come, Miss Hale," Dixon urged, her voice unusually tender. "You must not give way. Who else is fit to direct things? The funeral arrangements, the guests, the location—it is all to be settled. Master Frederick is beside himself with weeping, and Master Hale is lost in his sorrow. Death comes to us all, my dear, but you are fortunate not to have lost a friend until now."

Perhaps Dixon was right, yet this loss felt unique, incomparable to any other tragedy. Finding no comfort in the servant's words, Margaret was nonetheless moved by Dixon's unexpected kindness. Rising with a forced smile, she thanked her and went to announce that breakfast was ready.

Mr. Hale entered as if in a trance, his movements unconscious, his mind elsewhere. Frederick followed, feigning cheerfulness until he grasped Margaret's hand and broke down in tears. Throughout the meal, Margaret spoke of trivialities, desperate to distract them from the memory of their last shared meal, which had been haunted by the strained silence of waiting for news from the sickroom.

Afterward, Margaret approached her father regarding the funeral plans. He nodded vacantly, agreeing to contradictory suggestions, offering no real guidance. As she prepared to leave the room to consult Dixon, Mr. Hale stopped her.

"Ask Mr. Bell," he said, his voice hollow. "Mr. Bell of Oxford?"

"Yes," Margaret replied, recognizing the connection. "He was your groom's-man."

"I will write today," she promised. Mr. Hale slumped back into his listlessness.

By evening, Dixon returned with news that sent a fresh wave of anxiety through Margaret. She had managed to rouse her master, Mr. Hale, from his stupor by informing him that it was unsafe for Frederick to remain. She had encountered a man named Young Leonards, a former draper's son from Southampton known for his reckless behavior and a stint aboard the *Orion* during the mutiny.

"He was a scamp," Dixon recounted, her voice rising with indignation. "I called out his name, and he recognized me. He mocked me, asking if I was still 'Miss Dixon.' I told him I preferred to be addressed as a married woman, or at least one who had better prospects than helping old Mr. Leonards give George a good rating. He turned the conversation to Frederick, calling him a disgrace and mentioning the hundred-pound reward for his capture. He implied he could offer Frederick patronage if he wished to turn his life around, a suggestion that made my blood boil."

"But you didn't tell him where we are?" Margaret asked, relieved.

"No," Dixon admitted. "But he threatened to trap Lieutenant Hale and offered to share the reward if I helped him. He jumped on a bus, leering at me with a wicked smile, thinking he had the last word."

Margaret felt a surge of dread. "I must tell Frederick. What did he look like?"

"A bad-looking fellow," Dixon described. "Red whiskers, dressed in fustian like a laborer, despite claiming a confidential situation."

Frederick soon entered, his brightness dimmed but the violent edge of his grief subsiding. He kissed Margaret's forehead and urged her to rest on the sofa. As she lay down, covered by a shawl, her brother sat beside her, and they spoke in hushed tones. Margaret relayed Dixon's account. Frederick's reaction was one of stunned dismay.

"I'd rather anyone had the hundred pounds than that rascal," he declared. "If he knew I was within twenty miles, he'd ferret me out to settle old scores. I wish Dixon could be given a pension; she deserves it."

Mr. Hale, overhearing their conversation, stepped forward, trembling. "My boy, you must go. It is very bad, but you must. You have done all you could."

"Oh, papa, must he go?" Margaret pleaded, though she knew the necessity.

"I declare, I have a mind to face it out," Frederick said, his voice bitter. "If I could only gather my evidence! I cannot endure being in the power of such a blackguard. It has a certain charm, this stolen visit, like the forbidden pleasures the Frenchwomen speak of."

Margaret recalled his childhood disgrace for stealing apples, noting that his feelings had not changed much since then. Mr. Hale confirmed Frederick's departure was mandatory.

Before leaving, Frederick revealed a secret that shifted the atmosphere. He spoke of Dolores Barbour, a Roman Catholic girl of nineteen whom he intended to marry. He confessed that he himself was inclined toward Catholicism, a fact that explained his earlier silence regarding his father's religious struggles. He proposed that if they could not clear his name, they might flee to Spain.

"No more removals," Mr. Hale stated firmly. "One has cost me my wife. She will be here, and here I will stay."

Margaret, however, seized upon the opportunity to help. "For Dolores' sake, Fred, you must try to clear yourself. Even if the charge of mutiny is true, you must show that your disobedience was an act of heroism, not crime. People need to know the truth."

Frederick argued that a court-martial was merely a display of authority, where evidence counted for little. Yet, Margaret persisted, suggesting they consult a lawyer. She named Mr. Henry Lennox, the husband of her friend Edith.

"He must go to London tomorrow," Margaret decided. "I will write a note to Mr. Lennox at his chambers in the Temple. You can leave a list of names from the *Orion* with him."

Frederick agreed, though he groaned at the thought of parting. He assured her he could sail from anywhere and would not stay in London long. As Margaret wrote the note, Frederick watched over her shoulder. Before she could finish reviewing it, he snatched the paper, tucked it into his pocket-book, and produced a lock of black hair that had fallen from it. His eyes glowed with pleasure as he showed it to her.

"Now you would like to see that, wouldn't you?" he teased. "No! You must wait until you see her herself. She is too perfect to be known by fragments. No mean brick shall be a specimen of the building of my palace."

"What! remain to be denounced—dragged, it may be, in chains?" Werner's words echoed in the silence of the Hale household, a grim prophecy hanging over the days that followed.

For the entire next day, the three sat together in a heavy, suspended quiet. Mr. Hale spoke only when his children demanded answers, forcing himself into the present tense as if the past had ceased to exist. Frederick's grief had receded into a stunned silence; the initial paroxysm of emotion had passed, leaving behind a man ashamed of his own collapse. Yet, beneath his composure lay a sorrow for his lost mother so profound it would haunt him for the rest of his life, a pain he deemed too sacred to utter aloud. Margaret, initially less volatile, now bore a deeper, more consuming suffering. Tears came easily to her, and even in conversation, her manner carried a mournful tenderness that intensified whenever her gaze met Frederick's, reminding her of his imminent departure. She welcomed his escape for her father's sake, despite her own heart breaking at the thought of losing him.

The terror gripping Mr. Hale had grown since Mrs. Hale's death, fed by his exclusive dwelling upon the danger. Every unusual sound made him jump; he found comfort only when Frederick remained unseen by anyone entering the room. As evening approached, his anxiety crystallized into a specific command.

"You will go with Frederick to the station, Margaret? I must know he is safely off. Promise me you will bring word that he is clear of Milton?"

"Certainly," Margaret replied softly. "I shall like it, if you won't be lonely without me, papa."

"No, no!" Mr. Hale insisted, his voice tight. "Unless you tell me you've seen him off, I shall constantly imagine someone has recognized him, that he has been stopped. Go to the Outwood station. It is nearly as close, yet far fewer people congregate there. Take a cab. The risk of him being seen is diminished. What time is your train, Fred?"

"Ten minutes past six. Very nearly dark."

"So, what will you do, Margaret?"

"Oh, I can manage," she assured him, trying to summon a strength she did not fully feel. "I am becoming braver, harder. The road home is well-lit, even if it grows dark. I was out last week much later."

Margaret felt a profound relief when the parting finally arrived—a separation from both the dead mother and the living father. She hurried Frederick into the cab to abbreviate a scene that would wound her father further. However, fate, in its capricious way, intervened. Due to a combination of her father's haste and a common error in the Railway Guide regarding arrival times at minor stations, they discovered upon reaching Outwood that they possessed nearly twenty minutes to spare. The booking office was closed; tickets could not be purchased. Instead, they descended the steps to the ground level beside the tracks. A broad path of cinders cut diagonally across a field adjacent to the carriage road, and there they paced back and forth, filling the void of time with silence.

Margaret's hand rested in Frederick's arm; he held it with an affectionate grip.

"Margaret," he began, his voice low and urgent, "I intend to consult Mr. Lennox regarding the possibility of exculpating myself. This is for your sake, more than for mine. I cannot bear the thought of your lonely position should anything happen to my father. He is terribly shaken, changed. I wish you could persuade him to consider the Cadiz plan. What could you do if he were taken away? We are curiously bereft of relations."

Margaret struggled to suppress tears at the tender anxiety with which he outlined a future she feared was already inevitable. The cares of the last few months had ravaged Mr. Hale. Yet, she rallied, attempting to steer the conversation toward the present.

"There have been such strange, unexpected changes in my life during these last two years," she said, her voice trembling slightly, "that I feel it is not worth while to calculate too closely what I should do if a future event takes place. I try to think only upon the present."

She paused. They stood still on the field side of the stile leading to the road, bathed in the golden light of the setting sun. Frederick held her hand, his eyes searching hers with wistful anxiety, reading a depth of care and trouble that her words failed to convey.

"We shall write often," she continued, her resolve hardening. "And I promise—for I see it will set your mind at ease—to tell you every worry I endure. Papa is—" She started, a barely perceptible jerk, but Frederick felt the sudden tension in her hand. He turned his full face toward the road, where a horseman was slowly riding, passing precisely where they stood. Margaret bowed stiffly; the rider returned the gesture with a cold, formal nod.

"Who is that?" Frederick asked, almost before the figure was out of earshot.

"Mr. Thornton," Margaret replied, her face flushing slightly, her spirit drooping. "You saw him before, you know."

"Only his back. He is an unprepossessing-looking fellow. What a scowl he has!"

"Something has happened to vex him," Margaret apologized gently. "You would not have thought him unprepossessing if you had seen him with mamma."

"I fancy it is time to go and take my ticket. If I had known how dark it would be, we wouldn't have sent back the cab, Margaret."

"Oh, don't fidget about that," she urged, trying to mask her own panic. "I can take a cab here, or return by the railway when I should have shops and people and lamps all the way from Milton. Don't think of me; take care of yourself. I am sick with the thought that Leonards may be in the same train with you. Look well into the carriage before you get in."

They returned to the station. Margaret insisted on entering the bright glare of the gas lamps to purchase her ticket. Several idle-looking young men lounged near the station-master. Margaret thought she recognized the face of one of them and returned a proud look of offended dignity for his undisguised, impertinent stare. She moved quickly to her brother, who stood outside, seizing his arm. "Have you got your bag? Let us walk about here on the platform," she said, her voice flurried, her bravery leaking away faster than she cared to admit.

A step followed them along the flagstones, halting as they halted, gazing down the line and listening to the whistle of the approaching train. They did not speak; their hearts were too full. Another moment, and the train would be here; a minute more, and he would be gone. Margaret almost repented the urgency with which she had urged him toward London; it seemed to throw more chances of detection in his path. If he had sailed for Spain via Liverpool, he might have been safe within hours.

Frederick turned, facing the lamp where the gas flared in vivid anticipation of the train's arrival. A man dressed as a railway porter stepped forward—a bad-looking fellow who appeared drunk into a state of brutality, though his senses seemed perfectly sharp.

"By your leave, miss!" he barked, shoving Margaret roughly aside and seizing Frederick by the collar. "Your name is Hale, I believe?"

In an instant, how Margaret did not see, for the world danced before her eyes, Frederick executed a sudden, desperate maneuver. He tripped the man, sending him crashing from the height of the platform onto the soft ground beside the railroad. He lay there, stunned.

"Run, run!" Margaret gasped. "The train is here. Was it Leonards? Oh, run! I will carry your bag."

She grabbed his arm, pushing him forward with all her feeble strength. A door swung open in a carriage; he leaped inside. As he leaned out to cry, "God bless you, Margaret!" the train surged past her, leaving her standing alone on the platform.

She was terribly sick and faint, grateful to retreat into the ladies' waiting-room and sit down for a moment. At first, she could only gasp for breath. The hurry, the sickening alarm, the nearness of the disaster—it all washed over her. Had the train not arrived at that precise moment, the man would have risen and called for assistance to arrest him. She wondered if he had gotten up, if she had seen him move, if he could have been seriously hurt. Venturing out, she found the platform brightly lit yet utterly deserted. She walked to the end and looked over the edge, somewhat fearfully. No one was there. Relief flooded her; otherwise, terrible thoughts would haunt her dreams. Even so, she trembled so violently that walking home along the lonely, dark road seemed impossible. She decided to wait for the down train to pass before taking her seat. But what if Leonards recognized her as Frederick's companion?

She peered around before venturing into the booking office to buy her ticket. Only railway officials remained, talking loudly among themselves.

"So Leonards has been drinking again!" said one, seemingly in authority. "He'll need all his boasted influence to keep his place this time."

"Where is he?" asked another. Margaret, her back turned, counted her change with trembling fingers, daring not to turn until she heard the answer.

"I don't know. He came in not five minutes ago with some long story about a fall he'd had, swearing awfully. He wanted to borrow money to go to London by the next up-train. He made all sorts of tipsy promises, but I had something else to do. I told him to go about his business, and he went off at the front door."
