"He's at the nearest vaults, I'll be bound," the first speaker declared. "Your money would have gone there too, if you'd been such a fool as to lend it."

"Catch me! I knew better what his London meant. Why, he has never paid me off that five shillings"—and so they went on.

Now, all Margaret's anxiety focused solely on the train's arrival. She hid once more in the ladies' waiting-room, imagining every noise was Leonards' step, every boisterous voice his. But no one approached her until the train drew up. She was civilly helped into a carriage by a porter, into whose face she dared not look until they were in motion. Then she saw that it was not Leonards'.

The silence that settled over the Hale household felt unnatural, a heavy, suffocating blanket following the storm of terror and noise. Mr. Hale, having ensured his daughter was attended to, retreated to his customary chair, sinking once more into his melancholy reverie. Dixon had enlisted Mary Higgins to manage the kitchen, her whispers sharp with suppressed anger, for to speak loudly while a body lay cold in the house seemed an act of irreverence. Margaret resolved to spare her father the final shock of the accident; the event had ended well, and dwelling on it served no purpose. Her only lingering fear was that Mr. Lenards might secure funds to pursue Frederick to London, but she dismissed the thought as futile. Frederick would be vigilant, and within days, he would be safe beyond England's shores.

"I suppose we shall hear from Mr. Bell tomorrow," Margaret said softly.

"Yes," her father replied, his voice flat. "I suppose so."

"If he can travel, he will likely be here by evening."

"If he cannot, I shall ask Mr. Thornton to accompany me to the funeral. I cannot go alone; I shall collapse entirely."

"Papa, do not ask Mr. Thornton," Margaret interjected, her tone impulsive. "Let me go with you."

"You? My dear, women rarely attend funerals."

"No," Margaret insisted, her eyes glistening. "Because they lack the power to control their emotions, yet feel too ashamed to display them. Poor women go, indifferent to being seen weeping. But I promise you, Papa, if you allow me, I shall be no burden. Do not have a stranger present and exclude me. If Mr. Bell cannot come, I will go. I will not press my wish against your will if he does arrive."

Mr. Bell, however, could not come. Gout had confined him. His letter was tender, expressing profound regret and citing urgent business regarding his Milton property as the cause of his absence. He hoped to visit soon, driven by a desire to comfort his old friend.

Margaret struggled mightily to dissuade her father from inviting Mr. Thornton. The night before the funeral, a formal note arrived from Mrs. Thornton, stating that, at her son's request, their carriage would attend the ceremony if it did not inconvenience the family. Margaret tossed the note onto the table.

"Oh, let us avoid such formalities," she said. "Let us go alone, Papa. They do not truly care for us; otherwise, he would have offered to walk beside us, not sent an empty carriage."

"I thought you were so averse to his presence, Margaret," Mr. Hale said, surprised.

"And I am," she admitted, her voice trembling. "I do not want him to come at all, and I dislike the idea of asking him. But this gesture feels like a mockery of mourning, and I did not expect it from him." Overwhelmed, she burst into tears. Her usual composure, her gentleness and patience, had vanished, replaced by agitation. As her father lavished tenderness upon her, her crying only intensified.

That night was terrible for Margaret, leaving her ill-prepared for the anxiety brought by a letter from Frederick. Mr. Lennox was out of town, expected no sooner than Tuesday, perhaps Monday. Consequently, Frederick decided to remain in London for a few more days. He had considered returning to Milton, tempted by the proximity, but the prospect of Mr. Bell residing in his father's house and the terrifying recognition he had suffered at the railway station compelled him to stay. Margaret was grateful her father was absent in her mother's room when she read the letter. Had he been present, her distress would have triggered a nervous alarm he could not soothe. Beyond Frederick's detention, the letter alluded to the recognition at Milton and the possibility of pursuit, chilling her blood. How would this affect her father? Margaret bitterly repented urging the consultation with Mr. Lennox. It had seemed prudent then, adding little risk, yet every subsequent event proved it a mistake. She battled her self-reproach, knowing her father's fragile state would only deepen his morbid regrets over the past.

Mr. Hale, however, was too broken to engage with such thoughts. He was consumed by the singular idea that the last visible token of his wife was to be removed from his sight. He trembled pitifully as the undertaker arranged the black drapery around him. When released, he tottered toward Margaret, his voice a whisper. "Pray for me, Margaret. I have no strength left. I cannot pray. I give her up because I must. I try to bear it; I know it is God's will. But I cannot understand why she died. Pray for me, my child, that I may find the faith to pray. It is a great strait."

In the carriage, Margaret supported him, reciting verses of holy comfort and texts of faithful resignation. Her voice never faltered, and strangely, she found strength in the act. Her father's lips moved in echo, repeating the words mechanically, a terrible struggle to achieve a resignation he lacked the power to accept.

Margaret's fortitude nearly shattered when Dixon gestured toward Nicholas Higgins and his daughter, standing apart yet deeply attentive. Nicholas wore his usual fustian, save for a strip of black cloth sewn to his hat—a mark of mourning he had never worn for his daughter Bessy. Mr. Hale saw nothing. He continued to repeat the funeral service internally, sighing twice as the service concluded, before taking Margaret's arm and mutely begging to be led away as if blind, trusting her as his guide.

Dixon sobbed openly, hiding her face, so absorbed in her grief that she failed to notice the crowd dispersing until someone spoke to her. It was Mr. Thornton. He had stood the entire time, head bowed behind a cluster of mourners, unrecognized.

"I beg your pardon," he said, his voice cool. "Can you tell me how Mr. Hale is? And Miss Hale?"

"Of course, sir. They are as expected. Master is terribly broken down. Miss Hale bears up better than likely."

Mr. Thornton preferred to hear of her suffering. A selfish pleasure stirred within him—the thought that his love might comfort her, a sensation akin to a mother's joy when an infant nestles close. Yet, this vision was miserably shattered by the memory of the young man standing with such familiar confidence near the Outwood station. The image shot through him like agony, forcing him to clench his fists to suppress the pain. So far from home, it took a monumental effort to revive his trust in Margaret's purity; the moment the effort ceased, that trust died, replaced by wild, chaotic fantasies. Here was confirmation, gnawing and miserable: she bore up better than likely. She had hope, a light so bright it illuminated her dark hours. Yes, he knew how she would love. Her soul would shine in glorious sunlight if a worthy man could win it. Even in mourning, she would rest in peaceful faith upon his sympathy. Whose sympathy? That other man's. This realization made Mr. Thornton's pale face grow doubly wan and stern.

"I suppose I may call," he said coldly. "On Mr. Hale, I mean. He will perhaps admit me after tomorrow."

He spoke as if the answer mattered little, though deep down, he longed to see the author of his pain. Though he hated Margaret at times, recalling that gentle, familiar attitude, he possessed a restless desire to renew her image in his mind, to breathe the atmosphere she inhabited. Trapped in the whirlpool of passion, he was forced to circle ever closer to the fatal center.

"I dare say, sir, master will see you," Dixon replied smoothly. "He was very sorry to have to deny you the other day, but circumstances were not agreeable just then."

For reasons unknown, Dixon never mentioned this encounter to Margaret. It may have been mere chance, but it was fate that Margaret never learned Mr. Thornton had attended her mother's funeral.

"Truth will fail thee never, never!
Though thy bark be tempest-driven,
Though each plank be rent and riven,
Truth will bear thee on for ever!"

The burden of "bearing up better than likely" pressed upon Margaret with a weight that threatened to crush her spirit. In moments of quiet, the sudden, sharp realization that she was alone in the world, bereft of her mother, would seize her throat, forcing a cry of pain despite her efforts to maintain cheerfulness before her father. Her mind was equally tormented by the silence from Frederick. The Sunday post had failed to deliver their London correspondence, and by Tuesday, the absence of a letter left her in a state of disheartened darkness. She was adrift without knowledge of his plans, a void that drove her father into a frantic restlessness. He abandoned his recent habit of lingering in one chair, pacing the room, darting out of it, and haunting the landing, opening and shutting bedroom doors with a nervous energy that tore through the house's fragile peace. Margaret found solace only in reading aloud to him, though his attention could not hold, and she felt a profound gratitude that Mr. Thornton's arrival would inevitably shift her father's focus elsewhere.

Mr. Thornton entered with a directness that spoke volumes. He approached Mr. Hale, seizing his hands and wringing them tightly, holding them for a moment in a silence that conveyed a depth of sympathy no words could match. Turning to Margaret, he found her far from "better than likely." Her stately beauty was dimmed by sleepless nights and weeping; her face bore the marks of gentle, patient sorrow, bordering on acute suffering. He had intended to greet her with the studied coldness he had practiced, yet he could not resist stepping toward her as she stood apart, rendered timid by his recent aloofness. In a voice tender enough to break her resolve, he offered the few necessary pleasantries, causing her eyes to fill with tears as she turned away to hide her emotion. She retreated to her work, sitting in a heavy, silent quiet. Mr. Thornton's heart hammered against his ribs, the memory of Outwood Lane momentarily erased. He attempted to converse with Mr. Hale, finding in the older man's presence a familiar anchor of safety and decision, a dynamic that pleased Mr. Hale deeply, as Margaret observed.

Suddenly, Dixon appeared at the door, his manner so flustered that it sent a wave of sickness through Margaret's heart. Something had happened to Frederick; she knew it with absolute certainty. Fortunately, her father and Mr. Thornton were engrossed in conversation.

"What is it, Dixon?" Margaret asked the moment she had closed the drawing-room door.

"Come this way, miss," Dixon said, ushering her into what had been Mrs. Hale's chamber, now Margaret's sanctuary since her father refused to sleep there following his wife's passing. "It's nothing, miss," he choked out, struggling for composure. "Only a police-inspector. He wishes to see you. But I dare say, it concerns nothing serious."

"Did he name—" Margaret began, her voice barely audible.

"No, miss. He merely asked if you resided here and if he might speak with you. Martha admitted him; he is currently in Master's study. I attempted to intervene, but no—it is you, miss, he requires."

Margaret remained silent until her hand rested on the study door's lock. Turning, she warned, "Ensure Papa does not descend. Mr. Thornton is with him."

The inspector was initially intimidated by the haughty demeanor she projected upon entering. There was a suppressed indignation in her gaze, restrained with such control that it radiated a superb air of disdain. She showed no surprise, no curiosity, simply waiting for the interrogation to begin. She asked no questions.

"I beg your pardon, ma'am, but my duty compels me to ask a few plain questions," the inspector stated. "A man died at the Infirmary following a fall sustained at Outwood station between five and six o'clock on Thursday evening, the twenty-sixth. Initially, the fall seemed minor, but physicians attribute the fatality to an underlying internal condition exacerbated by the deceased's habitual drinking."

Her large, dark eyes widened slightly as they locked onto his face, yet her body remained still. Her lips curved into a tighter, richer line due to the tension of her muscles, betraying a sullen defiance she did not realize she displayed. She neither flinched nor trembled, fixing him with an unblinking stare. When he paused, she encouraged him, almost sleepily, "Well—go on!"

"It is anticipated that an inquest will be held. There is slight evidence suggesting the blow, push, or scuffle that precipitated the fall was provoked by the deceased's half-intoxicated insolence toward a young lady. This lady was walking with the man who pushed the victim off the platform. This was witnessed by someone on the platform, who dismissed it as trivial at the time. Furthermore, there is reason to identify this lady as yourself. If so—"

"I was not there," Margaret replied, her expressionless eyes fixed on his face with the vacant intensity of a sleepwalker.

The inspector bowed but said nothing. The woman before him exhibited no fear, no fluttering anxiety, no desire to conclude the interview. The information he possessed was vague: a porter, rushing to catch a train, had seen a scuffle at the far end of the platform between Leonards and a gentleman accompanied by a lady, hearing no sound. Before the train gained speed, the enraged, half-drunk Leonards, swearing and cursing, had nearly knocked the porter down. The porter had not dwelled on it until the inspector unearthed his testimony. Subsequent inquiries at the station revealed that a remarkably handsome young lady and gentleman had been present around that hour; a grocer's assistant identified the lady as Miss Hale of Crampton. While certainty was lacking, the probability was high. Leonards, driven mad by rage and pain, had fled to a gin-palace, where waiters recalled him cursing himself for not utilizing the electric telegraph sooner, before leaving to seek further comfort. Overcome by pain or drink, he collapsed in the road, where police found him and transported him to the Infirmary. He never regained sufficient consciousness to provide a coherent account, though brief lucid moments led authorities to summon a magistrate. However, the magistrate found the dying man rambling, mixing names of captains and lieutenants with railway porters, his final words a curse on the "Cornish trick" that had cost him a hundred pounds.

The inspector reviewed this tapestry of vagueness and Margaret's unflinching, calm denial. She awaited his next word with a composure that seemed supreme.

"Then, madam, I have your denial that you were the lady accompanying the gentleman who struck the blow or delivered the push that caused this poor man's death?"

A sharp, searing pain lanced through Margaret's brain. *Oh God! that I knew Frederick were safe!* A deep observer might have witnessed the momentary agony flash through her gloomy eyes, like a creature trapped and tortured. Yet, the inspector, though keen, lacked the depth to perceive it fully. He was nonetheless struck by the mechanical repetition of her answer, unchanged and unmodified to address his specific query.

"I was not there," she said slowly and heavily. Throughout, she never closed her eyes or ceased that glassy, dream-like stare. His suspicions sharpened; it was as if she had forced herself into a single falsehood and been stunned into an inability to vary it.

He deliberately closed his notebook of notes and looked up. She had not moved, resembling a great Egyptian statue.

"I hope you will not deem me impertinent when I say I may need to call upon you again. I may have to summon you to the inquest to prove an alibi should my witnesses persist in testifying to your presence at this unfortunate event." He looked at her sharply. She remained perfectly quiet, her proud face showing no change of color or shadow of guilt. He thought he saw a wince, but he did not know Margaret Hale. Abashed by her genuine composure, he concluded it must be a mistake of identity.

"It is highly unlikely, ma'am, that I shall have to proceed further. I hope you will excuse me for performing my duty, even if it appears impertinent."

Margaret bowed her head as he turned toward the door. Her lips were stiff and dry; she could not muster the common words of farewell. Suddenly, she stepped forward, opened the study door, and preceded him to the house's entrance, throwing it wide for his exit. She kept her dull, fixed eyes upon him until he was safely outside. She closed the door, walked halfway into the study, then turned back, moved by some passionate impulse, and locked it from the inside.

She entered the study, paused, tottered forward, paused again, swayed for an instant, and fell prone upon the floor in a dead swoon.

"There is no thread so fine,
But it must reach the sun."

Mr. Thornton sat on and on. He sensed that his presence brought a quiet comfort to Mr. Hale, and he was moved by the silent, pleading wish for him to stay a little longer—the soft, persistent "Do not go yet" that his weary friend voiced from time to time. He wondered why Margaret did not return, yet he lingered not with the hope of seeing her. In this hour, and in the company of a man who so acutely felt the emptiness of earthly things, he remained reasonable and self-possessed. He listened with deep interest to everything his father-in-law said.

"How death, and the heavy silence,
And the mind that grows dull."

It was remarkable how Mr. Thornton's presence compelled Mr. Hale to unlock secrets he kept hidden even from Margaret. Perhaps it was because her sympathy would be so keen and vivid that he feared the emotional toll upon himself; perhaps it was because his speculative nature demanded resolution for every doubt, and he knew she would recoil from expressing such uncertainties—or from him, as someone capable of conceiving them. Whatever the reason, he found it easier to unburden his frozen thoughts, fears, and fancies to Mr. Thornton than to anyone else. Mr. Thornton spoke little, yet every sentence deepened Mr. Hale's trust and affection. When Mr. Hale hesitated over a remembered agony, Mr. Thornton's few words completed the thought, revealing how deeply he understood its meaning. When a doubt or fear wandered aimlessly, its eyes blinded by tears, Mr. Thornton did not recoil; instead, having passed through that very stage of thought himself, he guided the speaker toward the precise ray of light that would illuminate the darkness. Though a man of action, busy in the world's great struggles, there was a profound religion binding him to God in his heart, transcending his wilfulness and mistakes, a depth Mr. Hale had never imagined. They never spoke of such matters again, but this single conversation transformed them into peculiar souls for one another, knitting them together in a bond that loose, indiscriminate talk on sacred things could never achieve.

While this unfolded, Margaret lay as still and pale as death on the study floor. She had succumbed to her burden. It had been heavy in weight and long carried; she had been meek and patient until, suddenly, her faith broke, and she groped in vain for help. There was a pitiful contraction of suffering upon her beautiful brow, though no other sign of consciousness remained. Her mouth, which had recently projected sullenly in defiance, was now relaxed and livid.

"As from her lips a gentle spirit moves,
Full of love, whispering to the soul: 'Sigh!'"

The first sign of returning life was a quiver about the lips—a mute, soundless attempt at speech—but her eyes remained closed, and the trembling sank back into stillness. Then, leaning feebly on her arms to steady herself, Margaret gathered her strength and rose. Her comb had fallen from her hair. With an instinctive desire to erase the traces of weakness and restore order, she searched for it, sitting down periodically to regain her strength. Her head drooped forward; her hands lay meekly one upon the other. She tried to recall the force of the temptation that had thrown her into such terror, attempting to remember the details that had caused her dread, but she could not. She grasped only two facts: that Frederick had been in danger of being pursued and detected in London, not merely for manslaughter but as the unpardonable leader of the mutiny, and that she had lied to save him. There was one solace: her lie had bought him time. If the inspector returned tomorrow, after she received the letter she longed for to confirm her brother's safety, she would face her bitter penance—she, the lofty Margaret—acknowledging before a crowded justice hall, if necessary, that she had been "a dog, and done this thing." But if he came before she heard from Frederick; if he returned, as he had half-threatened, within hours! Then she would tell the lie again. Though she did not know how the words would emerge after such a terrible pause for reflection and self-reproach without betraying her falsehood, she knew that repeating it would gain time—time for Frederick.

She was roused by Dixon's entrance; she had just been letting Mr. Thornton out.

He had hardly taken ten steps into the street when a passing omnibus stopped beside him, and a man alighted, approaching him with a respectful nod. It was the police inspector.

Mr. Thornton had secured his first position in the police force and had heard updates on his protégé, but they had rarely met, and initially, Mr. Thornton did not recognize him.

"My name is Watson—George Watson, sir, the one you employed—"

"Ah, yes! I hear you are progressing famously."

"Yes, sir. I owe you thanks. But I approached you regarding a small matter of business. I believe you were the magistrate who took the deposition of the poor man who died in the Infirmary last night."

"Yes," replied Mr. Thornton. "I heard a rambling statement, which the clerk deemed of little use. I'm afraid he was drunk, though there is no doubt he died by violence. One of my mother's servants was engaged to him, I believe, and she is in great distress today. What about him?"

"Why, sir, his death is strangely connected with someone in the house I saw you leaving just now; it was Mr. Hales's, I think."

"Yes!" said Mr. Thornton, turning sharply to look into the inspector's face with sudden interest. "What about it?"

"Well, sir, it appears I have a fairly clear chain of evidence implicating a gentleman who was walking with Miss Hale that night at Outwood station as the man who struck or pushed Leonards off the platform, causing his death. But the young lady denies she was there at the time."

"Miss Hale denies she was there!" repeated Mr. Thornton, his voice altered. "Tell me, what evening was it? What time?"

"About six o'clock, on the evening of Thursday, the twenty-sixth."

They walked on in silence for a minute or two. The inspector spoke first.

"You see, sir, there is likely to be a coroner's inquest. I have a young man who is quite positive—at least, he was at first; since hearing of the young lady's denial, he says he wouldn't like to swear. Still, he is convinced he saw Miss Hale at the station, walking with a gentleman, not five minutes before the time when a porter witnessed a scuffle. He attributed it to some of Leonards' impudence, but it led to the fall that caused his death. Seeing you come out of the very house, sir, I thought I might ask if... well, it's always awkward dealing with cases of disputed identity, and one doesn't like doubting the word of a respectable young woman without strong proof to the contrary."

"And she denied having been at the station that evening!" repeated Mr. Thornton in a low, brooding tone.

"Yes, sir, twice over, as distinctly as could be. I told her I would call again, but seeing you just as I was returning from questioning the young man who claimed it was her, I thought I would seek your advice, both as the magistrate who saw Leonards on his deathbed and as the gentleman who got me my place in the force."

"You were quite right," said Mr. Thornton. "Do not take any steps until you have seen me again."

"The young lady expects me to call, from what I said."

"I only wish to delay you an hour. It is now three. Come to my warehouse at four."

"Very well, sir!"

They parted. Mr. Thornton hurried to his warehouse, sternly ordering his clerks to admit no interruptions, then went to his private room and locked the door. He indulged himself in the torture of reviewing every detail. How could he have lulled himself into the unsuspicious calm in which her tearful image had mirrored itself not two hours prior, until he had weakly pitied her and yearned toward her, forgetting the savage, distrustful jealousy that the sight of her—and that unknown to him—at such an hour, in such a place, had inspired? How could one so pure stoop from her decorous and noble bearing? But was it decorous—was it? He hated himself for the fleeting idea that forced itself upon him, thrilling him with its old potency of attraction toward her image. And then this falsehood—how terrible must be the dread of shame to be revealed! For, after all, the provocation given by a man like Leonards, when excited by drink, might, in all probability, be enough to justify anyone stating the circumstances openly! How creeping and deadly that fear which could bow the truthful Margaret to falsehood! He could almost pity her. What would be the end? She could not have considered all she was entering upon; if there was an inquest and the young man came forward. Suddenly, he started up. There should be no inquest. He would save Margaret. He would take the responsibility of preventing the inquest, the outcome of which, from the uncertain medical testimony he had vaguely heard the night before, could only be doubtful. The doctors had discovered an advanced internal disease, sure to prove fatal; they had stated that death might have been accelerated by the fall or by the subsequent drinking and exposure to cold. If he had known how Margaret would become involved, if he had foreseen that he would stain her whiteness with a falsehood, he could have saved her with a word. The question of inquest or no inquest had hung trembling in the balance only the night before. Miss Hale might love another, be indifferent and contemptuous to him—but he would yet perform faithful acts of service she would never know. He might despise her, but the woman he had once loved should be kept from shame; and shame it would be to pledge herself to a lie in a public court, or to stand and acknowledge her reason for desiring darkness rather than light.

Very gray and stern did Mr. Thornton look as he passed through his wondering clerks. He was away for about half an hour; scarcely less stern did he appear when he returned, though his errand had been successful.

He wrote two lines on a slip of paper, put it in an envelope, and sealed it. Giving it to a clerk, he said:

"I appointed Watson—he who was a packer in the warehouse and went into the police—to call on me at four o'clock. I have just met a gentleman from Liverpool who wishes to see me before he leaves town. Take care to give this note to Watson when he calls."

The note read:

"There will be no inquest. Medical evidence not sufficient to justify it. Take no further steps. I have not seen the coroner; but I will take the responsibility."

"Well," thought Watson, "it relieves me from an awkward job. None of my witnesses seemed certain of anything except the young woman. She was clear and distinct; the porter at the railroad had seen a scuffle! Or when he realized it might bring him in as a witness, perhaps it wasn't a scuffle, only larking, and Leonards might have jumped off himself; he wouldn't stick to anything. And Jennings, the grocer's shopman—well, he wasn't quite so bad, but I doubt if I could get him to an oath after hearing Miss Hale flatly deny it. It would have been a troublesome job with no satisfaction. Now I must go and tell them they won't be needed."

He accordingly presented himself again at Mr. Hale's that evening. Her father and Dixon would fain have persuaded Margaret to go to bed, but neither knew the reason for her continued refusal. Dixon had learned part of the truth—but only part. Margaret would not tell any human being what she had said, nor reveal the fatal termination of Leonards' fall. So Dixon's curiosity, combined with her allegiance, urged Margaret to rest, which her appearance, as she lay on the sofa, showed she desperately required. She spoke only when spoken to; she tried to smile back at her father's anxious looks and tender inquiries, but instead of a smile, her wan lips resolved into a sigh. He was so miserably uneasy that finally, she consented to go to her own room and prepare for bed. She was indeed inclined to give up the idea that the inspector would call again that night, as it was already past nine.

She stood by her father, holding onto the back of his chair.

"You will go to bed soon, papa, won't you? Don't sit up alone!"

What his answer was she did not hear; the words were lost in the far smaller sound that magnified itself to her fears and filled her brain. There was a low ring at the doorbell.

She kissed her father and glided downstairs with a rapidity of motion no one would have thought possible, given what they had seen of her the minute before. She put aside Dixon.

"Don't come; I will open the door. I know it is him—I can—I must manage it all myself."

"As you please, miss!" said Dixon, testily; but a moment later she added, "But you're not fit for it. You are more dead than alive."

"Am I?" said Margaret, turning round, her eyes aglow with strange fire, her cheeks flushed, though her lips remained baked and livid.

She opened the door to the Inspector and preceded him into the study. She placed the candle on the table and snuffed it carefully before turning to face him.

"You are late!" she said. "Well?" She held her breath for the answer.

"I am sorry to have given any unnecessary trouble, ma'am; for, after all, they've given up all thoughts of holding an inquest. I have had other work to do and other people to see, or I should have been here sooner."

"Then it is ended," said Margaret. "There is to be no further enquiry."

"I believe I have Mr. Thornton's note," said the Inspector, fumbling in his pocket-book.

"Mr. Thornton's!" said Margaret.

"Yes! He's a magistrate—ah! Here it is."

She could not see to read it—not even though she was close to the candle. The words swam before her

"The steps of the bearers, heavy and slow,
The sobs of the mourners, deep and low."

At the hour appointed the day before, Margaret and Mr. Hale set forth on their walk to visit Nicholas Higgins and his daughter. The recent loss weighed heavily upon them, manifesting in a peculiar shyness regarding their new attire and the fact that it had been many weeks since they had deliberately ventured out together. Yet, they drew close, bound by an unspoken sympathy.

Nicholas sat by the fireside in his usual corner, yet without his customary pipe. Leaning his head upon his hand, his arm resting on his knee, he did not rise when they entered, though Margaret perceived the welcome in his gaze.

"Sit ye down, sit ye down. Fire's welly out," he muttered, poking the embers vigorously as if to divert attention from his own state. He appeared disordered, sporting a black, unshaven beard of several days' growth that rendered his pale face even paler, clad in a jacket that would have benefited greatly from patching.

"We thought we should have a good chance of finding you, just after dinner-time," Margaret said.

"We have had our sorrows too, since we saw you," Mr. Hale replied.

"Ay, ay. Sorrows are more plentiful than dinners just now; I reckon, my dinner hour stretches all o'er the day; you're pretty sure of finding me."

"Are you out of work?" Margaret asked.

"Ay," he replied shortly. After a moment's silence, he looked up. "I'm not wanting brass. Dunno you think it. Bess, poor lass, had a little stock under her pillow, ready to slip into my hand at the last moment, and Mary is fustian-cutting. But I'm out of work a' the same."

"We owe Mary some money," Mr. Hale said, before Margaret's sharp pressure on his arm could arrest the words.

"If hoo takes it, I'll turn her out o' doors. I'll bide inside these four walls, and she'll bide out. That's a'."

"But we owe her many thanks for her kind service," Mr. Hale began again.

"I ne'er thanken your daughter theer for her deeds o' love to my poor wench. I ne'er could find th' words. I'll have to begin to try now, if you start making an ado about what little Mary could serve you."

"Is it because of the strike you're out of work?" Margaret asked gently.

"Strike's ended. It's o'er for this time. I'm out o' work because I ne'er asked for it. And I ne'er asked for it, because good words is scarce, and bad words is plentiful."

He was in a mood to take a surly pleasure in giving answers that were like riddles, yet Margaret sensed he desired an explanation.

"And good words are—"

"Asking for work. I reckon them's almost the best words that men can say. 'Gi' me work' means 'and I'll do it like a man.' Them's good words."

"And bad words are refusing you work when you ask for it."

"Ay. Bad words is saying 'Aha, my fine chap! You've been true to your order, and I'll be true to mine. You did the best you could for them as wanted help; that's your way of being true to your kind: and I'll be true to mine. You've been a poor fool, as knowed no better nor be a true faithful fool. So go and be d—d to you. There's no work for you here.' Them's bad words. I'm not a fool; and if I was, folk ought to ha' taught me how to be wise after their fashion. I could m'appen ha' learnt, if any one had tried to teach me."

"Would it not be worth while," said Mr. Hale, "to ask your old master if he would take you back again? It might be a poor chance, but it would be a chance."

He looked up again, with a sharp glance at the questioner; and then tittered a low and bitter laugh.

"Measter! if it's no offence, I'll ask you a question or two in my turn."

"You're quite welcome," said Mr. Hale.

"I reckon you'n some way of earning your bread. Folk seldom lives i' Milton just for pleasure, if they can live anywhere else."

"You are quite right. I have some independent property, but my intention in settling in Milton was to become a private tutor."

"To teach folk. Well! I reckon they pay you for teaching them, dunnot they?"

"Yes," replied Mr. Hale, smiling. "I teach in order to get paid."

"And them that pays you, dun they tell you whatten to do, or whatten not to do wi' the money they gives you in just payment for your pains—in fair exchange like?"

"No; to be sure not!"

"They dunnot say, 'You may have a brother, or a friend as dear as a brother, who wants this here brass for a purpose both you and he think right; but you mun promise not give it to him. You may see a good use, as you think, to put your money to; but we don't think it good, and so if you spend it a-thatens we'll just leave off dealing with you.' They dunnot say that, dun they?"

"No: to be sure not!"

"Would you stand it if they did?"

"It would be some very hard pressure that would make me even think of submitting to such dictation."

"There's not the pressure on all the broad earth that would make me," said Nicholas Higgins. "Now you've got it. You've hit the bull's eye. Hampers—that's where I worked—makes their men pledge themselves they'll not give a penny to help the Union or keep turn-outs from clemming. They may pledge and make pledge," he continued scornfully, "they nobbut make liars and hypocrites. And that's a less sin, to my mind, to make men's hearts so hard that they'll not do a kindness to them as needs it, or help on the right and just cause, though it goes against the strong hand. But I'll ne'er forswear myself for all the work the king could give me. I'm a member o' the Union; and I think it's the only thing to do the workmen any good. And I've been a turn-out, and known what it was to clem; so if I get a shilling, sixpence shall go to them if they axe it from me. Consequence is, I dunnot see where I'm to get a shilling."

"Is that rule about not contributing to the Union in force at all the mills?" Margaret asked.

"I cannot say. It's a new regulation at ours; and I reckon they'll find that they cannot stick to it. But it's in force now. By-and-by they'll find out, tyrants makes liars."

There was a little pause. Margaret hesitated whether she should say what was in her mind; she was unwilling to irritate one who was always gloomy and despondent enough. At last, it came out. But in her soft tones, and with her reluctant manner, showing that she was unwilling to say anything unpleasant, it did not seem to annoy Higgins, only to perplex him.

"Do you remember poor Boucher saying that the Union was a tyrant? I think he said it was the worse tyrant of all. And I remember, at the time I agreed with him."

It was a long while before he spoke. He was resting his head on his two hands, looking down into the fire, so she could not read the expression on his face.

"I'll not deny but what the Union finds it necessary to force a man into his own good. I'll speak truth. A man leads a dreary life who's not in the Union. But once in the Union, his interests are taken care of better than he could do it for himself, or by himself, for that matter. It's the only way working men can get their rights, by all joining together. More the members, more chance for each one separate man having justice done him. Government takes care of fools and madmen; and if any man is inclined to do himself or his neighbour a hurt, it puts a bit of a check on him, whether he likes it or no. That's all we do in the Union. We can't clap folk into prison; but we can make a man's life so heavy to be borne, that he's obliged to come in, and be wise and helpful in spite of himself. Boucher were a fool all along, and ne'er a worse fool than at the last."

"He did you harm?" Margaret asked.

"Aye, that did he. We had public opinion on our side, till he and his sort began rioting and breaking laws. It were all o'er with the strike then."

"Then would it not have been far better to have left him alone, and not forced him to join the Union? He did you no good; and you drove him mad."

"Margaret," said her father, in a low and warning tone, for he saw the cloud gathering on Higgins's face.

"I like her," said Higgins, suddenly. "She speaks plain out what's in her mind. She doesn't comprehend the Union for all that. It's a great power: it's our only power. I ha' read a bit o' poetry about a plough going o'er a daisy, as made tears come into my eyes, afore I'd other cause for crying. But the chap ne'er stopped driving the plough, I'll warrant, for all he was pitiful about the daisy. He'd too much mother-wit for that. The Union's the plough, making ready the land for harvest-time. Such as Boucher—'twould be setting him up too much to liken him to a daisy; he's liker a weed lounging over the ground—mun just made up their mind to be put out o' the way. I'm sore vexed with him just now. So m'appen, I dunnot speak him fair. I could go o'er him with a plough myself, with all the pleasure in life."

"Why? What has he been doing? Anything fresh?"

"Ay, to be sure. He's ne'er out o' mischief, that man. First of all he must go raging like a mad fool, and kick up yon riot. Then he'd to go into hiding, where he'd a been yet, if Thornton had followed him out as I'd hoped he would ha' done. But Thornton, having got his own purpose, didn't care to go on with the prosecution for the riot. So Boucher slunk back again to his house. He ne'er showed himself abroad for a day or two. He had that grace. And then, where think ye that he went? Why, to Hampers'. Damn him! He went with his mealy-mouthed face, that turns me sick to look at, asking for work, though he knowed well enough the new rule, of pledging themselves to give nought to the Unions; nought to help the starving turn-out! Why he'd a clemmed to death, if the Union had na helped him in his pinch. There he went, vowing to promise aught, and pledge himself to aught—to tell all he knew on our proceedings, the good-for-nothing Judas! But I'll say this for Hamper, and thank him for it at my dying day, he drove Boucher away, and would na listen to him—ne'er a word—though folk standing by, says the traitor cried like a babby!"

"Oh! how shocking! how pitiful!" exclaimed Margaret. "Higgins, I don't know you to-day. Don't you see how you've made Boucher what he is, by driving him into the Union against his will—without his heart going with it. You have made him what he is!"

Made him what he is! What was he?

Gathering, gathering along the narrow street, came a hollow, measured sound; now forcing itself on their attention. Many voices were hushed and low: many steps were heard, not moving onwards, at least not with any rapidity or steadiness of motion, but as if circling round one spot. Yes, there was one distinct, slow tramp of feet, which made itself a clear path through the air, and reached their ears; the measured, laboured walk of men carrying a heavy burden. They were all drawn towards the house-door by some irresistible impulse; impelled thither—not by a poor curiosity, but as if by some solemn blast.

Six men walked in the middle of the road, three of them being policemen. They carried a door, taken off its hinges, upon their shoulders, on which lay some dead human creature; and from each side of the door there were constant droppings. All the street turned out to see, and, seeing, to accompany the procession, each one questioning the bearers, who answered almost reluctantly at last, so often had they told the tale.

"We found him i' th' brook in the field beyond there."

"The brook!—why there's not water enough to drown him!"

"He was a determined chap. He lay with his face downwards. He was sick enough o' living, choose what cause he had for it."

Higgins crept up to Margaret's side, and said in a weak, piping kind of voice: "It's not John Boucher? He had na spunk enough. Sure! It's not John Boucher! Why, they are all looking this way! Listen! I've a singing in my head, and I cannot hear."

They put the door down carefully upon the stones, and all might see the poor drowned wretch—his glassy eyes, one half-open, staring right upwards to the sky. Owing to the position in which he had been found lying, his face was swollen and discoloured; besides, his skin was stained by the water in the brook, which had been used for dyeing purposes. The fore part of his head was bald; but

"A spade! a rake! a hoe!
A pickaxe or a bill!
A hook to reap, or a scythe to mow,
A flail or what ye will—
And here's a ready hand
To ply the needful tool,
And skill'd enough, by lessons rough,
In Labour's rugged school."

The following day, Higgins's door remained barred when Margaret and her father arrived to pay their respects to the widow Boucher. An officious neighbor informed them that Higgins had departed for his daily labor before visiting the widow, though he had made the attempt. The visit proved deeply unsatisfying. Mrs. Boucher viewed herself as a victim of her husband's suicide, a sentiment rooted in enough truth to make refutation nearly impossible. Yet, her grief was entirely self-absorbed, extending even to her children, whom she regarded as burdens despite her animalistic affection for them. Margaret attempted to connect with the children, while Mr. Hale tried to elevate the widow's thoughts beyond her helpless querulousness. He discovered that the children were far more sincere mourners than their mother; each could stammer eagerly of specific acts of kindness and indulgence from their lost father.

"It is that thing upstairs, isn't it? It doesn't look like him. I'm frightened, and I never was frightened of Daddy."

Margaret's heart ached to hear that the mother, in her desperate need for sympathy, had dragged her children upstairs to view their dead, disfigured father. She felt it was a cruel mingling of coarse horror with profound natural grief. She tried to redirect their thoughts toward practical duties—what they could do for their mother, or, more effectively, what their father would have wished of them. The children, seeing their small tasks within reach, began to attempt cleaning the slatternly room. Mr. Hale, however, set a standard too high and too abstract for the indolent invalid. He could not rouse her torpid mind to imagine her husband's misery before the final step; she could only view the tragedy through the lens of her own suffering. She could not comprehend the enduring mercy of God who had not intervened to save her prostrate husband. Though she secretly blamed her husband for his despair and denied any excuse for his rash act, she remained inveterate in her abuse of anyone who might have driven him to such desperation. The masters—Mr. Thornton in particular, whose mill had been attacked and for whom the rioting warrant was later withdrawn—the Union, represented by Higgins, the numerous hungry children; all constituted a vast army of personal enemies responsible for her current helplessness.

Margaret heard enough of this unreasonableness to be disheartened, finding it impossible to cheer her father upon their return.

"It is the town life," she said. "Their nerves are quickened by the haste and bustle of everything around them, not to mention the confinement of these pent-up houses, which alone induces depression. In the country, people live much more outdoors, even children, even in winter."

"But people must live in towns. And in the country, some develop such stagnant habits of mind that they become almost fatalists."

"Yes; I acknowledge that. Each mode of life produces its own trials. The town dweller finds it as difficult to be patient as the country-bred man finds it to be active and equal to unwonted emergencies. Both struggle to realize a future: the one because the present is so living, hurrying, and close; the other because his life tempts him to revel in mere animal existence, caring for no pungent pleasure requiring planning and self-denial."

"And thus, the necessity for engrossment and the stupid contentment in the present produce similar effects. But this poor Mrs. Boucher! How little we can do for her."

"And yet we dare not leave her without effort, though it may seem useless. Oh, Papa! It is a hard world to live in!"

"So it is, my child. We feel it acutely just now, but we have been very happy even amidst our sorrow. What a pleasure Frederick's visit was!"

"Yes, it was," Margaret said brightly. "Such a charming, snatched, forbidden thing." She suddenly stopped. She had spoiled the memory of Frederick's visit through her own cowardice. Of all faults, she most despised in others the lack of bravery; the meanness of heart that leads to untruth. And here she had been guilty of it. Then came the thought of Mr. Thornton's knowledge of her falsehood. She wondered if she would mind detection less from anyone else. She imagined facing her Aunt Shaw, Edith, her father, Captain and Mr. Lennox, and Frederick. The thought of Frederick knowing, even in his own behalf, was most painful given their fresh mutual regard, but even a fall in his opinion was nothing compared to the shrinking shame she felt at the prospect of meeting Mr. Thornton again. Yet, she longed to see him, to resolve where she stood. Her cheeks burned as she recalled how proudly she had implied an objection to trade, citing the deceit of passing off inferior goods or assuming credit for wealth not possessed. She remembered Mr. Thornton's look of calm disdain as he explained that in the great scheme of commerce, all dishonorable ways proved injurious in the long run, and that testing actions by the poor standard of success revealed folly rather than wisdom in all deceit. She remembered herself, then strong in her untempted truth, asking if buying cheap and selling dear lacked transparent justice, using the word "chivalric"—corrected by her father to "Christian"—drawing the argument upon himself while she sat silent with a slight feeling of contempt.

No more contempt for her! No more talk of the chivalric! Henceforth, she must feel humiliated and disgraced in his sight. But when would she see him? Her heart leaped at every ring of the doorbell, yet sank with sadness at each disappointment. It was evident her father expected him and was surprised by his absence. The truth was, their previous conversation had left points unexpanded, with an understanding that they should meet the following evening if possible. Mr. Hale had looked forward to this meeting since they parted. Having not yet resumed his instruction to his pupils due to his wife's serious illness, he had fewer occupations than usual, and the recent tragedy of Boucher's suicide had driven him back to his speculations with renewed eagerness. He was restless all evening, muttering, "I quite expected to see Mr. Thornton. I think the messenger who brought the book last night must have had a note and forgot to deliver it. Do you think there has been any message left today?"

"I will go and inquire, Papa," Margaret said. "Stay, there's a ring!" She sat instantly, bending her head over her work. She heard a step on the stairs, but it was only one, and she knew it was Dixon's. She lifted her head and sighed, believing she felt glad.

"It's Higgins, sir. He wants to see you, or else Miss Hale. Or perhaps Miss Hale first, then you, sir; he's in a strange kind of way."

"He had better come up here, Dixon; then he can see us both and choose his listener."

"Oh! Very well, sir. I have no wish to hear what he has to say, I'm sure; only, if you could see his shoes, I'm sure you'd say the kitchen is the fitter place."

"He can wipe them, I suppose," said Mr. Hale. Dixon flung off to bid him walk upstairs. She was slightly mollified when Higgins looked at his feet with a hesitating air, then sat on the bottom stair, removed the offending shoes, and walked upstairs without a word.

"Servant, sir!" he said, slicking his hair down upon entering the room. "If you'll excuse me (looking at Margaret) for being in my stockings; I've been tramping all day, and the streets are none of the cleanest."

Margaret thought fatigue might account for the change in his manner; he was unusually quiet and subdued, evidently struggling to say what he came to say. Mr. Hale's ever-ready sympathy with shyness or hesitation came to his aid.

"We shall have tea up directly, and then you'll take a cup with us, Mr. Higgins. I am sure you are tired if you've been out much this wet relaxing day. Margaret, my dear, can't you hasten tea?"

Margaret could only hasten tea by taking the preparation into her own hands, thereby offending Dixon, who was emerging from her sorrow for her late mistress into a touchy, irritable state. But Martha, like all who came in contact with Margaret—even Dixon herself in the long run—felt it a pleasure and honor to forward her wishes. Her readiness and Margaret's sweet forbearance soon made Dixon ashamed of herself.

"Why, Master and you must always be asking the lower classes upstairs since we came to Milton; I cannot understand it. Folk at Helstone were never brought higher than the kitchen; and I've let one or two of them know before now that they might think it an honor to be even there."

Higgins found it easier to unburden himself to one than to two. After Margaret left the room, he ensured the door was shut, then approached Mr. Hale closely.

"Master," he said, "you wouldn't guess easy what I've been tramping after today. Especially if you remember my manner of talk yesterday. I've been seeking work. I have," he continued. "I said to myself, I'd keep a civil tongue in my head, let who would say what they would. I'd set my teeth into my tongue sooner than speak in haste. For that man's sake—you understand," jerking his thumb back in some unknown direction.

"No, I don't," said Mr. Hale, seeing he waited for assent and completely bewildered as to who "that man" could be.

"That chap as lies there," he said with another jerk. "Him as went and drowned himself, poor chap! I didn't think he'd got it in him to lie still and let the water creep over him till he died. Boucher, you know."

"Yes, I know now," said Mr. Hale. "Go back to what you were saying: you'd not speak in haste..."

"For his sake. Yet not for his sake; for wherever he is and whatever, he'll never know other clothing or cold again; but for the wife's sake, and the bits of children."

"God bless you!" said Mr. Hale, starting up; then, calming down, he said breathlessly, "What do you mean? Tell me out."

"I have told you," said Higgins, a little surprised at Mr. Hale's agitation. "I would not ask for work for myself; but them's left as a charge on me. I reckon I would have guided Boucher to a better end; but I set him off of the road, and so I must answer for him."

Mr. Hale got hold of Higgins's hand and shook it heartily without speaking. Higgins looked awkward and ashamed.

"There, there, Master! There's never a man, to call a man, amongst us, but what would do the same; ay, and better too; for, believe me, I've never got a stroke of work, nor yet a sight of any. For all I told Hamper that, let alone his pledge—which I would not sign—no, I could not, not even for this—he'd never have such a worker on his mill as I would be; he'd have none of me—no more would none of the others. I'm a poor black feckless sheep—children may claim for ought I can do, unless, Parson, you'd help me?"

"Help you! How? I would do anything—but what can I do?"

"Miss there"—for Margaret had re-entered the room and stood silent, listening—"has often talked grand of the South, and the ways down there. Now I don't know how far off it is, but I been thinking if I could get 'em down there, where food is cheap and wages good, and all the folk, rich and poor, master and man, friendly like; you could, maybe, help me to work. I'm not forty-five, and I've a deal of strength in me, Master."

"But what kind of work could you do, my man?"

"Well, I reckon I could spade a bit..."

"And for that," said Margaret, stepping forward, "for anything you could do, Higgins, with the best will in the world, you would, maybe, get nine shillings a week; maybe ten, at the outside. Food is much the same as here, except that you might have a little garden..."

"The children could work at that," he said. "I'm sick of Milton anyways, and Milton is sick of me."

"You must not go the South," said Margaret. "You could not stand it. You would have to be out all weathers. It would kill you with rheumatism. The mere bodily work at your time of life would break you down. The fare is far different to what you have been accustomed to."

"I'm nought particular about my meat," he said, as if offended.

"But you've reckoned on having butcher's meat once a day if you're in work; pay for that out of your ten shillings, and keep those poor children if you can. I owe it to you—since it's my way of talking that has set you off on this idea—to put it all clear before you. You would not bear the dulness of the life; you don't know what it is; it would eat you away like rust. Those that have lived there all their lives are used to soaking in the stagnant waters. They labour on from day to day in the great solitude of steaming fields—never speaking or lifting up their poor, bent, downcast heads. The hard spadework robs their brain of life; the sameness of their toil deadens their imagination; they don't care to meet to talk over thoughts and speculations, even of the weakest, wildest kind, after their work is done; they go home brutishly tired, poor creatures! caring for nothing but food and rest. You could not stir them up into any companionship, which you get in a town as plentiful as the air you breathe, whether it

"Then proudly, proudly up she rose,
Tho' the tear was in her e'e,
Whate'er ye say, think what ye may,
Ye's get na word frae me!"

It was not merely that Margaret was known to Mr. Thornton to have spoken falsely; though she imagined that for this reason only was she so turned in his opinion. The wound ran deeper. In his mind, that falsehood bore a distinct reference to some other lover. He could not forget the fond and earnest look that had passed between her and some other man—the attitude of familiar confidence, if not of positive endearment. The thought of this perpetually stung him; it was a picture before his eyes, wherever he went and whatever he was doing. In addition to this (and he ground his teeth as he remembered it), was the hour, dusky twilight; the place, so far away from home, and comparatively unfrequented. His nobler self had said at first that all this last might be accidental, innocent, justifiable; but once allow her right to love and be beloved (and had he any reason to deny her right?—had not her words been severely explicit when she cast his love away from her?), she might easily have been beguiled into a longer walk, on to a later hour than she had anticipated. But that falsehood! which showed a fatal consciousness of something wrong, and to be concealed, which was unlike her. He did her that justice, though all the time it would have been a relief to believe her utterly unworthy of his esteem. It was this that made the misery—that he passionately loved her, and thought her, even with all her faults, more lovely and more excellent than any other woman; yet he deemed her so attached to some other man, so led away by her affection for him, as to violate her truthful nature. The very falsehood that stained her was a proof how blindly she loved another—this dark, slight, elegant, handsome man—while he himself was rough, and stern, and strongly made. He lashed himself into an agony of fierce jealousy. He thought of that look, that attitude!—how he would have laid his life at her feet for such tender glances, such fond detention! He mocked at himself, for having valued the mechanical way in which she had protected him from the fury of the mob; now he had seen how soft and bewitching she looked when with a man she really loved. He remembered, point by point, the sharpness of her words—"There was not a man in all that crowd for whom she would not have done as much, far more readily than for him." He shared with the mob, in her desire of averting bloodshed from them; but this man, this hidden lover, shared with nobody; he had looks, words, hand-cleavings, lies, concealment, all to himself.

Mr. Thornton was conscious that he had never been so irritable as he was now, in all his life long; he felt inclined to give a short abrupt answer, more like a bark than a speech, to every one that asked him a question; and this consciousness hurt his pride: he had always piqued himself on his self-control, and control himself he would. So the manner was subdued to a quiet deliberation, but the matter was even harder and sterner than common. He was more than usually silent at home; employing his evenings in a continual pace backwards and forwards, which would have annoyed his mother exceedingly if it had been practised by any one else; and did not tend to promote any forbearance on her part even to this beloved son.

"Can you stop—can you sit down for a moment? I have something to say to you, if you would give up that everlasting walk, walk, walk."

He sat down instantly, on a chair against the wall.

"I want to speak to you about Betsy. She says she must leave us; that her lover's death has so affected her spirits she can't give her heart to her work."

"Very well. I suppose other cooks are to be met with."

"That's so like a man. It's not merely the cooking, it is that she knows all the ways of the house. Besides, she tells me something about your friend Miss Hale."

"Miss Hale is no friend of mine. Mr. Hale is my friend."

"I am glad to hear you say so, for if she had been your friend, what Betsy says would have annoyed you."

"Let me hear it," said he, with the extreme quietness of manner he had been assuming for the last few days.

"Betsy says, that the night on which her lover—I forget his name—for she always calls him 'he'——"

"Leonards."

"The night on which Leonards was last seen at the station—when he was last seen on duty, in fact—Miss Hale was there, walking about with a young man who, Bessy believes, killed Leonards by some blow or push."

"Leonards was not killed by any blow or push."

"How do you know?"

"Because I distinctly put the question to the surgeon of the Infirmary. He told me there was an internal disease of long standing, caused by Leonards' habit of drinking to excess; that the fact of his becoming rapidly worse while in a state of intoxication, settled the question as to whether the last fatal attack was caused by excess of drinking, or the fall."

"The fall! What fall?"

"Caused by the blow or push of which Betsy speaks."

"Then there was a blow or push?"

"I believe so."

"And who did it?"

"As there was no inquest, in consequence of the doctor's opinion, I cannot tell you."

"But Miss Hale was there?"

No answer.

"And with a young man?"

Still no answer. At last he said: "I tell you, mother, that there was no inquest—no inquiry. No judicial inquiry, I mean."

"Betsy says that Woolmer (some man she knows, who is in a grocer's shop out at Crampton) can swear that Miss Hale was at the station at that hour, walking backwards and forwards with a young man."

"I don't see what we have to do with that. Miss Hale is at liberty to please herself."

"I'm glad to hear you say so," says Mrs. Thornton, eagerly. "It certainly signifies very little to us—not at all to you, after what has passed! but I—I made a promise to Mrs. Hale, that I would not allow her daughter to go wrong without advising and remonstrating with her. I shall certainly let her know my opinion of such conduct."

"I do not see any harm in what she did that evening," said Mr. Thornton, getting up, and coming near to his mother; he stood by the chimney-piece with his face turned away from the room.

"You would not have approved of Fanny's being seen out, after dark, in rather a lonely place, walking about with a young man. I say nothing of the taste which could choose the time, when her mother lay unburied, for such a promenade. Should you have liked your sister to have been noticed by a grocer's assistant for doing so?"

"In the first place, as it is not many years since I myself was a draper's assistant, the mere circumstance of a grocer's assistant noticing any act does not alter the character of the act to me. And in the next place, I see a great deal of difference between Miss Hale and Fanny. I can imagine that the one may have weighty reasons, which may and ought to make her overlook any seeming impropriety in her conduct. I never knew Fanny have weighty reasons for anything. Other people must guard her. I believe Miss Hale is a guardian to herself."

"A pretty character of your sister, indeed! Really, John, one would have thought Miss Hale had done enough to make you clear-sighted. She drew you on to an offer, by a bold display of pretended regard for you,—to play you off against this very young man, I've no doubt. Her whole conduct is clear to me now. You believe he is her lover, I suppose—you agree to that."

He turned round to his mother; his face was very gray and grim. "Yes, mother. I do believe he is her lover." When he had spoken, he turned round again; he writhed himself about, like one in bodily pain. He leant his face against his hand. Then before she could speak, he turned sharp again:

"Mother. He is her lover, whoever he is; but she may need help and womanly counsel;—there may be difficulties or temptations which I don't know. I fear there are. I don't want to know what they are; but as you have ever been a good—ay! and a tender mother to me, go to her, and gain her confidence, and tell her what is best to be done. I know that something is wrong; some dread, which must be a terrible torture to her."

"For God's sake, John!" said his mother, now really shocked, "what do you mean? What do you mean? What do you know?"

He did not reply to her.

"John! I don't know what I shan't think unless you speak. You have no right to say what you have done against her."

"Not against her, mother! I _could_ not speak against her."

"Well! you have no right to say what you have done, unless you say more. These half-expressions are what ruin a woman's character."

"Her character! Mother, you do not dare—" he faced about, and looked into her face with his flaming eyes. Then, drawing himself up into determined composure and dignity, he said, "I will not say any more than this, which is neither more nor less than the simple truth, and I am sure you believe me,—I have good reason to believe, that Miss Hale is in some strait and difficulty connected with an attachment which, of itself, from my knowledge of Miss Hale's character, is perfectly innocent and right. What my reason is, I refuse to tell. But never let me hear any one say a word against her, implying any more serious imputation than that she now needs the counsel of some kind and gentle woman. You promised Mrs. Hale to be that woman!"

"No!" said Mrs. Thornton. "I am happy to say, I did not promise kindness and gentleness, for I felt at the time that it might be out of my power to render these to one of Miss Hale's character and disposition. I promised counsel and advice, such as I would give to my own daughter; I shall speak to her as I would do to Fanny, if she had gone gallivanting with a young man in the dusk. I shall speak with relation to the circumstances I know, without being influenced either one way or another by the 'strong reasons' which you will not confide to me. Then I shall have fulfilled my promise, and done my duty."

"She will never bear it," said he passionately.

"She will have to bear it, if I speak in her dead mother's name."

"Well!" said he, breaking away, "don't tell me any more about it. I cannot endure to think of it. It will be better that you should speak to her any way, than that she should not be spoken to at all.—Oh! that look of love!" continued he, between his teeth, as he bolted himself into his own private room. "And that cursed lie; which showed some terrible shame in the background, to be kept from the light in which I thought she lived perpetually! Oh, Margaret, Margaret! Mother, how you have tortured me! Oh! Margaret, could you not have loved me? I am but uncouth and hard, but I would never have led you into any falsehood for me."

The more Mrs. Thornton thought over what her son had said, in pleading for a merciful judgment for Margaret's indiscretion, the more bitterly she felt inclined towards her. She took a savage pleasure in the idea of "speaking her mind" to her, in the guise of fulfilment of a duty. She enjoyed the thought of showing herself untouched by the "glamour," which she was well aware Margaret had the power of throwing over many people. She snorted scornfully over the picture of the beauty of her victim; her jet black hair, her clear smooth skin, her lucid eyes would not help to save her one word of the just and stern reproach which Mrs. Thornton spent half the night in preparing to her mind.

"Is Miss Hale within?" She knew she was, for she had seen her at the window, and she had her feet inside the little hall before Martha had half answered the question.

Margaret was sitting alone, writing to Edith, and giving her many particulars of her mother's last days. It was a softening employment, and she had to brush away the unbidden tears as Mrs. Thornton was announced.

She was so gentle and ladylike in her mode of reception that her visitor was somewhat daunted; and it became impossible to utter the speech, so easy of arrangement with no one to address it to. Margaret's low rich voice was softer than usual; her manner more gracious, because in her heart she was feeling very grateful to Mrs. Thornton for the courteous attention of her call. She exerted herself to find subjects of interest for conversation; praised Martha, the servant whom Mrs. Thornton had found for them: had asked Edith for a little Greek air, about which she had spoken to Miss Thornton. Mrs. Thornton was fairly discomfited. Her sharp Damascus blade seemed out of place, and useless among rose-leaves. She was silent because she was trying to task herself up to her duty. At last she stung herself into its performance by a suspicion which, in spite of all probability, she allowed to cross her mind, that all this sweetness was put on with a view of propitiating Mr. Thornton; that, somehow, the other attachment had fallen through, and that it suited Miss Hale's purpose to recall her rejected lover. Poor Margaret! there was perhaps so much truth in the suspicion as this; that Mrs. Thornton was the mother of one whose regard she valued, and feared to have lost; and this thought unconsciously added to her natural desire of pleasing one who was showing her kindness by her visit. Mrs. Thornton stood up to go, but yet

"Nay, I have done; you get no more of me;
And I am glad, yea glad with all my heart,
That thus so clearly I myself am free."

Margaret withdrew to her chamber, leaving Mrs. Thornton behind. Once inside, she paced the floor, a rhythmic, agitated movement that had become her habit whenever distress clouded her mind. Yet, mindful that the small house offered no privacy, her steps ceased the moment she heard the latch lift and Mrs. Thornton depart. Only then did she allow herself to sit, forcing her memory to replay the conversation, word by word, until the scene was etched in her mind.

Finally, she rose, her voice heavy with a quiet despair. "At least, her words do not strike me; they slide off, for I am innocent of the motives she imputes. Yet, it is a bitter pill to swallow that any woman—any human soul—could so readily believe such things of another. It is hard, and it is sad. Where I have erred, she does not accuse; she does not know. And he... he never told her. I should have known he would not!"

She lifted her chin, finding a strange pride in the delicacy of Mr. Thornton's conduct. But a new, sharper thought pierced her resolve. "He, too, must suspect poor Frederick of some other attachment." A flush warmed her cheeks at the admission. "I see it now. It is not merely that he knows of my deceit, but that he believes another cares for me; and that I—Oh, dear! Oh, dear! What am I to do? What do I mean? Why does it matter what he thinks, beyond the loss of his regard for my honesty? I cannot say. But I am wretched! Oh, how this past year has drained me! I have skipped from childhood to old age. I have known no youth, no womanhood; the dreams of womanhood have closed forever, for I shall never marry. I anticipate sorrows as if I were already an elder, with a tear-stained spirit. I am weary of this constant demand for strength. I could endure it for my father; that is a natural, holy duty. I could even muster the energy to resent Mrs. Thornton's unjust, impertinent suspicions. But it is crushing to feel how completely he misunderstands me. What has come over me to make me so morbid today? I do not know. I only know I cannot help it. I must yield sometimes. No, I will not," she declared, springing to her feet. "I will not dwell on myself or my station. I will not dissect my own feelings. It serves no purpose now. Someday, if I live to be an old woman, I may sit by the fire, gazing into the embers, mourning the life that might have been."

Throughout this turmoil, she hastily dressed to leave the house, pausing only to wipe her eyes with impatient gestures, knowing the tears would return despite her bravado.

"I suppose many a woman makes as tragic a mistake as I have, only discovering it too late. How proudly and impertinently I spoke to him that day! But I did not know then. It crept upon me slowly, and I cannot pinpoint its origin. Now, I will not yield. It will be difficult to treat him as before, with this miserable burden on my conscience; but I will be calm, quiet, and speak little. Though, to be sure, I may not see him; he keeps his distance, evidently. That would be worse than all. And yet, no wonder he avoids me, believing what he must."

She stepped out, moving rapidly toward the countryside, attempting to drown her reflections in the speed of her motion.

Upon her return to the doorstep, her father approached. "Good girl!" he said. "You've been to Mrs. Boucher's. I was just meaning to go there, if I had time, before dinner."

"No, papa; I have not," Margaret replied, flushing. "I never thought about her. But I will go directly after dinner; I will go while you are taking your nap."

True to her word, she visited the sick woman. Mrs. Boucher was truly ill, not merely ailing. The kind neighbor who had arrived previously had assumed charge of the household. Some children had been sent to neighbors; Mary Higgins had fetched the three youngest at dinner, and since then, Nicholas had sought the doctor. He had not yet arrived; Mrs. Boucher was dying, and all that remained was waiting. Margaret hoped to hear the doctor's opinion later, so she decided to visit the Higginses in the meantime, perhaps learning if Nicholas had managed to reach Mr. Thornton.

She found Nicholas busily spinning a penny on the dresser for the amusement of three small children who clung to him fearlessly. He, along with them, smiled at the coin's long rotation, and Margaret took this happy engagement as a good sign. When the penny finally stopped, little Johnnie began to cry.

"Come to me," Margaret said, lifting the child from the dresser and holding him close. She held her watch to his ear while asking Nicholas if he had seen Mr. Thornton.

The look on Nicholas's face shifted instantly.

"Ay!" he said. "I've seen and heard too much on him."

"He refused you, then?" Margaret asked, sorrowfully.

"To be sure. I knew he'd do it all along. It's no good expecting mercy from masters like him. You're a stranger and a foreigner, unlikely to know their ways; but I knew it."

"I am sorry I asked. Was he angry? Did he not speak to you as Hamper did?"

"He wasn't overly civil!" Nicholas spun the penny again, partly for his own amusement, partly for the children's. "Never you fret. I'm exactly where I was. I'll go on tramping tomorrow. I gave him as good as I got. I told him I didn't hold the good opinion of him that would make me come a second time on my own; but you advised me to come, and I was beholden to you."

"You told him I sent you?"

"I dunno if I called you by your name. I don't think I did. I said a woman who knew no better had advised me to come and see if there was a soft spot in his heart."

"And he—?" Margaret prompted.

"Said I should tell you to mind your own business. That's the longest spin yet, my lads. And those are civil words compared to what he used to me. But never mind. We're where we were; and I'll break stones on the road before I let these little ones starve."

Margaret placed the struggling Johnnie back on the dresser.

"I am sorry I asked you to go to Mr. Thornton's. I am disappointed in him."

A faint noise sounded behind her. Both she and Nicholas turned simultaneously to find Mr. Thornton standing there, his face a mask of displeased surprise. Acting on a swift impulse, Margaret stepped out before him, saying nothing, bowing low to conceal the sudden pallor that washed over her face. He bowed equally low in return before closing the door. As she hurried toward Mrs. Boucher's, she heard the latch click, and it seemed to complete the measure of her mortification. He, too, was annoyed to find her there.

In his heart, Mr. Thornton possessed tenderness—a "soft place," as Nicholas had called it—but he guarded it with jealous pride, keeping it sacred and safe, wary of any circumstance that might intrude. Yet, if he dreaded exposing this tenderness, he was equally eager that all men recognize his justice. He felt unjust in having given such a scornful hearing to anyone who had waited with humble patience for five hours to speak to him. That the man had spoken saucily when given the chance meant nothing to Mr. Thornton; he actually liked him for it. He was conscious of his own irritability at the time, which likely made them even. It was the five hours of waiting that struck him. He had not five hours to spare himself, but he had sacrificed an hour or two of his hard intellectual and physical labor to gather evidence regarding the truth of Higgins's story, the nature of his character, and the tenor of his life. He tried not to be, but was convinced that all Higgins had said was true. Then the conviction settled in, like a spell, touching the latent tenderness of his heart. The patience of the man, the simple generosity of the motive (for he had learned of the quarrel between Boucher and Higgins), made him forget entirely the mere reasonings of justice, leaping over them by a diviner instinct. He came to offer Higgins work, and he was more annoyed to find Margaret there than by hearing her last words; for then he understood she was the woman who had urged Higgins to approach him. He dreaded admitting any thought of her as a motive for what he was doing, simply because it was right.

"So that was the lady you spoke of as a woman?" he said indignantly to Higgins. "You might have told me who she was."

"And then, maybe, you'd have spoken of her more civilly than you did; you'd gotten a mother who might have kept your tongue in check when you were talking of women being at the root of all plagues."

"Of course you told that to Miss Hale?"

"In course I did. Leastways, I reckon I did. I told her she wasn't to meddle again in aught that concerned you."

"Whose children are those—yours?" Mr. Thornton had a pretty good notion whose they were from what he had heard, but he felt awkward turning the conversation from this unpromising beginning.

"They're not mine, and they are mine."

"They are the children you spoke of to me this morning?"

"When you said," replied Higgins, turning around with ill-smothered fierceness, "that my story might be true or might not, but it were a very unlikely one. Master, I've not forgotten."

Mr. Thornton was silent for a moment; then he said, "No more have I. I remember what I said. I spoke to you about those children in a way I had no business to. I did not believe you. I could not have taken care of another man's children myself if he had acted toward me as I hear Boucher did toward you. But I know now you spoke truth. I beg your pardon."

Higgins did not turn or immediately respond. But when he did speak, it was in a softened tone, though the words remained gruff.

"You've no business to pry into what happened between Boucher and me. He's dead, and I'm sorry. That's enough."

"So it is. Will you take work with me? That's what I came to ask."

Higgins's obstinacy wavered, recovered strength, and stood firm. He would not speak. Mr. Thornton would not ask again. Higgins's eye fell on the children.

"You've called me impudent, and a liar, and a mischief-maker, and you might have said with some truth that I was now and then given to drink.
