And, I've called you a tyrant, and an old bulldog, and a hard, cruel master; that's where it stands. But for the children, Master, do you think we can ever get on together?"

"Well!" said Mr. Thornton, half-laughing, "it was not my proposal that we should go together. But there's one comfort on your own showing. We neither of us can think much worse of the other than we do now."

"That's true," said Higgins, reflectively. "I've been thinking ever since I saw you what a mercy it was you didn't take me on, for that I never saw a man whom I could less abide. But that's maybe been a hasty judgment; and work's work to such as me. So, Master, I'll come; and what's more, I thank you; and that's a deal from me," he said more frankly, suddenly turning round and facing Mr. Thornton fully for the first time.

"And this is a deal from me," said Mr. Thornton, giving Higgins's hand a good grip. "Now mind you come sharp to your time," he continued, resuming the master's tone. "I'll have no laggards at my mill. What fines we have, we keep pretty sharply. And the first time I catch you making mischief, off you go. So now you know where you are."

"You spoke of my wisdom this morning. I reckon I may bring it with me; or would you rather have me about my brains?"

"'Bout your brains if you use them for meddling with my business; with your brains if you can keep to your own."

"I shall need a deal of brains to settle where my business ends and yours begins."

"Your business has not begun yet, and mine stands still for me. So good afternoon."

Just before Mr. Thornton reached Mrs. Boucher's door, Margaret emerged. She did not see him, and he followed her for several yards, admiring her light, easy walk and her tall, graceful figure. Suddenly, this simple emotion of pleasure was tainted, poisoned by jealousy. He wished to overtake her, to speak to her, to see how she would receive him now that she must know he was aware of some other attachment. He wished, too, though he was somewhat ashamed of the wish, that she should know he had justified her wisdom in sending Higgins to him for work, and that he had repented his morning's decision.

He came up to her. She started.

"Allow me to say, Miss Hale, that you were rather premature in expressing your disappointment. I have taken Higgins on."

"I am glad of it," she said coldly.

"He tells me he repeated to you what I said this morning about—" Mr. Thornton hesitated. Margaret took it up:

"About women not meddling. You had a perfect right to express your opinion, which was a very correct one, I have no doubt. But," she went on a little more eagerly, "Higgins did not quite tell you the exact truth." The word "truth" reminded her of her own untruth, and she stopped short, feeling exceedingly uncomfortable.

Mr. Thornton was initially puzzled to

"I have no wrong, where I can claim no right,
Naught ta'en me fro, where I have nothing had,
Yet of my woe I cannot so be quite;
Namely, since that another may be glad
With that, that thus in sorrow makes me sad."

Margaret had not anticipated much personal delight from Mr. Bell's visit; she had merely looked forward to it for her father's sake. Yet, when her godfather arrived, she instantly settled into the most natural posture of friendship. He declared she possessed no merit in being what she was—a girl entirely after his own heart. It was an hereditary power, he insisted, that allowed her to walk in and take possession of his regard. In return, she credited him with being refreshingly young beneath his Fellow's cap and gown.

"Fresh and young in warmth and kindness, I mean," Mr. Bell corrected. "I must admit, however, that I think your opinions are the oldest and mustiest I have encountered in a long time."

"Hear this daughter of yours, Hale! Her residence in Milton has quite corrupted her. She's a democrat, a red republican, a member of the Peace Society, a socialist—"

"Papa, it is simply because I am standing up for the progress of commerce," Margaret replied. "Mr. Bell would have preferred keeping still, exchanging wild-beast skins for acorns."

"No, no. I'd dig the ground and grow potatoes. And I'd shave the wild-beast skin and make the wool into broadcloth. Don't exaggerate, missy. But I am tired of this bustle. Everyone rushing over everyone else, in their hurry to get rich."

"It is not everyone who can sit comfortably in a set of college rooms and let his riches grow without any exertion of his own. No doubt there are many men here who would be thankful if their property increased as yours has, without taking any trouble," said Mr. Hale.

"I don't believe they would. It is the bustle and the struggle they like. As for sitting still, learning from the past, or shaping the future by faithful work done in a prophetic spirit—Why! Pooh! I don't believe there is a man in Milton who knows how to sit still; and it is a great art."

"Milton people, I suspect, think Oxford men don't know how to move. It would be a very good thing if they mixed a little more."

"It might be good for the Miltoners. Many things might be good for them which would be very disagreeable for other people."

"Are you not a Milton man yourself?" asked Margaret. "I should have thought you would have been proud of your town."

"I confess, I don't see what there is to be proud of. If you'll only come to Oxford, Margaret, I will show you a place to glory in."

"Well!" said Mr. Hale. "Mr. Thornton is coming to drink tea with us tonight, and he is as proud of Milton as you are of Oxford. You two must try and make each other a little more liberal-minded."

"I don't want to be more liberal-minded, thank you," said Mr. Bell.

"Is Mr. Thornton coming to tea, Papa?" Margaret asked in a low voice.

"Either to tea or soon after. He could not tell. He told us not to wait."

Mr. Thornton had determined that he would make no inquiry of his mother regarding how far she had executed her project of speaking to Margaret about the impropriety of her conduct. He felt pretty sure that, if this interview had taken place, his mother's account of what passed would only annoy and chagrin him, though he would all the time be aware of the coloring it received by passing through her mind. He shrank from hearing Margaret's very name mentioned; he, while he blamed her—while he was jealous of her—while he renounced her—loved her sorely, in spite of himself. He dreamt of her; he dreamt she came dancing towards him with outspread arms, and with a lightness and gaiety which made him loathe her, even while it allured him. But the impression of this figure of Margaret—with all Margaret's character taken out of it, as completely as if some evil spirit had got possession of her form—was so deeply stamped upon his imagination, that when he wakened he felt hardly able to separate the Una from the Duessa; and the dislike he had to the latter seemed to envelop and disfigure the former. Yet he was too proud to acknowledge his weakness by avoiding the sight of her. He would neither seek an opportunity of being in her company nor avoid it. To convince himself of his power of self-control, he lingered over every piece of business this afternoon; he forced every movement into unnatural slowness and deliberation; and it was consequently past eight o'clock before he reached Mr. Hale's. Then there were business arrangements to be transacted in the study with Mr. Bell; and the latter kept on, sitting over the fire, and talking wearily, long after all business was transacted, and when they might just as well have gone upstairs. But Mr. Thornton would not say a word about moving their quarters; he chafed and chafed, and thought Mr. Bell a most prosy companion; while Mr. Bell returned the compliment in secret, by considering Mr. Thornton about as brusque and curt a fellow as he had ever met with, and terribly gone off both in intelligence and manner. At last some slight noise in the room above suggested the desirableness of moving there. They found Margaret with a letter open before her, eagerly discussing its contents with her father. On the entrance of the gentlemen, it was immediately put aside; but Mr. Thornton's eager senses caught some few words of Mr. Hale's to Mr. Bell.

"A letter from Henry Lennox. It makes Margaret very hopeful."

Mr. Bell nodded. Margaret was red as a rose when Mr. Thornton looked at her. He had the greatest mind in the world to get up and go out of the room that very instant, and never set foot in the house again.

"We were thinking," said Mr. Hale, "that you and Mr. Thornton had taken Margaret's advice, and were each trying to convert the other, you were so long in the study."

"And you thought there would be nothing left of us but an opinion, like the Kilkenny cat's tail. Pray whose opinion did you think would have the most obstinate vitality?"

Mr. Thornton had not a notion what they were talking about, and disdained to inquire. Mr. Hale politely enlightened him.

"Mr. Thornton, we were accusing Mr. Bell this morning of a kind of Oxonian medieval bigotry against his native town; and we—Margaret, I believe—suggested that it would do him good to associate a little with Milton manufacturers."

"I beg your pardon. Margaret thought it would do the Milton manufacturers good to associate a little more with Oxford men. Now wasn't it so, Margaret?"

"I believe I thought it would do both good to see a little more of the other,—I did not know it was my idea any more than papa's."

"And so you see, Mr. Thornton, we ought to have been improving each other downstairs, instead of talking over vanished families of Smith's and Harrison's. However, I am willing to do my part now. I wonder when you Milton men intend to live. All your lives seem to be spent in gathering together the materials for life."

"By living, I suppose you mean enjoyment."

"Yes, enjoyment,—I don't specify of what, because I trust we should both consider mere pleasure as very poor enjoyment."

"I would rather have the nature of the enjoyment defined."

"Well! enjoyment or leisure—enjoyment of the power and influence which money gives. You are all striving for money. What do you want it for?"

Mr. Thornton was silent. Then he said, "I really don't know. But money is not what _I_ strive for."

"What then?"

"It is a home question. I shall have to lay myself open to such a catechist, and I am not sure that I am prepared to do it."

"No!" said Mr. Hale; "don't let us be personal in our catechism. You are neither of you representative men; you are each of you too individual for that."

"I am not sure whether to consider that as a compliment or not. I should like to be the representative of Oxford, with its beauty and its learning, and its proud old history. What do you say, Margaret; ought I to be flattered?"

"I don't know Oxford. But there is a difference between being the representative of a city and the representative man of its inhabitants."

"Very true, Miss Margaret. Now I remember, you were against me this morning, and were quite Miltonian and manufacturing in your preferences."

Margaret saw the quick glance of surprise that Mr. Thornton gave her, and she was annoyed at the construction which he might put on this speech of Mr. Bell's. Mr. Bell went on—

"Ah! I wish I could show you our High Street—our Radcliffe Square. I am leaving out our colleges, just as I give Mr. Thornton leave to omit his factories in speaking of the charms of Milton. I have a right to abuse my birth-place. Remember I am a Milton man."

Mr. Thornton was annoyed more than he ought to have been at all that Mr. Bell was saying. He was not in a mood for joking. At another time, he could have enjoyed Mr. Bell's half testy condemnation of a town where the life was so at variance with every habit he had formed; but now, he was galled enough to attempt to defend what was never meant to be seriously attacked.

"I don't set up Milton as a model of a town."

"Not in architecture?" slyly asked Mr. Bell.

"No! We've been too busy to attend to mere outward appearances."

"Don't say _mere_ outward appearances," said Mr. Hale, gently. "They impress us all, from childhood upward—every day of our life."

"Wait a little while," said Mr. Thornton. "Remember we are of a different race from the Greeks, to whom beauty was everything, and to whom Mr. Bell might speak of a life of leisure and serene enjoyment, much of which entered in through their outward senses. I don't mean to despise them, any more than I would ape them. But I belong to Teutonic blood; it is little mingled in this part of England to what it is in others; we retain much of their language; we retain more of their spirit; we do not look upon life as a time for enjoyment, but as a time for action and exertion. Our glory and our beauty arise out of our outward strength, which makes us victorious over material resistance, and over greater difficulties still. We are Teutonic up here in Darkshire in another way. We hate to have laws made for us at a distance. We wish people would allow us to right ourselves, instead of continually meddling, with their imperfect legislation. We stand up for self-government, and oppose centralisation."

"In short, you would like the Heptarchy back again. Well, at any rate, I revoke what I said this morning—that you Milton people did not reverence the past. You are regular worshippers of Thor."

"If we do not reverence the past as you do in Oxford, it is because we want something which can apply to the present more directly. It is fine when the study of the past leads to a prophecy of the future. But to men groping in new circumstances, it would be finer if the words of experience could direct us how to act in what concerns us most intimately and immediately; which is full of difficulties to be encountered; and upon the mode in which they are met and conquered—not merely pushed aside for the time—depends our future. But no! People can speak of Utopia much more easily than of the next day's duty; and yet when that duty is all done by others, who so ready to cry, 'Fie, for shame!'"

"And all this time I don't see what you are talking about. Would you Milton men condescend to send up your to-day's difficulty to Oxford? You have not tried us yet."

Mr. Thornton laughed outright at this. "I believe I was talking with reference to a good deal that has been troubling us of late; I was thinking of the strikes we have gone through, which are troublesome and injurious things enough, as I am finding to my cost. And yet this last strike, under which I am smarting, has been respectable."

"A respectable strike!" said Mr. Bell. "That sounds as if you were far gone in the worship of Thor."

Margaret felt, rather than saw, that Mr. Thornton was chagrined by the repeated turning into jest of what he was feeling as very serious. She tried to change the conversation from a subject about which one party cared little, while, to the other, it was deeply, because personally, interesting. She forced herself to say something.

"Edith says she finds the printed calicoes in Corfu better and cheaper than in London."

"Does she?" said her father, "I think that must be one of Edith's exaggerations. Are you sure of it, Margaret?"

"I am sure she says so, papa."

"Then I am sure of the fact," said Mr. Bell. "Margaret, I go so far in my idea of your truthfulness, that it shall cover your cousin's character. I don't believe a cousin of yours could exaggerate."

"Is Miss Hale so remarkable for truth?" said Mr. Thornton, bitterly. The moment he had done so, he could have bitten his tongue out. What was he? And why should he stab her with her shame in this way? How evil he was that night: possessed by ill-humour at being detained so long from her; irritated by the mention of some name, because he thought it belonged to a more successful lover; now ill-tempered because he had been unable to cope, with a light heart, against one who was trying, by gay and careless speeches, to make the evening pass pleasantly away,—the kind old friend to all parties, whose manner by this time might be well known to Mr. Thornton, who had been acquainted with him for many years. And

The winter deepened, yet the days grew longer without the promised warmth of hope that usually greets a February sun. Mrs. Thornton had withdrawn entirely from the household, and Mr. Thornton's visits, though occasional, were restricted to the study where he attended to his father. To Mr. Hale, these rare encounters seemed to hold a value precisely because of their scarcity. Margaret observed that Mr. Thornton spoke of her with a calm, steady friendliness, neither shunning nor seeking mention of her name, suggesting his silence stemmed from the complexities of his business affairs rather than any personal grievance.

Margaret herself lacked the spirit to lift her father's mood. The long period of anxiety and storm that had preceded this dreary peace had drained her mind of its elasticity. She busied herself teaching the younger Boucher children and striving for goodness, yet her heart remained dead to the effort; she performed her duties with painful punctuality but remained distant from any cheerfulness. Her life felt bleak, save for her silent, unconscious piety in comforting her father. She anticipated his quiet wishes with a meek obedience that was both complete and beautiful. Then came the news of Frederick's marriage. Letters arrived from him and Dolores; Dolores wrote in a mix of Spanish and English, while Frederick's letters showed the influence of his bride's tongue, filled with playful inversions.

Upon receiving Henry Lennox's letter, which cast little hope for Frederick's exoneration at a court-martial due to missing witnesses, Frederick had written to Margaret a vehement renunciation of England. He expressed a desire to "unnative" himself, refusing a pardon or a return to the country. At first, these words made Margaret weep, seeming so unnatural, but upon reflection, she recognized them as the raw poignancy of crushed hope. In his subsequent letters, Frederick spoke joyfully of his future, and Margaret found within herself the patience she had craved. The charming, timid letters from Dolores began to delight both Margaret and her father. The young Spaniard's evident desire to impress her lover's English relations revealed a feminine care in every correction, and her wedding announcement included a magnificent black lace mantilla chosen for Margaret, whom Frederick described as a paragon of beauty and virtue. With this union, Frederick's social standing soared; he became a junior partner in Barbour and Co., one of Spain's most extensive trading houses. Margaret smiled and then sighed, recalling her old rants against trade, watching her "preux chevalier" brother transform into a merchant. Yet, she rebelled against the confusion of equating a Spanish trader with a Milton millowner. Regardless, Frederick was undeniably happy, and the mantilla was exquisite.

Spring brought a new trial: her father experienced difficulties breathing, distressing him greatly. Though Margaret was less alarmed as the symptoms subsided, she urged him to accept Mr. Bell's invitation to visit him in Oxford that April. Mr. Bell's letter specifically commanded her attendance, yet Margaret felt a profound relief in remaining quietly at home, free from responsibility, allowing her mind and heart to rest in a way she had not for over two years.

When her father departed for the railroad, Margaret acutely felt the immense pressure she had carried. It was almost stunning to realize she was finally at liberty, with no one depending on her for cheer or care, no invalid to tend. She could be idle, silent, forgetful, and, most liberating of all, unhappy if she chose. For months, her personal sorrows had been stuffed into a dark cupboard; now, she had the leisure to retrieve them, to mourn, to analyze, and to seek a method of subduing them into peace. Sitting motionless in the drawing-room for hours, she reviewed the bitterness of every memory with unwincing resolution. Once, at the stinging thought of the faithlessness that bred her abasing falsehood, she cried aloud. She refused to acknowledge the force of that temptation anymore; her plans for Frederick had failed, leaving the temptation a dead mockery. The lie had been despicably foolish in the light of events, proving faith in truth to be infinitely wiser.

In her nervous agitation, she opened a book belonging to her father. The words that caught her eye seemed tailored to her state of acute self-abasement: a French passage urging her not to despair in shame, but to correct her heart through compassion, to reveal themselves from the pit, and to reclaim the path of humility. "The way of humility," Margaret thought. "That is what I have missed! But courage, little heart. We will turn back, and by God's help, we may find the lost path."

Rising immediately, she decided to engage in work that would take her out of herself. Calling in Martha as she ascended the stairs, she attempted to pierce the grave, respectful, almost mechanical manner that crusted over the servant's individuality. Finally, naming Mrs. Thornton struck the right chord. Martha's face brightened, and she recounted how her father, in his early life as a warehouse clerk, had once shown kindness to Mrs. Thornton's husband. Years later, when Martha and her sister were nearly grown and destitute after her mother's death, it was Mrs. Thornton who had sought them out, saving them from being "lost." Martha recalled how Mrs. Thornton and Mr. Thornton had nursed her through a fever, ignoring the doctor's warnings about contagion, while Miss Fanny visited others destined for marriage.

"Miss Fanny going to be married!" Margaret exclaimed.

"Yes, to a wealthy gentleman named Watson, older than she, with mills beyond Hayleigh. It is a very good match," Martha replied, quickly sweeping the hearth and retreating with her usual wooden demeanor. Margaret suppressed a bad habit of imagining how news of Mr. Thornton would affect him.

The next day, after lessons with the Boucher children and a long walk, Margaret visited Mary Higgins. To her surprise, Nicholas was already home, the lengthening light having deceived her about the hour. He seemed to have entered a phase of humility, quieter and less self-asserting.

"So the old gentleman is away on his travels?" Nicholas said. "The little ones told me. They are sharp, though one of them is in her grave. There's something in the weather that sets folk wandering. My master is spinning about the world somewhere."

"Is that why you are home so early?" Margaret asked innocently.

"You know nought about it," Nicholas replied contemptuously. "I am not one with two faces. I counted every clock in the town before leaving. Thornton is good enough to fight, but too good to be cheated. You got me the place, and I thank you. Thornton's is not a bad mill. Stand down, lad, and say your pretty hymn to Miss Marget."

The little boy recited a Methodist hymn, far above his comprehension, yet he repeated it with the cadence of a parliamentarian. When Margaret applauded, Nicholas demanded another, revealing an odd, unconscious interest in sacred things he had once scorned.

Returning home late, Margaret found comfort in knowing no one had waited for her. She examined a packet of letters, intending to destroy them, but paused at four or five from Henry Lennox regarding Frederick's affairs. Initially reading them to assess the chances of her brother's justification, she was struck by the personal revelation within their stiff wording. Lennox had never forgotten his connection to her. The letters were clever but lacked warmth; still, she preserved them as valuable.

After this task, a reverie took hold, and the thought of her absent father haunted her. She almost blamed herself for feeling her solitude as a relief, but these two days had restored her strength. Plans that seemed like tasks now appeared as pleasures. The morbid scales had fallen from her eyes. If Mr. Thornton would restore their friendship, or even simply visit her father as he once did, she felt her future might lie clear and even, though not brilliant. She sighed as she prepared for bed; despite her duty to her father, an anxiety and pang of sorrow lingered at her heart.

That April evening, Mr. Hale thought of Margaret with the same persistence she felt for him. He had grown weary of visiting old friends, fearing his altered opinions would alienate them. Yet, those who had once loved him for his delicate thought and feeling welcomed him with protective kindness, forgetting his views or remembering them only to add a tender gravity to their manner.

"I'm afraid we've done too much," Mr. Bell said. "You're suffering from living too long in that Milton air."

"I am tired," Mr. Hale replied. "But it is not the air. I am fifty-five."

"Nonsense! I am over sixty and feel no loss. Why, you're quite a young man."

Mr. Hale shook his head. "These last few years!" he said, then rose with trembling earnestness. "Bell, do not think that if I could have foreseen the suffering of the one I loved, I would have undone my change of opinion or my resignation. Even if I knew the cruellest martyrdom awaited me, I would have taken that step of openly leaving the church. I might have acted more wisely for my family, but I do not think God endowed me with over-much wisdom or strength."

Mr. Bell blew his nose ostentatiously. "He gave you strength to do what your conscience told you was right. I know I am set down as a wise man, strong-minded, yet the veriest idiot who obeys his simple law of right is wiser than I. What gulls men are!"

After a pause, Mr. Hale spoke of Margaret. "If I die..."

"Nonsense!"

"What will become of her? I suppose the Lennoxes will ask her to live with them. Her Aunt Shaw loved her, but she forgets to love the absent."

"A common fault. Who are the Lennoxes?"

"The Captain is handsome and fluent. Edith is a sweet, spoiled beauty. Margaret loves her, and Edith loves her as much as she can spare."

"Now, Hale, you know that girl has nearly all my heart. I became her slave during my visit here. She looks grand and serene, as if she has secured victory despite her struggles. All I have is at her service, and when I die, she shall be my principal charge. I will be her *preux chevalier*, gouty and sixty though I be. Seriously, your daughter shall be my life's work."

Mr. Bell had a prophetic eye; he might have seen the torch inverted and the angel of death beckoning. That night, Mr. Hale laid his head down on the pillow where it would never stir again. The servant entering in the morning found no answer, only a calm, beautiful face lying white and cold under the seal of death. The heart had ceased as he lay down.

Mr. Bell was stunned, recovering only when he could dismiss the idea of foul play. "A coroner's inquest? Pooh. Natural end of a heart complaint. Poor old Hale! You wore out that tender heart before its time." He ordered a carpet-bag packed and a cab hired, rushing to the railway station within twenty minutes. On the train, he tried to comprehend how a man alive yesterday could be dead today, tears stealing out between his lashes before he forced a severe cheerfulness.

Sitting far away on the same side was Mr. Thornton. Mr. Bell peered over the *Times* and recognized him.

"Why, Thornton! Is that you?" Mr. Bell exclaimed, moving closer and shaking his hand until it relaxed to wipe away tears. "I am going to Milton on a melancholy errand. To break to Hale's daughter the news of his sudden death!"

"Death! Mr. Hale dead!"

"Ay. He went to bed well last night and was quite cold this morning."

"Where?"

"At Oxford. He hadn't been there in seventeen years. This is the end of it."

Silence stretched for a quarter of an hour. Then Mr. Thornton said, "And she!" and stopped abruptly.

"Margaret you mean. Yes, I am going to tell her. Poor fellow! How full his thoughts were of her all last night! Good God! Last night only. And how immeasurably distant he is now! But I take Margaret as my child for his sake. I said last night I would take her for her own sake. Well, I take her for both."

"What will become of her?"

"I fancy two people will wait for her: myself, and the Lennoxes. I would take a live dragon into my house if I could make my old age happy with Margaret as a daughter. But there are the Lennoxes!"

"Who are they?" Mr. Thornton asked with trembling interest.

"Smart London people. The Captain married her cousin. There is her Aunt, Mrs. Shaw. And there is that brother!"

"What brother?"

"No, a clever Lennox, a young barrister who has been setting his cap at Margaret for five years. He was kept back by want of fortune, but that will be done away with."

"How?"

"She'll have my money at my death. If she likes him, I might find another way of getting a home through marriage. I'm dreadfully afraid of being tempted at an unguarded moment by her aunt."

Neither man was in a laughing mood. Mr. Bell whistled a long, hissing breath and shifted seats, while Mr. Thornton sat immovably still, eyes fixed on a spot in the newspaper.

"Where have you been?" Mr. Bell asked eventually.

"To Havre. Trying to detect the secret of the great rise in cotton prices."

"Ugh! Cotton and smoke. Poor old Hale! If you could have known the change it was to him from Helstone. Do you know the New Forest?"

"Yes."

"Then you can fancy the difference between it and Milton. What part were you in? Were you ever at Helstone?"

"I have seen it. It was a great change to leave it and come to Milton."

Mr. Bell took up his newspaper, resolved to avoid further conversation, while Mr. Bell focused on how to break the news.

She stood at an upstairs window; she saw him alight; she guessed the truth with an instinctive flash. Standing in the middle of the drawing-room, as if arrested by a thought that turned her to stone, she was white and immovable.

"Oh! don't tell me! I know it from your face! You would have sent—you would not have

"When some beloved voice that was to you
Both sound and sweetness, faileth suddenly,
And silence, against which you dare not cry,
Aches round you like a strong disease and new,—
What hope? what help? what music will undo
That silence to your sense?"

The blow had struck with such force that Margaret sank into a state of total prostration. It was not a collapse marked by sobs or tears, nor did she find solace in words. She lay upon the sofa, eyes closed, speaking only in faint whispers when addressed, and remaining otherwise utterly still. Mr. Bell sat beside her, perplexed and paralyzed by his inability to act. He dared not leave her side, yet he dared not suggest returning to Oxford; her physical exhaustion was absolute, rendering any journey impossible, and the prospect of the sights awaiting her there was a horror he could not impose. Sitting by the fire, he wrestled with the silence of the room. Margaret lay motionless, almost breathless, beside him. He refused to depart even for the dinner Dixon had prepared downstairs. With a sobbing hospitality that seemed desperate, Dixon attempted to tempt him to eat, bringing a plate of food up to his chair. Usually particular and discerning about flavor, Mr. Bell now found the devilled chicken tasting of sawdust. He minced some of the fowl for Margaret, seasoning it heavily, but when Dixon, following his instructions, tried to feed her, the languid shake of her head made it clear that in her condition, food would only choke her rather than nourish her.

Mr. Bell sighed deeply, lifted his stiff, travel-worn limbs from their easy position, and followed Dixon out of the room.

"I cannot leave her," he muttered. "I must write to them at Oxford to ensure preparations are underway; they can proceed until my arrival. Can Mrs. Lennox not come? I will write to insist she must. The girl needs a woman-friend nearby, if only to talk her into a good, cathartic fit of crying."

Dixon was weeping enough for two, but after wiping her eyes and steadying her voice, she informed Mr. Bell that Mrs. Lennox was too close to her confinement to undertake any journey.

"Well! I suppose we must summon Mrs. Shaw; she has returned to England, has she not?"

"Yes, sir, she has returned; but I do not think she would wish to leave Mrs. Lennox at such a critical time," Dixon replied, harboring a distinct disapproval of a stranger entering the household to share in her ruling care of Margaret.

"Critical time be—" Mr. Bell cut himself off with a cough. "She could be content to remain in Venice or Naples, or some of those Popish locales, during the last 'critical time' which occurred in Corfu, I believe. And what does that little prosperous woman's 'critical time' signify, in comparison with that poor creature there—that helpless, homeless, friendless Margaret—lying as still on that sofa as if it were an altar-tomb, and she the stone statue upon it. I tell you, Mrs. Shaw shall come. See that a room, or whatever she requires, is prepared by tomorrow night. I will ensure she arrives."

True to his command, Mr. Bell penned a letter. Mrs. Shaw, with many tears, declared it so reminiscent of the dear General when he was about to suffer a fit of gout that she would always treasure and preserve it. Had he offered her the choice, urging her as if refusal were a viable option, she might have declined—her sympathy for Margaret was genuine and sincere. It required the sharp, uncourteous command to overcome her inertia and allow herself to be packed by her maid after the boxes were completed. Edith, dressed in cap, shawl, and tears, emerged onto the top of the stairs as Captain Lennox assisted her mother down to the carriage:

"Do not forget, Mamma; Margaret must come and live with us. Sholto will go to Oxford on Wednesday, and you must send word via Mr. Bell when we are to expect you. And if you require Sholto, he can travel on from Oxford to Milton. Do not forget, Mamma; you are to bring Margaret back."

Edith retreated into the drawing-room. Mr. Henry Lennox was there, cutting open the pages of a new review. Without lifting his head, he remarked, "If you do not wish Sholto to be absent for so long, Edith, I hope you will permit me to descend to Milton and offer whatever assistance I can."

"Oh, thank you," said Edith. "I am sure old Mr. Bell will manage everything; perhaps more help is not needed. One does not expect much *savoir-faire* from a resident Fellow. But, dear, darling Margaret; won't it be nice to have her here again? You were both great allies years ago."

" Were we?" he asked indifferently, feigning interest in a passage of the review.

"Well, perhaps not—I forget. I was so consumed with thoughts of Sholto. But isn't it fortunate that if my uncle were to die, it should be just now, when we have returned home, settled in the old house, and are fully prepared to receive Margaret? Poor thing! What a change it will be for her from Milton! I shall have new chintz for her bedroom, make it look new and bright, and cheer her up."

In the same spirit of kindness, Mrs. Shaw journeyed to Milton. She occasionally dreaded the first meeting, wondering how it would be endured, but more frequently planned how soon she could extract Margaret from "that horrid place" and return her to the pleasant comforts of Harley Street.

"Oh dear!" she told her maid, gazing out the window. "Look at those chimneys! My poor sister Hale! I do not think I could have rested at Naples if I had known what it was! I must have come and fetched her and Margaret away." To herself, she acknowledged that she had always considered her brother-in-law somewhat weak, but never so weak as now, seeing the lovely Helstone home he had exchanged for such a desolate location.

Margaret remained in the same state: white, motionless, speechless, tearless. They had told her that her Aunt Shaw was coming, yet she expressed neither surprise, pleasure, nor dislike. Mr. Bell, whose appetite had returned and who appreciated Dixon's efforts to satisfy it, vainly urged her to taste some sweetbreads stewed with oysters. She shook her head with the same quiet obstinacy as the previous day, and he was obliged to console himself for her rejection by consuming them all himself. Yet, Margaret was the first to hear the cab stopping outside, bringing her aunt from the railway station. Her eyelids quivered, her lips colored and trembled. Mr. Bell went down to meet Mrs. Shaw; and when they ascended, Margaret was standing, trying to steady her dizzy frame. Upon seeing her aunt, she moved forward into the open arms waiting for her, and first found the passionate relief of tears on her aunt's shoulder. All thoughts of quiet, habitual love, of tenderness for years, of relationship to the dead, all that inexplicable likeness in look, tone, and gesture that seemed to belong to one family, and which reminded Margaret so forcibly at this moment of her mother, came to melt and soften her numbed heart into an overflow of warm tears.

Mr. Bell slipped out of the room and descended into the study, where he ordered a fire and attempted to divert his thoughts by examining the various books. Each volume brought a remembrance or suggestion of his dead friend. It might have been a change of employment from his two days of watching Margaret, but it was no change of thought. He was glad to catch the sound of Mr. Thornton's voice making inquiries at the door. Dixon rather cavalierly dismissed him; for with the appearance of Mrs. Shaw's maid came visions of former grandeur, of the Beresford blood, of the "station" (as she pleased to term it) from which her young lady had been ousted, and to which she was now, please God, to be restored. These visions, which she had dwelt upon with complacency in her conversation with Mrs. Shaw's maid—while skilfully eliciting all circumstances of state and consequence connected with the Harley Street establishment for the edification of the listening Martha—made Dixon inclined to be supercilious in her treatment of any inhabitant of Milton. Thus, though she always stood in some awe of Mr. Thornton, she was as curt as she dared be in telling him he could see none of the inmates of the house that night. It was rather uncomfortable to be contradicted in her statement by Mr. Bell opening the study door and calling out:

"Thornton! Is that you? Come in for a minute or two; I want to speak to you."

So Mr. Thornton entered the study, and Dixon had to retreat into the kitchen, reinstating her own esteem with a prodigious story of Sir John Beresford's coach and six when he was high sheriff.

"I do not know what I wanted to say to you after all. Only it is dull enough to sit in a room where everything speaks to you of a dead friend. Yet Margaret and her aunt must have the drawing-room to themselves!"

"Is Mrs.—is her aunt come?" asked Mr. Thornton.

"Come? Yes! Maid and all. One would have thought she might have come alone at such a time! And now I shall have to turn out and find my way to the Clarendon."

"You must not go to the Clarendon. We have five or six empty bedrooms at home."

"Well aired?"

"I think you may trust my mother for that."

"Then I will only run upstairs to wish that wan girl good-night, make my bow to her aunt, and go off with you straight."

Mr. Bell remained upstairs for some time. Mr. Thornton began to think it long, for he was full of business and had hardly been able to spare the time for running up to Crampton to inquire how Miss Hale was.

When they had set out upon their walk, Mr. Bell said:

"I was detained by those women in the drawing-room. Mrs. Shaw is anxious to get home—on account of her daughter, she says—and wants Margaret to go off with her at once. Now she is no more fit for traveling than I am for flying. Besides, she says, and very justly, that she has friends she must see—that she must wish good-bye to several people; and then her aunt worried her about old claims, and was she forgetful of old friends? And she said, with a great burst of crying, she should be glad enough to go from a place where she had suffered so much. Now I must return to Oxford tomorrow, and I do not know on which side of the scale to throw in my voice."

He paused, as if asking a question; but he received no answer from his companion, the echo of whose thoughts kept repeating—

"Where she had suffered so much." Alas! and that was the way in which this eighteen months in Milton—to him so unspeakably precious, down to its very bitterness, which was worth all the rest of life's sweetness—would be remembered. Neither the loss of father, nor the loss of mother, dear as she was to Mr. Thornton, could have poisoned the remembrance of the weeks, the days, the hours, when a walk of two miles, every step of which was pleasant as it brought him nearer and nearer to her, took him to her sweet presence—every step of which was rich, as each recurring moment that bore him away from her made him recall some fresh grace in her demeanor, or pleasant pungency in her character. Yes! Whatever had happened to him, external to his relation to her, he could never have spoken of that time, when he could have seen her every day—when he had her within his grasp, as it were—as a time of suffering. It had been a royal time of luxury to him, with all its stings and contumelies, compared to the poverty that crept round and clipped the anticipation of the future down to sordid fact, and life without an atmosphere of either hope or fear.

Mrs. Thornton and Fanny were in the dining-room; the latter in a flutter of small exultation as the maid held up one glossy material after another to try the effect of the wedding dresses by candlelight. Her mother really tried to sympathize with her, but could not. Neither taste nor dress were in her line of subjects, and she heartily wished that Fanny had accepted her brother's offer of having the wedding clothes provided by some first-rate London dressmaker, without the endless troublesome discussions and unsettled waverings that arose from Fanny's desire to choose and superintend everything herself. Mr. Thornton was only too glad to mark his grateful approbation of any sensible man who could be captivated by Fanny's second-rate airs and graces, by giving her ample means for providing herself with the finery, which certainly rivaled, if it did not exceed, the lover in her estimation. When her brother and Mr. Bell came in, Fanny blushed and simpered, fluttering over the signs of her employment in a way that could not have failed to draw attention from anyone else but Mr. Bell. If he thought about her and her silks and satins at all, it was to compare her and them with the pale sorrow he had left behind him, sitting motionless, with bent head and folded hands, in a room where the stillness was so great that one might almost fancy the rush in one's straining ears was occasioned by the spirits of the dead, yet hovering round their beloved. For when Mr. Bell had first gone upstairs, Mrs. Shaw lay asleep on the sofa; and no sound broke the silence.

Mrs. Thornton gave Mr. Bell her formal, hospitable welcome. She was never so gracious as when receiving her son's friends in her son's house; and the more unexpected they were, the more honor to her admirable housekeeping preparations for comfort.

"How is Miss Hale?" she asked.

"About as broken down by this last stroke as she can be."

"I am sure it is very well for her that she has such a friend as you."

"I wish I were her only friend, madam

"The meanest thing to which we bid adieu,
Loses its meanness in the parting hour."

Mrs. Shaw harbored a dislike for Milton that was as vehement as her gentle nature allowed. To her, the town was a cacophony of noise and smoke; the poor walked the streets in filth, the wealthy ladies were overdressed, and not a single man, regardless of station, wore clothes that fitted him. She was certain Margaret would never reclaim her lost strength while remaining in Milton, and she herself feared a recurrence of her own nervous ailments. Margaret must return with her immediately. With such urgency, she pressed her point until the weary, broken-spirited Margaret reluctantly promised to depart as soon as Wednesday concluded. She would leave Dixon to manage the final arrangements: settling bills, disposing of furniture, and securing the house.

Before that mournful Wednesday—when Mr. Hale was to be interred far from the homes he had known, and far from the wife who lay lonely among strangers—a letter arrived from Mr. Bell.

"MY DEAR MARGARET:—I intended to return to Milton on Thursday, but unluckily, one of those rare occasions has arisen when we, as Plymouth Fellows, are summoned to perform a duty, and I cannot be absent from my post. Captain Lennox and Mr. Thornton are here. The former appears to be a smart, well-meaning man; he has proposed visiting Milton to assist you in searching for the will. Of course, there is none, or you would have found it by now if you had followed my instructions. Then the Captain declares he must take you and his mother-in-law home; given his wife's current state, I see no way you can expect him to stay away longer than Friday. However, your Dixon is trusty and can hold her—or your own—until I arrive. I shall entrust matters to my Milton attorney if no will exists, for I doubt this smart Captain possesses great business acumen. Nevertheless, his moustachios are splendid. A sale will be necessary, so select what items you wish reserved, or send a list later.

Two more points, and I am finished. You know, or if you do not, your poor father did, that you are to receive my money and goods upon my death. Not that I intend to die soon; I mention this merely to clarify the arrangement. These Lennoxes seem very fond of you now; perhaps they will continue to be, perhaps not. Therefore, it is best to establish a formal agreement: you shall pay them two hundred and fifty pounds annually as long as you and they find it pleasant to live together. (Do mind you are not cajoled into paying more for Dixon.) Thus, you will not be cast adrift if the Captain eventually desires his house for himself, though you may carry yourself and your two hundred and fifty pounds elsewhere; unless, indeed, I have not already claimed you to come and keep house for me. Regarding dress, Dixon, personal expenses, and confectionery (all young ladies consume until wisdom arrives with age), I shall consult a lady of my acquaintance to determine how much remains from your father before finalizing this.

Now, Margaret, have you flown out before reading this far, wondering what right the old man has to settle your affairs so cavalierly? I make no doubt you have. Yet the old man has a right. He has loved your father for thirty-five years; he stood beside him on his wedding day; he closed his eyes in death. Moreover, he is your godfather. Since he cannot do you much spiritual good, possessing a hidden consciousness of your superiority in such matters, he would fain do you the poor good of endowing you materially. Furthermore, the old man has no known relations on earth. 'Who is there to mourn for Adam Bell?' His whole heart is bent on this one thing, and Margaret Hale is not the girl to say him nay. Write by return if only two lines to tell me your answer. But _no thanks_."

Margaret took up a pen and scrawled with a trembling hand, "Margaret Hale is not the girl to say him nay." In her weakened state, she could conceive of no other words, yet she was vexed to use them. However, she was so fatigued even by this slight exertion that if she had thought of another form of acceptance, she could not have sat up to write a single syllable of it. She was obliged to lie down again and try not to think.

"My dearest child! Has that letter vexed or troubled you?"

"No!" said Margaret feebly. "I shall be better when tomorrow is over."

"I feel sure, darling, you won't be better until I get you out of this horrid air. How you have borne it these two years I cannot imagine."

"Where could I go? I could not leave papa and mamma."

"Well, do not distress yourself, my dear. I dare say it was all for the best, only I had no conception of how you were living. Our butler's wife lives in a better house than this."

"It is sometimes very pretty—in summer; you cannot judge by what it is now. I have been very happy here," and Margaret closed her eyes to stop the conversation.

The house now teemed with comfort compared to its previous state. The evenings were chilly, and by Mrs. Shaw's direction, fires were lit in every bedroom. She petted Margaret in every possible way, buying every delicacy or soft luxury in which she herself would have burrowed for comfort. Yet Margaret remained indifferent to these things; if they forced themselves upon her attention, it was merely as causes for gratitude to her aunt, who was putting herself so much out of her way to think of her. She was restless, though weak. All day long, she kept herself from thinking of the ceremony occurring at Oxford by wandering from room to room, languidly setting aside articles she wished to retain. Dixon followed her by Mrs. Shaw's desire, ostensibly to receive instructions, but with a private injunction to soothe her into repose as soon as possible.

"These books, Dixon, I will keep. All the rest you will send to Mr. Bell? They are of a kind that he will value for themselves, as well as for papa's sake. This... I should like you to take this to Mr. Thornton after I am gone. Stay; I will write a note with it." And she sat down hastily, as if afraid of thinking further, and wrote:

"DEAR SIR,—The accompanying book I am sure will be valued by you for the sake of my father, to whom it belonged.

Yours sincerely,

She set out again upon her travels through the house, turning over articles known to her from childhood with a sort of caressing reluctance to leave them—old-fashioned, worn, and shabby as they might be. But she hardly spoke again; and Dixon's report to Mrs. Shaw was that she doubted whether Miss Hale heard a word of what she said, though she talked the whole time in order to divert her intention. The consequence of being on her feet all day was excessive bodily weariness in the evening, and a better night's rest than she had had since hearing of Mr. Hale's death.

At breakfast time the next day, she expressed her wish to go and bid one or two friends goodbye. Mrs. Shaw objected:

"I am sure, my dear, you can have no friends here with whom you are sufficiently intimate to justify you in calling upon them so soon, before you have been at church."

"But today is my only day; if Captain Lennox comes this afternoon, and if we must—if I must really go tomorrow—"

"Oh, yes, we shall go tomorrow. I am more and more convinced that this air is bad for you, and makes you look so pale and ill; besides, Edith expects us; and she may be waiting for me; and you cannot be left alone, my dear, at your age. No; if you must make these calls, I will go with you. Dixon can get us a coach, I suppose?"

So Mrs. Shaw went to take care of Margaret and took her maid with her to manage the shawls and air-cushions. Margaret's face was too sad to lighten into a smile at all this preparation for paying two visits that she had often made by herself at all hours of the day. She was half afraid of owning that one place to which she was going was Nicholas Higgins'; all she could do was to hope her aunt would be indisposed to get out of the coach and walk up the court, and at every breath of wind have her face slapped by wet clothes hanging out to dry on ropes stretched from house to house.

There was a little battle in Mrs. Shaw's mind between ease and a sense of matronly propriety; but the former gained the day. With many injunctions to Margaret to be careful of herself and not catch any fever, such as was always lurking in such places, her aunt permitted her to go where she had often been before without taking any precaution or requiring any permission.

Nicholas was out; only Mary and one or two of the Boucher children were at home. Margaret was vexed with herself for not having timed her visit better. Mary had a very blunt intellect, although her feelings were warm and kind; and the instant she understood Margaret's purpose in coming to see them, she began to cry and sob with so little restraint that Margaret found it useless to say any of the thousand little things which had suggested themselves to her as she came along in the coach. She could only try to comfort her a little by suggesting the vague chance of their meeting again, at some possible time, in some possible place, and bid her tell her father how much she wished, if he could manage it, that he should come to see her when he had done his work in the evening.

As she was leaving the place, she stopped and looked around; then hesitated a little before she said:

"I should like to have some little thing to remind me of Bessy."

Instantly Mary's generosity was keenly alive. What could they give? And on Margaret singling out a little common drinking-cup, which she remembered as the one always standing by Bessy's side with a drink for her feverish lips, Mary said:

"Oh, take summat better; that only cost fourpence!"

"That will do, thank you," said Margaret; and she went quickly away, while the light caused by the pleasure of having something to give yet lingered on Mary's face.

"Now to Mrs. Thornton's," thought she to herself. "It must be done." But she looked rather rigid and pale at the thought of it, and had hard work finding the exact words to explain to her aunt who Mrs. Thornton was and why she should go to bid her farewell.

They—for Mrs. Shaw alighted here—were shown into the drawing-room, in which a fire had only just been kindled. Mrs. Shaw huddled herself up in her shawl and shivered.

"What an icy room!" she said.

They had to wait some time before Mrs. Thornton entered. There was some softening in her heart toward Margaret now that she was going away out of her sight. She remembered her spirit, as shown at various times and places, even more than the patience with which she had endured long and wearing cares. Her countenance was blander than usual as she greeted her; there was even a shade of tenderness in her manner as she noticed the white, tear-swollen face and the quiver in the voice which Margaret tried to make so steady.

"Allow me to introduce my aunt, Mrs. Shaw. I am going away from Milton tomorrow; I do not know if you are aware of it, but I wanted to see you once again, Mrs. Thornton, to—to apologize for my manner the last time I saw you; and to say that I am sure you meant kindly—however much we may have misunderstood each other."

Mrs. Shaw looked extremely perplexed by what Margaret had said. Thanks for kindness! and apologies for failure in good manners! But Mrs. Thornton replied:

"Miss Hale, I am glad you do me justice. I did no more than I believed to be my duty in remonstrating with you as I did. I have always desired to act the part of a friend to you. I am glad you do me justice."

"And," said Margaret, blushing excessively as she spoke, "will you do me justice, and believe that though I cannot—I do not choose—to give explanations of my conduct, I have not acted in the unbecoming way you apprehended?"

Margaret's voice was so soft, and her eyes so pleading, that Mrs. Thornton was for once affected by the charm of manner to which she had hitherto proved herself invulnerable.

"Yes, I do believe you. Let us say no more about it. Where are you going to reside, Miss Hale? I understood from Mr. Bell that you were going to leave Milton. You never liked Milton, you know," said Mrs. Thornton, with a sort of grim smile; "but for all that, you must not expect me to congratulate you on quitting it. Where shall you live?"

"With my aunt," replied Margaret, turning toward Mrs. Shaw.

"My niece will reside with me in Harley Street. She is almost like a daughter to me," said Mrs. Shaw, looking fondly at Margaret; "and I am glad to acknowledge my own obligation for any kindness that has been shown to her. If you and your husband ever come to the town, my son and daughter, Captain and Mrs. Lennox, will, I am sure, join with me in wishing to do anything in our power to show you attention."

Mrs. Thornton thought in her own mind that Margaret had not taken much care to enlighten her aunt as to the relationship between the Mr. and Mrs. Thornton, toward whom the fine-lady aunt was extending her soft patronage; so she answered shortly,

"My husband is dead. Mr. Thornton is my son. I never go to London; so I am not likely to be able to avail myself of your polite offers."

At this instant Mr. Thornton entered the room; he had only just returned from Oxford. His mourning suit spoke of the reason that had called him there.

"John," said his mother, "this lady is Mrs. Shaw, Miss Hale's aunt. I am sorry to say that Miss Hale's call is to wish us goodbye."

"You are going

"A dull rotation, never at a stay,
Yesterday's face twin image of to-day."

"Of what each one should be, he sees the form and rule,
And till he reach to that, his joy can ne'er be full."

For Margaret, the profound stillness of the Harley Street residence, maintained during Edith's convalescence, offered the necessary respite to process the seismic shift in her fortune over the past two months. She found herself suddenly ensconced in a palace of luxury, a place where the mere whisper of worry seemed alien to the air. The intricate gears of domestic life here ran with a frictionless, oily smoothness. Mrs. Shaw and Edith treated Margaret with an abundance of affection, insisting on the house being her home, yet a quiet pang of ingratitude gnawed at her. She could not shake the memory of Helstone vicarage, or even the modest dwelling at Milton, where her father's anxiety and her mother's frailty formed the true texture of home. Edith, eager to reclaim her health, rushed to fill Margaret's room with the soft comforts and ornamental trinkets that adorned her own. Mrs. Shaw and the maid busied themselves restoring Margaret's wardrobe to a state of elegant variety. Captain Lennox was a model of easy kindness, spending hours with his wife or playing with his son, while the remainder of his time was spent at his club or dining out.

Just as Margaret began to feel the suffocating weight of this eventless ease, Edith descended the stairs to resume her role in the household. Margaret, accustomed to service, gladly assumed the burden of managing Edith's social obligations—answering correspondence, reminding her of engagements, and caring for her when the gaiety of the season waned. Consequently, Margaret often felt inclined to feign illness herself. Yet, the rest of the family was immersed in the full business of the London season, leaving Margaret frequently alone. In these solitary moments, her thoughts drifted back to Milton, struck by a strange dissonance between that life and this. She was becoming surfeited by a peace that required no struggle, fearing she might slowly deaden herself into a forgetfulness of everything beyond the luxury lapping at her shores. Though toilers existed in London, she never saw them; the servants inhabited an underground world of their own, appearing only to serve the whims of their masters. A vacuum of unsatisfied purpose opened in Margaret's heart. Once, she had dimly hinted at this to Edith, who, weary from dancing, had languidly stroked Margaret's cheek.

"Poor child!" Edith had said. "It is a little sad to be left, night after night, when the world is so gay. But we shall have dinner-parties soon—as soon as Henry returns from circuit—and then there will be variety. No wonder you are moping, darling!"

Margaret did not believe dinner-parties were a panacea. Edith, however, took pride in them, boasting of how they differed from the dowager-style dinners of her mother's regime. Mrs. Shaw seemed to derive equal pleasure from the new, lighter entertainments suited to the Lennoxes' tastes as she had from her own formal gatherings. Captain Lennox remained consistently kind and brotherly, though Margaret's affection for him wavered whenever he became anxiously attentive to Edith's appearance, seeking to impress the world. In those moments, the latent Vashti in Margaret stirred, and she struggled to suppress her feelings.

Margaret's days followed a predictable rhythm: quiet hours before a late breakfast; a lazy, drawn-out meal where she was expected to offer sympathy on plans that concerned her nothing; an endless stream of notes to write, which Edith left to her with compliments on her "eloquence"; playing with Sholto; caring for the children during the servants' dinner; drives or calls; and finally, engagements for her aunt and cousins that left her free but exhausted by the day's inactivity.

She looked forward with silent longing to Dixon's return from Milton, where the old servant was winding up the Hale family's affairs. The sudden cessation of news from the people she had known for so long felt like a famine to her heart. True, Dixon's letters occasionally quoted Mr. Thornton's opinions on furniture or landlords, but the name rarely appeared, nor did any other Milton connection. One evening, sitting alone in the Lennox drawing-room, Margaret held Dixon's letters but refused to open them. Instead, she recalled the busy life from which she had been plucked, wondering if anyone in that whirlwind missed her—or Higgins, whom she was not thinking of. Suddenly, Mr. Bell was announced. Margaret hurried the letters into her work-basket, rising with a blush that suggested guilt.

"Oh, Mr. Bell! I never thought of seeing you!"

"You give me a welcome, I hope, as well as that very pretty start of surprise."

"Have you dined? How did you come? Let me order you some dinner."

"If you're going to have any. Otherwise, there is no one who cares less for eating than I do. But where are the others? Gone out?"

"Oh yes! and it is such a rest. I was just thinking—But will you risk dinner? I don't know if there is anything in the house."

"To tell you the truth, I dined at my club. They don't cook as well as they did, so I thought, if you were dining, I might try to make out a meal. But never mind! There aren't ten cooks in England to be trusted at impromptu dinners. Their tempers won't stand it. You shall make me some tea, Margaret. And now, what were you thinking of? Whose letters were those, god-daughter, that you hid away so speedily?"

"Only Dixon's," Margaret replied, turning very red.

"Whew! Is that all? Who do you think came up in the train with me?"

"I don't know," Margaret said, resolving against guessing.

"Your what d'ye call him? What's the right name for a cousin-in-law's brother?"

"Mr. Henry Lennox?"

"Yes," Mr. Bell replied. "You knew him formerly, didn't you? What sort of a person is he, Margaret?"

"I liked him long ago," Margaret said, glancing down, then looking up in her natural manner. "You know we have been corresponding about Frederick since; but I have not seen him for nearly three years, and he may be changed. What did you think of him?"

"I don't know. He was so busy trying to find out who I was, and what I was, that he never let out what he was; unless that veiled curiosity as to what manner of man he had to talk to was not a good piece, and a fair indication of his character. Do you call him good-looking, Margaret?"

"No! certainly not. Do you?"

"Not I. But I thought perhaps you might. Is he a great deal here?"

"I fancy he is when he is in town. He has been on circuit now since I came. But—Mr. Bell—have you come from Oxford or from Milton?"

"From Milton. Don't you see I'm smoke-dried?"

"Certainly. But I thought that might be the effect of the antiquities of Oxford."

"Come now, be a sensible woman! In Oxford, I could have managed all the landlords in the place with half the trouble your Milton landlord has given me. He won't take the house off our hands till next June. Luckily, Mr. Thornton found a tenant for it. Why don't you ask after Mr. Thornton, Margaret? He has proved himself a very active friend of yours. Taken more than half the trouble off my hands."

"And how is he? How is Mrs. Thornton?" Margaret asked hurriedly, below her breath.

"I suppose they're well. I've been staying at their house till I was driven out by the perpetual clack about that Thornton girl's marriage. It was too much for Thornton himself, though she was his sister. He used to sit in his own room perpetually. He's getting past the age for caring for such things. I was surprised to find the old lady falling into the current, carried away by her daughter's enthusiasm for orange-blossoms and lace. I thought Mrs. Thornton had been made of sterner stuff."

"She would put on any assumption of feeling to veil her daughter's weakness," Margaret said in a low voice.

"Perhaps so. You've studied her, have you? She doesn't seem over fond of you, Margaret."

"I know it," Margaret said. "Oh, here is tea, at last!" she exclaimed, as if relieved.

With the arrival of tea came Mr. Henry Lennox, who had walked up after a late dinner, evidently expecting to find his brother and sister-in-law at home. Margaret suspected he was as thankful as she was for the presence of a third party, on this their first meeting since the memorable day of his offer and her refusal at Helstone. She could hardly find words at first, grateful for the tea-table occupations that gave her an excuse for silence and him an opportunity to recover. To tell the truth, he had forced himself to Harley Street to get over an awkward meeting, doubly awkward now that he found her the only lady there. She was the first to recover her composure.

"Mr. Lennox, I have been so much obliged to you for all you have done about Frederick."

"I am only sorry it has been so unsuccessful," he replied, with a quick glance toward Mr. Bell, reconnoitering how much he might say. Margaret, reading his thought, addressed herself to Mr. Bell, including him in the conversation.

"That Horrocks—the very last witness of all—has proved as unavailing as all the others. Mr. Lennox has discovered that he sailed for Australia only last August; only two months before Frederick was in England, and gave us the names of—"

"Frederick in England! you never told me that!" Mr. Bell exclaimed in surprise.

"I thought you knew. I never doubted you had been told. Of course, it was a great secret, and perhaps I should not have named it now," Margaret said, a little dismayed.

"I have never named it to either my brother or your cousin," Mr. Lennox replied with professional dryness.

"Never mind, Margaret. I am not living in a talking, babbling world, nor among people trying to worm facts out of me; you needn't look so frightened because you have let the cat out of the bag to a faithful old hermit like me. I shall never name his having been in England; I shall be out of temptation, for no one will ask me. Stay!" He interrupted himself abruptly. "Was it at your mother's funeral?"

"He was with mamma when she died," Margaret said softly.

"To be sure! To be sure! Why, some one asked me if he had not been over then, and I denied it stoutly—not many weeks ago—who could it have been? Oh! I recollect!"

But he did not say the name. Although Margaret would have given much to know if her suspicions were right, and it had been Mr. Thornton who had made the inquiry, she could not ask Mr. Bell, much as she longed to.

There was a pause. Then Mr. Lennox said, "I suppose as Mr. Bell is now acquainted with all the circumstances attending your brother's unfortunate dilemma, I cannot do better than inform him exactly how the research into the evidence we once hoped to produce in his favour stands at present. So, if he will do me the honour to breakfast with me to-morrow, we will go over the names of these missing gentry."

"I should like to hear all the particulars, if I may. Cannot you come here? I dare not ask you both to breakfast, though I am sure you would be welcome. But let me know all you can about Frederick, even though there may be no hope at present."

"I have an engagement at half-past eleven. But I will certainly come if you wish it," Mr. Lennox replied with a willingness that made Margaret shrink into herself, wishing she had not proposed her natural request. Mr. Bell got up to look for his hat.

"Well!" he said, "I don't know what Mr. Lennox is inclined to do, but I'm disposed to be moving off homewards. I've been a journey to-day, and journeys begin to tell upon my sixty and odd years."

"I believe I shall stay and see my brother and sister," Mr. Lennox said, making no movement of departure. Margaret was seized with a shy dread of being left alone with him. The scene on the little terrace in the Helstone garden was so present to her that she could hardly believe it wasn't so with him.

"Don't go yet, please Mr. Bell," she said hastily. "I want you to see Edith; and I want Edith to know you. Please!" She laid a light but determined hand on his arm. He looked at her, seeing the confusion stirring in her countenance, and sat down again, as if her little touch possessed resistless strength.

"You see how she overpowers me, Mr. Lennox," he said. "And I hope you noticed the happy choice of her expressions; she wants me to 'see' this cousin Edith, who, I am told, is a great beauty; but she has the honesty to change her word when she comes to me—Mrs. Lennox is to 'know' me. I suppose I am not much to 'see,' eh, Margaret?"

He joked to give her time to recover from the slight flutter he had detected, and she caught the tone, throwing the ball back. Mr. Lennox wondered how his brother, the Captain, could have reported her as having lost all her good looks. In her quiet black dress, she was a contrast to Edith, dancing in white crape mourning with long floating golden hair. She dimpled and blushed becomingly when introduced to Mr. Bell, conscious that she had her reputation as a beauty to keep up. Mrs. Shaw and Captain Lennox, each in their separate way, gave Mr. Bell a kind welcome, winning him over almost in spite of himself, especially when he saw how naturally Margaret took her place as sister and daughter of the house

The melody of youth had faded into silence, leaving behind only the hollow echo of a world where the listeners were gone. As William Bell lay awake, the ghost of Helstone haunted his mind, summoned by his conversation with Mr. Lennox. In the theater of his sleep, he was once again the tutor at the college, a Fellow in his prime, visiting his newly wedded friend, the Vicar of Helstone. They leaped across babbling brooks that seemed to suspend them in an eternal, weightless afternoon. Time and space dissolved; reality was measured not by the clock, but by the swelling tide of emotion. The trees blazed with autumn fire, and the air was thick with the perfume of wild herbs. There, he saw the young wife moving through the house, caught in that peculiar tension of a woman proud of her devoted husband yet acutely aware of her precarious financial standing—a dynamic Mr. Bell had observed with such clarity a quarter-century ago.

The dream was so vivid, so indistinguishable from truth, that when he jolted awake, his present existence felt like a phantom. Where was he? In the stifling, ornately furnished room of a London hotel. Who were the figures who had moved around him, touched him, spoken to him just moments before? Dead. Buried. Lost forever to the reach of the earth. He was an old man, stripped of the exultant strength of his manhood, drowning in an utter loneliness that threatened to crush him. With a gasp, he rose, forcing himself to dress in a frantic rush for the breakfast awaiting him in Harley Street.

In the lawyer's office, the weight of the situation pressed down on Margaret. She watched as Mr. Bell's presence failed to soften the blow; instead, her eyes widened and her lips grew pale as each piece of evidence, meant to exonerate Frederick, was revealed to be missing. Even Mr. Lennox, usually so composed, softened his professional tone as he guided her toward the final extinction of hope. It was not that Margaret had been unaware of the outcome; she had known it all along. It was the relentless, minute-by-minute dismantling of every possible defense that finally broke her resolve, reducing her to tears.

"I had better not go on," Mr. Lennox said, his voice thick with concern. "It was a foolish proposal of mine. Lieutenant Hale..." He paused, granting him the title of the service from which he had been so harshly cast aside, a small courtesy that seemed to soothe Margaret. "Lieutenant Hale is happy now. More secure in fortune and future prospects than he could ever have been in the navy; and he has, doubtless, adopted his wife's country as his own."

"That is it," Margaret whispered, forcing a fragile smile. "It seems so selfish in me to regret it... and yet he is lost to me, and I am so lonely."

Mr. Lennox turned over his papers, wishing silently for the prosperity he believed awaited him someday. Mr. Bell blew his nose but remained silent, respecting the gravity of the moment. Within minutes, Margaret appeared to regain her composure. She thanked Mr. Lennox with a courtesy that bordered on graciousness, keenly aware that her breakdown might have seemed ungrateful to a man who had done his duty. Yet, it was pain she would not have been without.

As Mr. Bell rose to bid her farewell, he hesitated, fumbling with his gloves. "Margaret!" he began. "I am going down to Helstone tomorrow to look at the old place. Would you like to come with me? Or would it give you too much pain? Speak out, don't be afraid."

"Oh, Mr. Bell," she managed, unable to find further words. But she reached out, taking his old, gout-afflicted hand and pressing a kiss to it.

"Come, come; that's enough," he said, flushing with awkwardness. "I suppose your aunt Shaw will trust you with me. We'll go tomorrow morning, and we shall arrive about two o'clock, I fancy. We'll take a snack, order dinner at the little inn—the Lennard Arms, it used to be—and go and get an appetite in the forest. Can you stand it, Margaret? It will be a trial, I know, to both of us, but it will be a pleasure to me, at least. And there we'll dine—it will be but doe-venison, if we can get it at all—and then I'll take my nap, while you go out and see old friends. I'll give you back safe and sound, barring railway accidents, and I'll insure your life for a thousand pounds before starting, which may be some comfort to your relations; but otherwise, I'll bring you back to Mrs. Shaw by lunch time on Friday. So, if you say yes, I'll just go upstairs and propose it."

"It's no use my trying to say how much I shall like it," Margaret replied, her voice trembling through her tears.

"Well, then, prove your gratitude by keeping those fountains of yours dry for the next two days," Mr. Bell insisted with a grin. "If you don't, I shall feel queer myself about the lachrymal ducts, and I don't like that."

"I won't cry a drop," Margaret promised, winking her eyes to shake the moisture from her lashes and forcing a smile.

"There's my good girl. Then we'll go upstairs and settle it all."

Margaret left the office in a state of almost trembling eagerness. Meanwhile, Mr. Bell discussed his plan with Aunt Shaw. She was first startled, then doubtful and perplexed, her mind racing with propriety and safety. Yet, in the end, she yielded to the rough force of Mr. Bell's conviction rather than her own lingering fears. Until Margaret's safe return and the happy fulfillment of the project, she could not settle whether it was right or wrong, proper or improper. But when the plan was set, her decision crystallized instantly. "She was sure it had been a very kind thought of Mr. Bell's," she declared, "and just what she herself had been wishing for Margaret, as giving her the very change which she required, after all the anxious time she had had."

"So on those happy days of yore
Oft as I dare to dwell once more,
Still must I miss the friends so tried,
Whom Death has severed from my side.

But ever when true friendship binds,
Spirit it is that spirit finds;
In spirit then our bliss we found,
In spirit yet to them I'm bound."

Margaret had risen before the dawn, allowing herself a solitary moment of quiet grief unseen by others, followed by a bright composure for the journey ahead. Her only lingering fear was missing the train, yet she arrived with the party just in time, breathing a sigh of relief as the carriage swept past the familiar stations of the south. The landscape unfolded in warm, golden hues, the tiled roofs glowing under a sun that seemed to bathe the countryside in a ruddier light than the cold slates of the North. Pigeons circled the quaint gables, settling softly to enjoy the heat, while the stations themselves were surprisingly still, devoid of the bustling trade that characterized the northern lines. Spectators lounged idly, hands in pockets, seemingly content to watch the trains depart and stare blankly at the empty tracks left behind. The air danced over the golden stillness of the fields, evoking images of German idylls and ancient legends, until the carriage halted at the point where the fly would take them to Helstone.

As the vehicle slowed, a sharp pang pierced Margaret's heart, a mixture of pain and pleasure she could scarcely define. Every mile was steeped in memory, each turn and familiar tree identical to those of her childhood summers, yet now flooded with sunlight that felt alien to her orphaned state. Nature remained eternally young, indifferent to the profound loss she carried; her parents, once so present, had vanished from the earth, leaving her alone on a road that seemed to mock her solitude with its radiant beauty.

Mr. Bell, sensing her turmoil, wisely maintained a respectful silence. They arrived at the Lennard Arms, a sturdy establishment standing apart from the road, its front facing the village green where an ancient lime tree sheltered the Lennard escutcheon. The inn door stood open, but there was no frantic hospitality. When the landlady, Mrs. Purkis, finally appeared, she offered a warm, motherly welcome, apologizing profusely for her delay due to the demands of hay-making. Her eyes widened in recognition when she spotted Margaret, calling loudly for her daughter Jenny before rushing to shake Margaret's hand with affectionate familiarity.

"How are you all?" Mrs. Purkis asked, her voice trembling slightly. "How's the Vicar and Miss Dixon? God bless the Vicar! We've never stopped grieving for his departure."

Margaret attempted to speak of her father's death and her mother's passing, but the effort choked her. She could only touch her deep mourning and whisper the single word, "Papa."

"Never!" Mrs. Purkis exclaimed, turning to Mr. Bell for confirmation. "A gentleman visited us in the spring, mentioning Mrs. Hale's passing, but never a word of the Vicar's illness!"

"It is true," Mr. Bell confirmed gently. "He died suddenly while visiting me in Oxford. He was a good man, Mrs. Purkis, and I am grateful for such a calm end. Come, Margaret, my dear. Your father was my oldest friend, and she is my goddaughter. We came to see the old place, and I know you can provide us with comfortable rooms and a fine dinner. You may not remember me clearly, but my name is Bell; I have slept here often when the parsonage was full and tasted your excellent ale."

"To be sure! I ask your pardon," Mrs. Purkis stammered, distracted by her focus on Margaret. "Let me show you to a room where you can remove your bonnet and wash your face.
