This morning I placed fresh roses head-downward in the water-jug, hoping someone might arrive, for nothing scents spring water like a musk rose. To think the Vicar is dead! Well, we must all die; that gentleman said he was recovering from his sorrow over Mrs. Hale."

"Come down to me later, Mrs. Purkis," Mr. Bell instructed, taking charge. "I wish to consult with you regarding dinner."

In Margaret's chamber, the casement window was nearly obscured by climbing vines and roses. Pushing them aside, she caught a glimpse of the parsonage chimneys above the trees, their outlines familiar yet distant. Mrs. Purkis smoothed the bed and sent Jenny for lavender-scented towels, lamenting the changes in the village. "Times have changed, miss," she noted. "Our new Vicar has seven children and is building a nursery where the old arbour stood. He has installed new grates and plate-glass windows. He and his wife are energetic, doing much 'good,' though I suspect they merely turn things upside down for little purpose. He is a teetotaller and magistrate, and his wife makes yeastless bread and talks incessantly. They are so loud one cannot think until they leave. He will inspect the hay-fields and peep in, causing a fuss if they drink ginger-beer instead of malt liquor. My ancestors sent good ale to haymakers and used salts for ailments, though Mrs. Hepworth wants to replace medicine with comfits. I must go now, miss, though I wish to hear much more."

Mr. Bell prepared a rustic luncheon of strawberries, cream, brown bread, milk, Stilton, and port for Margaret. After this simple meal, they set out for a walk, unsure of which direction to take amidst the familiar scenery.

"Shall we pass the vicarage?" Mr. Bell suggested.

"No, not yet," Margaret replied. "We will make a round and return by it."

As they walked, Margaret noted the disappearance of old trees felled the previous autumn and the replacement of a decaying squatter's cottage with a tidy, respectable new one. She grieved for these losses as if they were old friends. They passed the spot where she and Mr. Lennox had once sketched; the lightning-scarred beech tree was gone, replaced by a small garden. The old man who inhabited the ruined cottage was dead.

"I did not think I had grown so old," Margaret murmured, turning away with a sigh.

"Yes," Mr. Bell agreed. "It is the first changes among familiar things that confuse the young. Later, we accept instability as a matter of course. For you, it is new and oppressive."

"Let us visit little Susan," Margaret said, leading him up a grassy path under a forest glade.

"With all my heart," Mr. Bell replied, adding, "I have a kindness for all Susans, for simple Susan's sake."

"My little Susan was disappointed when I left without saying goodbye," Margaret confessed, feeling a pang of guilt. "It weighs on my conscience. But it is a long way; are you sure you won't tire?"

"Quite sure," Mr. Bell assured her, slowing his pace. "Unless I am Hamlet, Prince of Denmark, walking with someone 'fat and scant o' breath' sounds romantic. Have compassion on my infirmities for his sake."

"I will walk slower for your own sake. I like you twenty times better than Hamlet."

"On the principle that a living ass is better than a dead lion?"

"Perhaps so. I do not analyze my feelings."

"I am content to accept your liking for me without scrutinizing its origins. Only, let us not walk at a snail's pace."

"Very well. Walk at your own speed, and I will follow. Or stop and meditate like your Hamlet if I go too fast."

"Thank you. But since my mother did not murder my father and marry my uncle, I have little to ponder except the prospect of a well-cooked dinner. What do you think?"

"I am hopeful. She was renowned as a cook in Helstone."

"But have you considered the distraction of mind caused by all this haymaking?"

Margaret felt Mr. Bell's kindness in attempting to lift her spirits with trivial talk, preventing her from dwelling too deeply on the past. Yet, she would have preferred silence, or even solitude, over his cheerful distractions.

They reached the cottage of Susan's widowed mother, only to find Susan absent, having gone to the parochial school. Margaret felt a disappointment the mother sensed, prompting an apologetic explanation.

"Oh! it is quite right," Margaret said, relieved. "I am glad to hear it. Only she used to stay home with you."

"Yes, she did. I miss her sadly. I used to teach her what little I knew at night. She was becoming such a handy girl, I miss her sore. But she is far above me in learning now," the mother sighed.

"I am all wrong," Mr. Bell grumbled. "I am a century behind the world. But I believe the child received a better, simpler education staying home, helping her mother, and reading the New Testament nightly, than from all the schooling under the sun."

Margaret, wishing to avoid prolonging the discussion before the mother, turned the conversation to old Betty Barnes.

"How is old Betty Barnes?" she asked.

"I don't know," the woman replied shortly. "We are not friends."

"Why not?" Margaret queried, recalling her former role as the village peacemaker.

"She stole my cat."

"Did she know it was yours?"

"I don't know. I reckon not."

"Well, could you not retrieve it when you told her?"

"No! For she had burnt it."

"Burnt it!" Margaret and Mr. Bell exclaimed in unison.

"Roasted it!" the woman clarified.

It was no clarification. Through questioning, Margaret learned the horrifying truth: Betty Barnes, having lent her husband's Sunday clothes to a gypsy fortune-teller on the promise of their return before Goodman Barnes missed them, grew terrified when they failed to appear. Driven by fear of her husband's anger and fueled by savage country superstitions, she believed the cries of a cat being boiled or roasted could compel the powers of darkness to fulfill the gypsy's wishes. The poor woman genuinely believed in the charm's efficacy, feeling only indignation that her cat had been chosen for sacrifice. Margaret listened in horror, unable to enlighten the woman, who insisted that while the act was cruel, it was unmatched in granting wishes. Margaret walked away sick at heart.

"You are a good girl not to triumph over me," Mr. Bell remarked.

"How? What do you mean?"

"I admit I was wrong about schooling. Anything rather than have that child raised in such practical paganism."

"Oh! I remember. Poor little Susan! I must see her. Would you mind calling at the school?"

"Not a bit. I am curious to see the teaching she receives."

Emerging from the forest, the buzz of voices from the schoolhouse filled the air like a hive of bees. The door stood open, and a brisk lady in black greeted them with the hostess-like air Margaret remembered from her mother, though softer and more languid. Recognizing the current Vicar's wife, Margaret felt a urge to retreat but quickly conquered it, advancing modestly as she exchanged glances of recognition with old acquaintances. The Vicar's lady heard the name "Miss Hale" and her manner shifted instantly to one of kindness, bordering on patronization. She extended her hand to Mr. Bell, assuming he was the Vicar's father based on a resemblance, and expressed her gladness to see him.

Margaret corrected her, stammering about her father's death, wondering how Mr. Hale could revisit Helstone if the Vicar's wife held such assumptions. She did not hear Mrs. Hepworth's words, leaving Mr. Bell to respond while she scanned the room for familiar faces.

"Ah! I see you wish to take a class, Miss Hale," Mrs. Hepworth announced. "First class, stand up for a parsing lesson with Miss Hale."

Margaret, whose visit was sentimental rather than academic, felt a sense of intrusion. Yet, seeing the eager faces of girls who had been baptized by her father, she sat down, tracing their changing features. She held Susan's hand briefly, unnoticed, as Mrs. Hepworth explained the Phonetic system to Mr. Bell. Margaret bent over her book, hearing only the children's voices, until old memories surged forth and her eyes filled with tears. Suddenly, a pause occurred as a girl stumbled over the simple word "a."

"A, an indefinite article," Margaret said softly.

"I beg your pardon," Mrs. Hepworth interrupted, alert and eager. "But we are taught by Mr. Milsome to call 'a' an—who can remember?"

"An adjective absolute," half a dozen voices chorused. Margaret sat abashed; the children knew more than she did. Mr. Bell turned away, smiling.

Margaret said nothing during the remainder of the lesson. Afterward, she quietly spoke with a few old favorites, noting how they were growing into young women, slipping from her memory as she faded from theirs. Though sad, she was glad to have seen them again. When school ended, Mrs. Hepworth invited Margaret and Mr. Bell to the parsonage to see the "alterations" the Vicar was making. Margaret cared little for the improvements, which jarred against her cherished memories, but she longed to see the old place one last time, bracing herself for the pain she knew awaited.

The parsonage had been transformed, both inside and out. The garden, once meticulously trimmed, was strewn with children's toys and a straw hat crushed onto a rose tree, destroying a delicate branch that would have been treasured in her time. The hall bore the marks of rough, healthy childhood.

"You must excuse this untidiness, Miss Hale," Mrs. Hepworth said. "When the nursery is finished, I shall insist on order. We are converting your room into a nursery, I believe. How did you manage without one?"

"We were but two," Margaret replied. "You have many children, I presume?"

"Seven. Look here! We are throwing out a window to the road. Mr. Hepworth is spending immense money on this house; it was scarcely habitable for a family of our size when we arrived." Every

"Experience, like a pale musician, holds / A dulcimer of patience in his hand; / Whence harmonies we cannot understand, / Of God's will in His worlds, the strain unfolds / In sad, perplexed minors." — *Mrs. Browning.*

Just as the dust of the strike began to settle, Dixon returned from Milton, bringing with her the heavy, gossip-laden air of the town. She settled into her post as Margaret's maid, eager to fill the silence with the trivialities of the world she had left behind. Her reports were a tapestry of petty grievances and social observations: Martha's departure to live with Miss Thornton following the marriage; the elaborate details of the bridesmaids' gowns and the breakfasts that followed; the whispered judgment that Mr. Thornton had thrown away too much dignity on a wedding, given his financial losses from the failed contracts. Dixon relished recounting the auction of the Thorntons' furniture, noting with satisfaction how little the items Dixon herself had coveted had fetched, a fact she found deeply unjust considering the wealth of Milton's inhabitants. She recounted Mrs. Thornton's shrewd bargains and Mr. Thornton's foolish attempts to outbid himself, a spectacle Dixon found delightful as it leveled the playing field.

She spoke of Mr. Bell's confusing correspondence regarding books, his particularity making letters seem like riddles compared to a personal visit, and offered a vague, aristocratically filtered account of the Higginses. Her memory betrayed her when it came to those beneath her station; she recalled Nicholas as "very well," the only one besides Mr. Thornton to inquire after Miss Margaret, while Mary was dismissed as a "great, stout, slatternly thing." There was a rumor, perhaps a dream, that Mary had taken work at the mill to learn cooking—a notion Margaret found incoherent enough to be dismissed as fantasy. Yet, it was a relief to have Dixon to discuss Milton, even if Dixon preferred to keep that part of her life in shadow. Instead, she dwelled on Mr. Bell's speeches, convinced they hinted at a grand design: making Margaret his heiress. Margaret, however, offered no encouragement, shutting down Dixon's insinuations with polite indifference.

Beneath the chatter, a strange, undefined longing gnawed at Margaret. She waited for Mr. Bell to return to Milton, for they had agreed at Helstone that the explanation she craved must come from him, delivered only to Mr. Thornton, and never forced. Though he wrote occasionally, his letters were now short, complaining, and laced with an unusual bitterness. He seemed to regret the past and weary of the present, mentioning an "old-fashioned complaint called the spleen," leaving Margaret to wonder if it was physical or mental. She stopped asking, accepting the silence as a barrier she could not breach.

The only spark of hope came accidentally from Edith. During a conversation with Mr. Bell in London, he had half-thought of taking Margaret to Cadiz to meet her new sister-in-law and hear Frederick's account of the mutiny. Edith, weary of Margaret's cross-questioning, admitted it was merely a fleeting fancy, yet the idea captivated Margaret. It promised a bright escape from the monotony of her current existence. But Edith, sensitive to any joy that excluded her, felt slighted. Margaret had to soothe her, hiding her own delight behind the safety valve of asking Dixon if she would enjoy seeing Master Frederick and his new wife.

"She's a Papist, Miss, isn't she?" Dixon asked, her voice dropping.

"I believe—oh yes, certainly!" Margaret replied, dampened instantly by the thought.

"And they live in a Popish country?"

"Yes."

"Then I'm afraid my soul is dearer to me than even Master Frederick," Dixon declared, feigning terror lest she be converted. "I should be in perpetual dread."

"Oh," Margaret said gently, "I do not know that I am going. And if I do, I am not such a fine lady as to travel without you. You shall have a long holiday."

"But I think it's a long 'if,'" Dixon countered, her curiosity piqued despite her protestations. She asked if avoiding priests and churches would truly save her from conversion, noting that Master Frederick had gone over unaccountably.

"I fancy it was love that first predisposed him to conversion," Margaret sighed.

"Indeed, Miss!" Dixon exclaimed. "Well, I can preserve myself from priests and churches; but love steals in unawares! I think it's as well I should not go."

Margaret feared dwelling too much on the Spanish plan, for it distracted her from her desperate need to speak plainly with Mr. Thornton. Mr. Bell remained stationary at Oxford, bound by some secret restraint that prevented him from visiting Milton or confirming the trip to Spain.

Her greatest solace lay in Edith's son. He was a storm of will and emotion, a creature of strong passions that drove Edith to despair. Yet, Margaret found him more endearing in his tantrums than in his good moods. She would carry him off to a private room, where they battled it out alone. With a firm power that subdued him into peace, she countered his every wile with charm and reason until he would bury his hot, tear-smeared face in her neck, kissing her until sleep claimed him. These were Margaret's sweetest moments, fleeting tastes of a maternal warmth she feared was denied to her forever.

Mr. Henry Lennox also brought a new element to the household, colder but more brilliant than before. His conversation was flavored with varied knowledge and a slight contempt for his brother's frivolous life. He questioned his brother sharply about relinquishing his profession, and when Captain Lennox replied that he had enough to live upon, Mr. Lennox curled his lip with disdain: "And is that all you live for?"

Despite the friction, the brothers remained attached, the clever leading the patient. Mr. Lennox was calculating, far-seeing, and proud, pushing ahead in his career. Since their first deep conversation in Mr. Bell's presence, their interactions had cooled, drifting apart in opinion and taste. Yet, whenever Mr. Lennox spoke with epigrammatic brilliance, his eyes would seek Margaret's countenance first, listening to her opinion with a deference that was both reluctant and concealed.

"My own, my father's friend!
I cannot part with thee!
I ne'er have shown, thou ne'er hast known,
How dear thou art to me."

The social gatherings hosted by Mrs. Lennox were constructed with a specific alchemy: her friends supplied the beauty, Captain Lennox provided the effortless mastery of current topics, and Mr. Henry Lennox, accompanied by a select circle of rising young men, contributed wit and sharp intellect, all deployed with such finesse that they appeared spontaneous rather than calculated. These dinners were undeniably delightful spectacles. Yet, even within this glittering arena, Margaret's dissatisfaction remained an uninvited guest. She observed that every talent, every sentiment, and even every inclination toward virtue was consumed as fuel for fireworks; the sacred, hidden fire of the soul was reduced to mere sparkle and crackle. They discussed art through a purely sensuous lens, fixated on surface effects rather than seeking deeper truths. They fanned themselves into artificial enthusiasm about lofty subjects in company, only to let those thoughts wither when alone, squandering their capacity for genuine appreciation into a fluent stream of empty words.

One evening, after the gentlemen had retired to the drawing-room, Mr. Lennox approached Margaret. It was nearly the first voluntary word he had spoken to her since her return to Harley Street.

"You did not look pleased at what Shirley was saying at dinner," he observed.

"Didn't I? My face must be remarkably expressive," Margaret replied.

"It always has been. It has not lost the art of eloquence."

"I did not like," she said quickly, "his manner of advocating what he knew to be wrong—so glaringly wrong, even in jest."

"But it was exceedingly clever. How every word landed! Did you recall the apt epithets?"

"Yes."

"And yet you would like to despise them. Please do not hesitate, though he is my friend."

"There! That is precisely the tone in you, that—" she broke off abruptly.

He paused, listening to see if she would complete her thought. She only flushed deeply and turned away. Before she could withdraw completely, she heard him speak in a low, clear voice:

"If my tones, or my modes of thought, are what you dislike, will you grant me the justice of telling me so? And thus give me the opportunity to learn how to please you."

For weeks, there had been no news of Mr. Bell's departure for Milton. He had mentioned it at Helstone as a potential journey in the near future, yet he must have handled his affairs by correspondence. Margaret reasoned that if he could, he would avoid traveling to a place he disliked and one he would struggle to comprehend, especially regarding the secret significance she attached to an explanation that demanded the intimacy of speech. She knew he felt the necessity of it, yet whether in summer, autumn, or winter, the timing mattered little. It was August now, and there had been no mention of the Spanish voyage he had hinted at with Edith. Margaret tried to reconcile herself to the fading of this illusion.

Then, one morning, a letter arrived. It stated that next week he intended to come to town to discuss a plan forming in his mind; furthermore, he planned to indulge in some medical treatment, having begun to agree with her view that it was preferable to attribute his irritability to his health rather than his character. There was a tone of forced cheerfulness in the letter, which Margaret noted only in retrospect. At the time, her attention was captured by Edith's exclamations.

"Coming up to town! Oh dear! And I am so exhausted by the heat that I doubt I have the strength for another dinner. Besides, everyone has left except our dear, stupid selves, who cannot decide where to go. There would be no one to meet him."

"I am certain he would much prefer dining with us alone rather than with the most agreeable strangers you could assemble. Moreover, if he is unwell, he will not seek invitations. I am glad he has finally admitted it. I was sure he was ill from the entire tone of his letters, yet he refused to answer me when I asked, leaving me with no third party to whom I could turn for news."

"Oh! He is not very ill, or he would not think of Spain."

"He never mentions Spain."

"No! But his proposed plan evidently concerns it. However, would you truly go in weather like this?"

"Oh, it will cool down daily. Yes! Think of it. I am only afraid I have thought and wished too much—in that absorbing, wilful way that is destined either to be disappointed or gratified to the letter, yielding no spiritual pleasure."

"But that is superstitious, I'm sure, Margaret."

"No, I do not think so. Only it ought to warn me and check me from yielding to such passionate wishes. It is a sort of 'Give me children, or else I die.' I fear my cry is, 'Let me go to Cadiz, or else I die.'"

"My dear Margaret! You will be persuaded to stay there; and then what shall I do? Oh! I wish I could find someone for you to marry here, so I could be sure of you!"

"I shall never marry."

"Nonsense, and double nonsense! Why, as Sholto says, you are such an attraction to the house that he knows many men who would gladly visit next year for your sake."

Margaret straightened herself haughtily. "Do you know, Edith, I sometimes think your life in Corfu has taught you just a shade or two of coarseness."

"Well!"

Edith began to sob bitterly, declaring with such vehemence that Margaret had lost all love for her and no longer regarded her as a friend, that Margaret realized she had expressed too harsh an opinion to soothe her own wounded pride. She ended the day as Edith's slave, while the younger lady, overwhelmed by hurt feelings, lay like a victim on the sofa, heaving profound sighs until she finally fell asleep.

Mr. Bell did not appear on the day he had twice postponed his visit. The following morning, a letter from Wallis, his servant, arrived. It stated that his master had been unwell for some time—the true reason for delaying his journey—and that at the very moment he was scheduled to depart for London, he had suffered an apoplectic fit. Wallis added that the medical men believed he could not survive the night, and it was more than probable that by the time Miss Hale received this letter, her poor master would be no more.

Margaret received this letter at breakfast and turned pale as she read it. Silently, she handed it to Edith and left the room.

Edith was terribly shocked, crying in a sobbing, frightened, childish manner that distressed her husband greatly. Mrs. Shaw was eating breakfast in her own room, and the task of reconciling his wife to the terrifying proximity of death fell to him, for the first time in her life. Here was a man who was to have dined with them today, lying dead or dying instead! It took some time before she could think of Margaret. Then she sprang up and followed her upstairs into her room. Dixon was packing a few toiletries, and Margaret was hastily putting on her bonnet, weeping throughout, her hands trembling so violently she could barely tie the strings.

"Oh, dear Margaret! How shocking! What are you doing? Are you going out? Sholto would telegraph or do anything you like."

"I am going to Oxford. There is a train in half an hour. Dixon has offered to go with me, but I could have gone alone. I must see him again. Besides, he may be better and need care. He has been like a father to me. Do not stop me, Edith."

"But I must. Mamma will not like it at all. Come and ask her about it, Margaret. You do not know where you are going. I should not mind if he had his own house; but in his Fellow's rooms! Come to Mamma and ask her before you go. It will not take a minute."

Margaret yielded and missed her train. In the suddenness of the event, Mrs. Shaw became bewildered and hysterical, and the precious time slipped away. However, another train departed in a couple of hours. After various discussions regarding propriety and impropriety, it was decided that Captain Lennox should accompany Margaret, for the one thing she held constant was her resolution to go, alone or otherwise, by the next train, regardless of what was said about the step's propriety. Her father's friend, her own friend, was lying at the point of death; the thought of this struck her with such vividness that she was surprised at the firmness with which she asserted her right to independent action. Five minutes before departure, she found herself sitting in a railway carriage opposite Captain Lennox. It was always a comfort to her to think that she had gone, even if only to hear that he had died during the night. She saw the rooms he had occupied and associated them ever after most fondly in her memory with the idea of her father and his one cherished, faithful friend.

They had promised Edith before starting that, if all ended as they feared, they would return to dinner; thus, the long, lingering look around the room where her father had died had to be interrupted, replaced by a quiet farewell to the kind old face that had so often offered pleasant words and merry quips.

Captain Lennox fell asleep on their journey home, and Margaret could cry at leisure, reflecting on this fatal year and all the woes it had brought her. No sooner was she fully aware of one loss than another came—not to supersede her grief for the one before, but to reopen wounds and feelings scarcely healed. Yet, at the sound of the tender voices of her aunt and Edith, at the merry glee of little Sholto at her arrival, and at the sight of the well-lit rooms with their mistress, pale yet full of eager sorrowful interest, Margaret roused herself from her heavy trance of almost superstitious hopelessness. She began to feel that even around her, joy and gladness might gather. She claimed Edith's place on the sofa; Sholto was taught to carry Aunt Margaret's cup of tea with great care; and by the time she went up to dress, she could thank God for having spared her dear old friend a long or painful illness.

Breathing Tranquillity.

"And down the sunny beach she paces slowly,
With many doubtful pauses by the way;
Grief hath an influence so hushed and holy."

The silence of the room after the journey to Oxford was heavy, broken only by the soft rustle of fabric as Edith leaned over her husband. In the dim light, she pulled his tall head down, rising on her toes to whisper a plea that he not be shocked by what followed. Captain Lennox, however, remained a blank slate; if he had ever heard of Margaret's standing, the memory had long since evaporated. To him, a Fellow of a minor college leaving his post was a trivial matter, hardly worth the currency of two hundred and fifty pounds a year, especially since she was known to eschew wine. Edith descended from her tiptoes, her spirits deflated, feeling the delicate architecture of her romance crumble under the weight of mundane reality.

A week later, the atmosphere shifted. Edith pranced toward her husband, executing a low, theatrical curtsey.

"I am right, and you are wrong, most noble Captain," she declared. "Margaret has received a lawyer's letter. She is the residuary legatee. The legacies total about two thousand pounds, with the remainder valued at approximately forty thousand pounds in Milton property."

"Indeed!" Lennox replied, his interest piqued. "And how does she receive this sudden windfall?"

"Oh, it appears she suspected she was entitled to it all along; she simply lacked the imagination to grasp the magnitude. She looks remarkably pale and white, claiming fear of the fortune, but that is merely nonsense, destined to fade. I left my mother pouring congratulations down her throat and slipped away to inform you."

It became the universal consensus that Mr. Lennox was henceforth Margaret's legal guardian. Her ignorance of business forms was absolute; every transaction required his intervention. He selected her attorney, delivered papers for her signature, and derived a peculiar joy from instructing her in the arcane symbols of the law.

One day, Edith approached him with an arch smile. "Henry, do you know what I hope and expect these prolonged conversations with Margaret to conclude with?"

"No, I do not," he replied, flushing slightly. "And I pray you do not tell me."

"Oh, very well; then I need not instruct Sholto to cease inviting Mr. Montagu so frequently to our home."

"As you wish," he said, maintaining a forced composure. "Whatever you envision may or may not come to pass. However, before I commit myself, I must ensure my position is secure. Ask whoever you please. It may lack civility, Edith, but if you interfere, you will ruin it. She has been fiercely resistant to me for some time, only beginning to thaw from her Zenobian demeanor. She possesses the makings of a Cleopatra, were she but a shade more pagan."

"For my part," Edith countered with a touch of malice, "I am delighted she remains a Christian. There are so few of us left!"

That autumn brought no Spain for Margaret. Though she clung to the hope that a fortunate circumstance might summon Frederick to Paris, where a convoy awaited, she was instead confined to Cromer. Her aunt Shaw and the Lennoxes, bound for the coast, had long wished her to accompany them; consequently, their efforts to facilitate her separate desires were sluggish and perfunctory. Perhaps Cromer was, in a sense, the ideal refuge. She required physical strengthening and bracing alongside rest.

Among the hopes that had dissolved was the trust she had placed in Mr. Bell to reveal the simple facts of the family circumstances preceding the tragic accident that claimed Leonards' life. Regardless of how his opinion might have shifted from what Mr. Thornton once held, Margaret had desired his judgment to be rooted in a truthful understanding of her actions and motives. It would have been a source of peace, granting her rest on a point that now threatened to haunt her forever unless she could resolve to stop thinking upon it. Time had rendered explanation impossible save through the lens of Mr. Bell's death. She might submit to being misunderstood, as many did, yet her heart ached with a longing that, years hence, before his passing, he might finally comprehend how deeply she had been tempted. She wished not necessarily to hear the explanation, but to be certain he knew. This wish proved vain, like so many others. Having schooled herself into this conviction, she turned her entire heart and strength to the immediate future, resolving to strive and make the best of the days ahead.

She spent long hours seated upon the beach, staring intently at the waves as they churned perpetually against the pebbly shore, or gazing at the distant heaves sparkling against the sky. She heard the eternal psalm rising continuously, soothing her without her conscious awareness. Listlessly, she sat on the ground, hands clasped around her knees, while her aunt Shaw engaged in minor shopping expeditions and Edith and Captain Lennox rode far and wide across the shore and inland. Nurses sauntering with their charges would pass and repass her, whispering in wonder at what she could possibly find so captivating to gaze upon, day after day. At dinner, Margaret was so silent and absorbed that Edith deemed her moped, welcoming her husband's proposal with great satisfaction that Mr. Henry Lennox be invited to stay at Cromer for a week upon his return from Scotland in October.

Yet, these hours of solitude allowed Margaret to arrange events in their proper order regarding origin and significance, both for her past and her future. Those seaside moments were not wasted; anyone with the perception to read or the care to understand could see the transformation in the look of Margaret's face. Mr. Henry Lennox was profoundly struck by the change.

"The sea has done Miss Hale an immense deal of good, I should fancy," he remarked when she first entered the room after his arrival in their family circle. "She looks ten years younger than she did in Harley Street."

"That's the bonnet I procured for her!" Edith announced triumphantly. "I knew it would suit her the moment I saw it."

"I beg your pardon," Mr. Lennox replied in his characteristic tone, a mixture of half-contempt and indulgence. "But I believe I discern the difference between the charms of a dress and the charms of a woman. No mere bonnet could render Miss Hale's eyes so lustrous yet so soft, or her lips so ripe and red—and her face altogether so full of peace and light. She is like, and yet more," he lowered his voice, "like the Margaret Hale of Helstone."

From this moment, the clever and ambitious man devoted all his energies to winning Margaret. He loved her sweet beauty and perceived the latent sweep of her mind, which he believed could easily be guided to embrace all the objects of his heart. He viewed her fortune merely as a component of her complete and superb character and position, yet he was acutely aware of the social ascent it would immediately afford him, the poor barrister. Eventually, he would earn such success and honors as would enable him to repay her, with interest, that initial advance in wealth he owed to her. He had visited Milton on business concerning her property, returning from Scotland, and with the keen eye of a skilled lawyer, always ready to weigh contingencies, he had observed that additional value accrued yearly to the lands and tenements she owned in that prosperous, expanding town. He was pleased to find that the present relationship of client and legal adviser was gradually superseding the memory of that unlucky, mismanaged day at Helstone. This afforded him unusual opportunities for intimate intercourse with her, beyond those arising from the connection between the families.

Margaret was only too willing to listen as long as he spoke of Milton, though he had seen none of the people she particularly knew. It had been the tone of her aunt and cousin to speak of Milton with dislike and contempt; feelings Margaret was ashamed to recall having expressed and felt upon first moving there. Yet Mr. Lennox surpassed Margaret in his appreciation of the character of Milton and its inhabitants. Their energy, their power, their indomitable courage in struggle and combat; their lurid vividness of existence—captivated and arrested his attention. He was never tired of talking about them, failing to perceive how selfish and material were too many of the ends they proposed as results of their mighty, untiring endeavor, until Margaret, even amidst her gratification, possessed the candor to point this out as the tainting sin within so much that was noble and admirable. Still, when other subjects palled and she offered but short answers to many questions, Henry Lennox discovered that an inquiry into some Darkshire peculiarity of character restored the light to her eye and the glow to her cheek.

Upon returning to town, Margaret fulfilled one of her seaside resolves and took her life into her own hands. Before departing for Cromer, she had been as docile to her aunt's laws as if she were still the frightened little stranger who had cried herself to sleep that first night in the Harley Street nursery. But those solemn hours of thought had taught her that she must one day answer for her own life and what she had done with it. She strove to settle the most difficult problem for a woman: how much must be utterly merged in obedience to authority, and how much might be set apart for freedom in action. Mrs. Shaw was as good-tempered as could be; Edith had inherited this charming domestic quality; Margaret herself likely possessed the worst temper of the three, for her quick perceptions and over-lively imagination made her hasty, and her early isolation from sympathy had bred pride. Yet she possessed an indescribable childlike sweetness of heart that made her manners, even in her rarely wilful moods, irresistible. Now, chastened even by what the world termed her good fortune, she charmed her reluctant aunt into acquiescence with her will. Thus, Margaret secured the acknowledgment of her right to follow her own ideas of duty.

"Only don't be strong-minded," Edith pleaded. "Mamma wants you to have a footman of your own; and I'm sure you're very welcome, for they are great plagues. Only, to please me, darling, don't go and acquire a strong mind; it's the only thing I ask. Footman or no footman, don't be strong-minded."

"Don't be afraid, Edith. I shall faint on your hands at the servants' dinner-time, the very first opportunity; and then, with Sholto playing with the fire and the baby crying, you'll begin to wish for a strong-minded woman, equal to any emergency."

"And you won't grow too good to joke and be merry?"

"Not I. I shall be merrier than I have ever been, now I have my own way."

"And you won't go a figure, but let me buy your dresses for you?"

"Indeed, I intend to buy them for myself. You may come with me if you like; but no one can please me but myself."

"Oh! I was afraid you'd dress in brown and dust-color, merely to show the dirt you'll pick up in all those places. I'm glad you're going to keep one or two vanities, just by way of specimens of the old Adam."

"I am going to be just the same, Edith, if you and my aunt could but imagine so. Only, as I have neither husband nor child to give me natural duties, I must make myself some, in addition to ordering my gowns."

In the family conclave composed of Edith, her mother, and her husband, it was decided that perhaps all these plans would only secure her more firmly to Henry Lennox. They kept her away from other friends who might have been eligible sons or brothers. It was also agreed that she never seemed to take much pleasure in the society of anyone but Henry, outside their own family. Other admirers, attracted by her appearance or the reputation of her fortune, were swept away by her unconscious, smiling disdain into the paths frequented by other beauties less fastidious, or other heiresses with a larger amount of gold. Henry and she grew slowly into closer intimacy, yet neither he nor she were people to brook the slightest notice of their proceedings.

"Here we go up, up, up;
And here we go down, down, downee!"

The air above Milton was thick with smoke, a gray shroud rising from chimneys that belched their burden into a sky already heavy with the promise of winter. Below, the factory floor was a cacophony of ceaseless roar, mighty beat, and dazzling whirr. Wood, iron, and steam labored in an endless, senseless cycle, driven by a persistence that rivaled the tireless endurance of the crowds moving through the streets. Yet, unlike the machinery, the people possessed sense and purpose, though their destination remained a frantic, unanswered question. There were no loiterers, no strollers seeking mere pleasure; every face was etched with lines of eagerness or anxiety. In the Mart and the Exchange, men jostled one another aside with the deep, selfish desperation of competitors fighting for survival.

A gloom hung over the town, heavier than the fog. Trade had stalled; buyers were few, and those who ventured to purchase were viewed with suspicion by sellers who knew that credit was a fragile thing. The stability of even the most established houses was threatened by the sweeping failures in the great neighboring port, where shipping houses had crumbled like sandcastles. While Milton had hitherto known no such disaster, the shadow of American speculation had fallen heavily upon it. Whispers circulated with fierce avidity: "What news? Who is gone? How will it affect me?" Men spoke in hushed tones, dwelling cautiously on the names of those who seemed safe, terrified that idle breath might drag a neighbor down into the abyss. "Thornton is safe," they murmured, praising his vast business and his prudent daring. But behind their backs, others leaned in closer, whispering the darker truth: "Thornton's business is large, but he has spent his profits extending it. He holds no capital laid by. His machinery is new, costing him a fortune we dare not speak aloud."

Mr. Harrison, a man who had inherited his father's trade and feared change, grumbled these criticisms, grudging every penny earned by the more daring souls around him. Yet the truth was that Mr. Thornton was hard pressed, and he felt the pressure acutely in his most vulnerable point: his pride. He had built his commercial character from the ground up, believing that commerce offered a unique path for the brave and honest to rise above the ordinary. He dreamed of a name known in the East and West, of a word that passed like gold across distant seas. His mother had once read to him, "Her merchants be like princes," a trumpet call that had invited him into this struggle. He had sought the influence of a global firm, a legacy for generations, and it had taken him long, silent years to reach even a glimmer of that vision, only to find himself now trapped in his own town, among the very people he had once kept at a distance.

For years, he and his workers had lived parallel lives—close, yet never touching—until the accident of his acquaintance with Higgins. That meeting, shifting the dynamic from master and workman to man and man, had revealed a shared humanity. But now, the fear of losing contact with the few workmen he had come to know as individuals gnawed at him. The thought of his experimental plans being nipped off without trial added a new poignancy to his anxiety. He realized too late how deeply he had invested in his role as a manufacturer, not just for profit, but for the rare opportunity to exercise power and influence among a race of people full of character and strong feeling.

He reviewed his history. The strike a year and a half prior, occurring in an untimely winter, had prevented him from fulfilling large orders. He had locked his capital into expensive new machinery and purchased vast quantities of cotton, only to find the Irish hands he imported lacked the necessary skill, damaging the very quality his house prided itself upon. The embarrassment of that period had festered, and often, when his gaze fell on Higgins, he felt a surge of angry resentment. But he curbed it, resolving to prove his mastery over his own temper by allowing Higgins access whenever business permitted. Slowly, the resentment faded, replaced by a wonder at how two men living the same trade could view their duties so differently. This newfound understanding fostered a charity between them, revealing facts previously hidden from one side or the other.

But now, the market had turned. The value of his large stocks had plummeted to nearly half. Orders ceased, interest on his capital vanished, and payment for completed goods became difficult to secure. Bills for cotton piled up, forcing him to borrow at exorbitant rates while remaining unable to realize any of his property. Yet, he did not despair. He worked day and night, foreseeing emergencies with a calm gentleness that surprised even his own household. To the women in his home, he was as tender as ever; to the workmen, he spoke little, but his demeanor had shifted. Where once there was smoldering antagonism, there was now sympathy for the visible weight pressing upon him.

"Higgins," the overseer noted one day, hearing Mr. Thornton's sharp inquiry followed by a suppressed sigh, "I reckon I know who'd ha' been sorry to see our measter sitting so like a piece o' grey calico! The old parson would ha' fretted his woman's heart out if he'd seen the woeful looks on our measter's face." Higgins and another man stayed over hours that night, unseen, to finish neglected work, ensuring the overseer received the credit.

Later, as Higgins approached Mr. Thornton in Marlborough Street, he watched his employer walk with a quick, resolved stride, his face frozen in a wintry frost of care.

"Measter," Higgins called, stopping him. The interruption caused Mr. Thornton to start, his eyes clearing as if a soft summer gale had blown away the anxiety.

"Have ye heard aught of Miss Marget lately?"

"Miss—who?" Mr. Thornton asked, momentarily confused.

"Miss Marget—Miss Hale—the old parson's daughter. Ye know who I mean, if ye'll only think a bit."

"Oh yes!" Suddenly, the grim look vanished from Mr. Thornton's face. Though his mouth remained compressed, his eyes smiled benignly. "She's my landlord now, you know, Higgins. I hear of her through her agent. She's well and among friends—thank you, Higgins." The "thank you" lingered, warm and laden with feeling, sending a signal to the astute Higgins.

"And she's not gotten married, measter?"

"Not yet." The clouds returned to Mr. Thornton's face. "There is some talk of it, with a connection of the family."

"Then she'll not be for coming to Milton again, I reckon."

"No!"

"Stop a minute, measter." Higgins moved closer, confidential. "Is the young gentleman cleared?" He winked, adding mystery to his words. "The young gentleman, I mean—Master Frederick, they called him—her brother who was over here."

"Over here."

"Ay, to be sure, at the missus's death. Ye need na be feared of my telling; for Mary and me, we knew it all along, only we held our peace."

"And he was over. It was her brother."

"Sure enough. I reckoned ye knew, or I'd never ha' let on. Ye knew she had a brother?"

"Yes, I know all about him. And he was over at Mrs. Hale's death?"

"Nay! I'm not going to tell more. I've maybe gotten them into mischief already. I nobbut wanted to know if they'd gotten him cleared?"

"Not that I know of. I know nothing. I only hear of Miss Hale, now, as my landlord, and through her lawyer."

Mr. Thornton broke away, leaving Higgins baffled. Alone, he reflected, "It was her brother. I am glad. I may never see her again; but it is a comfort—a relief—to know that much. I knew she could not be unmaidenly; and yet I yearned for conviction. Now I am glad!"

It was a golden thread running through the dark web of his fortunes, which grew ever gloomier. His agent had trusted a house in the American trade that collapsed alongside others, triggering a chain reaction of failures. Could he stand?

Night after night, Mr. Thornton took books and papers into his private room, staying up long after his family had retired. One morning, as daylight stole through the crevices of his shutters, he sat in hopeless indifference, thinking he could do without the rest he desperately needed. The door opened, and his mother stood there, dressed as she had been the day before. She had not slept either. Their eyes met, cold and rigid, wan from long watching.

"Mother! Why are you not in bed?"

"Son John," she said, "do you think I can sleep with an easy mind while you keep awake full of care? You have not told me what your trouble is; but sore trouble you have had these many days past."

"Trade is bad."

"And you dread—"

"I dread nothing," he replied, drawing up his head erect. "I know that no man will suffer by me. That was my anxiety."

"But how do you stand? Shall you—will it be a failure?" Her steady voice trembled.

"Not a failure. I must give up business, but I pay all men. I might redeem myself—I am sorely tempted—"

"How? Oh, John! Keep up your name—try all risks for that. How redeem it?"

"By a speculation offered to me, but full of risk. If successful, it places me high above water-mark, so that no one need ever know the strait I am in. Still, if it fails—"

"And if it fails," she advanced, laying her hand on his arm, her eyes full of eager light. She held her breath.

"Honest men are ruined by a rogue," he said gloomily. "As I stand now, my creditors' money is safe—every farthing of it; but I don't know where to find my own—it may be all gone, and I penniless at this moment. Therefore, it is my creditors' money that I should risk."

"But if it succeeded, they need never know. Is it so desperate a speculation? I am sure it is not, or you would never have thought of it. If it succeeded—"

"I should be a rich man, and my peace of conscience would be gone!"

"Why! You would have injured no one."

"No; but I should have run the risk of ruining many for my own paltry aggrandisement. Mother, I have decided! You won't much grieve over our leaving this house, shall you, dear mother?"

"No! but to have you other than what you are will break my heart. What can you do?"

"Be always the same John Thornton in whatever circumstances; endeavouring to do right, and making great blunders; and then trying to be brave in setting to afresh. But it is hard, mother. I have worked and planned. I have discovered new powers in my situation too late—and now all is over. I am too old to begin again with the same heart. It is hard, mother."

He turned away, covering his face with his hands.

"I can't think," she said with gloomy defiance, "how it comes about. Here is my boy—good son, just man, tender heart—and he fails in all he sets his mind upon: he finds a woman to love, and she cares no more for his affection than if he had been a common man; he labours, and his labour comes to nought. Other people grow rich, and hold their paltry names high and dry above shame."

"Shame never touched me," he said in a low tone.

"I sometimes have wondered where justice was gone to, and now I don't believe there is such a thing in the world—now you are come to this; you, my own John Thornton, though you and I may be beggars together—my own dear son!"

She fell upon his neck, kissing him through her tears.

"Mother!" he said, holding her gently, "who has sent me my lot in life, both of good and evil?"

She shook her head, refusing to speak of religion in that moment.

"Mother," he continued, seeing her silence. "I, too, have been rebellious; but I'm striving to be so no longer. Help me, as you helped me when I was a child. Then you said many good words—when my father died, and we were sometimes sorely short of comforts—which we shall never be now; you said brave, noble, trustful words then, mother, which I have never forgotten, though they may have lain dormant. Speak to me again in the old way, mother. Do not let us have to think that the world has too much hardened our hearts. If you would say the old good words, it would make me feel something of the pious simplicity of my childhood. I say them to myself, but they would come differently from you, remembering all the cares and trials you have had to bear."

"I have had a many," she sobbed, "but none so sore as this. To see you cast down from your rightful place! I could say it for myself, John, but not for you. Not for you! God has seen fit to be very hard on you, very."

She shook with convulsive sobs. The silence around her finally struck her, and she quieted herself to listen. No sound. She looked. Her son sat by the table, arms thrown half across it, head bent face downwards.

"Oh, John!" she said, lifting his face up. A strange, pallid look of gloom was on it, striking her for a moment as the forerunner of death. But as the rigidity melted and natural color returned, she saw he was himself once again. All worldly mortification sank to nothing before the consciousness of the great blessing that his simple existence was to her. She thanked God for this, with a fervor that swept away all rebellious feelings.

He did not speak readily, but he went and opened the shutters, letting the ruddy light of dawn flood the room.

"Bear up, brave heart! we will be calm and strong;
Sure, we can master eyes, or cheek, or tongue,
Nor let the smallest tell-tale sign appear
She ever was, and is, and will be dear."

It was a stifling summer evening, the air thick with the weight of the season. Edith entered Margaret's boudoir, the first time in her customary attire, the second time already dressed for dinner. The room was empty at first; upon her return, she found Dixon arranging Margaret's gown upon the bed, but Margaret herself was nowhere to be seen. Edith paced with restless agitation.

"Oh, Dixon! Those horrid blue flowers against that dead gold gown! What taste! Wait a moment, I shall fetch you some pomegranate blossoms instead."

"It is not dead gold, ma'am; it is straw-colour. And blue always suits straw-colour best." But Edith had already placed the brilliant scarlet blooms before Dixon could finish her protest.

"Where is Miss Hale?" Edith asked, once she had inspected the arrangement. "I cannot fathom," she continued, with a petulant air, "how my aunt permitted her to adopt such wandering habits in Milton! I am constantly expecting news of some dreadful accident among those wretched places she wanders into. I should never dare to traverse some of those streets without a servant; they are not fit for ladies."

Dixon remained sulky regarding her dismissed aesthetic judgment and replied curtly:

"It is no wonder, in my opinion, when I hear ladies discourse so extensively about ladies—and when they are such frightfully delicate, dainty creatures too—I say, it is no wonder that there are no longer any saints upon earth."

"Oh, Margaret! Here you are! I have been longing for you. But how flushed your cheeks are with the heat, poor child! Only think what that tiresome Henry has done; truly, he exceeds the bounds of a brother-in-law. Just when my party was assembled so beautifully, fitted with such precision for Mr. Colthurst, Henry arrives with an apology, using your name as an excuse, and asks if he may bring that Mr. Thornton of Milton—your tenant, you know—who is in London attending to some legal business. It will ruin my number entirely."

"I do not mind dinner. I require none," Margaret said in a low voice. "Dixon may bring me a cup of tea here, and I shall be in the drawing-room by the time you ascend. I shall be truly glad to lie down."

"No, no! That will never do. You do look wretchedly pale, to be sure; but that is merely the heat, and we cannot dispense with you. (Those flowers a trifle lower, Dixon. They look like glorious flames, Margaret, in your dark hair.) You know we planned for you to discuss Milton with Mr. Colthurst. Oh! Indeed! And this man comes from Milton. I believe it will prove capital, after all. Mr. Colthurst can interrogate him thoroughly on all subjects that interest him, and it will be great amusement to trace your experiences and this Mr. Thornton's wisdom in Mr. Colthurst's next speech in the House. Truly, I consider it a fortunate stroke of Henry's. I asked him if he was a man one would be ashamed of; he replied, 'Not if you possess any sense, my little sister.' So I suppose he is capable of pronouncing his h's, which is not a common accomplishment in Darkshire—eh, Margaret?"

"Did Mr. Lennox state why Mr. Thornton has come to town? Was it legal business connected with the property?" Margaret asked, her voice constrained.

"Oh! He has failed, or something of the sort; Henry told you of that very day you suffered from such a headache—what was it? (There, that is perfect, Dixon. Miss Hale does us credit, does she not?) I wish I were as tall as a queen and as brown as a gipsy, Margaret."

"But regarding Mr. Thornton?"

"Oh! I truly possess such a terrible head for legal affairs. Henry delights in telling you everything about it. The impression he made upon me was that Mr. Thornton is in dire straits, yet a very respectable man, and that I should treat him with great civility. Since I did not know how to proceed, I came to you to request your assistance. Now, come downstairs with me and rest upon the sofa for a quarter of an hour."

The favored brother-in-law arrived early. Margaret, flushing as she spoke, began to inquire of him the answers she sought concerning Mr. Thornton.

"He ascended regarding the sub-leasing of the property—Marlborough Mills, and the house and premises adjoining, I mean. He is unable to maintain it; there are deeds and leases to be examined and agreements to be drafted. I hope Edith will receive him properly; but she appeared somewhat displeased, as I could perceive, by the liberty I took in soliciting an invitation for him. However, I believed you would appreciate having him attended to; and one must be particularly scrupulous in showing every respect to a man who is descending in the world." He lowered his voice to address Margaret, seated beside him, but as he concluded, he sprang up and introduced Mr. Thornton, who had just entered, to Edith and Captain Lennox.

Margaret watched Mr. Thornton with an anxious gaze while he was thus occupied. It had been considerably more than a year since she had last seen him, and events had altered him significantly in that interval. His fine figure still elevated him above the average height of men, granting him a distinguished appearance derived from the ease of motion natural to him; yet his face appeared older and careworn. Nevertheless, a noble composure rested upon it, impressing those who had just heard of his changed circumstances with a sense of inherent dignity and manly strength. From the first glance he cast around the room, he was aware that Margaret was present; he had observed her intent look of occupation as she listened to Mr. Henry Lennox. He approached her with the perfectly regulated manner of an old friend. With his first calm words, a vivid color flashed into her cheeks, which never faded during the evening. She seemed to have little to say to him. She disappointed him by the quiet manner in which she asked what appeared to him to be merely necessary questions regarding her old acquaintances in Milton. Others entered—more intimate within the household than he—and he fell into the background, where he and Mr. Lennox conversed intermittently.

"You think Miss Hale looks well," said Mr. Lennox, "do you not? Milton did not agree with her, I imagine; for when she first came to London, I thought I had never seen anyone so changed. Tonight she appears radiant. But she is much stronger. Last autumn she was fatigued by a walk of two miles. On Friday evening we walked up to Hampstead and back. Yet on Saturday she looked as well as she does now."

"We!" Who? The two of them alone?

Mr. Colthurst was a very clever man and a rising member of Parliament. He possessed a keen eye for discerning character and was struck by a remark Mr. Thornton made during dinner. He inquired of Edith who that gentleman was; and, to her surprise, she found, from the tone of his "Indeed!", that Mr. Thornton of Milton was not such an unknown name to him as she had imagined. Her dinner proceeded well. Henry was in good spirits and displayed his dry, caustic wit admirably. Mr. Thornton and Mr. Colthurst discovered one or two mutual subjects of interest, which they could only touch upon then, reserving them for more private conversation after dinner. Margaret looked beautiful in the pomegranate flowers; and though she leaned back in her chair and spoke little, Edith was not annoyed, for the conversation flowed smoothly without her. Margaret was studying Mr. Thornton's face. He never looked at her; thus she might observe him unobserved, noting the changes this short time had wrought in him. Only at some unexpected word from Mr. Lennox did his face flash into the old look of intense enjoyment: the merry brightness returned to his eyes, the lips parted slightly to suggest the brilliant smile of former days; and for an instant, his glance instinctively sought hers, as if seeking her sympathy. But when their eyes met, his entire countenance changed; he became grave and anxious once more; and he resolutely avoided looking near her again during dinner.

There were only two ladies besides their own party, and as these were occupied in conversation by her aunt and Edith, when they ascended to the drawing-room, Margaret languidly employed herself with some work. Soon the gentlemen joined them, Mr. Colthurst and Mr. Thornton in close conversation. Mr. Lennox drew near to Margaret and said in a low voice:

"I truly think Edith owes me thanks for my contribution to her party. You have no idea what an agreeable, sensible fellow this tenant of yours is. He has been the very man to provide Mr. Colthurst with all the facts he needed coaching on. I cannot conceive how he managed to mismanage his affairs."

"With his powers and opportunities, you would have succeeded," said Margaret. He did not quite relish the tone in which she spoke, although the words merely expressed a thought that had passed through his own mind. As he remained silent, they caught a swell in the sound of conversation proceeding near the fireplace between Mr. Colthurst and Mr. Thornton.

"I assure you, I heard it spoken of with great interest—curiosity as to its result, perhaps I should rather say. I heard your name frequently mentioned during my short stay in the neighborhood." Then they lost some words; and when next they could hear Mr. Thornton speaking:

"I have not the elements for popularity—if they spoke of me in that way, they were mistaken. I fall slowly into new projects; and I find it difficult to let myself be known, even by those whom I desire to know, and with whom I would fain have no reserve. Yet, even with all these drawbacks, I felt that I was on the right path, and that, starting from a kind of friendship with one, I was becoming acquainted with many. The advantages were mutual: we were both unconsciously and consciously teaching each other."

"You say 'were.' I trust you intend to pursue the same course?"

"I must stop Colthurst," said Henry Lennox, hastily. And by an abrupt, yet appropriate question, he turned the current of the conversation so as not to give Mr. Thornton the mortification of acknowledging his lack of success and consequent change of position. But as soon as the newly-started subject had come to a close, Mr. Thornton resumed the conversation just where it had been interrupted and gave Mr. Colthurst the reply to his inquiry.

"I have been unsuccessful in business and have had to relinquish my position as a master. I am on the lookout for a situation in Milton, where I may find employment under someone willing to allow me to proceed in my own manner regarding such matters. I can rely on myself for having no forward theories that I would rashly bring into practice. My only wish is to have the opportunity of cultivating some intercourse with the hands beyond the mere 'cash nexus.' But it might be the point Archimedes sought from which to move the earth, judging by the importance attached to it by some of our manufacturers, who shake their heads and look grave as soon as I name the one or two experiments I should like to try."

"You call them 'experiments,' I notice," said Mr. Colthurst, with a delicate increase of respect in his manner.

"Because I believe them to be such. I am not sure of the consequences that may result from them. But I am sure they ought to be tried. I have arrived at the conviction that no mere institutions, however wise, and however much thought may have been required to organize and arrange them, can attach class to class as they should be attached, unless the working out of such institutions brings the individuals of the different classes into actual personal contact. Such intercourse is the very breath of life. A working man can hardly be made to feel and know how much his employer may have labored in his study at plans for the benefit of his workpeople. A complete plan emerges like a piece of machinery, apparently fitted for every emergency. But the hands accept it as they do machinery, without understanding the intense mental labor and forethought required to bring it to such perfection. But I would take an idea, the working out of which would necessitate personal intercourse; it might not go well at first, but at every hitch interest would be felt by an increasing number of men, and at last its success in working would come to be desired by all, as all had borne a part in the formation of the plan; and even then I am sure that it would lose its vitality, cease to be living, as soon as it was no longer carried on by that sort of common interest which invariably makes people find means and ways of seeing each other, and becoming acquainted with each others' characters and persons, and even tricks of temper and modes of speech. We should understand each other better, and I'll venture to say we should like each other more."

"And you think they may prevent recurrence of strikes?"

"Not at all. My utmost expectation only goes as far as this—that they may render strikes not the bitter, venomous sources of hatred they have hitherto been. A more hopeful man might imagine that a closer and more genial intercourse between classes might do away with strikes. But I am not a hopeful man."

Suddenly, as if a new idea had struck him, he crossed over to where Margaret was sitting and began, without preface, as if he knew she had been listening to all that had passed:

"Miss Hale, I received a round-robin from some of my men—I suspect in Higgins' handwriting—stating their wish to work for me, if ever I am in a position to employ men again on my own behalf. That was good, was it not?"

"Yes. Just right. I am glad of it," said Margaret, looking up straight into his face with her speaking eyes, and then dropping them under his eloquent glance. He gazed back at her for a minute, as if he did not know exactly what he was about. Then he sighed; and saying, "I knew you would like it," he turned away and never spoke to her again until he bid

"Pack clouds away."

"For joy or grief, for hope or fear,
For all hereafter, as for here,
In peace or strife, in storm or shine."

The next morning, Edith moved through the house on tiptoe, silencing Sholto whenever he raised his voice, as though a sudden noise might shatter the delicate conference underway in the drawing-room. By two o'clock, the doors remained shut, and the silence within seemed to hold its breath. Then came the sharp tread of a man's foot descending the stairs. Edith peeked out from behind the curtains.

"Well, Henry?" she asked, her gaze questioning.

"Well!" he replied, his tone curt.

"Come in to lunch!"

"No, thank you. I've wasted too much time here already."

"Then it isn't settled?" Edith asked, her shoulders slumping in despair.

"Not at all. Never will be, if 'it' refers to what I suspect you mean. That will never happen, Edith. Give up thinking about it."

"But it would be so wonderful for all of us," Edith pleaded. "I would feel secure regarding the children if Margaret were settled nearby. As it stands, I am constantly terrified she will drift off to Cadiz."

"I shall endeavor, when I marry, to find a young lady skilled in the management of children. That is all I can offer. Miss Hale will not have me, and I shall not ask her."

"So, what have you been discussing?"

"A thousand things beyond your comprehension: investments, leases, the valuation of land."

"Oh, leave if that is all. You and she would be unbearably tedious if you spent your time debating such wearisome subjects."

"Very well. I shall return tomorrow with Mr. Thornton to discuss further matters with Miss Hale."

"Mr. Thornton? What possible relevance does he have?"

"He is Miss Hale's tenant," Mr. Lennox said, turning away abruptly. "And he wishes to surrender his lease."

"Oh! Very well. I cannot grasp the details, so please spare me."

"The only detail that matters is that we secure the back drawing-room undisturbed, just as today. With the children and servants constantly coming and going, I can never conduct business satisfactorily; and the arrangements we must finalize tomorrow are of critical importance."

No one ever discovered why Mr. Lennox failed to appear the following day. Mr. Thornton arrived punctually, yet after waiting nearly an hour, Margaret entered looking pale and deeply anxious.

"I am so sorry Mr. Lennox is absent," she began hurriedly. "He could have handled this far better than I can. He is my adviser in this—"

"I am sorry I came if it causes you trouble. Shall I go to Mr. Lennox's chambers to search for him?"

"No, thank you. I wished to tell you how grieved I am to lose you as a tenant. But Mr. Lennox assures me things are bound to brighten—"

"Mr. Lennox knows little of the matter," Mr. Thornton said quietly. "Happy and fortunate in everything a man desires, he fails to comprehend what it is to realize one is no longer young, yet thrust back to the starting point requiring the hopeful energy of youth. To feel half a life gone, nothing accomplished, nothing remaining of wasted opportunity but the bitter memory of what has been. Miss Hale, I would rather not hear Mr. Lennox's opinion of my affairs. Those who are happy and successful themselves are too prone to dismiss the misfortunes of others."

"You are unjust," Margaret said gently. "Mr. Lennox has merely spoken of the high probability he believes exists for you to redeem—not merely redeem, but surpass what you have lost. Do not speak until I have finished, pray, do not." Gathering herself, she rapidly flipped through legal papers and account statements in a trembling haste. "Oh! Here it is! He drafted a proposal showing that if you were to accept eighteen hundred and fifty-seven pounds of mine, currently idle in the bank yielding only two and a half percent, you could pay me significantly better interest and continue operating Marlborough Mills." Her voice had steadied, and she continued searching for the document detailing the security proposals, desperate to view the situation strictly as a business arrangement where the advantage lay with her.

While she searched, her heart stopped at the tone of Mr. Thornton's voice. It was hoarse, trembling with tender passion as he said:

"Margaret!"

For an instant she looked up, then veiled her luminous eyes by pressing her forehead to her hands. Stepping closer, he beseeched her with another tremulous, eager call.

"Margaret!"

Her head bowed lower, her face more concealed, almost resting on the table. He approached, kneeling beside her to bring his face level with her ear, whispering, panting the words:

"Take care. If you do not speak, I shall claim you as my own in some strange, presumptuous way. Send me away at once if I must go—Margaret!"

At this third call, she turned her face, still covered by her small white hands, toward him, laying it upon his shoulder, hiding it there. It was too exquisite to feel her soft cheek against his for him to wish to see either deep blushes or loving eyes. He clasped her close. They remained silent. Finally, she murmured in a broken voice:

"Oh, Mr. Thornton, I am not good enough!"

"Not good enough! Do not mock my own profound sense of unworthiness."

After a moment or two, he gently freed her hands from her face and laid her arms as he had once placed them to protect him from the rioters.

"Do you remember, love?" he murmured. "And how I repaid you with my insolence the next day?"

"I remember how wrongly I spoke to you—that is all."

"Look here! Lift your head. I have something to show you!" She slowly faced him, glowing with beautiful shame.

"Do you know these roses?" he said, producing his pocket-book, wherein were treasured some dried flowers.

"No!" she replied with innocent curiosity. "Did I give them to you?"

"No! Vanity; you did not. You may have worn similar roses, perhaps."

She examined them, wondering for a moment, then smiled slightly.

"They are from Helstone, are they not? I recognize the deep indentations around the leaves. Oh! Have you been there? When were you there?"

"I wanted to see the place where Margaret grew into what she is, even at the worst of times, when I held no hope of ever calling her mine. I went there upon my return from Havre."

"You must give them to me," she said, attempting to take them from his hand with gentle force.

"Very well. Only you must pay me for them!"

"How shall I ever tell Aunt Shaw?" she whispered after a long, delicious silence.

"Let me speak to her."

"Oh, no! I owe her—but what will she say?"

"I can guess. Her first exclamation will be, 'That man!'"

"Hush!" said Margaret, "or I shall try to show you your mother's indignant tones as she says, 'That woman!'"
